They said that its length, counted from west to east, from the farthest end of Ethiopia to the farthest end of India, is 8000 miles, and its breadth 2700 miles, of which it extends 1900 miles south of the island where night and day are equal. It has a gulf in the land of Ethiopia that reaches towards al-Barbar and is called the Berber Gulf; its length is 500 miles and the breadth of its channel 100 miles. Another gulf goes out towards the land of Ayla: this is the Sea of al-Qulzum. Its length is 1400 miles; the breadth of its channel, which is called the Green Sea, is 200 miles, and its breadth at its mouth 700 miles. Another gulf goes out towards the land of Persia; it is called the Persian Gulf, and it is the Sea of Basra. Its length is 1400 miles, its breadth at its mouth 500 miles, and the breadth of its channel 150 miles. Between these two gulfs, I mean the gulf of Ayla and the gulf of Persia, lie the lands of the Ḥijāz and Yemen; the distance between these two gulfs is 1500 miles. Another gulf also goes out from it, near its end, towards the farthest land of India; it is called the Green Gulf, and its length is 1500 miles.\footnote{The measures of the seas go back, through a Syriac source also used by Jacob of Edessa, to Ptolemy's map: at 75 miles to the degree they agree with it (Nallino). Al-Barbar is the country of Berbera, on the coast of Somalia. The Sea of al-Qulzum is the Red Sea: al-Qulzum is the Greek Clysma, near Suez, and Ayla is the present al-ʿAqaba. Nallino takes the Green Gulf to be Ptolemy's Prasōdēs Sea, the «leek-green sea» (\emph{Geography} VII 2, 1).}\par
\par In this whole sea, I mean the Sea of India and China, there are 1370 islands, inhabited and uninhabited. Among them is an island at its far end, near the land of China, called Taprobane, which is Sarandīb; it is 3000 miles round. It faces India on the east. It has great mountains and many rivers, from which come rubies and sapphires. Around it are 59 inhabited islands, with many towns and villages.\footnote{Taprobane and Sarandīb are names of Sri Lanka. «Rubies and sapphires» is literally «red \emph{yāqūt} and the colour of the sky»: \emph{yāqūt} is corundum, red in the ruby and blue in the sapphire.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{Jacob of Edessa, Ibn Rusta and al-Kharaqī give 19 islands, the number that Ptolemy lists around Taprobane (\emph{Geography} VII 4, 11–13); Nallino concludes that al-Battānī's source had 19, written in letters (\RL{\ArNote يط}), which al-Battānī read as 59 (\RL{\ArNote نط}).}\par
\par As for the western Ocean, which is called the Encircling [Sea], only its western and northern part is known, from the farthest land of Ethiopia to Britain; it is a sea on which ships do not sail. The six islands in it, facing the land of Ethiopia, are the inhabited islands, which are also called the Islands of the Blessed. Another island, facing al-Andalus and called Gadira, lies at the strait. This strait goes out from the Ocean; it is 7 miles wide where it goes out, it lies between al-Andalus and Tangier, it is called Sabṭā, and it goes out into the Sea of the Rūm. In the Ocean, on the north side, are also the islands of Britain, which are twelve islands. Beyond them it goes far away from the inhabited land, and no one knows what it is like or what it holds.\footnote{Gadira is Cadiz, and Sabṭā is Ceuta; the strait is the Strait of Gibraltar. The Sea of the Rūm (the Byzantines) is the Mediterranean.}\par
\par \ArPage{27}The Sea of the Rūm and of Egypt begins at the strait that goes out from the western Ocean near the island called Gadira, facing al-Andalus, and runs east to Tyre and Sidon. Its length is 5000 miles; its breadth is 600 miles in one place, 700 miles in another and 800 miles in another. It has one gulf that goes out towards the north near Rome, 500 miles long, called Adris, and another gulf that goes out towards the land of Narbonne, 200 miles long. In this whole sea there are 162 inhabited islands, five of them large: the island of Corsica, 200 miles round; Sardinia, 300 miles round; Cyprus, 350 miles round; Sicily, 500 miles round; and Crete, 300 miles round.\footnote{Adris is the Adriatic. Corsica is called by its Greek name, Kyrnos (\emph{Qurnus}).}\par
\par The Sea of Pontus extends from Lādhiqa to Constantinople the Great; its length is 1060 miles and its breadth 300 miles. The river called Tanais flows into it; it comes from the north, from the lake called Maeotis, which is a large sea although it is called a lake: its length from east to west is 300 miles and its breadth 100 miles. At Constantinople a channel bursts out of the Pontus, flowing like a river, and pours into the Sea of Egypt; at Constantinople it is about 3 miles wide, and Constantinople lies on it.\footnote{The Pontus is the Black Sea, the Tanais the Don and the Maeotis the Sea of Azov; the channel is the Bosporus. Lādhiqa, also written Lāziqa, is a city or region of the Caucasus (Nallino). For the length of the Pontus Nallino prefers 1300 miles, the figure of Jacob of Edessa, Ibn Rusta and others.}\par
\par The Sea of Jurjān, which is the Sea of al-Bāb, is 800 miles long from west to east and 600 miles wide. In it, facing Jurjān, are two islands that were inhabited in the past. These are the inhabited places in the known seas of the earth; God knows best.\footnote{The Caspian Sea; al-Bāb is Derbent. For its length Nallino prefers 1800 miles, the figure of Ibn Rusta and Jacob of Edessa.}\par
\par The earth has been divided into three parts. The first of them is [bounded] by the Green Sea on the north side, by the channel that goes out from the Pontus into the Greatest Sea, and by what lies between the lake Maeotis and the Pontus. So the boundaries of this region are, on the west and the north, the western sea, which is the Ocean; on the south, the Sea of Egypt and of the Rūm; and on the east, the Tanais and the lake Maeotis. This land is like an island; they called it Europe.\footnote{The Green Sea is here the Atlantic, as often in Arabic authors, and the Greatest Sea the Mediterranean, the «Great Sea» of Orosius (Nallino, after Reinaud).}\par
\par The second part lies to the south, from the Sea of Egypt to the Sea of Ethiopia. The boundaries of this region are, on the west, the Green Sea; on the north, the Sea of Egypt and of the Rūm; on the east, al-ʿArīsh; and on the south, the Sea of Ethiopia. This part is called Libya.\par
\par \ArPage{28}The third part is all the rest of the inhabited earth, to its farthest end. Its boundaries are, on the west, the Tanais, the river, the channel, al-ʿArīsh and Ayla; on the south, the Sea of Yemen and of India; and on the east, the farthest inhabited land of China towards the east, and China itself. This part is called Greater Asia. These three parts take in the climes, the districts and all the inhabited regions.\footnote{The river is the channel at Constantinople that flows like a river; the channel is the Sea of the Rūm, which reaches Tyre and Sidon (Nallino).}\par
\par What is not known to be either inhabited or desolate is 11 parts out of 12. The part that holds the known inhabited land, from the place of the line of equality, also holds the seas and the deserts. If someone asks whether there are plants, animals and habitation in these eleven parts, the answer can only come from inference by analogy and from opinion. The inhabited earth on our side does not go beyond the limits and boundaries we have mentioned, and no one has brought us word of what lies beyond them. But opinion and conjecture, reasoning by analogy, arrive at what no one of understanding will deny. The sun, the moon and the stars move with us, and from their motion, their nearness and their distance come summer and winter, plants, animals and habitation, and all that everyone knows. If the sun and the stars rise over every place of the rest of the circle of the earth as they do with us, then there can be plants, animals, seas and mountains there as with us; and so it should be. One degree comes to about 65 of the miles mentioned above, which is about two days' journey; God knows best.\footnote{The 13,500 miles of the half circumference give 75 miles to the degree. Nallino takes 65 for a slip for 75: the Arabic words for sixty and seventy are easily confused when their dots are left out. Plato of Tivoli has 85.}\par
\par As for the longitudes and latitudes of the cities as they are set down in the \emph{Book of the Image of the Earth}: their positions in longitude, which is the distance between west and east, were counted from the inhabited islands in the western Ocean towards the east. This was done according to what they found of the times of eclipses, those of the moon in particular, which occur earlier in some localities than in others. From this they learned that noon in each locality precedes noon in another locality to the west of it by as many times of the equator as lie between [the times of] the eclipse in the two localities. Some of it they took, approximately, from the reports of travellers.\footnote{The \emph{Book of the Image of the Earth} (\emph{Kitāb ṣūrat al-arḍ}) is not al-Khwārizmī's book of that title: Nallino shows that its author used both Ptolemy's \emph{Geography} and al-Khwārizmī's book, and suggests that it was the Arabic recension of the \emph{Geography} made by Thābit ibn Qurra. Plato of Tivoli's Latin adds «which is called Geography».}\par
\par The latitudes of the cities they took from measuring the sun at noon in the localities. They found its distance from the zenith, as we have explained earlier in this book, and so learned the distance of each locality from the line of equality, which is the distance between south and north. Under each city they set down, approximately, its distance from the Eternal Islands in longitude and from the line of equality in latitude. We have entered these according to the description that we found in the well-known \emph{Book of the Image of the Earth}, and we have also listed separately the middles of the known regions and districts, as Ptolemy did; they are 94 regions. There are errors in the longitudes and latitudes in that book; we shall come back to what is needed of this in the later parts of our book.\footnote{Nallino could not find such a list in Ptolemy: the \emph{Geography} gives no coordinates for the middles of its provinces.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 7. On finding the winter and summer risings and settings on the horizon circles of the localities, from the excess of the longest day or from the altitude of the pole, whichever of the two is known}{07}{\ArPage{29}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: Suppose you wish to find the amounts of the arcs of the horizon circle that lie between the equator and the ecliptic at the horizon in any locality; this is the azimuth of the rising and of the setting of any part of the ecliptic you want. Take the given excess of the longest day and find its amount in degrees, by multiplying each hour of it by 15 degrees. Take half the result and add it to 90; the sum is half the arc of the longest day. Then take the whole inclination, which is the declination of the point of the beginning of Cancer, and subtract it from 90. Find the sine of the remainder: this is the sine of the complement of the declination of Cancer. Multiply it by the sine of half the arc of the longest day, divide the product by the radius, and find the arc of the quotient. Subtract this arc from 90; the remainder is the amount [of the arc] on the horizon circle between the rising and the setting of the beginning of Cancer and the equator, to the north of the equator.\footnote{The azimuth of a rising (\emph{samt al-maṭlaʿ}) is measured on the horizon from the point where the equator rises; in modern terms it is the amplitude of the rising. The excess of the longest day is its excess over 12 hours. In modern terms the rule is cos A = cos δ · sin H, where A is the amplitude, δ the declination and H half the arc of the day.}\par
\par Likewise, if you work with half the arc of the shortest day, which is the day of the beginning of Capricorn, the amount comes out the same, and it is known that the beginning of Capricorn rises and sets to the south of the equator. Clearly the azimuth of the beginning of Cancer in the north is equal to the azimuth of the beginning of Capricorn in the south. These are the summer and winter risings and settings: those on the north side are called the summer ones, and those in the south the winter ones.\par
\par \ArPage{30}If you wish to find the azimuth of the rising and setting of points of the ecliptic other than these two, take the declination of any degree you like, subtract it from 90, and find the sine of the remainder. Then find half the arc of the day of that degree, or take it to be whatever you like; find its sine and multiply it by the sine of the complement of the declination of the degree. Divide the product by the radius, find the arc of the quotient, and subtract it from 90; the remainder is the azimuth of the rising and setting of that degree on the horizon circle. If the declination is northern, it lies to the north of the equator; if it is southern, to the south.\footnote{«Half» is supplied by Nallino; the codex and Plato of Tivoli have «the arc of the day».}\par
\par If the latitude of the locality is given and you wish to find from it the azimuth of the rising and setting of any degree you like, subtract the latitude of the locality from 90; the remainder is the altitude of the beginning of Aries there. Find its sine. Then take the declination of the degree you want and find its sine; multiply it by the radius and divide the product by the sine of the altitude of the beginning of Aries. Find the arc of the quotient: this arc is the distance of the rising and setting of that degree from the rising and setting of the beginning of Aries, on the horizon circle, towards the side of the declination. This is the azimuth, if God wills.\footnote{In modern terms, sin A = sin δ / cos φ, where φ is the latitude; 90° − φ is the altitude of the beginning of Aries when it culminates.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 8. On finding the altitude of the pole from the excess of the longest day}{08}{\ArPage{30}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: Suppose you wish to find the altitude of the north pole of the equator above the horizon, which is the latitude of the locality, from the excess of the longest day over the equinoctial day, or from the shortfall of the shortest day. Take half the excess of the longest day, the day of the first degree of Cancer, in degrees, and add it to 90; the sum is half the arc of the longest day. Or, if you wish, subtract it from 90; the remainder is half the arc of the shortest day. Whichever of the two you work with, it comes to the same. Then subtract the whole inclination from 90 and find the sine of the remainder: this is the sine of the complement of the whole inclination. Multiply the sine of half the arc of the day by the sine of the complement of the whole inclination, divide the product by the radius, and find the arc of the quotient. The arc you obtain is the distance of the rising of the beginning of Cancer from the north point. Subtract it from 90; the remainder is the distance of the rising of the beginning of Cancer from the equinoctial rising point, as we have explained in the preceding chapter. Then multiply the sine of half the excess of the longest day by the sine of the distance of the rising of the beginning of Cancer from the north point, and divide the product by the sine of the distance of the rising of the beginning of Cancer from the rising of the beginning of Aries. Multiply the quotient by the radius, divide the result by the sine of half the arc of the longest day, and find the arc of the quotient. The arc you obtain is the altitude of the pole where the excess of the longest day is the given amount with which you worked.\footnote{The equinoctial day (\emph{al-nahār al-muʿtadil}) is the day of 12 hours. In modern terms the rule is sin φ = sin e · cos A / (sin A · sin H), where φ is the latitude, e half the excess of the longest day, H = 90° + e half the arc of the longest day, and A the amplitude of the rising of the beginning of Cancer. The rule is exact, since sin e = tan φ · tan ε, sin A = sin ε / cos φ and cos A = cos ε · sin H.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{Here the codex writes 90 in Indian figures, «\RL{\ArNote ٩٠}» (Nallino: «so the codex itself»); elsewhere the text writes numbers in words or in abjad letters.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 9. On finding the excess of the longest day, and the lesser excesses of the day, from the altitude of the pole}{09}{\ArPage{31}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: Suppose you wish to find the amount of the excess of the longest day, and of the shortfall of the shortest day, with respect to the equinoctial day, from the altitude of the pole when it is given. Take the latitude of the locality and find its sine. Then subtract the latitude of the locality from 90 and find the sine of the remainder: this is the sine of the complement of the latitude of the locality. Then find the sine of the whole inclination, and the sine of what the whole inclination falls short of 90. Multiply the sine of the latitude of the locality by the sine of the whole inclination, divide the product by the sine of the complement of the whole inclination, multiply the quotient by the radius, divide the result by the sine of the complement of the latitude of the locality, and find the arc of the quotient. The arc you obtain is half the excess of the longest day, and half the shortfall of the shortest day is the same. Double it: the result is the whole excess of the longest day and the whole shortfall of the shortest day. Every 15 degrees of it make one equal hour. Add the hours you obtain to the 12 hours that are the length of the equinoctial day; the sum is the hours of the longest day. Subtract the same excess from 12; the remainder is the hours of the shortest day.\footnote{In modern terms, sin e = tan φ · tan ε, where e is half the excess of the longest day, φ the latitude and ε the inclination.}\par
\par If you wish to know the excess of the day at degrees of the ecliptic other than these two, take the declination of any degree of the signs you like and work with it in place of the whole inclination. What you obtain at the end of the work, done in the same way, is the difference of the day at that degree: if the declination of the degree is northern, it is an excess of the day; if it is southern, it is a shortfall at that degree.\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 10. On finding the altitude and the shadow, each from the other, by computation or from the table}{10}{\ArPage{31}}{}
\par \ArPage{32}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to find the shadow from the altitude, find the sine of the altitude and the sine of what the altitude falls short of 90. Then divide the gnomon into as many parts as you like, multiply the sine of the complement of the altitude by the number of parts of the gnomon, and divide the product by the sine of the altitude. The quotient is the amount by which the shadow extends and stretches over the level ground, in the measure in which the gnomon has that number of parts. What the calculators have worked with, and what we have worked with in this book for the amounts of the shadow, is that the gnomon has 12 parts. It may be divided into fewer or more, as the person computing wishes, because the parts of the shadow are related only to the parts of the gnomon: one says that the shadow is so many parts long, in the measure in which the gnomon was made so many.\footnote{In modern terms the horizontal shadow is g · cot h, where g is the gnomon and h the altitude, and the vertical shadow, described below, is g · tan h.}\par
\par If you wish to find the altitude from the horizontal shadow that we have described, multiply the whole [shadow] by itself, and add to the product the parts of the gnomon multiplied by themselves, which, on the basis we have laid down for the computation, is 144, the gnomon having 12 parts. Take the root of the sum: it is the hypotenuse of the shadow triangle. Keep it. Then multiply the parts of the gnomon by the radius; on the basis we have laid down, this always comes to 720. Divide these 720 by the hypotenuse of the shadow triangle that you kept, and find the arc of the quotient; the arc you obtain is the amount of the altitude. If you wish to compute it another way, multiply the shadow by the radius, divide the product by the hypotenuse of the shadow triangle, and find the arc of the quotient. The arc you obtain is the distance of the degree of the sun, or of another degree, from the zenith on the circle of altitude; subtract it from 90, and the remainder is the altitude.\footnote{«The whole» (\emph{al-kull}) is the whole length of the shadow; Nallino translates «the square of the shadow». The hypotenuse of the shadow triangle is literally «the diameter of the triangle of the shadow» (\emph{quṭr muthallathat al-ẓill}): the line from the tip of the gnomon to the tip of the shadow.}\par
\par As for the erect shadow, I mean the vertical shadow, it is the reverse of the horizontal one: it is longest at noon and shortest at sunrise. If you wish to find this shadow from the altitude, multiply the sine of the altitude by the number of parts of the gnomon, and divide the product by the sine of what the altitude falls short of 90; the quotient is the amount of the shadow in parts of the gnomon.\par
\par If you wish to find the altitude from this vertical shadow, multiply the shadow by itself, add to it the product of the parts of the gnomon by themselves, and take the root of the sum: this is the hypotenuse of the shadow triangle. Then, if you wish, multiply the parts of the gnomon by the radius, divide the product by the hypotenuse of the shadow triangle, and find the arc of the quotient. The result is the amount between the degree of the sun and the zenith on the circle of altitude; subtract it from 90, and the remainder is the altitude. Or, if you wish, multiply the shadow by the radius, divide it by the hypotenuse of the shadow triangle, and find the arc of the quotient: the arc you obtain is the altitude.\par
\par \ArPage{33}If you wish to find either of the two shadows from the altitude with the table, and you want the horizontal shadow, look in the table of the altitude and the shadow, in the column of the altitude, for the altitude you have, and take what stands opposite it in the column of the shadow: that is the amount of the shadow of that altitude. If you wish to find the altitude from this shadow, look for the shadow you want in the column of the digits of the shadow; where you find it, take the degrees of altitude entered opposite it in the column of the altitude: that is the amount of the altitude for that shadow.\footnote{The digit (\emph{iṣbaʿ}, literally «finger») is a twelfth of the gnomon.}\par
\par If there are minutes with the degrees of the altitude, or minutes with the digits of the shadow, find the value by interpolation, as I described to you in the chapter on the declination. That is: if there are minutes with the altitude, see what part of 60 they are, and take the same part of the difference between [the shadows of] the whole degrees and of the degree one greater. Always subtract it from the digits of the shadow of the whole degrees, since every greater altitude has a smaller shadow than a lesser one; the remainder is the shadow of that altitude. If there are minutes with the shadow whose altitude you wish to find, look at the shadow that you find in the table and subtract it from the shadow you have. Find what part the remainder is of the difference between that shadow and the one that belongs to the degree of altitude one less, and take the same part of the 60 minutes by which the column of the altitude increases. Subtract the minutes you obtain from the altitude that you found opposite the shadow you took in the table, the one nearest to your shadow among those smaller than it; the remainder is the altitude.\par
\par If you wish to find the erect shadow, which is the vertical shadow, from the altitude with the table, subtract the altitude from 90 and take the shadow opposite the remainder, interpolating in the same way; the result is the vertical shadow. If you wish to find the altitude from this shadow, look for the shadow you want in the table, take what stands opposite it in the column of the altitude, interpolating, and subtract the result from 90; the remainder is the altitude. The shadow has been entered in this table on the basis that the gnomon is 12 parts, so whatever you work out with it of the shadow is on the basis that the gnomon is 12 digits.\footnote{The vertical shadow of an altitude is the horizontal shadow of its complement: g · tan h = g · cot(90° − h).}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 11. On finding the azimuth of the altitude and of the shadow on the horizon circle, in any locality you want, at all times}{11}{\ArPage{33}}{}
\par \ArPage{34}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to find the azimuth of the altitude and of the shadow at any degree of all the signs, in any locality you want, take the declination of that degree and find its sine, and the direction of the declination. Then subtract this declination from 90 and find the sine of the remainder: this is the sine of the complement of the declination of the degree. Then find the sine of the latitude of the locality and the sine of what the latitude of the locality falls short of 90. Then take the altitude at whatever time of the day you like, and find its sine and the sine of what the altitude falls short of 90. Then multiply the sine of the declination of the degree by the radius and divide the product by the sine of the complement of the latitude of the locality: the result is the sine of the amplitude of the rising. Keep it, and note its direction, which is the direction of the declination. Then multiply the sine of the altitude by the sine of the latitude of the locality and divide the product by the sine of the complement of the latitude of the locality: the result is the sine of the difference of the horizon, which is always southern. If the sine of the amplitude of the rising and the sine of the difference of the horizon are in the same direction, add them; if they are in different directions, subtract the smaller from the greater and note the direction of the remainder. Multiply what you obtain from the addition or the subtraction by the radius, divide it by the sine of the complement of the altitude, and find the arc of the quotient: the result is the azimuth of the altitude.\footnote{In modern terms, sin Z = (sin δ / cos φ − sin h · tan φ) / cos h, where Z is the azimuth counted from the east or west point, positive towards the north, δ the declination, φ the latitude and h the altitude. sin δ / cos φ is the sine of the amplitude of the rising, and sin h · tan φ, always southern, is what al-Battānī calls the sine of the difference of the horizon.}\par
\par The shadow is then at the degree for which you computed. If at that time the degree is between the ascendant and the middle of the sky, this azimuth is counted on the horizon circle from the rising point of the beginning of Aries and of Libra towards the direction that you obtained.\footnote{The codex gives only the case of the eastern half of the sky. Plato of Tivoli's Latin shows that words are missing after «direction»; Nallino restores them in Arabic in the apparatus, and the Latin translation includes them: «[the direction] in which you found the two sines, or the greater of them if they differ; and if the degree is between the middle of the sky and the setting point, this azimuth is counted from the setting point of the beginning of Aries and of Libra on the horizon circle, towards that direction».}\par
\par The azimuth can also be found by another method, with the angles that are used in finding the parallax, which we shall explain later in this book.\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 12. On finding the meridian line, which is the direction of the south}{12}{\ArPage{34}}{}
\par \ArPage{35}Al-Battānī said: Suppose you wish to find the direction of the south, which is the meridian line, in any locality you want and at any time of the year you like, and the place of the sun is not known to you. Go to a place with an open horizon and a smooth, level surface that does not slope, and draw on it a circle of any size you like. Fix at the centre of the circle a thin rod with a pointed tip, straight and without any bend; its best length is a quarter of the diameter of the circle. Measure [the distance] from four sides of the circle to the tip of the rod, so that it stands truly upright on the centre. Then watch the shadow at the beginning of the day, when it is long. It keeps getting shorter; when it reaches the circumference of the circle and is about to enter it, mark its place on the circumference with a point, as a sign of the tip of the shadow. Then wait until noon has passed and the shadow begins to grow; when it reaches the circumference of the circle and is about to leave it, mark the place of its tip on the circumference with a second point, as [a sign of the tip of the shadow].\footnote{The words in brackets at the end are Nallino's conjecture.}\par
\par Then divide the arc between the two points into halves, mark the place of the half of the arc with a point, and from this point draw a straight line through the centre of the circle to the other side of the circumference, extending it as far as you like. To do this, lay the edge of the ruler on the point at the half of the arc and on the centre, and draw a line through both the point and the centre to the circumference on the other side. This line is always the meridian line: whenever the shadow of the rod at the centre falls on this line, it is noon, whether the shadow is long or short. This line is the direction between south and north. Then quarter the circle with another line through the centre of the circle at right angles; the circle is divided by these two lines into four equal quarters, and this line is the direction between east and west. Then write at the ends of the lines the directions of the horizon, that is, east, west, north and south.\footnote{«Half», in «the half of the arc», is lacking in the codex and supplied by Nallino.}\par
\par The nearer the sun is to one of the two solstitial points, the more accurate the observation, because the sun moves slowly in declination between the two observations, I mean the two observations of the shadow. It is known that the moment of noon cannot be fixed exactly, because the sun passes quickly in declination through the meridian circle; but the moment of noon, or the moment nearest to it, is found. This is a diagram of the circle described, if God, the Exalted, wills.\footnote{«In declination», in the second sentence, is Nallino's conjecture (in the emendanda printed in Part II).}\par
\par\begin{center}\begin{tikzpicture}\draw[line width=1.64pt] (124.10pt,116.26pt) circle[radius=95.09pt];
\draw[line width=1.32pt] (123.51pt,211.34pt) -- (124.82pt,21.18pt);
\draw[line width=1.32pt] (29.01pt,115.99pt) -- (219.18pt,116.70pt);
\draw[line width=1.32pt] (57.30pt,48.59pt) -- (191.91pt,49.61pt);
\draw[line width=1.31pt] (124.16pt,116.74pt) -- (57.89pt,48.01pt);
\draw[line width=1.40pt] (124.17pt,115.73pt) -- (192.00pt,49.70pt);
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (121.62pt,227.04pt) {\normalsize D};
\node[inner sep=0pt, rotate=-45.0] at (99.93pt,141.09pt) {\scriptsize indicating rod};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (129.06pt,121.80pt) {\normalsize H};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (248.64pt,111.18pt) {\scriptsize west};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (227.16pt,115.32pt) {\normalsize Ṭ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (23.58pt,113.34pt) {\normalsize Z};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (0.00pt,111.48pt) {\scriptsize east};
\node[inner sep=0pt, rotate=-45.0] at (168.36pt,86.58pt) {\scriptsize decreasing shadow};
\node[inner sep=0pt, rotate=45.0] at (80.76pt,85.80pt) {\scriptsize increasing shadow};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (195.30pt,43.98pt) {\normalsize A};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (128.94pt,44.16pt) {\normalsize W};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (48.72pt,44.46pt) {\normalsize B};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (127.56pt,0.00pt) {\normalsize G};\end{tikzpicture}\footnote{As in the print, the south (D) is at the top, so that the east (Z) is on the left and the west (Ṭ) on the right. The letters of the figures are transliterated: \RL{\ArNote ا} A, \RL{\ArNote ب} B, \RL{\ArNote ج} G, \RL{\ArNote د} D, \RL{\ArNote ه} H, \RL{\ArNote و} W, \RL{\ArNote ز} Z, \RL{\ArNote ح} Ḥ, \RL{\ArNote ط} Ṭ, \RL{\ArNote ك} K, \RL{\ArNote ل} L, \RL{\ArNote م} M.}\end{center}
\par \ArPage{36}Al-Battānī said: Draw a circle about the centre H, and fix the indicating rod at the place of point H. Mark point A at the place of the tip of the shadow before noon, and the sign B at the place of its tip after noon. Divide the arc AB into halves at the sign G, and draw the line from G to the sign D. The line GHD is the meridian line; point D is the direction of the south, and point G the direction of the north. Then divide the arc GAD into halves at point Ṭ, and draw through the points Ṭ and H a line that goes out to point Z. Point Z is the direction of the east, and point Ṭ the direction of the west. Whenever the shadow of the indicating rod falls on the line HG, it is always noon.\footnote{The indicating rod (\emph{al-ʿamūd al-mūrī}, «the rod that shows») is the rod set up at the centre.}\par
\par If the place of the sun is known to you, find the azimuth of any altitude you like at some time of the day, and watch for the given altitude. When the altitude becomes equal to it, mark on the circumference of the circle, at the shadow of the indicator, a sign that stands in the middle of the breadth of the shadow of the indicating rod. Then divide the quarter of the circle in which this point falls into 90 parts, and count from that point, towards the side opposite to the direction of the azimuth of that altitude, as many parts as the azimuth of the altitude. The division at which you arrive is the east point or the west point, according to the time of the altitude with which you worked, before noon or after it. From this sign draw a line through the centre of the circle, and quarter the circle with another line through the centre at right angles. Then you know the meridian line by this line, and the line of east and west by the line before it.\par
\par Likewise, if the horizon is in view from this circle at sunrise or sunset, the east point or the west point is known from the azimuth of the rising or setting of the degree of the sun on the horizon circle, which we have drawn as ABGD.\par
\par \ArPage{37}If you wish to find the line between east and west in another way, and through it the line between north and south, this is done by finding the altitude whose azimuth has no inclination from the equinoctial rising or setting point. This is possible only when the sun is in the northern signs, from the beginning of Aries to the end of Virgo. This altitude is found as follows. Find the place of the sun in the ecliptic on the day you want, and its altitude at noon on the day for which you work. Then find the sine of this altitude and the sine of what it falls short of 90. Then find the azimuth of the rising or setting of the sun, at its place in the ecliptic at the noon for which you work; by the condition we have laid down it is always northern. Then take the sine of this azimuth and multiply it by the sine of the altitude, divide the product by the sine of the azimuth and the sine of the complement of the altitude added together, and find the arc of the quotient. The arc you obtain is the altitude whose azimuth has no inclination. When you know this altitude, watch the sun; when its altitude becomes equal to this altitude, mark a point on the circumference of the circle in the middle of the shadow of the indicator. It is the east point or the west point, according to the time at which you measure, that is, the equinoctial rising or setting point. Quarter the circle at this point with two lines that intersect at the centre at right angles, and with them mark the directions of the horizon.\footnote{The altitude whose azimuth has no inclination is the altitude of the sun when it stands due east or due west; al-Battānī uses it again for the sundial in chapter 56 (Nallino). In modern terms the rule is sin h = sin A · sin H / (sin A + cos H), where A is the amplitude of the rising and H the altitude at noon; it is exact, and equal to sin δ / sin φ.}\par
\par We give an example of this, and place it in the fourth clime, where the altitude of the pole is 36° 22′. We take the place of the sun at the beginning of Cancer. The altitude of the sun at noon is then 77° 13′, and its altitude at midnight, from the northern horizon, 30° 3′; it is known that this is equal to the altitude above the earth, on the line of the middle of the sky, of the degree opposite to it, which is 30° 3′. We also find this in another way, by doubling the altitude of the beginning of Aries in the said clime and subtracting from the result the altitude of the beginning of Cancer in the middle of the sky. Clearly the altitude of the beginning of Aries in the middle of the sky in this clime is 53° 38′, and its double 107° 16′; when 77° 13′ is subtracted from this, there remains its altitude in the middle of the sky below the earth, 30° 3′. We shall show that the azimuth of the beginning of Cancer at its rising in this clime lies 30° 0′ to the north of the rising of Aries.\footnote{For 30° 0′ the codex has 30° 3′; Nallino reads 30° 0′ with Plato of Tivoli, the value used at the end of the chapter, where the sine of this azimuth is taken as 30 parts.}\par
\par Since this is as we have described, we draw a circle of the middle of the sky, ABL, about its centre H, with the diameter LB. Let the diameter LB be half the horizon, and let point A be the place of the zenith. We join point A to point H, so that the arc BA is the quarter of the circle between the zenith and the horizon. Point H is the place of the rising of the beginning of Aries, and point G the place of the rising of the beginning of Cancer: for HB is the southern half of the horizon, the line HL the northern half of the horizon, and the line HA the line of the quarter circle that passes through the zenith and the rising point of the beginning of Aries. We mark at the point of the beginning of Cancer on the circle of the middle of the sky the point Z. The arc BZ is the altitude of the sun at noon, and the arc ZA its distance from the zenith, which is the complement of the altitude to a quarter of the circle. We mark at the altitude of the sun at midnight the point Ṭ, so that the arc ṬL is the arc of the altitude at midnight below the earth, and we draw the line ṬZ, which passes through the point G at which the beginning of Cancer rises. The point common to the line ṬZ and the line HA is the place at which the sun, when it rises to it, is on the azimuth of H, from which the beginning of Aries rises; it then has no inclination from the azimuth of the equinoctial risings, since the line from the zenith passes through the place of the sun and through point H of the horizon. We mark the place of the sun on the line HA with the sign M.\footnote{The figure is the meridian circle, with the horizon, the vertical through the east point and the day circle of the sun seen edge on as the straight lines LB, HA and ṬZ. Here and below Nallino corrects several letters of the figure where the codex has others; the translation follows Nallino's text.}\par
\par \ArPage{38}It is clear in this figure that the line HG is the line of the azimuth of the rising of the beginning of Cancer, and it is the sine of the azimuth. Further, we draw from point Z a perpendicular to the line HB, parallel to the line HA: this is the perpendicular ZK, which is the sine of the altitude at noon, so that the line KH that remains is the sine of the arc ZA, which is the complement of the altitude. We want to find the line HM, which is the sine of the altitude whose azimuth has no inclination; for the line HM is equal to the perpendicular DḤ, which is clearly the sine of the arc BD, the amount of the altitude that we seek, since the circle ABL passes through the zenith and the point of the beginning of Cancer. Now the right-angled triangle ZKG has known sides, and it is similar to the small triangle MHG, since the angle MHG is equal to the angle ZKH, the angle GMH is equal to the angle KZG, and the angle KGZ is common to the two triangles. Therefore the ratio of the line ZK to the line KG is as that of the line MH to the line HG. Further, the ratio of the line HG to the line GK is as that of HM to KZ, and this is also the ratio of the line GM to the line GZ. If we take of the line KZ the part given by the ratio [of the line GM to the line GZ], there remains for us the ratio of the line HM to the line KZ; and it is the same if we take of the line KZ the part that the line GH is of the line GK: we obtain the ratio of HM to KZ.\footnote{The print names the small triangle «\RL{\ArNote م ه ح}», MHḤ; the angles that follow show that it is MHG, as in Nallino's translation. The words in brackets are Nallino's; the codex has «the line KG to the ratio of the line GM».}\par
\par\begin{center}\begin{tikzpicture}\draw[line width=1.42pt] (122.20pt,109.83pt) circle[radius=109.83pt];
\draw[line width=1.20pt] (121.18pt,219.65pt) -- (123.05pt,0.01pt);
\draw[line width=1.32pt] (12.37pt,109.23pt) -- (232.02pt,110.87pt);
\draw[line width=1.31pt] (69.36pt,206.11pt) -- (219.21pt,58.35pt);
\draw[line width=1.32pt] (69.31pt,206.08pt) -- (69.36pt,109.65pt);
\draw[line width=1.20pt] (21.68pt,154.07pt) -- (121.74pt,154.65pt);
\draw[line width=1.44pt] (21.50pt,153.67pt) -- (22.10pt,109.30pt);
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (123.60pt,236.26pt) {\normalsize A};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (62.76pt,210.94pt) {\normalsize Z};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (127.38pt,157.84pt) {\normalsize M};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (16.26pt,155.14pt) {\normalsize D};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (242.16pt,114.10pt) {\normalsize L};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (161.88pt,102.16pt) {\normalsize G};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (117.06pt,104.62pt) {\normalsize H};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (68.82pt,101.68pt) {\normalsize K};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (22.56pt,100.78pt) {\normalsize Ḥ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (0.00pt,107.56pt) {\normalsize B};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (225.60pt,58.06pt) {\normalsize Ṭ};\end{tikzpicture}\end{center}
\par The computation of this: multiply the line HG, which has been shown to be 30 parts, by the line KZ, which is 58 parts 31 minutes and is the sine of the arc BZ; the result is 1755 parts and half a part. The line KH, which is the sine of the complement of the altitude, is 13 parts 17 minutes, so the lines HG and HK together are 43 parts 17 minutes, which is the whole line KG. When we divide that by the line KG, the result is 40 parts 33 minutes, which is the amount of the line HM that we seek, and the line DḤ is equal to it. Therefore the arc DB is 42° 32′, and this is the altitude whose azimuth has no inclination. This is what we wished to show, if God wills.\footnote{«And five», in 1755, is supplied by Nallino; the codex has 1750. The quotient 1755;30 / 43;17 is 40;33,29 (Nallino).}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{At latitude 36° 22′ the sine of the amplitude of the beginning of Cancer, HG, is 29;49 parts, not 30, and the rule then gives 42° 26′, the exact altitude of the sun on the prime vertical (sin h = sin δ / sin φ). The 42° 32′ of the text comes from taking HG as 30.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 13. On finding the ascensions of the signs in every locality in two ways, by computation and from the table, and on the procedures with them that follow from this, if God wills}{13}{\ArPage{39}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: The ascensions of the signs at the place of the equator we have described above: there the signs cross the middle of the sky and the horizon with the same amount of times of the equator, and with this amount they also cross the middle of the sky in every locality. At the other places, those that decline from it towards the north, in all the localities, their ascensions differ from horizon to horizon. For when a locality has a latitude, that is, when it declines from the equator, the ascensions of the signs vary there, being greater or smaller than their ascensions in the middle of the sky, which are their ascensions in the right sphere. Every sign that rises in a locality with more than it rises with in the right sphere has a counterpart that rises in that locality with less than it rises with in the right sphere, by the amount of that excess; and in every locality each sign sets with as much as its counterpart rises with there.\footnote{The counterpart (\emph{naẓīr}) of a sign or a degree is the sign or degree opposite to it on the ecliptic.}\par
\par \ArPage{40}If you wish to know the amount of the equator that rises together with given parts of the ecliptic in any locality you want, take [the arc] from the beginning of Aries to the degree of the ecliptic you want, in ascensions of the right sphere. Find its sine, multiply it by the sine of half the excess of the longest day in that locality, divide the product by the radius, and find the arc of the quotient. The arc you obtain is the share of the difference of the day, in the quarter of the circle, that belongs to [the arc] from the first degree of Aries to that degree. Keep it. If you wish to find it another way, multiply the sine of the latitude of the locality by the sine of the declination of the degree, divide the product by the sine of what the latitude of the locality falls short of 90, multiply the quotient by the radius, divide it by the sine of what the declination of the degree falls short of 90, and find the arc of the quotient. The arc you obtain is the difference of the day in the quarter of the circle of the sphere. When you have found this share in either of the two ways, look: if the declination of the degree is northern, subtract the share you obtained from the times of the ascensions in the right sphere between the beginning of Aries and that degree; if the declination of the degree is southern, add the share to those ascensions. The ascensions you obtain after the addition or the subtraction are the ascensions from the beginning of Aries to that degree in that locality.\footnote{In modern terms the share is the ascensional difference Δ: sin Δ = sin α · sin e = tan φ · tan δ, where α is the ascension in the right sphere, e half the excess of the longest day, φ the latitude and δ the declination. The ascension at the locality is α − Δ for a northern declination and α + Δ for a southern one.}\par
\par Know that the ascension of Pisces is equal to that of Aries, the ascension of Virgo to that of Libra, the ascension of Aquarius to that of Taurus, the ascension of Capricorn to that of Gemini, the ascension of Sagittarius to that of Cancer, and the ascension of Leo to that of Scorpio. So for finding the ascensions it is enough to know the shares between the beginning of Aries and the beginning of Cancer, that is, from 1 degree to 90 degrees.\par
\par If you wish to tabulate the ascensions degree by degree, or at larger intervals, find the share of the difference of the day for one degree, for two degrees, three and four, up to the full 90 that complete the difference of the whole quarter of the circle. When you have done this, take the ascension in the right sphere of the first degree of Aries and put it in two places. Then subtract the share of the degree from one of the two places and add it to the other. The one subtracted from is the ascension of the first degree of Aries, and the one added to is the ascension of the first degree of Libra. Add the latter to 180: the sum is the ascension from the beginning of Aries to the first degree of Libra. Subtract it from 180: the remainder is the ascension from the beginning of Aries to 29 degrees of Virgo. Also subtract the ascension of the degree of Aries from 360: the remainder is the ascension from the beginning of Aries to 29 degrees of Pisces. Do the same with the share of two degrees, three and four, up to the full 90, until you have divided [the ascensions] over the whole sphere at whatever interval of the parts you want, if God wills.\footnote{«Subtract it from 180»: the codex has «from 360»; Nallino corrects it.}\par
\par We have entered the ascensions of the signs for the city of al-Raqqa in full, degree by degree, and for the other climes at intervals of 10 degrees, because the ascensions differ little within an interval of this size. In the ascensions entered in the tables we have made the excess of the day increase by a quarter of an equal hour [from one table to the next], so that they are more accurate for the work with the ascensions that is needed, and more exact than ascensions computed at intervals of half an hour.\par
\par \ArPage{41}If you wish to find the ascension of any degree you like from the table, look for the degrees whose ascension you wish to know, of whatever sign you like, in the common column of numbers in the table of the ascensions of the signs for the given clime, or in the ascensions of the right sphere, whichever you want, and take the times of ascension that stand opposite them in the column of the sign to which that number belongs. If you are working with the ascensions of the climes, this is the ascension from the beginning of Aries to that degree; if you are working with the ascensions of the right sphere, it is the ascension from the beginning of Capricorn to that degree. If there are minutes with the degrees, find what part of 60 they are, when the numbers increase by one degree, and take the same part of the difference between those ascensions and the ascensions that follow them, one degree further. Add the result to the ascensions you obtained opposite the whole degrees; the sum is the ascension of the degree and minute that you wanted. If the numbers increase by 10 degrees, see what part of the 10 degrees are the degrees and minutes that you have left over beyond what you find in the table, and take the same part of the differences of the ascensions in the table, I mean of the ascensions you found and those 10 degrees further on in the numbers. Add the result to the ascensions under the tens; the sum is the ascension of that degree.\par
\par If you wish to find the degrees of the signs from the ascensions, which is called taking the arc of the ascensions and converting them into degrees of equality, that is, degrees of the signs: look for the number of times of ascension that you have in the table of the ascensions of the right sphere or of the clime, whichever you want. Where you find it, or the nearest value smaller than it, take the degree of the signs entered opposite it in the common column of numbers: that is the degree you want of the sign in which you found the number of times. Then subtract [the times found in the table from] the times you have, and look at the remainder: if the numbers increase by one degree, multiply it by 60 minutes; if they increase by 10 degrees, multiply it by 600 minutes. Divide the product by the difference of the ascensions between that entry and the entry that follows it, and add the degrees and minutes that result from the division to the degrees you obtained first. The result is the amount of that sign that rises, or that culminates, according to which [table] you worked with. Or, if you wish, see what part of the difference of the ascensions the remainder is, and take the same part of the interval of the numbers; add the result to the degrees you had obtained.\footnote{Degrees of equality (\emph{daraj al-sawāʾ}) are degrees of the ecliptic, as against the times of the equator that rise or culminate with them; the Alfonsine Tables call them «equal degrees» (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{42}If you wish to find the arc of the day and of the night from the table, that is, the amount of the equator that rises from sunrise to sunset, or from sunset to sunrise the next day: find the degree in which the sun is on the day you want, and take the times of ascension opposite it in the given clime whose latitude is equal to that of the city, or nearer to it than the other climes. Subtract them from the ascensions opposite the degree opposite to the degree of the sun in that clime; the remainder is the amount of the arc of the day. If the ascensions of the degree of the sun are greater than those of the degree opposite to it, which is the counterpart of the degree of the sun, add a revolution to the ascensions of the counterpart of the degree of the sun and subtract from the sum the ascensions of the degree of the sun: the remainder is the arc of the day. When you know the arc of the day, subtract it from a revolution: the remainder is the arc of the night.\par
\par If you want the arc of the day in another way, take the times of ascension opposite the degree of the sun in the clime, and also the times of ascension opposite the degree of the sun in the right sphere, and subtract 90 from the latter, so that they are counted from the beginning of Aries. When you have done this, take the difference between them and the ascensions that you obtained from the climes. Then look: if the times of the ascensions of the climes are the greater, subtract this difference from 90; if they are the smaller, add this difference to 90. The 90 after the addition or subtraction is the amount of half the arc of the day; double it, and you have the whole arc of the day. Know that this difference between the ascensions is the share of the degree of the sun in the difference, I mean the difference of the day. When you know it, look at the degree of the sun: if it is in the northern signs, add the difference to 90; if it is in the southern ones, subtract it from 90. The result is half the arc of the day, which is the part of the equator from the rising of the sun to its culmination at noon, and its double is the whole arc of the day; the two procedures come to the same.\par
\par If you wish to know the number of the equal hours of the day and of the night, divide the arc of the day or the arc of the night by 15; the result is the hours of whichever of the two you computed. When you know the hours of one of them, subtract them from 24; the remainder is the hours of the other.\par
\par If you wish to find the times of the seasonal hours of the day and of the night, of which there are always 12 in the day and as many in the night, and which are called the crooked hours, divide the arc of whichever you like, the day or the night, by 12; the result is the times of its hours. Subtract the times of the hours of whichever you computed from 30; there remain the times of the hours of the other. For these 30 are the times of two equal hours, and what the times of an hour of the night or of the day lack is added to the other.\footnote{The times of a seasonal hour are its length in degrees of the equator; an equal hour is 15 times. The translation follows Nallino's emendanda (Part II, p. XXX) in reading «\RL{\ArNote المُعْوَجَّة}», «the crooked», at p. 42, line 15.}\par
\par \ArPage{43}If you wish to find the times of the hours in another way, take a sixth of the difference of the day mentioned earlier in this chapter. If the sun, or the degree you want, is in the northern half of the sphere, add this sixth to 15; if it is in the southern half, subtract it from 15. The result after the addition or subtraction is the times of the hours of the day. If you wish to find the times of the hours of the day from the table, enter the degree of the sun, or any other degree of the signs, in the common column of numbers of the table of the ascensions of the clime that is given for that city, and take the times of the hours entered opposite it in the column of the sign to which that number belongs; the result is the times of the hours of the day. If you wish to know the times of the hours of the night, enter in those ascensions the counterpart of the degree of the sun, which is opposite to it, or the counterpart of the degree you want, and take the times of the hours opposite it in the same way; the result is the times of the hours of the night. Either can also be found from the other, by subtracting one of them from 30 degrees: there remain the times of the other.\footnote{The 12 seasonal hours of the day take 180° + 2Δ of the equator, where Δ is the difference of the day; one of them is therefore 15 + Δ/6 times.}\par
\par If you wish to find the arc of the day from the times of the hours, or the arc of the night from them, whichever you like, multiply the times of whichever you want by 6; the result is the arc of half the day or of half the night, whichever you computed. Then double it; the result is its whole arc. If you multiply the parts of the times by 12, the result is the amount of the arc of the day or of the night for the degree for which you computed.\par
\par If you wish to convert equinoctial hours into seasonal ones, multiply the equal hours by 15 and divide by the times of the hours of the day or of the night, whichever you like; the result is seasonal hours of the night or of the day, according to what those equal hours were. If you wish to convert seasonal hours into equal ones, multiply the hours of the day by the times of the hours of the day, and the hours of the night by the times of the hours of the night, and divide the product by 15; the result is equal hours, with their fractions if a fraction remains, if God wills.\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 14. On finding the latitudes of localities by observation}{14}{\ArPage{43}}{}
\par \ArPage{44}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to find the latitude of any locality you like, which is the altitude of the north pole there and also its distance from the equator, find the altitude of the sun at noon, in whichever way you like, when the sun passes the meridian line: with the quadrant or by finding the shadow. When you know the altitude, in either of the two ways, find the declination of the degree of the sun at that time. If it is northern, subtract it from the altitude; if it is southern, add it to it. The altitude after the addition or subtraction is the altitude of the beginning of Aries or of the beginning of Libra in that locality; subtract it from 90, and the remainder is the latitude of that locality. If you take the latitude of the locality from the table of the latitudes of the cities, it is approximate, and not as accurate as one found by observation.\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 15. On finding the altitude of the sun at noon on any day you want}{15}{\ArPage{44}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: If you wish to find the altitude of the sun at noon on any day you want, find the declination of the degree in which the sun is. If it is northern, subtract it from the latitude of the locality; if it is southern, add it to it. Subtract the latitude of the locality, after the addition or the subtraction, from 90; the remainder is the altitude of the sun at noon. If the declination is greater than the latitude of the locality, you know that the sun is to the north of the zenith: add 90 to the latitude of the locality and subtract the declination of the degree; the remainder is equal to the altitude above the northern horizon.\par
\par If you wish to find the altitude of the sun at noon in another way, subtract the latitude of the locality from 90; the remainder is the altitude of the beginning of Aries. If the declination is northern, add it to the altitude [of the beginning of Aries]; if it is southern, subtract it from it. The altitude of the beginning of Aries after the addition or the subtraction is the altitude of the sun at noon. If it exceeds 90, subtract it from 180; the remainder is the altitude above the northern horizon.\footnote{The words in brackets are supplied by Nallino; the codex lacks them.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 16. On finding how many hours of the day have passed, by measuring the sun, and on finding the ascendant}{16}{\ArPage{44}}{}
\par \ArPage{45}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know how many hours of the day have passed, by measuring the sun, find the altitude of the sun at noon on that day, and then half the arc of the day on that day. Then measure the altitude of the sun with a quadrant or by the shadow. When you know the altitude, at whatever time of the day you like, find the versed sine of half the arc of the day, in the way set out at the beginning of the book, in the chapter on finding the versed sines from the arcs. Then take the sine of the altitude of the sun at the time of the measurement, multiply it by the versed sine of half the arc of the day, and divide the product by the sine of the altitude at noon. Subtract the quotient from the versed sine of half the arc of the day, and find the arc of the remainder as a versed sine, as I described for taking the arc of the versed sines; keep the arc you obtain. If the measurement was made before noon, subtract this arc from half the arc of the day; if it was made after noon, add it to half the arc of the day. Half the arc of the day, after the addition or the subtraction, is the amount of the sphere that has turned from sunrise to the time of the measurement. Divide it by the times of the hours of the day, I mean of that day, found from the degree of the sun; the result is the number of seasonal hours of the day that have passed. If you divide it by 15, they are equal hours.\footnote{In modern terms vers t = vers H · (1 − sin h / sin h₀), where t is the distance of the sun from the meridian in times (the hour angle), H half the arc of the day, h the altitude and h₀ the altitude at noon. The rule is exact.}\par
\par If you wish to find the ascendant from the amount of the sphere that has turned, add the amount of the sphere that has turned to the times of ascension opposite the degree of the sun in the clime; from the sum you find the ascendant, in the way that we have explained earlier in the book. If you wish, take the arc that you obtained as a versed sine: it is the distance of the sun from the line of the middle of the sky. Divide it by the times of the hours of the day, and subtract the result from 6 hours if the measurement was made before noon, or add it to 6 hours if the measurement was made after noon; the result is the number of seasonal hours of the day that have passed, which you can convert into equinoctial hours if you wish. If you wish to find the ascendant from this arc, subtract it from the ascension of the degree of the sun in the right sphere if the measurement was made before noon, and add it to the ascension if the measurement was made after noon. With the ascension you obtain you find the ascendant and the middle of the sky, by taking the arc of these ascensions in the clime and in the right sphere and finding the degrees of the signs that correspond to them, in the way described in the chapter on finding the degrees of the signs from the times of ascension.\footnote{The ascension in the right sphere is counted from the beginning of Capricorn (chapter 5): taken in the table of the clime, it gives the ascendant; taken in the table of the right sphere, the degree in the middle of the sky.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 17. On finding the altitude from the hours}{17}{\ArPage{46}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: If you wish to find the altitude from the hours of the day that have passed, take the hours from sunrise to the given hour. If they are equinoctial hours, multiply them by 15; if they are seasonal, multiply them by the times of the hours of that day. Look at what you obtain in either way: if it is less than half the arc of the day, subtract it from half the arc of the day; if it is more, take half the arc of the day from it. The result is the distance of the sun from the middle of the sky. Find its versed sine and subtract it from the versed sine of half the arc of the day. Multiply the remainder by the sine of the altitude of the sun at noon on that day, divide the product by the versed sine of half the arc of the day, and find the arc of the quotient, as I described to you for taking the arc of the sines. The arc you obtain is the amount of the altitude above the horizon at the given hour. [If the hour is] before noon, the altitude is on the east side; if it is after noon, it is on the west side.\footnote{In modern terms sin h = sin h₀ · (vers H − vers t) / vers H, with the letters of the note to chapter 16: the same relation, solved for the altitude.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 18. On finding the distances of the stars from the equator, and the parts of the signs that culminate with them}{18}{\ArPage{46}}{\footnote{The translation reads «\RL{\ArNote تجوز}» for «\RL{\ArNote نجوز}» (p. 47, line 3), a misprint that the corrigenda do not list.}}
\par \ArPage{47}Al-Battānī said: Suppose you wish to find the distances of the stars from the equator, and the part of the signs that culminates [with them], from their positions in longitude and latitude. Every star that is on the circle of the signs, that is, that has no latitude, behaves like the sun in its declination from the equator, which is its distance from it. But for a star that has a latitude on one side or the other, its distance from the equator falls short of its latitude and the declination of the part in which it is, when the two are added together or one is subtracted from the other, as the case requires. For the latitude of a star is measured on an arc that passes through the poles of the ecliptic and the position of the star in longitude and latitude, while its distance from the equator is measured on an arc that passes through the poles of the equator and the position of the star. Therefore a star that has a latitude culminates with a part of the signs other than its own, except one that in its course coincides with the point of the beginning of Cancer or of the beginning of Capricorn. Only then do its distance from the equator and its latitude lie on one arc, so that its distance is equal to the declination with the latitude of the star added to it or subtracted from it; and so it culminates with that one of the two points in which it is. Of the stars that have latitude, one between the beginning of Cancer and the end of Sagittarius culminates after the culmination of the degree in which it is, if its latitude is to the north of the belt of the signs, and before the culmination of the degree in which it is, if its latitude is to the south. One between the beginning of Capricorn and the end of Gemini culminates, if its latitude is to the north, with parts that precede the part in which it is, I mean before the culmination of the part in which it is; and if its latitude is to the south, with parts that follow the part in which it is, I mean after the culmination of the part of the signs in which it is.\footnote{Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary, found the second sentence obscure. With «the declination of the part» understood as its second declination (see the next note), it is a correct statement: the declination δ of a star is never greater than its latitude β and the second declination β′ of its degree taken together, since sin δ = sin(β ± β′) · cos ε / cos β′ and cos β′ ≥ cos ε; it is equal to them only when β′ = ε, at the beginnings of Cancer and Capricorn, as the following sentences say.}\par
\par If you wish to find the distance from the equator of one of the stars that have latitude, and the parts of the signs with which it culminates, find the latitude of the star and its direction, and the declination of the part in which it falls. If the latitude and the declination are in the same direction, add them together; if they are in different directions, subtract the smaller from the greater. The result is the adjusted latitude; note the direction in which it falls. Then take the sine of this adjusted latitude, multiply it by the sine of what the whole inclination falls short of 90, divide the product by the sine of what the part falls short of 90, that is, of the complement of its declination, and find the arc of the quotient. The arc you obtain is the distance of the star from the equator, in the direction of the adjusted latitude. Keep it.\footnote{The «declination» of the part here must be its second declination: the arc of the circle through the poles of the ecliptic between the degree of the star and the equator. With it the rule is exact, sin δ = sin(β ± β′) · cos ε / cos β′ (Nallino, after Schiaparelli, who derives it from Menelaus' theorem). Al-Battānī calls both declinations by the same name, and does not say here how the second is found.}\par
\par \ArPage{48}Then take the distance of the degree in which the star is from the beginning of Cancer or the beginning of Capricorn, whichever the degree is nearer to, before it or behind it. That is: take the times of ascension in the right sphere opposite the part in which the star is. If they are less than 90, take them as they are; if they are more than 270, subtract them from 360. What you obtain in either case is the distance of the star from the beginning of Capricorn. If they are more than 90, up to 180, subtract them from 180 and take the remainder; if they are more than 180, up to the full 270, take 180 from them. What you obtain in either case is the distance of the degree from the beginning of Cancer. Keep whichever it is, and find its sine as well. Then take the sine of the latitude of the star, multiply it by the sine of the whole inclination, and divide the product by the sine of the complement of the distance of the star from the equator. Multiply the quotient by the sine of the distance of the degree in which the star is from the beginning of Cancer or of Capricorn in the right sphere, which you have kept, divide the product by the radius, and find the arc of the quotient. The arc you obtain is the difference of the passage of the star through the middle of the sky.\footnote{The translation keeps the reading of the codex and of Plato of Tivoli, «by the radius». Nallino, following Schiaparelli, prints in its place «by the sine of what the declination of the degree in which the star is falls short of 90», holding the rule to be false otherwise. The emendation is not needed. The distance of the star's degree from the solstitial point is taken here in the right sphere, as an arc of the equator, and the sine of that distance is cos α, where α is the right ascension of the degree; with it the rule of the codex is exact: sin Δ = sin β · sin ε · cos α / cos δ, where Δ is the difference of the passage. Schiaparelli's own derivation, sin Δ = sin β · sin ε · cos L / (cos D · cos δ), with L the longitude and D the declination of the degree, is the same rule, since cos L = cos α · cos D; Schiaparelli's objection takes the distance to be measured on the ecliptic. With the emendation the result is too large by the factor 1/cos D: for a star at longitude 60° and latitude 10° north, the codex's rule gives 2.45°, the true value, and the emended one 2.61°.}\par
\par If the star is between the beginning of Cancer and the end of Sagittarius and its latitude is northern, add the difference of its passage through the middle of the sky to the times of ascension in the right sphere opposite the part in which the star is; if its latitude is southern, subtract the difference of its passage from these times, and proceed as before. If the star is between the beginning of Capricorn and the end of Gemini and its latitude is northern, subtract the difference of its passage from these times; if its latitude is southern, add the difference of its passage to the times. Then take the degree of the signs that stands opposite the times of ascension of the star's degree in the right sphere, after the addition or the subtraction, in the ascensions of the right sphere: that is the part of the signs that culminates with the star, I mean [a degree] of the sign in which you found the number of times, if God wills.\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 19. On finding the arc of the day of a star, and the times of its hours, in every locality}{19}{\ArPage{48}}{}
\par \ArPage{49}Al-Battānī said: Suppose you wish to find the arc of the day of any star you like, that is, its stay above the earth from the time it rises until it sets, which is the part of the equator that rises from the rising of the star to its setting. Multiply the sine of the distance of the star from the equator by the sine of the latitude of the locality, and divide the product by the sine of what the latitude of the locality falls short of 90. Multiply the quotient by the radius, divide the product by the sine of what the distance of the star from the equator falls short of 90, and find the arc of the result. The arc you obtain is the difference of the quarter of the circle of the star. If the distance of the star from the equator is northern, add the difference of the quarter of its circle to 90; if it is southern, subtract it from 90. The result after the addition or the subtraction is half the arc of the day of the star; keep it. Then take a sixth of it: the result is the times of its hours above the earth. Then double half the arc of its day: the result is the whole arc of the day of the star above the earth. Subtract it from 360: the remainder is the whole arc of its night below the earth. Likewise subtract the times of its hours of the day from 30: the remainder is the times of its hours of the night, which are below the earth. As for a star that has no latitude, its arc of the day is the arc of the day of the degree in which it is, without any difference, because it runs exactly the same course as the sun.\footnote{The difference of the quarter of the circle of the star is its ascensional difference, the share of chapter 13: sin Δ = tan φ · tan δ, where δ is here the distance of the star from the equator.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 20. On finding the degree of the ecliptic with which a star rises and the one with which it sets, in every locality}{20}{\ArPage{49}}{\footnote{The print has «\RL{\ArNote الّني}» for «\RL{\ArNote الّتي}» four times on p. 50 (lines 1, 6, 10 and 12), a dot missing.}}
\par Al-Battānī said: If you wish to find the degree of the signs with which a star rises and the one with which it sets, take half the arc of the day of the star and half the arc of the day of the degree that culminates with it, and find the difference between them: this is half the difference of the two days; keep it. Then look: if half the arc of the day of the star is greater than half the arc of the day of the degree that culminates with it, subtract half the difference of the two days from the times of ascension opposite the part that culminates with the star in the given clime; if half the arc of the day of the star is the smaller, add half the difference of the two days to those times. The times of the part that culminates with the star in the clime, after the addition or the subtraction, are the times of ascension of the degree with which the star rises in that locality. Find the degree of the signs opposite them in the ascensions of the clime: it is the part with which the star rises in that locality, of the sign in which the number of times fell.\par
\par \ArPage{50}If you wish to find the degree with which that star sets, take the times opposite the degree that is the counterpart of the degree culminating with the star, in that clime. Then look: if half the arc of the day of the star is greater than half the arc of the day of the degree that culminates with it, add half the difference of the two days to those times; if half the arc of the day of the star is the smaller, subtract half the difference of the two days from those times. The times after the addition or the subtraction are the times of ascension of the degree opposite to the degree with which the star sets. Take the degree of the signs that stands opposite them in the ascensions of the clime: it is the degree opposite to the degree with which the star sets. Find the counterpart of that degree: it is the degree of the ecliptic that sets with the setting of the star. It is known that if the star has no latitude, its passage through the middle of the sky does not differ [from that of its degree], and it rises and sets with the part of the ecliptic in which it is.\par
\par Or, if you wish, add the whole arc of the day of the star to the times of ascension, in the clime, of the degree with which it rises; the sum is the ascension of the degree that is the counterpart of the degree with which it sets. Find the degree of the signs that corresponds to it: the star sets with the counterpart of that degree. You can also find the degree with which the star rises and the one with which it sets in another way. Take the times of ascension in the right sphere of the part that culminates with the star, and both add half the arc of the day of the star to them and subtract half its arc of the day from them. The one added to is the ascension of the counterpart of the degree with which it sets, and the one subtracted from is the ascension of the degree with which it rises, in the clime. Take the degrees of the signs opposite them according to the rule.\footnote{The ascensions in the right sphere are counted from the beginning of Capricorn (chapter 5), so that they can be entered directly in the table of the clime.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 21. On finding how many hours of the night have passed, by measuring a star, and on finding the ascendant}{21}{\ArPage{50}}{\footnote{The print has «\RL{\ArNote الّني}» for «\RL{\ArNote الّتي}» on p. 52, line 1, a dot missing.}}
\par \ArPage{51}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know how many hours of the night have passed, by measuring a star, find the degree that culminates with the star, half the arc of the day of the star, and the degree with which the star rises, as has been explained to you before this chapter. Find also the altitude of the star in the middle of the sky. That is: take the distance of the star from the equator; if it is northern, add it to the altitude of the beginning of Aries in that locality, and if it is southern, subtract it from it. The altitude of Aries after the addition or the subtraction is the altitude of that star in the middle of the sky. Whenever it comes out greater than 90, subtract it from 180: the remainder is its altitude above the northern horizon, and the star is then on the north side of the zenith. Then find the versed sine of half the arc of the day of the star, multiply it by the sine of the altitude of the star at the time of the measurement, and divide the product by the sine of the altitude of the star in the middle of the sky. Subtract the quotient from the versed sine of [half] the arc of the day of the star, and find the arc of the remainder as a versed sine; keep the arc you obtain. If at the time of the measurement the star is on the east side of the middle of the sky, subtract this arc from half the arc of the day of the star; if the star is on the west side, add it to it. Half the arc of the day of the star, after the addition or the subtraction, is the amount of the sphere that has turned from the rising of the star to the hour of the measurement; keep it.\footnote{This is the rule of chapter 16, applied to a star. «Half» is lacking in the Arabic at its second occurrence; Nallino's translation supplies it.}\par
