\par Then look: if the part with which the star rises is between the degree of the sun and the degree of its counterpart, the star rose by day; if it is between the counterpart of the degree of the sun and the degree of the sun, it rose by night. If it rose by day, take the times of ascension in the given clime of the degree with which the star rose, and subtract them from the times of ascension opposite the counterpart of the degree of the sun, which is opposite to it, in that clime. Subtract the remainder from the amount of the sphere that has turned since the rising of the star, which you have kept; the remainder is the amount of the sphere that has turned from sunset to the hour of the measurement. Divide it by the times of the hours of the night; the result is the number of seasonal hours of the night that have passed. If the star rose by night, subtract the times of ascension opposite the counterpart of the degree of the sun from the times opposite the part with which the star rose in that clime, and add the remainder to the amount of the sphere that has turned since the rising of the star; the sum is the amount of the sphere that has turned from sunset to the hour of the measurement. Divide it by the times of the hours of the night, in the same way; the result is the number of seasonal hours of the night that have passed. If you divide the amount of the sphere that has turned since sunset by 15, the result is the number of equal hours of the night that have passed.\par
\par \ArPage{52}If you wish to find the ascendant from the amount of the sphere that has turned since the rising of the star, add the amount of the sphere that has turned since the rising of the star to the times of ascension opposite the part with which the star rises in the clime, and with the sum find the ascendant and the middle of the sky by the rule given before. If you wish to find the ascendant in another way, take the arc that you obtained as a versed sine and subtract it from the times of ascension, in the right sphere, of [the part] that culminates with the star, if the star is on the east side, and add it to these ascensions if it is on the west side. With the times you obtain after the addition or the subtraction you find the ascendant in the same way, and the middle of the sky as well.\par
\par If you measure with the moon in particular, you need to know its parallax, so that you have its visible position correct, I mean the position in which it is seen, in longitude and latitude. From it you then find its visible distance from the equator, the visible part that culminates with it, half the arc of the day of its visible position on the ecliptic, half the arc of the day of the visible part that culminates with it, and the visible degree with which it rises. When you know all this, take the altitude of the moon and work with it, after finding the altitude of the moon in the middle of the sky from its visible distance from the equator.\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 22. On finding the altitude of the stars from the hours of the night, in every locality}{22}{\ArPage{52}}{\footnote{The print has «\RL{\ArNote الّني}» for «\RL{\ArNote الّتي}» on p. 52, line 15, a dot missing.}}
\par Al-Battānī said: If you wish to find the altitude of any star you like from the hours, take the degree that is in the middle of the sky at that time, and the rising and the setting degree. Then find the distance of the star you want from the line of the middle of the sky at that time. That is: take the times between the part of the middle of the sky and the part that culminates with the star, in the right sphere. If the degree that culminates with the star is to the east of the degree of the middle of the sky, subtract the times of ascension of the degree of the middle of the sky from the times of ascension of the degree that culminates with the star; if it is to the west, subtract the times of ascension of that degree from the times of ascension of the degree of the middle of the sky. The result after the addition or the subtraction is the distance of the degree that culminates with the star from the line of the middle of the sky.\par
\par \ArPage{53}If you wish to find this in another way, take the times opposite the part with which the star rises in the clime, and take the times opposite the counterpart of the part with which the star sets. If the part that culminates with the star is to the east of the middle of the sky, subtract the times opposite the degree with which the star rises from the times opposite the rising part; if the degree is to the west of the middle of the sky, subtract the times opposite the rising part from the times opposite the counterpart of the part with which the star sets. What you obtain in either case is its distance from the horizon. Subtract it from half the arc of the day of the star; the remainder is the distance of the star from the line of the middle of the sky.\par
\par When you know the distance of the star from the line of the middle of the sky, on whichever side it is, find the versed sine of this distance and subtract it from the versed sine of half the arc of the day. Multiply the remainder by the sine of the altitude of the star in the middle of the sky, divide the product by the versed sine of half the arc of the day of the star, and find the arc of the quotient as a direct sine, as you take the arc of the sines; the arc you obtain is the altitude of the star at that hour. It is known that if the part with which the star rises follows the part that is rising at that time, the star has not yet risen; if it precedes the rising part, the star has risen and is above the earth. If the part with which the star sets follows the setting part, the star has not yet set and is above the earth; if it precedes it, the star has set. For a star does not rise until the part with which it rises has risen, and does not set until the part with which it sets has set. As for the moon, you find its visible altitude from its distance from the equator, if God wills.\footnote{This is the rule of chapter 17, applied to a star: sin h = sin h₀ · (vers H − vers t) / vers H, where h₀ is the altitude of the star in the middle of the sky.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 23. On finding the azimuth of any star from its altitude and its place in the sphere}{23}{\ArPage{53}}{}
\par \ArPage{54}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to find the azimuth of any star you like on the horizon circle, find the altitude of the star at that time, the distance of the star from the equator and the latitude of the locality. Then proceed with them in the way that I described to you in the chapter on finding the azimuth of the altitude and of the shadow, leaving out nothing, except that you use the distance of the star from the equator in place of the declination of the degree of the sun. If you wish to find the azimuth of the moon, do this with its visible distance from the equator, so that you have the azimuth of the moon on the circle of altitude correct; and likewise for every star whose azimuth on the horizon circle you wish to find, if God wills.\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 24. On finding the distance of a star from the equator, and the part that culminates with it, from the parts of the signs that rise or set [with it] and from the amplitude of its rising and setting on the horizon circle}{24}{\ArPage{54}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: Suppose you wish to find which degree of the signs culminates with a star, and the distance of the star from the equator, from the azimuth of its rising or setting and the part of the ecliptic that rises or sets with it, when these are known from some measurements. Multiply the sine of the altitude of the beginning of Aries in that locality by the sine of the azimuth of the rising or setting of the star, divide the product by the radius, and find the arc of the quotient. The arc you obtain is the distance of the star from the equator, on the side of the azimuth.\footnote{In modern terms sin δ = cos φ · sin A, where A is the amplitude; this is the rule of chapter 7 solved for the declination.}\par
\par With it find half the arc of its day, in the way described for finding half the arc of the day of a star from its distance from the equator. Then look: if the star is on the eastern horizon, add half the arc of its day to the times of ascension, in the clime, of the degree that rises with it; if it is on the western horizon, subtract half the arc of its day from the ascensions of the part with which it itself sets. Enter what you obtain, in either case, in the ascensions of the right sphere and take the degree of the signs opposite it: that is the part that culminates with the star.\footnote{The rule for the eastern horizon is exact, with the ascensions in the right sphere counted from the beginning of Capricorn (Nallino). The rule for the western horizon is wrong as it stands: the ascension to be taken is that of the counterpart of the part that sets with the star, as in chapter 20, since that counterpart rises when the star sets. With the setting part itself the result is wrong by 180° plus twice the ascensional difference of that part. Nallino's commentary states the rule as printed, without remark.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 25. On finding the part in which a star is, and the latitude of the star, from its distance from the equator and the degree of the signs that culminates with it}{25}{\ArPage{54}}{}
\par \ArPage{55}Al-Battānī said: Suppose you wish to find the part in which a star is, and the latitude of the star, from its distance from the equator and what culminates with it, when these are known from measuring the altitude of the star in the middle of the sky and the degree that culminates with it, or from measuring it at the horizon. When the altitude of a star in the middle of the sky has been taken and the difference between it and the altitude of the beginning of Aries in that locality is known, this is its distance from the equator, on the side on which it falls: if the altitude is greater than the altitude of the beginning of Aries, the distance is to the north, and if it is less than the altitude of the beginning of Aries, the distance is to the south. The part that culminates with it at that time is found from the part of the signs that culminates at that hour, by measuring another star whose position is known, or by other measurements with which the part of the middle of the sky can be found. If it can be known only from the rising or setting of the star on the horizon circle and the part that rises or sets with it, [it is found] as we have explained in the preceding chapter, from whatever allows the rising or setting part of the ecliptic and its azimuth on the horizon to be known.\par
\par \ArPage{56}When you know this, in whichever way you found it, take the declination of the part that culminates with the star and the distance of the star from the equator. If both are on the same side, subtract the smaller from the greater; the remainder is the adjusted distance. Keep it, and find its sine and the sine of what this distance falls short of 90. Then find the sine of the whole inclination and the sine of what the whole inclination falls short of 90. Then subtract the sine of the complement of the whole inclination from 120; the remainder is the longest chord. Then subtract the declination of the part that culminates with the star from 90, find the sine of the remainder, and subtract that too from 120; the remainder is the exceeding chord. Keep all these by their names. Then multiply the sine of the whole inclination by the radius, and divide the product by the sine of what the declination of the part that culminates with the star falls short of 90: the result is the sine of the adjusted inclination; keep it with its name and its direction. Then find its arc, subtract the arc from 90 and find the sine of the remainder: this is the sine of the complement of the adjusted inclination. Then multiply the sine of the adjusted inclination that you kept by the sine of the adjusted distance mentioned earlier in this chapter, and divide the product by the sine of the complement of the adjusted distance. Multiply the quotient by the exceeding chord and divide the result by the longest chord. Multiply the quotient by the sine of the complement of the whole inclination and divide it by the sine of the complement of the adjusted inclination. Multiply the quotient by the sine of the distance of the degree that culminates with the star from the beginning of Cancer or the beginning of Capricorn, whichever it is nearer to on either side, I mean behind it or in front of it, in ascensions of the right sphere. Divide the result by the radius and find the arc of the quotient: the arc you obtain is the variation of the star. Keep it.\par
\par Then look: if the part that culminates with the star is between the beginning of Cancer and the end of Sagittarius, and the distance of the star from the equator is northern, subtract the variation of the star from the times of ascension in the right sphere of the part that culminates with the star; if it is southern, add it to them. If the star is between the beginning of Capricorn and the end of Gemini, and its distance from the equator is northern, reverse this and add the variation of the star to those times; if it is southern, subtract it from them. Find the degree of the signs opposite the times obtained after the addition or the subtraction, in the ascensions of the right sphere: the result is the degree and minute of the signs in which the star is, if God wills.\par
\par If the distance of the star from the equator and the declination of the part that culminates with the star are on different sides, the way to work is to add them together: the sum is the adjusted distance. Multiply the sine of the whole inclination by the sine of the distance of the star from the equator, divide the product by the sine of what the adjusted distance falls short of 90, multiply the quotient by the sine of what the declination of the part that culminates with the star falls short of 90, and divide the product by the radius: the result is the sine of the adjusted inclination. Multiply it by the sine of the distance of the star from the equator as well, divide the product by the sine of the complement of the distance of the star from the equator, multiply the quotient by the exceeding chord, and divide the result by the longest chord: the result is the dividend; keep it with its name. Then find the arc of the sine of the adjusted inclination that you obtained, subtract the arc from 90, take the sine of the remainder and multiply it by the dividend that you kept. Divide the product by the sine of the complement of the whole inclination, and multiply the quotient by the sine of the distance of the part that culminates with the star from the beginning of Cancer or the beginning of Capricorn, whichever it is nearer to in front of it or behind it, in the right sphere. Divide the product by the radius and find the arc of the quotient: the arc you obtain is the variation of the star. Follow the first rule with it, until you know the part of the ecliptic in which the star is.\footnote{Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary, judges these rules for the longitude false. Schiaparelli suggests that they are not al-Battānī's, but were written to fill a leaf lost from the exemplar from which the Escorial codex and Plato of Tivoli's version both derive; al-Battānī could have found the longitude by inverting the rules of chapter 18. A numerical check bears out the judgement. When the star and its culminating degree are on the same side of the equator, the first rule comes close to the true variation without being exact: for a star at longitude 60° and latitude 10° north it gives 2.48°, against the true 2.45°, and for one at 150° and 25° north 10.63°, against 10.47°. When they are on opposite sides, the second rule is far off: for a star at longitude 200° and latitude 12° north it gives 0.07°, against the true 4.53°.}\par
\par \ArPage{57}If you wish to find the latitude of the star and the direction of its latitude, multiply the sine of the distance of the star from the equator by the sine of what the declination of the degree in which you have found the star falls short of 90, divide the product by the sine of what the whole inclination falls short of 90, and find the arc of the quotient. Then look: if the arc is greater than the declination of the degree in which you found the star, subtract the declination of that degree from it; if the arc is the smaller, subtract it from the declination of that degree. The result after the addition or the subtraction is the latitude of the star. If you wish to know the direction of the latitude, look: if the arc is greater than the declination of the degree in which the star is, the latitude is on the side of that declination; if it is less, the latitude is on the side opposite to the declination of the degree of the star.\footnote{The «declination» of the degree is here again its second declination β′ (chapter 18). With it the rule is exact (Nallino, after Schiaparelli): sin x = sin δ · cos β′ / cos ε, and the latitude is the difference between x and β′.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 26. On finding the distances between the stars according to their configuration in the sphere and the positions of one relative to another; by it the true distance of the moon from the sun is found, according to the latitude of the moon}{26}{\ArPage{57}}{\footnote{The translation follows Nallino's corrigenda in reading «\RL{\ArNote ضرب}», «product», for «\RL{\ArNote صرب}» (p. 57, line 16).}}
\par Al-Battānī said: We wish to show how the amounts of the distances between the stars are found on the greatest circle, the one that lies between the two poles of the ecliptic, which is the circle of the signs. It has been shown by proof that in every quadrilateral inscribed in a circle, the products of each two opposite sides, one by the other, added together, are equal to the product of one of its diagonals by the other. In every quadrilateral inscribed in a circle in which two of the sides are parallel, the two remaining sides are opposite and, for the reason given above, equal; when they are produced they meet in a point. Its diagonals too are equal, and the product of one of them by the other is equal to the product of one of the parallel sides by the other together with the product of one of the opposite sides by the other.\footnote{The first theorem is Ptolemy's theorem on the quadrilateral inscribed in a circle (\emph{Almagest} I 10). For «inscribed in a circle», in the second sentence, the codex has «on a sphere or on a plane»; the reading is Nallino's.}\par
\par \ArPage{58}Since this is so, we draw a line for a piece of the ecliptic and mark its ends A and B, and from the points A and B we draw two lines that meet at Z; let the point Z be one of the poles of the ecliptic, whichever it is. Each of the lines AZ and BZ is then the line of a quarter of the circle that passes through the poles of the ecliptic and the positions of the two stars. We put one of the two stars at the point A of the ecliptic, and the other inclined from the ecliptic in latitude, at the point Ṭ; its position on the ecliptic is known to be the point B. The arc BṬ is the latitude of the star, and we draw the line AṬ, which is the amount of the distance between the two stars. The line AṬ and the arc AṬ are found by drawing from the point Ṭ a line parallel to the line BA, the line ṬH, and from the point Z, the pole, a line to the middle of the line BA, the line ZG, and by marking the sign M at the place where it cuts the line ṬH. The quadrilateral ṬBAH is then a figure of four sides, of which the sides BA and ṬH are parallel, and ṬB and HA equal and opposite; produced, they meet at the point Z. It is clear on the sphere that each of the arcs ZA, ZB and ZG is a quarter of a circle; therefore the arcs ZṬ, ZM and ZH are equal, and so each of the arcs ṬB, MG and HA is equal. By what has been said before, the line ṬM has also been shown to be half the line ṬH. Since the right-angled triangle BGZ is similar to the small right-angled triangle ṬMZ, the line ṬM is known in amount from the line BG, which is already known; and since they are in one triangle, the ratio of ṬM to BG is as the ratio of ZṬ to ZB, and also as the ratio of ZM to ZG. When the line ṬM is known, the whole line ṬH is known, since it is twice ṬM.\footnote{The sentence from «therefore the arcs ZṬ, ZM and ZH» to «is equal» is printed by Nallino in brackets as the addition of an unskilled reader. Its equalities hold if M lies on the circle of latitude through Ṭ and H; but the arc ṬH of the computation below is an arc of the great circle through Ṭ and H, whose middle is nearer to the pole. The computation does not depend on them: the ratio of ṬM to BG is that of ZṬ to ZB, and the sine of ZṬ is the 51;57,42 used below.}\par
\par Let the arc AB between the two stars in longitude be 60 parts, so that the arc BG is half of that, 30 parts. We take the latitude of the star whose position in longitude is the point B to be 30 parts: this is the arc BṬ. Then by what has been said about the sphere the arc MG is also 30 parts, and so the arc MZ that remains is 60 parts; its sine, which is the line MZ, is [51 parts] 57 minutes 42 seconds, approximately. The arc BG has been shown to be 30, and its sine, the line BG, is also 30 parts. The whole arc ZG is a quarter of a circle, and the line ZG is its sine, which is 60 parts, the radius. So if we take of the line BG the part given by the ratio of the line ZM to ZG, there remains the ratio of the line ṬM to the line BG; this is what we wished to show.\footnote{«51 parts» is supplied by Nallino; the codex lacks it. Nallino takes the passage on the arcs MG and MZ to be corrupt, and reads «the arc ZṬ that remains is 60 parts, and its sine, the line ZṬ, 51;57,42»; and in the last sentence Nallino understands: if BG is reduced in the ratio of ZM to ZG, the result is ṬM.}\par
\par\begin{center}\begin{tikzpicture}\draw[line width=1.44pt] (10.97pt,19.21pt) -- (99.33pt,19.88pt);
\draw[line width=1.47pt] (54.83pt,117.51pt) -- (10.97pt,19.21pt);
\draw[line width=1.42pt] (54.83pt,117.51pt) -- (99.33pt,19.88pt);
\draw[line width=1.44pt] (54.63pt,117.05pt) -- (55.29pt,19.55pt);
\draw[line width=1.38pt] (25.72pt,52.26pt) -- (84.35pt,52.76pt);
\draw[line width=1.49pt] (25.69pt,52.19pt) -- (99.57pt,19.89pt);
\draw[line width=1.54pt] (84.21pt,53.05pt) -- (12.37pt,19.22pt);
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (46.62pt,116.10pt) {\normalsize Z};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (90.18pt,54.84pt) {\normalsize H};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (59.88pt,59.76pt) {\normalsize M};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (18.54pt,55.92pt) {\normalsize Ṭ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (105.60pt,22.86pt) {\normalsize A};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (0.00pt,16.80pt) {\normalsize B};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (55.62pt,0.00pt) {\normalsize G};\end{tikzpicture}\end{center}
\par \ArPage{59}Al-Battānī said: The computation of this is to multiply ZM by BG, which gives 1558 parts 51 minutes, approximately. When this is divided by the line ZG, the result is 25 parts 58 minutes 51 seconds, approximately, which is the amount of the line ṬM. So the arc ṬM is 25° 39½′, and the whole arc ṬH is twice that, 51° 19′. The quadrilateral BṬHA is then known in its sides, and the diagonal ṬA is known too, by what has been said before about finding the full chords that fall in these sides. Since the sine ṬM has been shown to be 25;58,51, the line ṬH, which is the full chord of ṬH, is twice that, 51 parts 57 minutes 42 seconds. The full chord of the arc BA is twice the sine BG, which is 60 parts. The full chord of the arc ṬB is 31 parts 3 minutes 30 seconds: this is the chord of the 30 parts that are the latitude of the star. The line ṬB is equal to it: it is the sine of 15 parts, doubled, and these 15 are half the arc ṬB. When the side BA is multiplied by the side ṬH parallel to it, the product is 3117 parts 42 minutes, and the product of ṬB by HA, which is equal to it, is 964 parts 37 minutes, approximately. Added together, they equal the product of ṬA by itself, since ṬA is equal to HB. So ṬA by itself is 4082 parts 19 minutes, and its root is 63 parts 54 minutes, approximately, which is the amount of the line ṬA. So the arc ṬA, the arc of the full chord, is 64° 19′, and this is the true distance between the two stars, whose difference in longitude was only 60 degrees.\footnote{Nallino reads ZṬ and ZB for ZM and ZG in the first sentence, with the same numbers. For 51° 19′ the codex has 51° 59′. The true distance is 64° 20′, since cos d = cos 30° · cos 60°.}\par
\par Further, if the two stars are both at the point B in longitude, and one of them at the point Ṭ in latitude, the distance between them is the amount of the latitude alone, the arc ṬB. If one of them is at the point Ṭ and the other at the point H, and they have equal latitudes in this figure, the distance between the two stars is the arc ṬH. Likewise, if one of them were at the point H and the other at D, the distance between them would be known, by drawing the line DK parallel to the line BA and to the line ṬH and finding the amount of DK in the way we have described: the quadrilateral DṬHK then becomes known in its sides, and so the line HD between the two stars, the diagonal of the quadrilateral, becomes known too. The distance of the star at the point D from the one at the point A is known from the quadrilateral DBAK.\footnote{The codex has no figure with the points D and K; Nallino adds one to the Latin translation.}\par
\par \ArPage{60}Likewise, if you wish to know the distance between two stars, look. If one of them has no latitude, like the sun or another of the stars that are on the belt of the signs, and the other has a latitude on either side, take the amount of degrees of longitude between them: this is the first side. Then take half of it and find its sine, multiply it by the sine of what the latitude of the star falls short of 90, divide the product by the radius, and keep the result as it is. Then take its arc and double it: this is the second side. Then find the full chord of the latitude of the star by the rule given to you at the beginning of the book, which is to take the sine of half the latitude and double it: the result is the full chord of the latitude. Then find the full chord of the first side and the full chord of the second side as well; the fourth side is equal to the third, whose full chord is the chord of the latitude. When you have done this, multiply the full chord of the first side by the full chord of the second side, and add to it the full chord of the latitude multiplied by itself, which is the product of the third side by the fourth. Take the root of the sum and find its arc as you find the arcs of full chords, that is, take half of it, find its arc and double the arc: the result is the distance between the two stars.\footnote{In modern terms, with chords in a circle of radius 1: crd² d = crd Δλ · crd s + crd² β, where Δλ is the difference in longitude, β the latitude and s the second side, sin ½s = cos β · sin ½Δλ. The rule is exact: it amounts to cos d = cos β · cos Δλ.}\par
\par If both stars have latitude on the same side, and the two latitudes are equal, find the arc of the second side: it is the amount between them. If the latitudes on the same side are unequal, subtract the smaller from the greater: the remainder is the third side, and the fourth side is equal to it; keep it. Then subtract the latitude of each of them from 90, find the sine of the remainder, and multiply it by the sine of half the degrees of longitude between them. Divide what results for each of them by the radius, find the arc of the quotient and double it: the result is the amount of each of the two sides of longitude; the longer of them is the first side and the shorter the second. Find their full chords, which are twice what results from each of them by the division, multiply one of the two chords by the other, and add to the product the third side multiplied by itself. Take the root of the sum, take half the root, find its arc and double the arc: the result is the distance between the two stars.\footnote{«The third side multiplied by itself» is its full chord multiplied by itself, as in the next paragraph.}\par
\par If the latitudes of the two stars are on different sides, add the two latitudes together: the sum is the third side, and the fourth side is equal to it. Then subtract each of the two latitudes from 90 and find the sine of each, which is the sine of what each of them falls short of 90. Multiply it by the sine of half the degrees of longitude between them and divide what results for each of them by the radius; double the quotient: what results for each of them is the full chord of the first side and of the second side. Multiply one of them by the other, and add to the product the full chord of the third side multiplied by itself. Take the root of the sum and take half of it; find the arc of the result and double the arc: the result is the distance between the two stars.\footnote{The rules of these two paragraphs are exact as well: they amount to cos d = sin β₁ · sin β₂ + cos β₁ · cos β₂ · cos Δλ.}\par
\par \ArPage{61}It is known that when the two stars are together in one degree, and only one of them has a latitude, or both have latitudes, on the same side or on different sides, the distance between them is only the amount of latitude between them; and when neither has a latitude, the distance between them is the amount of the degrees of longitude, whatever it is. This chapter is needed above all for working out the directions in nativities.\footnote{The directions (\emph{tasyīrāt}) are the astrological progressions of a nativity (Nallino's \emph{profectiones}).}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 27. On finding the length of the year and the motion of the sun in it}{27}{\ArPage{61}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: The ancients disagreed about the length of the year. Some of the ancient people of Egypt and Babylon said that it is 365 days, a quarter of a day and 1/120 of a day. Ptolemy says that they reckoned it from the sun's leaving one of the fixed stars until it returns to it, and finds fault with them for this and says that it is most absurd. For if this view were allowed, nothing at all would prevent us from saying that the year is the time from the sun's leaving Saturn, or another of the wandering stars, until it returns to it, and this view is very plainly false. The year is only the time from the sun's leaving an unmoving point of the sphere until it returns to it, either from one of the two equinoctial points to the same point, or from one of the two solstitial points to the same point; for no starting points on the ecliptic are more fitting than these points.\footnote{365 1/4 + 1/120 days is 365 days 6 hours 12 minutes, a year measured by the fixed stars (Nallino). Ptolemy discusses the length of the year in \emph{Almagest} III 1.}\par
\par Hipparchus worked with a year of 365 days and a quarter of a day only, although it had become clear to Hipparchus that it is less than that, as Ptolemy reports in collecting Hipparchus's views. For Hipparchus said that the year is in truth 365 days and less than a quarter of a day, having found that the summer solstice came before the time of the full quarter day in excess of 365 days. This made Hipparchus uncertain about the motion of the sun, to the point of imagining that it had another sphere, eccentric to the centres of the two spheres.\footnote{Hipparchus found 365 1/4 − 1/300 days, that is 365 days 5 hours 55 minutes 12 seconds (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{62}The ancients took this mostly from summer observations, made when the sun passes the summer solstitial point. These are not found to be as accurate as observations made when the sun passes one of the two equinoctial points, especially the autumnal equinoctial point, because the air is clearer and purer at that time than at the time of the spring equinox. For when the sun passes the solstitial point it moves slowly in declination, and when it passes the two equinoctial points its motion in declination is very fast. That is why Ptolemy relied only on the autumnal observations and based the reckoning on them. One of the observations of Hipparchus on which Ptolemy relied, and of whose truth there was no doubt, is the observation reported by Hipparchus, who found that the sun passed the autumnal equinoctial point in the year 178 after the death of Alexander, on the third of the five epagomenal days, at midnight at Alexandria, of the night whose morning was the fourth of the epagomenal days, and was sure of it.\footnote{Ptolemy does not say that Hipparchus made this observation at Alexandria; Hipparchus observed at Rhodes, which was taken to lie on the meridian of Alexandria (Nallino). The epagomenal days are the five days added after the twelve months of 30 days of the Egyptian year.}\par
\par Ptolemy observed 285 Egyptian years later; this is the observation that Ptolemy's book says was verified and made exact with the greatest care. Ptolemy found that the sun passed the autumnal equinoctial point in the third year of the reign of Antoninus, which is the year 463 after the death of Alexander, on the ninth day of Athyr, one of the months of the Copts, about one hour after sunrise at Alexandria. Taking the time between the two observations, Ptolemy found it to be in truth 285 Egyptian years, 70 days and a quarter of a day and 1/20 of a day, in place of the 71 days and a quarter that should accumulate from the full quarters in these 285 years. The ratio of this one day less 1/20 of a day, by which the time of the observation came before the time of the quarter day in excess of 365 days, to the 285 years between the two observations is as the ratio of one day to 300 years. So the length of the year found from these two observations is 365 days and a quarter of a day less 1/300 of a day, which is one part and a fifth of a part out of 360 parts.\par
\par \ArPage{63}Ptolemy also reports taking the ancient summer observations made before Hipparchus, namely the observation made in the time of Apseudes, king of Athens, in which the sun passed the summer solstitial point 108 Egyptian years before the death of Alexander, in the morning of the twenty-first of Pharmouthi, one of the months of the Copts, in that year; and observing the sun in person and finding that it passed the summer solstitial point in the year 463 after the death of Alexander, on the eleventh of Mesori, one of the months of the Copts, about two hours after midnight of the night whose morning was the twelfth of that month. Between these two observations there were about 571 Egyptian years, 140 days and a half and a third of a day, in place of the 142 days and a half and a quarter of a day that would accumulate from the quarters of the said years if the quarters were full in the years. So Ptolemy found that the summer solstice came before the time of the full quarter by one day, two thirds and a quarter of a day, and the ratio of this one day, two thirds and a quarter of a day to the said 571 years is as the ratio of two full days to 600 years. This agreed with what Ptolemy had worked with, that the observation comes before the time of the full quarter day by one day in every 300 years, although these summer observations are not as reliable as the autumnal ones, for the reason we have given. It is clear that the observation made before Hipparchus was earlier than the observation of Hipparchus by about the time between the observation of Hipparchus and that of Ptolemy, for it was 286 years before Hipparchus.\footnote{The observation in the time of Apseudes, archon of Athens, is the summer solstice observed by Meton and Euctemon, 27 June 432 BC (Nallino). For 108 years the codex has 168.}\par
\par \ArPage{64}Then we observed at the city of al-Raqqa. One of our autumnal observations, on which we rely and whose accuracy we trust as far as the instrument showed us, is the observation made 743 years after the autumnal observation of Ptolemy mentioned above. When we measured, we found that the sun passed the autumnal equinoctial point in the year 1194 of the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn, which is the year 1206 after the death of Alexander, about four hours and a half and a quarter of an hour before sunrise on the nineteenth day of Aylūl, one of the months of the Rūm, which is the eighth day of Pachon, one of the months of the Copts. Since the meridian circle of Alexandria comes before the meridian circle of al-Raqqa by about two thirds of an equal hour, there are between the two observations, I mean our observation and the autumnal observation of Ptolemy, 743 Egyptian years, 178 days and a half and a quarter, less about two fifths of an hour, in place of the 185 days and a half and a quarter of a day that should accumulate from the quarters in these years if the quarters were full between the two observations. When we divide these 7 days and two fifths of an hour, by which the time of the observation came before the time of the quarter day in excess of 365 days, by the 743 years between the two observations, the share of one year comes to 3 parts 24 minutes of the 360 parts that make up the revolution of a day and its night. When we subtract this from the time of the quarter day, which is 90 parts, there remains as the excess over the full 365 days 86 parts 36 minutes; so the true length of the year is 365 days, 14 minutes and 26 seconds [of a day], approximately.\footnote{The years of Dhū l-Qarnayn are those of the Seleucid era, which al-Battānī begins on 1 September; the observation fell before sunrise on 19 September 882 (Nallino). Two thirds of an hour is 10° of longitude; al-Battānī's own table of cities gives al-Raqqa 73° 15′ and Alexandria 60° 30′, a difference of 12° 45′ (Nallino).}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{The minutes and seconds of the year are sixtieths of a day: 365;14,26 days is 365 days 5 hours 46 minutes 24 seconds. Hipparchus' and Ptolemy's year is 365;14,48 days, 2;12 parts of a day of 360 parts longer.}\par
\par When we divide the 360 parts of the circle of the sphere by the length of the year that has been found, the mean motion of the sun in a day and its night comes to 0;59,8,20,46,56,14; in the 30 days that make the Egyptian month, 29;34,10,23,28,6,47; and in the 365 days that make the Egyptian year, 359 parts 45 minutes 46 seconds 25 thirds 32 fourths 2 fifths 31 sixths, approximately. We have compounded these motions and entered them in the tables for the collected years and the single years, the months, the days and the hours, in the era of the Arabs and the era of the Rūm, so that the position of the sun by its mean motion, which is called the mean of the sun, can easily be found at any time we want, in whichever of the two eras we like. It is clear that the length of the year that we obtained by observation is less than the length that Ptolemy gives by 2 parts and a fifth of a part, and so the motion of the sun that we found exceeds the motion that Ptolemy gives by 0;0,0,3,33,43,43 in a day, and in the Egyptian year by 0;0,21,40,10,53,56, approximately, if God wills.\footnote{Sexagesimal numbers with many places are written here in the usual notation: 0;59,8,20,46,56,14 is 0 degrees, 59 minutes, 8 seconds, 20 thirds, 46 fourths, 56 fifths and 14 sixths. Ptolemy's daily motion is 0;59,8,17,13,12,31 and the yearly motion 359;45,24,45,21,8,35; the two differences given here are exact. In the yearly difference the text has 50 fifths; the difference is 53, as Nallino's translation has it.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{The collected years are the periods of years used as arguments of the tables, the single years the years within a period. The era of the Arabs is that of the Hijra; the era of the Rūm is the Seleucid era of the Syrians.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 28. On finding the anomaly of the motion of the sun, and what appears with it of the position of its farthest distance from the centre of the earth}{28}{\ArPage{64}}{\footnote{The heading prints «\RL{\ArNote الثامن العشرون}» without «\RL{\ArNote و}». The translation reads «\RL{\ArNote مركزه}» for «\RL{\ArNote مركره}» (p. 68, line 2) and «\RL{\ArNote التي}» for «\RL{\ArNote الني}» (p. 70, line 20), misprints that the corrigenda do not list.}}
\par \ArPage{65}Al-Battānī said: Having finished with the length of the year and the mean motion of the sun, we try to explain the anomaly that appears in the course of the sun, and its greatest amount, and what appears with it of the position of the point of its farthest distance from the centre of the earth on the ecliptic. In this we follow the method that Ptolemy's book uses, from the sun's passage through the quarters of the ecliptic, by measurements that we made in successive years, renewing the observations as carefully as we could. We established that the sun passes from the autumnal equinoctial point to the spring equinoctial point in 178 days and about 14½ hours, and that it passes from the spring equinoctial point to the autumnal equinoctial point in a longer time: what we found by observation, as far as our diligence reached, is that its true course, which is seen by measurement, takes 186 days and about 14¾ equal hours. It became clear from what we have said that the point of its farthest distance is in this half.\footnote{Al-Battānī has not shown before why the farthest distance lies in the half that takes longer; Nallino, after Schiaparelli, suspects that something has fallen out. Ptolemy's method is that of \emph{Almagest} III 4.}\par
\par Then we observed and found that the sun passes from the beginning of Aries to the beginning of Cancer, that is, from the spring equinoctial point to the summer solstitial point, in 93 days and about 14 equal hours, a little less than that. It is also clear that its passage from the spring equinoctial point to the summer solstitial point takes a longer time than its passage from the summer solstitial point to the autumnal equinoctial point. So we knew that the point of the farthest distance, and the centre of the eccentric sphere on which the point of the farthest distance lies, fall, on the ecliptic, in this quarter, which takes longer than the remaining quarter. We found the mean motion of the sun in the 186 days 14¾ hours to be 183 parts 56 minutes 12 seconds, and in the 93 days 14 hours 92 parts 14 minutes 10 seconds, approximately.\footnote{Here and on p. 66 the text writes 183;56,2, but it gives 3;56,12 for the excess over 180 and 1;58,6 for its half; Nallino's translation has 183;56,12. With al-Battānī's daily motion the mean motion in 186 days 14¾ hours is 183;56,13. In 93 days 14 hours it is 92;14,26, as the tables give; Nallino keeps 92;14,10, which the computation below also uses, and takes the 16 seconds for the «little less» of the observed time.}\par
\par Since this is so, we draw a circle for the ecliptic, ABGD, about the centre H, with the diameters AG and BD at right angles. We take the point A as the spring equinoctial point; then the sign B is the summer solstitial point, the sign G the autumnal equinoctial point, and the point D the winter solstitial point. In the quarter of the circle on which AB lies, for the reason given above, we mark the sign Z and take it as the centre about which we draw the circle of the sun's eccentric sphere, inside the first circle. We write on it KLMN, on the diameters KM and LN, which intersect at the centre Z at right angles. We write the sign F where the lines BD and KM meet; the sign LA where the diameter AG cuts the circle KLMN on the side of [the point A]; and the sign Ḍ where the diameter BD cuts the circle KLMN on the side of [the point B]. We draw the perpendicular of the arc LA K from the point LA to the sign Y on the diameter KM, and also the perpendicular ḌḤ. We draw the line HZ, which passes through the two centres, and extend it to the ecliptic, which is the circle ABGD, and we write the sign Ṭ where it meets it, and the sign ʿ where it cuts the circle KLMN.\footnote{The words in brackets are supplied by Nallino. LA is the letter lām-alif, used here as a sign of the figure.}\par
\par \ArPage{66}It is established that the arc AB is 90 parts, and that the arc KL of the eccentric circle is also 90 parts. The point LA of the eccentric circle is its point of the beginning of Aries, and the point Ḍ the place of the beginning of Cancer. So the arc LA K L Ḍ M S of the eccentric is the amount of the eccentric circle that the sun passes over by its mean motion between the beginning of Aries and the beginning of Libra, which, as was said before, is 183 parts 56 minutes 12 seconds. The arc K L Ḍ M is half the eccentric circle, 180 parts; so each of the two arcs KL and SM is half of the 3 parts 56 minutes 12 seconds by which the sun's mean motion exceeded 180, that is, 1 part 58 minutes 6 seconds: the arc LA K is this amount, and the arc MS is equal to it. It is known too that the arc LA K L Ḍ is what the sun passes over on the eccentric by its mean motion from the beginning of Aries to the beginning of Cancer, so that the arc LA L Ḍ of the eccentric circle is of the 92 parts 14 minutes 10 seconds; and since the arc LA K L of it is known from what was said before, 91 parts 58 minutes 6 seconds, the arc LḌ is 16 minutes 4 seconds. Clearly the perpendicular LA Y is half the chord of twice the arc LA K, and the perpendicular ḌḤ half the chord of twice the arc LḌ; so the perpendicular LA Y is about 2 parts 3 minutes 39 seconds and the perpendicular ḌḤ about 16 minutes 45 seconds, the sines of the arcs LA K and LḌ. Since the line KM is parallel to the line AG, the line HF is equal to the line LA Y; and since the line LN is parallel to the line BD, the line ZF is equal to the line ḌḤ. So the side HZ of the right-angled triangle HFZ is known. The line HF multiplied by itself is about 4 parts 14 minutes 48 seconds, and the line ZF multiplied by itself 4 minutes 41 seconds; and the line HZ, which subtends the right angle, multiplied by itself is equal to HF and ZF, each multiplied by itself, added together. So HZ multiplied by itself is 4 parts 19 minutes 29 seconds, and its root is 2 parts 4¾ minutes: this is the line HZ between the two centres. In the measure in which a quarter of the circle about the right-angled triangle HFZ is 90 parts and its radius 60 parts, the arc HZ is about 1 part 59 minutes; this is the whole anomaly of the motion of the sun that appeared from these observations.\footnote{«The two arcs KL and SM»: the arc meant is LA K, as the next words say and Nallino's translation has it. The greatest equation of the sun is 1;59,10 in al-Battānī's tables, and Ibn Yūnus gives the same value for al-Battānī (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{67}Next we seek the amount of the arc BṬ of the ecliptic; when it is known, the remaining arc ṬA is known in amount. For the point ʿ is the point of the farthest distance of the sun's eccentric sphere: when we drew the line HZ through the two centres and extended it to the ecliptic, it cut the circle KLMN at the point ʿ and the ecliptic at the point Ṭ. So we wish to know the ratio of the line HZ to the line HṬ, which is the radius, and the amount of the arc BṬ of the ecliptic. It has been shown that the line HZ is 2 parts 4¾ minutes in the measure in which the radius is 60 parts; and in the measure in which the radius is 60 parts the line HṬ is that too, since it is equal to the line HB. So the line HṬ measures HZ about 28 5/6 times. Further, as was shown first, if the line HZ is made 60 parts, the line FZ is in that measure about 8 parts 4 minutes; for this is what results when it is multiplied 28 5/6 times. Or, if you wish, multiply the line FZ by the line HṬ, the radius of the sphere: by the order of the multiplication the product is 16 degrees 45 minutes. Divide this by the line HZ, which has been shown to be 2 parts 4¾ minutes; the result is 8 parts 4 minutes, which is the sine of the amount of the angle BHṬ. So the arc BṬ is about 7 parts 43 minutes. Clearly the point of the farthest distance of the eccentric sphere, which is the point ʿ, falls 7 parts 43 minutes from the summer solstitial point towards the preceding side of the ecliptic, that is, 82 parts 17 minutes from the beginning of Aries. This is what we wished to show.\footnote{The farthest distance (apogee) of the sun is thus at Gemini 22° 17′. Ptolemy (\emph{Almagest} III 4) put it at Gemini 5° 30′.}\par
\par\begin{center}\begin{tikzpicture}\draw[line width=1.37pt] (100.39pt,111.20pt) circle[radius=91.75pt];
\draw[line width=1.33pt] (77.89pt,128.47pt) circle[radius=54.62pt];
\draw[line width=1.08pt] (100.60pt,202.94pt) -- (100.34pt,19.45pt);
\draw[line width=1.08pt] (8.64pt,110.97pt) -- (192.14pt,111.43pt);
\draw[line width=1.20pt] (78.29pt,183.09pt) -- (78.58pt,73.85pt);
\draw[line width=1.08pt] (23.27pt,128.28pt) -- (132.51pt,128.49pt);
\draw[line width=1.15pt] (27.15pt,166.46pt) -- (100.78pt,111.20pt);
\draw[line width=0.96pt] (78.31pt,177.45pt) -- (100.56pt,177.37pt);
\draw[line width=0.74pt] (26.64pt,128.29pt) -- (27.61pt,111.01pt);
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (99.24pt,216.90pt) {\normalsize A};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (107.46pt,184.56pt) {\normalsize LA};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (77.94pt,190.56pt) {\normalsize K};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (71.34pt,173.34pt) {\normalsize Y};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (20.46pt,170.94pt) {\normalsize Ṭ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (35.94pt,151.50pt) {\LARGE ʿ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (139.38pt,128.82pt) {\normalsize N};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (82.92pt,135.42pt) {\normalsize Z};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (17.46pt,131.04pt) {\normalsize L};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (199.44pt,114.42pt) {\normalsize D};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (104.94pt,115.50pt) {\normalsize H};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (70.08pt,118.50pt) {\normalsize F};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (33.90pt,120.54pt) {\normalsize Ḥ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (0.00pt,109.92pt) {\normalsize B};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (19.44pt,102.96pt) {\normalsize Ḍ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (108.90pt,76.02pt) {\normalsize S};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (78.72pt,65.94pt) {\normalsize M};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (102.96pt,0.00pt) {\normalsize G};\end{tikzpicture}\end{center}
\par The observation on which we based this measurement was in the year 1194 of the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn, when we observed the course of the sun from the beginning of Aries to the beginning of Cancer and to the beginning of Libra.\footnote{That year began on 1 September 882 (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{68}What remains for us is to distribute this anomaly over the degrees of the signs, to find its share degree by degree and to enter it in the tables, so that the equation of the motion of the sun is easily found when it is needed. Ptolemy has shown and made clear that the irregular motions are seen and supposed in two ways. One is to imagine that the star has a sphere whose centre is the centre of the ecliptic, and on this sphere another sphere attached to it, whose centre runs on the circle of the first sphere and turns on it. This second sphere is a small sphere that does not surround the earth, and the large sphere is the one that carries the centre of the small sphere in the order of the signs, by the amount of the motion in longitude that is known for the star in the order of the signs, in either of the two ways. The star itself moves on the epicycle, which is the small sphere, either towards the preceding side or towards the following side; or this small sphere carries the star to either side, whichever it is. This motion is the motion of the proper anomaly of the star. The second of the two ways is to imagine that the star has a sphere whose centre is the centre of the ecliptic, and another sphere of the same size, whose centre is not the centre of the first sphere but outside it, and whose circle cuts the circle of the first sphere in two places; the star is on this eccentric sphere, the sphere carrying the star or the star moving on it. Whichever of the two ways you imagine, it comes to the same for this anomaly and what appears from it. We begin with the first way and give an example of it.\footnote{\emph{Almagest} III 3. The «sphere» on which the epicycle is carried is the circle of the deferent.}\par
\par \ArPage{69}We draw a figure for the circle of the signs, ABGD, about the centre H, and we take the centre of the epicycle to be at first at the point A. We draw about it a circle for the epicycle, ṬZ, and draw the diameter AG and extend it to the point Ṭ, which is the point of the farthest distance of the epicycle. We take the position of the sun on the epicycle to be the point Z, draw from it a perpendicular to the line AṬ, write M at its foot, and draw also the line AZ, which is equal to the line AṬ, since each of them is the radius of the circle of the epicycle. It has been made clear earlier in this chapter that the radius of the epicycle is the line HZ found in the first figure, which is 2 parts 4¾ minutes. Since what we have described is clear, consider the motion of the sun on the epicycle towards the opposite of the order of the signs, that is, towards the preceding side, or the epicycle carrying the sun to this side, every day by as much as the mean motion of the sun in a day, in the measure in which the circle of the epicycle is 360 parts. The mean motion of the sun that appears by measurement is the motion of the centre of the epicycle towards the following side, which is the motion we have posited, also in the measure in which the circle ABGD is 360 parts. Now we take the arc ṬZ, between the sun and the point of the farthest distance of the epicycle, to be 30 parts in the measure in which the epicycle is 360, draw the line HZ in this figure, and seek the arc of the line ZM, which is the anomaly of the motion of the sun there. It has been shown that the line HA is the radius of the parecliptic sphere, 60 parts by the measure on which we have based the computation, in which the diameter AG is 120; so the line HṬ, from the centre of the parecliptic sphere to the point of the farthest distance of the epicycle, from which the motion on the epicycle begins, is 62 parts 4 minutes 45 seconds. Since the triangle ZMA is right-angled, AZ multiplied by itself is equal to AM and ZM, each multiplied by itself, added together, and the angle MAZ is known; so the line ZM is known, and when ZM is known, the remaining side AM of the triangle is known, which is the sine of what the angle ZAṬ and the arc ZṬ fall short of a quarter of their circle. So the line HM is known in amount; and the triangle ZMH is right-angled, and its line HZ, which subtends the right angle, is known for this reason, so that the line ZM is known in amount in its measure, and the arc on it is the arc of the anomaly. If the arc ZṬ is 30 parts, as we took it, its sine is also 30 parts in the measure in which the line AZ, the radius, is 60 parts; but in the measure in which the line AZ is 2 parts 4¾ minutes, the line ZM is 1 part 2 minutes 22½ seconds, the remaining line AM 1 part 48 minutes 2 seconds, and the line HM 61 parts 48 minutes 2 seconds. Clearly then [the line HZ] is about 61 degrees 48 minutes 35 seconds. In the measure in which the line HZ is only 60 parts, the line ZM is 1 part 33 seconds, and the arc on it about 57 minutes 49 seconds; this is the amount of the arc ṬZ, which is the anomaly of the motion of the sun. So the arc ʿA of the ecliptic is 29 degrees 2 minutes 11 seconds, whereas it was 30 parts; for the centre of the epicycle has moved from the point ʿ to the point A as the sun has moved on the epicycle from the point Ṭ to the point Z.\footnote{The parecliptic sphere (\emph{al-falak al-mumaththal bi-falak al-burūj}, «the sphere made like the ecliptic») is the sphere concentric with the ecliptic, in the plane of which the deferent lies (Nallino). «The arc of the line ZM» is the arc of the circle about the right-angled triangle of which ZM is the sine. The words in brackets are supplied by Nallino.}\par
\par \ArPage{70}Further, we take the centre of the epicycle at the point B and draw about it a circle for the epicycle, ḤYQ. We take the position of the sun on it to be the point Ḥ, and the arc QḤ that the sun has passed over from the point Q, the farthest distance, to be 150 parts; so the arc YḤ that remains, from the position of the sun to the point of the nearest distance, is also 30 parts. We draw the line HḤ and the perpendicular ḤK. Clearly again each of the triangles BKḤ and ḤKH is right-angled, and each of the two sides BḤ and BH is known: BḤ is the radius of the epicycle and BH the radius of the ecliptic. The angle ḤY and the arc ḤY are given, so the perpendicular ḤK is known as well. If the arc ḤY is 30 parts, as we took it, its sine is also 30 parts, and the arc on KB, which completes the quarter, is 60, whose sine is 51 parts 57 minutes 41 seconds. In the measure in which the line BḤ is 2 parts 4¾ minutes, the perpendicular KḤ is 1 part 2 minutes 22½ seconds, and the line BK that remains is, in that measure, 1 part 48 minutes 2 seconds; so the line HK is about 58 degrees 11 minutes 58 seconds.\par
\par\begin{center}\begin{tikzpicture}\draw[line width=1.46pt] (113.42pt,94.15pt) circle[radius=75.13pt];

The dashed line Nʿ is the diameter of the ecliptic parallel to the line of the farthest and the nearest distance; F is not named in the text.}\end{center}
\par The line HḤ therefore comes to about 58 parts 12 minutes 34 seconds. But in the measure in which the line HḤ is 60 parts, the perpendicular KḤ is 1 part 4 minutes 17 seconds, and the arc on it 1 part 1 minute 24 seconds, in the measure in which the circle about the right-angled triangle BKḤ is 360; this is the arc of the anomaly, which is the arc ḤY. So the arc NB of the ecliptic is 31 parts 1 minute 24 seconds. This is what we wished to show.\footnote{For the arc NB the codex has FB; the reading NB is Nallino's.}\par
\par \ArPage{71}Al-Battānī said: We show it also in the second way, which is seen on the eccentric sphere. We draw a circle for the ecliptic, ABG, on the diameter AG about the centre H, and a circle for the eccentric sphere, ZMḤ, about its centre, the point Ṭ, so that the diameter AG passes through the two centres. The point Z is then the farthest distance from the parecliptic sphere, and the point Ḥ the nearest distance from it. We take the position of the sun on the eccentric sphere to be at first the point M, the arc ZM that the sun has moved on the eccentric being 30 parts; so the angle ZṬM is also 30 parts. It has been shown that the line HṬ between the two centres is 2 parts 4¾ minutes. Since this is as we have drawn, we draw the line ṬM, which is the radius of the eccentric sphere, and the line HM; we extend the line ṬM in a straight line to the point L, and draw from the point L a perpendicular to ML, the perpendicular LH. The triangle ṬLH is right-angled, and its angle LṬH is equal to the given angle ZṬM. The arc on HL of the circle about the triangle ṬLH, the circle being 360, is 30 parts, and its sine also 30 parts in the measure in which the line ṬH between the two centres is 60 parts; and the line LṬ that completes the quarter remains 51 degrees 57 minutes 41 seconds, since the arc LṬ completes the quarter, that is, is 60 parts. But in the measure in which the line ṬH between the two centres is 2 parts 4¾ minutes, the line HL is 1 part 2 minutes 22½ seconds, and the line LṬ that completes the quarter 1 part 48 minutes 2 seconds; so the whole line LM is 61 parts 48 minutes 2 seconds. The triangle MLH is right-angled, so its line HM, which subtends the right angle, is known: it is 61;48,35. But in the measure in which the line HM is 60 parts, HL is 1 part 33 seconds, and the arc on it 0;57,49, the circle about the triangle ṬLH being 360. So the arc AB of the ecliptic remains about 29;2,11.\footnote{The translation reads «33 seconds» with the codex. Nallino prints «33 minutes». The codex is right: HL is 1;2,22½ × 60 / 61;48,35 = 1;0,33 parts, the value found for the same line ZM in the first way, and only this gives the arc 0;57,49 that follows.}\par
\par \ArPage{72}Further, we put the sun at the point D of the sun's eccentric sphere and take the arc ZD to be 150; so the arc DḤ that remains, from the position of the sun to the nearest distance, is also 30 parts. We draw the two lines HK and ṬD, each of which is the radius of its sphere, and draw also the perpendicular HS. Since the triangle ṬSH is right-angled, and its side HṬ between the two centres is known, and the side HS and the angle DṬḤ are known, the side ṬS and the remaining angle ṬHS are known; so the line DS that remains is known, and HD, which subtends the right angle of the right-angled triangle HSD, is known. Since the arc DḤ and the angle ḤṬD are known and given, 30 parts as has been shown, their sine is also 30 parts; likewise the arc HS of the circle about the right-angled triangle HSṬ is 30, if this circle is 360, and its sine, which is the perpendicular HS, is also 30 parts in the measure in which the line HṬ, the radius of this circle, is 60 parts. But in the measure in which the line ṬH is 2 parts 4¾ minutes, the perpendicular HS is 1 part 2 minutes 22½ seconds, and so the line SṬ that remains of the sides of the triangle is 1 part 48 minutes 2 seconds. The line ṬD, the radius of the eccentric sphere, is 60; when SṬ is taken from it, the line SD that remains is 58 parts 11 minutes 58 seconds, and the line HD, which subtends the right angle of the triangle HSD, is about 58 parts 12 minutes 34 seconds. In the measure in which the line HD is 60 parts, the perpendicular HS is 1 part 4 minutes 17 seconds, and the arc on it 1 part 1 minute 24 seconds; this is the amount of the anomaly. So the arc KG of the ecliptic is about 31 parts 1 minute 24 seconds. This is enough about this anomaly; it is what we wished to show.\footnote{The text has «1 part 4 minutes 24 seconds» for the arc of the anomaly; the arc whose sine is 1;4,17 is 1;1,24, as in the first way, and the 31;1,24 that follows requires it (so Nallino's translation).}\par

\par \ArPage{73}Al-Battānī said: In this way we have computed it degree by degree and entered it in the tables, counted from the point of the farthest distance. In this way also the single equation of the moon is computed, and the mean equation of the planets, which is the radius of the epicycle of each of them, when its sine is taken and it is distributed in this way. Whenever you wish to compute this, look at the degrees that the star, or the sun, or the moon, has passed over on the epicycle from the point of the farthest distance, which is called the anomaly of the sun, the moon and the other stars. If this anomaly is less than 180, work with it; if it is more than 180, subtract it from 360 and work with the remainder. The procedure is to take the degrees that you obtain in either way. If they are less than 90, take their sine and the sine of what remains of them to 90, and multiply each of the two sines by the radius of the epicycle of the star, which is the sine of the whole equation. Divide each product by the radius. Add what results for the sine of the complement of the degrees to the 60 that are the radius, multiply the sum by itself, add to it what results for the sine of the degrees multiplied by itself, take the root of the sum and keep it. Then return to what resulted for those degrees, multiply it by the radius, and divide it by the root that you kept. If the degrees with which you work are more than 90, take 90 from them, take the sine of the remainder and the sine of what remains of it to 90, multiply each of the two sines by [the radius of] the epicycle, and divide each product by the radius. Subtract what results for the degrees from 60, multiply the remainder by itself, add to it what results for the complement of the degrees multiplied by itself, take the root of the sum and keep it. Then return again to what resulted for the complement of the degrees, multiply it by the radius and divide by the root that you kept. Find the arc of the quotient. The arc you obtain, in whichever of the two ways, I mean from the first way, which results from the division of the first quantity, or from the division of this second quantity, is the share of those degrees of the anomaly with which you worked, for whichever star you intended, in the anomaly of its motion: it is the equation of the star. The radius of the epicycle of the sun is 2;4,45, the radius of the epicycle of the moon 5;15, the radius of the epicycle of Saturn 6;29,50, the radius of the epicycle of Jupiter 11;30,0, the radius of the epicycle of Mars 39;27,22, the radius of the epicycle of Venus 43;9,0, and the radius of the epicycle of Mercury 22;30,30. This is what has been shown by the observations, and the computation agrees with it; it is the sine of the mean equation of all the stars, if God wills.\footnote{«Anomaly» renders al-Battānī's \emph{ḥāṣṣa}, the portion of motion that falls to the star on its epicycle; most other astronomers call it \emph{al-khāṣṣa}, «the proper [motion]» (Nallino's glossary). In modern terms, with r the radius of the epicycle and a the anomaly, the rule is sin q = r sin a / √((60 + r cos a)² + (r sin a)²), the radius being 60; it is exact, and its second form, for a > 90°, is the same rule written with a − 90°. The single equation of the moon is its first anomaly; the mean equation of a planet is the equation produced by its epicycle at its mean distance.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{Nallino corrects the codex in four of the radii: the sun (codex 2;5,45), the moon (6;15), Mars (19;25,22) and Mercury (29;30,30). For Mars the printed text has 39;25,22; the translation follows Nallino's emendanda, printed in Part II, and the Latin translation, which read 39;27,22. Ptolemy's values, in the same parts, are: sun 2;30 (the eccentricity), moon 5;15, Saturn 6;30, Jupiter 11;30, Mars 39;30, Venus 43;10, Mercury 22;30.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 29. On finding the inequality of the days with their nights, and on converting the one kind into the other}{29}{\ArPage{73}}{}
\par \ArPage{74}Al-Battānī said: Many people, and the common folk, think that the days with their nights are equal in length, I mean that each day with its night is 24 hours for them; but in truth it is not so. One mean day with its night is the rising of all the 360 times of the equator from the horizon circle or the meridian circle, together with the further times of the equator that rise with the 59 minutes that the sun moves by its mean motion in a day and night. One irregular day with its night is the rising of all the 360 times of the equator together with what further rises with the irregular course of the sun in a day and night, which is necessarily either more or less than 59 minutes. A beginning taken from the horizon circle changes and differs in every place with the difference of the ascensions of the signs there, while the beginning from noon is fixed in one state, without change or difference, because the signs pass through the meridian circle alike in every locality. Therefore the beginning of the days in the reckoning of the stars and the computation of their positions is not taken from sunrise or sunset, but from noon or midnight. Further, all the motions of the stars set down in the tables are set down for mean days of equal length. If what accumulates of the difference between the irregular days with their nights and the mean days with their nights is neglected, in the course of the sun and of the other stars it does not amount to anything from which a perceptible error appears; but in the moon it is very evident, because of its speed.\par
\par For the greatest amount that accumulates between the irregular days and the mean days is about half an hour, and in this time the motion of the moon is at times 18 minutes; between the days that exceed the mean days and the days that fall short of them it is twice that. This anomaly is composed of two parts: one is the anomaly of the motion of the sun, which is the equation; the other is the anomaly of the passage of the signs through the middle of the sky, since they do not all rise there with the same amount. The greatest amount that accumulates from the anomaly of the motion of the sun is about 3 parts and a quarter and a tenth, and the greatest amount that then accumulates from the passage of the signs through the middle of the sky about 4 parts and a quarter and a fifth; so what accumulates from the two together is 7 parts 48 minutes, which is about half an hour and a fifth of a tenth of an equal hour. The place of the shortfall is from about two thirds of Aquarius to about the beginning of Scorpio, and the place of the excess from about the beginning of Scorpio to about two thirds of Aquarius.\footnote{This is the inequality that is now called the equation of time. Al-Battānī counts it from its least value: 7;48 times of the equator are 31 minutes of an hour, close to the range of about 31 minutes over which the equation of time varies.}\par
\par \ArPage{75}In this book we have set out the mean motions in the tables on the basis that the assumed position of the sun is at 18 degrees 19 minutes [of Aquarius] by its mean motion, and at 20 parts of it by its true motion, in which it is seen; and with this day and its night you compare the other days of the year in this book.\par
\par Al-Battānī said: If you wish to convert irregular days into the mean days with which the mean motions are taken from the tables, take [the arc] between the first, assumed mean position of the sun and the second position to which it moves, also by its mean motion, and keep the number of degrees. Then take also [the arc] between its first true position, in which it was, and the second position to which it moves by its true motion, in times of the ascensions of the signs in the right sphere. If the number of these times is greater than the degrees of the mean motion that you kept, find what part of an equal hour the difference between them is, and add it to the given irregular days; if the number of times is smaller, subtract it from them. The days that you obtain after the addition or the subtraction are those converted from the irregular days into the mean days, in whichever of the two reckonings is used for the beginning of the days, I mean from noon or from midnight. If you wish to convert from the mean days obtained from the tables into the irregular days that are found, do the reverse: add the difference to the mean days when the number of times is smaller, and subtract it from them when the number of times is greater. The mean days after the addition or the subtraction are those converted from the mean days into the irregular days that are found.\par
\par On the basis that we have laid down in this book for the assumed position of the sun, the number of times is always the smaller, until a long period of time has passed in which the position of the farthest distance of the sun, as we found it on the ecliptic, has changed much, and so what arises from the anomaly of the motion of the sun has changed. Since this is as we have described, we have added 18 minutes to the mean position of the moon at the root of the computation. We have taken the share of each degree of the signs in the amount of the inequality of the days with their nights and entered it in the tables of the ascensions of the right sphere, in the column that follows the ascensions in each sign. When we take what stands opposite the true degree of the sun in that column, find its amount in equal hours and subtract it from the irregular days, what remains is the mean days with which the motions are found from the tables; and when it is added to the mean days, the result is the irregular days that are found by measurement.\footnote{The root of the computation is the epoch of the tables.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 30. On the description of the spheres of the moon, the anomaly of its motions, the increase and decrease of its light, the causes of the two eclipses, and the distances of the two luminaries from the centre of the earth, their diameters and the sizes of their bodies when they are compared with the earth}{30}{\ArPage{76}}{\footnote{The last words, «with the earth», are Nallino's conjecture: only the tops of the letters can be read in the codex.}}
\par Al-Battānī said: When the motions of the moon were investigated, two anomalies were found in it. One of them is single by itself: it appears alone, only at the times of the conjunctions with the sun and the oppositions to it that take place by the mean course of the sun and the moon, and it depends on the position of the moon on its epicycle. The second anomaly appears through the moon's distances from the sun; it is compounded with the first anomaly, so that the two act together. This is grasped by demonstration with geometrical lines, by imagining that the moon has four spheres. The first is made like the ecliptic: it lies level beneath it, moves with its motion without deviating from it, and has its centre at the centre of the ecliptic. The second sphere is inclined to it towards the north and the south; it is as large as this parecliptic sphere, and the two have one centre. Its greatest inclination to either side is about 5 parts, which is the amount of the moon's distance from the belt of the signs in latitude. The motion of this inclined sphere, against the order of the signs, is about 3 minutes a day. This is the motion of the two nodes. One of them is called the head: from it the moon passes in latitude towards the north. The other is called the tail: from it the moon passes towards the south. These two nodes are the places where the inclined sphere and the parecliptic sphere intersect.\par
\par \ArPage{77}Inside this inclined sphere is a third sphere, whose centre lies outside the centre of the other two. It hangs from the inclined sphere and touches it at a point that is its highest place; that point is called the farthest distance from the earth. It moves within the inclined sphere against the order of the signs, about 11 parts 12 minutes a day. There is a fourth sphere, called the moon's own epicycle. Its centre is on the circle of the eccentric sphere, and this centre moves on the circle of the eccentric sphere in the order of the signs about 24 parts 23 minutes [a day]. It begins its motion from the point of the farthest distance of the eccentric sphere, which is taken together with the mean position of the sun. Therefore the centre of the epicycle falls on the point of the farthest distance twice in the lunar month, once at the mean conjunction and again at the opposition. The moon moves on the circle of the epicycle about 13 degrees 4 minutes a day, against the order of the signs on the epicycle, beginning from the point of the farthest distance that is seen from the centre of the eccentric sphere. Since the centre of the epicycle falls on the circle of the inclined sphere at these two times, as we have said, nothing prevents us from taking it, in this way, to move on the circle of the inclined sphere about 13 degrees 14 minutes a day; this is its motion in latitude. The node, at the intersection of the two spheres, carries it back against the order of the signs by the 3 minutes that we said are the motion of the inclined sphere, so that its motion in longitude, in the order of the signs, remains about 13 degrees 11 minutes a day. The motion of the moon on the epicycle is the first motion mentioned.\footnote{The daily motions are Ptolemy's, rounded. The 24;23 is twice the daily excess of the moon's motion in longitude over the sun's, and the eccentric moves back by 24;23 − 13;14 + 0;3 = 11;12 (Nallino). The 13;4 is the daily motion in anomaly, 13;3,53,56,… in \emph{Almagest} IV 3.}\par
\par It is clear from what we have described that nothing enters the motion of the moon from the eccentric sphere at these two times, since then the moon is not distant from the mean position of the sun or from the point opposite it. So nothing of the second anomaly is mixed with the single anomaly until the moon moves away from the sun; at those distances the second anomaly, which comes from the eccentric sphere, is compounded with it according to the moon's distances from the sun. This is the figure of the four spheres of the moon; the demonstration will be clear to you from the example of their drawing.\par
\par\begin{center}\begin{tikzpicture}\draw[line width=1.41pt] (107.64pt,118.39pt) circle[radius=97.13pt];

\draw[line width=1.24pt] (107.67pt,137.43pt) -- (50.05pt,84.20pt);
\draw[line width=1.25pt] (107.62pt,118.63pt) -- (30.79pt,72.67pt);
\draw[line width=1.26pt] (107.62pt,118.49pt) -- (46.49pt,42.92pt);
\draw[line width=1.26pt] (107.62pt,118.86pt) -- (80.35pt,52.34pt);
