\node[inner sep=0pt] at (104.46pt,222.00pt) {\normalsize A};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (81.96pt,201.66pt) {\normalsize Ṭ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (116.82pt,184.44pt) {\normalsize Ḥ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (112.86pt,141.78pt) {\normalsize Z};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (0.00pt,125.58pt) {\normalsize B};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (212.52pt,130.74pt) {\normalsize D};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (122.52pt,125.70pt) {\normalsize L};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (112.20pt,115.08pt) {\normalsize H};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (68.34pt,112.86pt) {\normalsize Ḍ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (24.84pt,83.66pt) {\normalsize Ṭ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (50.24pt,76.26pt) {\normalsize M};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (28.24pt,69.31pt) {\normalsize Ḥ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (81.72pt,74.28pt) {\normalsize Ṭ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (114.36pt,73.02pt) {\normalsize Ḥ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (68.59pt,61.16pt) {\normalsize K};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (99.84pt,54.16pt) {\normalsize F};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (41.58pt,38.04pt) {\normalsize N};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (115.68pt,34.50pt) {\normalsize S};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (108.84pt,0.00pt) {\normalsize G};\end{tikzpicture}\footnote{The dashed circle about Z, as large as the circle of the ecliptic, is not mentioned in the text; Schiaparelli found no use for it (Nallino's commentary). The point L and the lines ZL and LH belong to the computation on p. 80.}\end{center}
\par \ArPage{78}Al-Battānī said: I drew a circle for the parecliptic sphere, marked ABGD, with H at its centre, and a circle for the inclined sphere, also marked ABGD; let its centre also be the point H, as is the case on the globe, which turns on its two poles. We draw the diameter AS and mark on it the centre of the eccentric sphere at the point Z, between the centre of the two spheres and the sign A. About the centre Z we draw a circle for the eccentric sphere, marked AMF, with the radius AZ. We take the arc AM, as much as we wish, as the motion of the centre of the epicycle from the point A, which is the point of the farthest distance and the position of the sun, to the point M. We take the point M as the centre of the epicycle and draw about it a circle for the epicycle, marked ḤṬḌK. We draw the two lines HMṬ and ZMḤ. Then the point Ṭ of the epicycle is the position of the farthest distance that is seen from the centre, the point H, which is the centre of the earth and the centre of the ecliptic, and Ḥ is the position of the true farthest distance, which is seen from the centre Z, I mean from the centre of the eccentric sphere. So the arc ṬḤ is clearly the anomaly of the motion of the moon in its proper course on the epicycle; it is the anomaly set down in the third column of the tables of the equation of the moon. We take the motion of the moon on the epicycle to be from the point Ḥ to the point Ṭ, then to Ḍ, and make the position that it has reached on the epicycle the sign K. We draw the line HKN touching the epicycle, and draw the line MK, the radius of the epicycle as displaced by the distance of its centre from the point A of the eccentric sphere. Since the moon is on the line that touches the epicycle, the radius of the epicycle is the whole single anomaly together with what is compounded with it of the second anomaly, according to the distance of the moon from the sun, which is at the point A. It is clear in this figure that when the moon is in the first half of the epicycle, on which are ḤṬḌ, its true position on the ecliptic, which is seen from the centre H, is less than its mean position in longitude, which is the position of the centre of the epicycle; so the equation is subtracted from the mean position of the moon when the anomaly is less than 180 degrees. When it is in the second half, on which are ḌKḤ, its true position on the ecliptic is greater than its mean position; so when its anomaly is more than half the circle, the equation is added to the mean position of the moon, if God wills.\footnote{In the printed figure the line from H through M ends at Ḥ, and Ṭ lies on the dashed line drawn through M from the point of the diameter that is as far from H as Z is, on the other side. The true farthest distance of the epicycle is seen from that point in Ptolemy's theory (\emph{Almagest} V 5), and al-Battānī's own computation uses it on p. 82. The text, with the codex and Plato of Tivoli, draws HMṬ and ZMḤ instead and has the true farthest distance seen from the centre Z of the eccentric; Nallino's translation, following Schiaparelli, corrects the text to agree with the figure. The arc ṬḤ is the same in either reading.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{The «displaced» radius of the epicycle is its radius measured in parts of which the distance of its centre from the earth, HM, is 60; it grows as the centre moves from A towards F: 5;15 at A, 7;12 at the doubled distance of 120, about 8 at F, as is shown below.}\par
\par \ArPage{79}As for the single equation, which appears at the times of the conjunctions and the oppositions and is set down in this book of ours in the second column of the tables of the equation, we have shown how it is computed, in the way in which we worked in computing the equation of the sun and distributing it in the tables. The greatest amount that this single anomaly of the moon reaches is 5 parts 1 minute, and its sine, which is then the radius of the epicycle, is about 5¼ parts: this is the ratio of the 60 of the radius to the 5¼ parts. This is what Ptolemy stated and showed from the lunar eclipses, in which the true position of the moon must be opposite the true position of the sun, in which the sun is seen on the ecliptic. At those times [the arc] between the position of the moon by its mean course and the degree opposite the true degree of the sun is the single anomaly of the motion of the moon according to the position of the moon on the epicycle, and so the whole of this single anomaly becomes known. We too have observed many lunar eclipses, established their exact times and middles, and found the amount of this single anomaly to be as we have described.\footnote{\emph{Almagest} IV 6. The single equation is computed as the equation of the sun is in chapter 28.}\par
\par \ArPage{80}As for the second anomaly, which is found from the distance of the moon from the sun, the greatest amount that they found reaches about 2⅔ parts. Compounded with the 5 parts 1 minute of the single anomaly, the two anomalies together come to about 7 parts 40 minutes. This happens when the centre of the epicycle is at the point F, at a distance of half the circle from the point A; the radius of the displaced epicycle is then about 8 parts, the sine of the 7⅔ parts. From what we have described it becomes clear that the line HZ between the two centres is 10 parts 19 minutes. The demonstration of this is as I shall describe. We again draw a circle for the epicycle, marked ṬḤ, about the point A, which is the farthest distance on the eccentric sphere, and draw the line HṬ touching the epicycle, and the line AṬ. Since the moon on the tangent line completes the whole single anomaly, which has been shown to be 5;1 in the measure in which the four right angles are 360, its sine is 5;15 in the measure in which the radius is 60 parts, the radius of the parecliptic, inclined, sphere in this figure. Again we take the centre of the epicycle at the sign F, which is the point of the nearest distance of the eccentric sphere, draw about it a circle for the epicycle, also marked ṬḤ, and draw the line HṬ touching the epicycle, and the line FṬ. Since the moon on the tangent line, I mean at the position Ṭ, completes the whole of the two anomalies, which is 7;40, its sine is about 8 in the measure in which the four right angles are 360 and the radius, the line HA, is 60. The line FṬ is equal to the line AṬ, and it was shown that the line AṬ is 5¼ parts in the measure in which the line HA is 60 parts. But since the centre of the epicycle has come to a place where the measure changes, because of its nearness to the point H, which is the centre of the earth and the true place of sight, [FṬ] appears in the measurement as about 8 parts, in the measure in which the line HA is 60. So in the measure in which the 8 parts are 60 parts, the 5¼ parts are 39 degrees 22 minutes, and that is the amount of the line HF, from the centre of the earth to the point of the nearest distance of the eccentric sphere. Likewise, by the inverse of this ratio, in the measure in which the 8 parts are 5¼ parts, the 60 parts are 39 parts 22 [minutes]. Since the amount of the line HF has become clear, 39 parts 22 minutes, when it is added to the line HA, which is 60, it comes to 99;22, which is the whole diameter of the eccentric sphere; and half of it, the radius of the eccentric sphere, is 49;41.\footnote{The greatest second anomaly is Ptolemy's (\emph{Almagest} V 3), and so is the distance between the centres (V 4). The resulting extreme equations of the moon, 5;1 at the syzygies and 7;40 at the quadratures, compare with the modern 5;2 and 7;34 (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary); the second anomaly is the inequality now called the evection.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{«Half» is supplied by Nallino. After «the point F» Plato of Tivoli has «which is the nearest distance of the eccentric sphere», words that Nallino takes to have fallen out of the codex. At the end of the paragraph Plato also has «so the distance between the two centres is 10;19 in the measure in which AH is 60 parts», that is, 60 − 49;41.}\par
\par \ArPage{81}Since the radius of the epicycle according to its displacement from the sun is known, and the distance between the centres and the radius of the eccentric sphere are known, what remains to complete our knowledge of this is to explain how to compute the arc ṬḤ, which is set down in the third column, and to explain what is compounded of the single equation with the second between the two distances, in the way in which I have set it out in the tables and entered it in the fourth and fifth columns. In the fourth: if these 2 degrees 40 minutes, which are entered in the fifth, are taken as 60, how much is compounded of them, and what is its ratio to the 60. This is known as I shall describe. We extend the line MH to the sign L and join the point L to the point Z; then the ratios of the sides of the triangle MLZ are known, and its angles. We take the arc AM, as Ptolemy took it in this matter, as 120 parts; this is the doubled distance of the moon from the sun. Since we take the ratio of the sines to the radius, the angle LZH is 30 parts, and the angle ZHL, which completes the quarter, 60 parts, in the measure in which the circle that goes round the triangle ZHL is 360 parts. Also the sine of the angle LZH is 30 parts, and the sine of the angle ZHL about 51;58, in the measure in which the line HZ is 60; but in the measure in which the line HZ is 10;19, the line HL is about 5;10 and the line ZL 9;16. Again, when the line HKN in the figure touches the epicycle and the position of the moon on the epicycle is the point K, the first anomaly together with what is compounded with it of the second anomaly is greatest. Since the line MK is the radius of the epicycle and the line ZM the radius of the eccentric sphere, by which you know these amounts, the ratio of LM is known from the ratio of ZM and ZL, and so the whole line ML is 48;53. When the line HL, which has been shown to be 5;10, is taken from it, the line HM from the centre remains 43;43. The line MK, the radius of the epicycle, has been shown to be 5;15; but in the measure in which the line HM from the centre is 60, the line KM, the radius of the displaced epicycle, is about 7 parts 12 minutes, and the arc on it about 6 parts 54 minutes; that is the amount of the arc MK. When the 5 parts 1 minute, the amount of the whole single anomaly, are taken from it, what is compounded with it of the second anomaly remains, 1 part 53 minutes. If the 2⅔ degrees, approximately, are 60, this 1 degree 53 minutes is 42;38 of the 60; that is what is set down under 120 in the fourth column, and it results from the ratio of the minutes to the one part, which is the ratio of 0;42,38 to 60. When these 42 and 38 are extended and made 60, the 1 part 53 minutes become 2 parts 39 minutes, which is what is set down in the fifth under 120.\footnote{The text prints LHZ for the angle of 30 parts, in this sentence and the next (the codex has AHM); the angle of 30 parts is the angle at Z, LZH, as in Nallino's translation. Ptolemy (\emph{Almagest} V 7) makes the angle AHM, not the arc AM, 120 degrees, which is what gives the angle ZHL of 60 (Schiaparelli).}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{On the base HZ = 10;19 the sine of 60 parts is 8;56 (10;19 × 51;58 / 60), and the line ML = 48;53 that follows agrees with it, not with the text's 9;16, which would give 48;49. Plato of Tivoli has 18;56.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{The 42;38 is reckoned with the greatest second anomaly 2;39, that is 7;40 − 5;1, rather than with the rounded 2⅔: 60 × 1;53 / 2;39 = 42;38, while 60 × 1;53 / 2;40 = 42;22,30. In al-Battānī's tables the 2;39 of the fifth column stands under 110, not 120 (Nallino). Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary, takes the last sentence for an interpolation by a reader who did not understand the computation.}\par
\par We also find [the arc] between the true farthest distance and the mean distance, which is the arc ṬḤ, in the figure set out, by taking the doubled distance of the moon from the sun by their mean course as 90;30, as Ptolemy took it in the figure to which the demonstration led, as we have said, so that the motion of the moon on its epicycle from the point Ṭ is 333;12. We draw this circle as an example of it.\footnote{These are the values of Ptolemy's example in \emph{Almagest} V 6.}\par
\par\begin{center}\begin{tikzpicture}\draw[line width=1.47pt] (95.09pt,91.25pt) circle[radius=71.70pt];
\draw[line width=1.37pt] (28.92pt,62.95pt) circle[radius=23.81pt];
\draw[line width=1.32pt] (95.01pt,162.95pt) -- (95.33pt,19.55pt);
\draw[line width=1.43pt] (95.17pt,90.94pt) -- (29.87pt,62.66pt);
\draw[line width=1.34pt] (102.65pt,74.84pt) -- (5.48pt,58.77pt);
\draw[line width=1.33pt] (5.20pt,64.98pt) -- (95.25pt,56.52pt);
\draw[line width=1.30pt] (95.17pt,89.78pt) -- (102.65pt,74.84pt);
\draw[line width=1.12pt] (89.10pt,72.60pt) -- (95.25pt,57.48pt);
\draw[line width=1.34pt] (29.49pt,62.69pt) -- (14.07pt,44.34pt);
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (94.62pt,178.80pt) {\normalsize A};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (100.44pt,93.48pt) {\normalsize D};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (108.54pt,79.44pt) {\normalsize K};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (87.84pt,76.83pt) {\normalsize S};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (99.12pt,70.68pt) {\normalsize H};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (89.46pt,49.80pt) {\normalsize Z};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (28.26pt,55.08pt) {\normalsize B};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (0.00pt,72.42pt) {\normalsize Ṭ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (1.08pt,52.44pt) {\normalsize Ḥ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (12.36pt,35.94pt) {\normalsize M};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (96.66pt,0.00pt) {\normalsize G};\end{tikzpicture}\end{center}
\par \ArPage{82}Al-Battānī said: This is the circle of the eccentric sphere, marked ABG, about the centre D, with the diameter AG, on which the centre of the ecliptic falls, at the sign H. The circle about the point B is the one called the epicycle, marked MḤṬ. We draw the line BM and the line HBḤ, extend the line BH to the point K, and join the point K to the point D. Then the angle KDH of the triangle is the half part by which [the doubled distance] exceeds 90, and the arc HK is half a part in the measure in which the circle that goes round the triangle DKH is 360 parts; its sine is 0;31,25 in the measure in which the line HD, the radius, is 60. The angle KHD remains 89;30, and so the arc KD is 89;30 and its sine about 60. But in the measure in which the line DH between the centres is 10;19, the line HK is about 5 minutes and the line KD about 10;19. Further, because of the difference of the centres that we have described, we make the line HZ equal to DH, the line HS equal to HK, and the line SZ equal to the line DK. The line DB, from the centre of the eccentric sphere to its circle, has been shown to be 49;41 in the measure in which the line MB, the radius of the epicycle, is 5;15. From what we have described, the whole line BK is 48;36. When HK, which has been shown to be 5 minutes, is taken from it, the line HB remains, in that measure, 48;31; and since the line HS is also 5 minutes, the line SB remains, in that measure, 48;26. From the ratio of SZ and SB you know the ratio of the line BZ, which is therefore about 49;31 in the measure in which the line SZ was about 10;19. When the line BZ is extended and made 60, the line SZ is in that measure about 12;32, and the arc on it about 12;1, in the measure in which one right angle is 90; that is the amount of the arc ṬḤ. So the true motion of the moon on the epicycle, which is seen from the centre of the sphere, counted from the point Ḥ, is 345 parts 13 minutes. Therefore, when the doubled distance is less than 180, the arc ṬḤ is added to the anomaly of the moon obtained from the table, and when it is more than 180, the arc ṬḤ is subtracted from that anomaly. For the centre of the epicycle is at first between the point A of the eccentric sphere and the point F, on the side of M; then it passes to its other half, between F and A, on the side of D. These 12 parts 1 minute are what is set down in the third column under 90½ parts.\footnote{Here Z is the point of the diameter that is as far from H as D is, on the other side; the true farthest distance Ṭ of the epicycle lies on the line from Z through B (Ptolemy, \emph{Almagest} V 5).}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{The line SZ is 10;19 × 60 / 49;31 = 12;30, as Plato of Tivoli has it, and its arc 12;1; the 12;32 of the text would give 12;3.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{The letters A, F, M and D of the last sentences are those of the first figure (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{83}Al-Battānī said: As for the motion of the moon in longitude, we find it as it stands in Ptolemy's book, after we add to it what we found in correcting the motion of the sun, and so we have entered it in the tables. Likewise its motion in anomaly is the motion set down in Ptolemy's book for the anomaly of the moon, with nothing added to it or taken from it. As for its motion in latitude, we found that it exceeds in Ptolemy's book by 27 minutes; so we distributed these minutes over the time between Ptolemy and us, subtracted them from the motion in latitude, and entered in the tables what remained. We have had no need to set down tables of the doubled distance between the sun and the moon, since we know the distance between the sun and the moon by their mean course at any time we wish; when we double it, that is what we use in place of what is obtained from the tables, without addition or subtraction. Likewise we found that the latitude of the moon reaches about 5 parts, which is set down in the seventh column of the tables of the equation, and we entered it as it was; except that its share exceeds, in our time and Ptolemy's, by a half and a quarter of a part, so we subtracted that from its proper course, and what remains is what you find there, if God Most High wills.\footnote{The correction of the motion of the sun is that of chapter 27; adding it to the moon as well keeps Ptolemy's mean distance of the moon from the sun. In 100 Roman years al-Battānī's mean motion of the moon in longitude exceeds whole revolutions by 307;57,46, Ptolemy's by 307;21,37; Hansen's tables of the nineteenth century give 307;53,40 (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{«Its share» (\emph{ḥiṣṣa}) is the argument of latitude; Nallino reads it for the codex's \emph{ḥāṣṣa}, «its anomaly». Schiaparelli explains the ¾ part as the correction of the motion in latitude accumulated over the roughly 1200 years from the epoch of the era of Dhū l-Qarnayn, at the rate of 27 minutes in the roughly 750 years between Ptolemy and al-Battānī (27′ × 1200 / 750 = 43′).}\par
\par As for the cause of the lunar eclipse, it is that the sun is greater than the earth and the earth greater than the moon. The rays of the sun go out along the sides of the earth until they end in the air on the other side in the shape of the head of a pine, and so the shadow of the earth is called the pine-shaped shadow; its end lies beyond the sphere of Mercury. When the passage of the moon is at one of the two nodes of its sphere at the times of its opposition to the sun, its centre comes to the centre [line] of the ecliptic: it falls on the belt of the signs, faces the sun across a diameter of the sphere, with no latitude by which it is inclined away from the sun, and is directly opposite it. Then the earth comes between the moon and the sun and screens it from it, so that the moon falls into the pine-shaped shadow of the earth that we have mentioned; its light is darkened, and it is eclipsed according to how far or near its path is to the belt of the signs, which is the middle of the shadow. If it has no latitude at all and is in the node itself, its passage at the middle of the eclipse is on the middle line of the shadow, and its eclipse is then the most complete of eclipses and the longest in duration. For this reason the moon never has its light complete: it receives all the light only when the sun falls on the half of its circle that faces it, and that happens only when it is on the diameter of the sun, with half the sphere between them, which it can reach only under the darkness, at the middle of the eclipse. If it is inclined from the path of the sun in latitude to the north or to the south, less than half the circle of the sphere lies between it and the sun, and its opposition to the sun is not along a straight diameter. So when it is opposite the sun while inclined from its path in latitude, its eclipse is according to the amount of its latitude, up to the greatest latitude at which its circle can touch the circle of the shadow.\footnote{The reading «head» (\emph{jumjuma}) is uncertain (Nallino). \emph{Ṣanawbarī}, «pine-shaped», is the Arabic word for conical.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{In this chapter (p. 92) the shadow reaches 254⅔ radii of the earth from the centre of the earth; in chapter 50 the distances of Mercury run from 64⅙ to 166 radii of the earth and those of Venus from 166 to 1070, so that the shadow passes the sphere of Mercury and enters that of Venus (Nallino).}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{The translation follows Nallino's emendanda (Part II, p. XXX), «its centre becomes the centre of the ecliptic», for the printed «its centre comes to be in the ecliptic»; the codex has «it becomes the centre of the ecliptic».}\par
\par \ArPage{84}It is clear from what we have said that none of the stars can be eclipsed at their opposition to the sun, since the shadow does not reach their centres; and Mercury does not move as far from the sun as opposition, to fall into the shadow then. But the moon may eclipse the other stars and hide them from sight when its visible path agrees [with theirs] in longitude and latitude, for then it falls on the line that goes out from the eye to the star in question. The stars also eclipse one another, when the lower of them passes under one of those above it and comes onto its course in longitude and latitude. If the sun were equal to the earth, the breadth of the shadow would be the same from its bottom to its top; it would have no end in the air, but would rise without end. The eclipse of the moon would then be of one and the same amount at the top of its epicycle and at the bottom, it would linger in the eclipse longer than it is found to linger, and all the stars would be eclipsed at their opposition to the sun. If the sun were smaller than the earth, the top of the shadow would be broader than its bottom, and it would rise in the sky without end; the higher the breadth of the shadow rose, the more the moon and the stars would be eclipsed, lingering in the eclipse for days that differ according to their course compared with the course of the sun.\footnote{Mercury and Venus are never in opposition; Nallino suggests that «upper» has fallen out after «all the stars».}\par
\par As for the sun, the cause of its eclipse is the moon. When at the times of the conjunctions its centre happens to be seen on the belt of the signs, it comes between the eyes of those who look at the sun and the sun, since it falls on the line that goes out from the eye to the sun, being nearer to the earth than the sun is; just as a small thing can always be seen to hide a large thing when it is nearer to the eyes. The amounts of the eclipse are according to the latitude of the moon as it is seen, up to the limit at which its circle cannot hide anything of the circle of the sun. For this reason the eclipse of the sun has different amounts in places of different latitude, while the eclipse of the moon is seen of one amount in all places.\par
\par As for the distances of the sun and the moon, their diameters and the sizes of their bodies compared with the earth, to find them Ptolemy put forward two lunar eclipses as the basis of the measurement, and carried it out on the assumption that the moon hides the whole sun from sight when it is at its farthest distance from the earth at the times of the conjunctions and is seen on the belt of the signs, and gave the diameter of the sun no perceptible variation, relative to the moon, between its far and near distances from the earth, but made it the same as the moon's. Ptolemy mentioned no observations of solar eclipses in the book and did not use them, and we do not know what prevented Ptolemy. But in the amounts of the solar eclipses that we observed we did not see anything that requires the circle of the moon to cover the circle of the sun and hide it from sight in the ratio mentioned. On the contrary, we also found a clear and evident change in the diameter of the sun, relative to the moon, between its farthest and nearest distances, as geometrical measurement requires, although in itself it is small and imperceptible.\footnote{\emph{Almagest} V 14.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{The translation reads «\RL{\ArNote منعه}», «what prevented Ptolemy», with Plato of Tivoli and Nallino's translation, for the printed «\RL{\ArNote صنعه}», «what Ptolemy did» (p. 84, line 18). Bailly and Delambre, cited by Nallino, explain that Ptolemy did not rely on solar eclipses because Ptolemy's lunar theory put the distance of the moon, and with it its parallax and apparent diameter, far from the truth.}\par
\par \ArPage{85}As our demonstration of what we have described we take two well-known solar eclipses that we observed in our time. In one of them the sun and the moon were in the region of their farthest distance; in the second the sun was in the region of its nearest distance and the moon near its mean distance. The middle of the first eclipse, as we found it by observation, was in the year 1202 of Dhū l-Qarnayn, which is the year 1214 from the death of Alexander, one seasonal hour after noon on the eighth day of Āb, in the city of al-Raqqa, and more than two thirds of the sun was eclipsed to the sight. By our computation the sun at the time of the conjunction was in Leo 20;54 by its mean course and in 19;14 of it by its true course. The moon by its mean course was in Leo 17;7, and in truth, necessarily, with the degree of the sun; so its proper course on the epicycle from the position of the true farthest distance was 333;13. Its mean motion in latitude was 174;43, and in truth 176;51. The visible conjunction, which is the middle of the eclipse, was about an eighth of an hour after the time of the true conjunction; so its motion in latitude at the middle of the eclipse was 176;55, its visible latitude 6 minutes to the south, and its true latitude about 16 minutes to the north. By Ptolemy's computation and on that ratio, the part of the sun eclipsed should have been more than a half and a quarter, and the middle of the eclipse should have been about an hour before the time at which it occurred by measurement.\footnote{8 August 891 (Nallino). Nallino's translation has «apogee» for «nearest distance» in the description of the second eclipse; the Arabic is right: the sun, at Aquarius 8;35, was 46 degrees from its nearest distance at Sagittarius 22;17.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{The translation reads 333;13 for the printed 333;17 (p. 85, line 7), a misprint that the corrigenda do not list: Nallino's apparatus and translation have 13 minutes (the codex has 332;57), and Schiaparelli's recomputation of the eclipse from al-Battānī's tables, in Nallino's commentary, gives 333;12,57. It also confirms Nallino's 17;7 for the mean position of the moon (the codex has 14;7), and puts the true conjunction 1 hour after noon in true time, 47 minutes after noon in mean time.}\par
\par \ArPage{86}The middle of the second eclipse, as we found it by observation in the city of Antioch in the year 1212 of the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn, which is 1224 from the death of Alexander, was about 3⅔ equal hours before noon on the 23rd day of Kānūn II, and the part of the sun eclipsed was a little more than half to the sight. The middle of the eclipse at al-Raqqa, as its time was taken for us, was 3 hours and less than half an equal hour before noon, and less than two thirds of the sun was eclipsed to the sight. By our computation the mean position of the sun at the time of the true conjunction was Aquarius 7;9, and in truth 8;35 of it. The moon by its mean course was in Aquarius 12;49, and in truth, necessarily, with the degree of the sun; so its course in anomaly from the point of the true distance on the epicycle was 126;37, its mean motion in latitude 173;25, and in truth 169;11. The middle of the eclipse to the sight was about a half and a third of an equal hour before the time of the conjunction; its visible latitude was about 10 minutes and its true latitude about a degree less a minute, but its motion in latitude at the middle of the eclipse was 168;45. By Ptolemy's computation and on those ratios the whole sun should have been eclipsed, and the middle of the eclipse should have been about two hours later than the time found. This is a defect of a kind that may not be tolerated in computation at all.\footnote{23 January 901, about 8:20 in the morning, true time (Nallino).}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{Al-Battānī thus puts somewhat more than 10 minutes of time, 2½ degrees of longitude, between Antioch and al-Raqqa, and for the second lunar eclipse below about a quarter of an hour (Nallino); the difference of their longitudes is 2° 55′, 11⅔ minutes of time.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{Schiaparelli's recomputation from al-Battānī's tables (Nallino's commentary) confirms the numbers as Nallino prints them; the codex has 126;32, 199;11 and 198;45 for 126;37, 169;11 and 168;45.}\par
\par We also mention two lunar eclipses of our time on which measurement can be based for what we intend in this kind [of inquiry]. The first of them was in the year 1194 of the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn, which is the year 1206 from the death of Alexander, on the 23rd day of Tammūz. We observed it, and the middle of the eclipse in the city of al-Raqqa was 8 equal hours and a little after noon of that day; a little more than a half and a third of the diameter of the moon was eclipsed. By our computation the sun was in Leo 5;21 by its mean course, and in truth in 4;2 of it; the mean position of the moon was Aquarius 8;45, and in truth, necessarily, opposite the degree of the sun. So its motion in anomaly from the point of the mean farthest distance on the epicycle was 113;0, and in truth 114;9; its mean motion in latitude was 190;49, and in truth 186;5, so that its latitude at the time of the opposition was about 32 minutes to the south. By Ptolemy's computation the part of the moon eclipsed should have been a half and a third and an eighth of its diameter, and the time of the middle of the eclipse should have preceded the time at which we found it by about a half and a quarter of an equal hour.\footnote{23 July 883 (Nallino).}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{113;0: for the minutes the text prints a sign that Nallino's translation and Schiaparelli read as the zero of the codex; Nallino's apparatus suggests 113;8, Halley's reading. Schiaparelli, recomputing the eclipse from al-Battānī's tables, finds 113;13 for the mean anomaly and 114;13 or 114;14 for the true anomaly, for the 114;9 of the text; Nallino's translation has 113;13 and 114;14.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{Oppolzer's canon of eclipses gives this eclipse a magnitude of 11.2 digits, against the 10 digits, ⅚ of the diameter, observed by al-Battānī; for the next, 12.6 digits against nearly 12 (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{87}The second eclipse was in the year 1212 of the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn, which is the year 1224 from the death of Alexander. We observed it ourselves: the middle of the eclipse in the city of Antioch was about 15⅓ equal hours after noon of the second day of Āb, and at al-Raqqa about 15 hours and a third and a quarter of an hour after noon, which is the time of the opposition; a little less than the diameter of the moon was eclipsed. By our computation the sun was in Leo 14;10 by its mean course, and in truth in 14;36 of it; the mean position of the moon was in Aquarius 19;24, and in truth, necessarily, opposite the true degree of the sun. So its course in anomaly from the point of the mean farthest distance on the epicycle was 110;7, and in truth 111;5; its mean motion in latitude was 190;10, and in truth 185;21, so that its true latitude at the middle of the eclipse, which is the time of the opposition, was about 28 minutes. By Ptolemy's computation and on those ratios only a half and a third of the diameter of the moon should have been eclipsed, and the time of the middle of the eclipse should have preceded the time at which it occurred by observation by about a half and a third of an equal hour.\footnote{2 August 901, 15⅓ hours after noon in true time at Antioch, that is, 3 August at about 3:20 in the morning (Nallino).}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{The mean position of the sun must be Leo 16;10: with al-Battānī's equation of the sun this gives the true position 14;36 of the text, and al-Battānī's tables give 16;10 (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary; Nallino's translation has 16;10). Schiaparelli also reads 110;11 for the mean anomaly (the text has 110;7, the codex 110;4) and 111;9 for the true anomaly (the text 111;5): in the Maghribi script of the codex the numerals 9 and 5 are easily confused.}\par
\par So the times and amounts of the eclipses and the positions of the two luminaries differed in everything that we have mentioned, and we found the like of that, and less and more, in many solar and lunar eclipses whose times we observed and whose amounts we established. We have contented ourselves with mentioning these two lunar eclipses. In both of them the sun was in the region of its farthest distance, and the moon in both was at one and the same place in its distance, about 1½ parts less than its mean distance; the latitude of the moon in both was on one side, and between its first latitude and its second there was 0;3,50. The difference between the two eclipses came to one part of 8 and a half and a quarter parts of the diameter of the moon. When that is multiplied by these minutes between the two latitudes, the whole diameter of the moon in these two eclipses comes out about 0;33,30. If the ratio of the diameter of the shadow to the diameter of the moon is the ratio that Ptolemy stated, which is about 2⅗ times the whole diameter of the moon, the radius of the shadow at the place of the moon's passage is about 0;43,30. In the measure in which the 36 minutes 10 seconds that the moon moves in an hour at the times of the conjunctions and oppositions, its greatest course at those times, are 35⅓ minutes, the amount of the diameter of the moon then, the 30 minutes 12 seconds that the moon moves in an hour at those times, its least course then, are about 29½ minutes; this happens when it is at its farthest distance, and that is then the diameter of the moon. Ptolemy, however, worked on the basis that it is 0;31,20, and carried out on that basis the computation and the derivation of the distances and the diameters.\footnote{The anomalies of the sun in the two eclipses were 42 and 52 degrees. The distances of the moon, in parts of which its mean distance is 60, were 58.04 and 58.32, that is, 1.96 and 1.68 parts less than the mean. The difference of the latitudes, 4 minutes by the rounded values given above, is 0;3,49 by an exact computation: al-Battānī computed the eclipses more precisely than the text reports them (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{The magnitudes reported above, a little more than ⅚ of the diameter and a little less than the whole, differ by about ⅙, which would give 6 rather than 8¾, and a diameter of the moon of only 23 minutes. Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary, takes the passage as a check that the observations do not contradict a diameter of 33½ minutes, not as a measurement of it.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{Al-Battānī takes the apparent diameters of the moon to be in the ratio of its hourly motions. The hourly motions given here, 0;36,10 and 0;30,12, are not quite those of al-Battānī's tables, 0;36,4 and 0;30,18 (Schiaparelli). Ptolemy's 0;31,20 is in \emph{Almagest} V 14.}\par
\par \ArPage{88}Since what we wanted has become clear, and the diameter of the moon according to its passage at each of the two distances has become evident, and we have taken the ratio of the diameter of the shadow to the diameter of the moon to be the ratio mentioned, the radius of the shadow at the farthest passage of the moon is by it about 0;38,30 when the sun is at its farthest distance; when the sun is at its farthest distance and the moon at its nearest distance, the radius of the shadow is about 46 minutes. So it is clear that the radius of the shadow at the farthest passage of the moon is less than what Ptolemy worked with by about 2⅙ minutes, because of the excess that fell in the diameter of the moon in Ptolemy's computation. As for the radius of the shadow at the nearest passage, it is equal in amount in the two computations. It must also be that the radius of the shadow has, between the farthest and the nearest distance of the sun, a variation that measurement requires, of about 50 seconds: the radius of the shadow when the sun is at its nearest must be less by this amount than when it is at its farthest distance.\footnote{Ibn Yūnus objected that al-Battānī and al-Nayrīzī make the radius of the shadow vary by only about 7 minutes between the farthest and the nearest passage of the moon, much less than the geometry of the shadow requires; Ibn Yūnus agreed that the nearness of the sun changes it by no more than a minute (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par As for the solar eclipses, Ptolemy worked in them as we have mentioned, and made the diameter of the moon, when it is at its farthest distance, subtend an arc of the ecliptic of 0;31,20, and held that it then hides the whole sun at the times of the visible conjunctions, when it is on the belt of the signs to the sight. For this reason Ptolemy made the diameter of the sun equal to the diameter of the moon, so that, though the sun is many times greater, the moon hides it; and gave the diameter of the sun, relative to the moon, no change between its two distances, as Ptolemy did for the moon. But it had become clear to us by measurement that the diameter of the moon at its farthest passage subtends an arc of the sphere of 29½ minutes, and that it cannot hide the whole sun from sight when it is at its farthest distance, since the sun's diameter is greater than its own, subtending 0;31,20. That is when the sun is at its farthest distance, when its course in an hour is 0;2,23; when it is at its nearest distance, its course in an hour is 0;2,33. In the measure in which the 2 minutes and 23 seconds are 31⅓ minutes, the 2 minutes and 33 seconds are about 33⅔ minutes; so we find that the diameter of the sun, relative to the moon, changes between its two distances by 2⅓ minutes. By this the amounts of the solar eclipses came out right for us, and it became clear that the radius of the shadow at the farthest passage of the moon subtends an arc of the circle of the sphere of 0;38,30, and its radius at the nearest passage of the moon an arc of about 46 minutes.\footnote{The translation follows the corrigenda in reading «\RL{\ArNote تَغْيِيرًا}», «change», for «\RL{\ArNote تَعْيِيرًا}» (p. 88, line 6).}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{From these values al-Battānī concludes that the moon at its farthest distance cannot hide the whole sun, so that an eclipse of the sun can leave a ring of the sun visible, which Ptolemy's equal diameters exclude (Delambre, cited by Nallino). The eclipse of 8 August 891 described above was annular along a band across Asia Minor and Mesopotamia, not far from al-Raqqa (Oppolzer's canon, cited by Schiaparelli). Al-Battānī's diameters of the sun, 0;31,20 at its farthest and 0;33,40 at its nearest distance, compare with the modern 31′ 32″ and 32′ 36″, and al-Battānī's diameter of the moon at its farthest distance, 0;29,30, with the modern 29′ 26″ (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par Let us try to show the distance of the sun and what appears with it. This cannot be done in the way close to the view on which Ptolemy worked, except by repeating the figure as it is and with the ratios given in Ptolemy's book, and then apportioning it according to what we found by our observations; for whenever we work with what we found by our observations, the ratios do not come out right and the distance is multiplied many monstrous times. So we draw this figure and then follow it with the explanation. Picture what is drawn for you in it, and picture how it is worked, and you will get it right, if God wills.\footnote{Ptolemy's figure and numbers, which follow, are those of \emph{Almagest} V 15. The figure rests on the relation that the parallaxes of the sun and the moon together equal the apparent radii of the shadow and the sun together. With Ptolemy's values (radius of the shadow 0;40,40, of the sun 0;15,40, parallax of the moon at 64;10 radii of the earth about 53½ minutes) the parallax of the sun comes to about 2¾ minutes; with al-Battānī's radius of the shadow, 0;38,30, to about ⅗ of a minute, and the distance of the sun grows almost fivefold.}\par
\par \ArPage{89}I began, with God's help, and drew three circles, their centres on a straight line, facing one another, some greater than others. On the greatest of them, which is the circle of the sun, I wrote ABG, and D at its centre; on the one next to it in size, which is the circle of the earth, KLM, and N on its centre; and on the smallest circle, in the middle, which is the third, the circle of the moon, HZḤ, and Ṭ on its centre. I placed it between the circle of the sun and that of the earth. I drew the two lines of the rays at the ends of the diameter of the sun, the signs A and G, touching the circle of the earth at the points K and M and meeting on the other side at the point S, so that the triangle ASG is the cone. The line DS divides it into two halves, so that it becomes two triangles, each of them right-angled. From the centre of the earth, the sign N, I also drew two lines that touch the circle of the moon at the signs Ḥ and H and pass through the signs A and G, touching the circle of the sun at them, because of the solar eclipses in which the moon hides the whole sun from the sign N. We draw the diameter AG, and the diameter HḤ, which we extend to the point Q; we draw also the diameter KM. We take the position of the moon when it is at its farthest distance from the earth at the times of the lunar eclipses to be the point F, and make the line ṬN equal to the line FN. Since the line DS passes through all the centres, it is clear in this figure that the line DA is the radius of the sun, the line ṬḤ the radius of the moon and the line MN the radius of the earth. We also draw the line ʿFḌ, which is the diameter of the shadow; so the line FḌ is the radius of the shadow.\footnote{The text calls the greatest circle «the circle of the earth» as well; it is the circle of the sun.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{The lines from A and G touch the circles near the ends of the diameters drawn, not at them; the difference is imperceptible, and Ptolemy says that the lines through the points of contact are equal to the diameters to the senses (Nallino).}\par
\begin{figure}[tp]\centering\begin{tikzpicture}\draw[line width=1.44pt] (61.29pt,424.62pt) circle[radius=53.61pt];
\draw[line width=1.24pt] (61.93pt,307.27pt) circle[radius=20.04pt];
\draw[line width=1.23pt] (62.33pt,232.92pt) circle[radius=27.58pt];
\draw[line width=1.20pt] (60.94pt,478.23pt) -- (63.59pt,22.26pt);
\draw[line width=1.32pt] (7.69pt,424.13pt) -- (114.90pt,424.92pt);
\draw[line width=1.24pt] (7.29pt,424.13pt) -- (63.60pt,21.82pt);
\draw[line width=1.25pt] (115.09pt,424.92pt) -- (63.60pt,21.82pt);
\draw[line width=1.26pt] (8.24pt,424.13pt) -- (81.52pt,162.09pt);
\draw[line width=1.29pt] (114.59pt,424.92pt) -- (62.16pt,231.33pt);
\draw[line width=1.09pt] (41.90pt,306.88pt) -- (100.13pt,307.80pt);
\draw[line width=1.09pt] (34.75pt,232.14pt) -- (89.91pt,233.01pt);
\draw[line width=1.08pt] (44.55pt,157.93pt) -- (81.04pt,158.31pt);
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (65.64pt,429.18pt) {\normalsize D};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (121.02pt,426.30pt) {\normalsize A};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (0.00pt,418.56pt) {\normalsize G};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (69.12pt,379.20pt) {\normalsize B};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (67.92pt,314.46pt) {\normalsize Ṭ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (106.56pt,309.72pt) {\normalsize Q};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (37.56pt,305.46pt) {\normalsize H};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (88.80pt,300.12pt) {\normalsize Ḥ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (56.82pt,280.26pt) {\normalsize Z};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (26.82pt,234.60pt) {\normalsize K};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (96.36pt,229.50pt) {\normalsize M};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (56.76pt,225.96pt) {\normalsize N};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (57.06pt,196.38pt) {\normalsize L};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (55.32pt,164.52pt) {\normalsize F};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (90.18pt,159.84pt) {\normalsize Ḍ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (37.80pt,155.70pt) {\LARGE ʿ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (64.20pt,0.00pt) {\normalsize S};\end{tikzpicture}\par\smallskip{\footnotesize The line ḌN is not in the figure of the codex or in that of Plato of Tivoli; Nallino added it.}\end{figure}
\par \ArPage{90}What Ptolemy worked with, and on which the course of the computation rests, is that the line AS is 60 parts in the measure in which the diameter is 120 parts; the triangle ADS is right-angled and the distance is great, so the line DS is also about 60 parts in that measure. The angle ḤNṬ is 0;15,40 in the measure in which the four right angles of the circle that goes round the right-angled triangle ADS are 360, and the angle ḌNF is in this measure 0;40,40. The sine ṬḤ, which subtends the angle ḤNṬ, is 0;16,24, and that is the line ṬḤ; the sine of the angle ḌNF is 0;42,35, and that is the line FḌ, in the measure in which the line FN is 60 parts. But in the measure in which the line MN, the radius of the earth, was one part, and in which the line FN, the distance of the moon from the centre of the earth, was 64;10, the line ṬḤ is 0;17,33 and the line FḌ 0;45,38; so the ratio of FḌ to ṬḤ is the ratio of 2⅗ to one, approximately. The line ṬN is equal to the line FN, and therefore the line FḌ and the line ṬQ together are twice the line MN. When the line FḌ, which has been shown to be 0;45,38, and the line ṬḤ, which has been shown to be 0;17,33, are added together, the sum is 1 part 3 minutes 11 seconds of the double; when the line MN, which is the single one, is taken from it, the line ṬN remains, 3 minutes 11 seconds, and the line DL, which completes the double, is 56 minutes 49 seconds. Likewise the line ṬN is 0;3,11 and the line DṬ 0;56,49 when the whole line DN is made one part, and the line DN measures the line ṬN about 18⅘ times. Likewise the line DA measures the line ṬḤ also about 18⅘ times, which is the ratio of the line DN to the line ṬN. It has been shown that the line ṬN is 64;10 in the measure in which MN is a line of one part; so the line DN, the distance of the sun from the centre of the earth, is about 1210 times the line MN, the radius of the earth. The diameter of the sun is about 18⅘ times the diameter of the moon, but the diameter of the earth is about 3⅖ times the diameter of the moon; so the diameter of the sun is 5½ times the diameter of the earth.\footnote{Nallino takes «that the line AS is 60 parts … about 60 parts in that measure» and «of the circle that goes round the right-angled triangle ADS» for interpolations, found also in Plato of Tivoli: neither AS nor DS is 60 parts, as what follows shows. The angles are the apparent radii of the moon and of the shadow at the moon's farthest distance (\emph{Almagest} V 14).}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{«Twice» is supplied by Nallino. The line that completes the double, 2 − 1;3,11 = 0;56,49, is ḤQ; the text calls it DL. The words «the line ṬN remains, 3 minutes 11 seconds, and» anticipate the next sentence, where DN is taken as one part; Nallino's translation leaves them out and reads ḤQ (they are in the codex and in Plato of Tivoli). DN is to DṬ as MN is to ḤQ (Nallino). Ptolemy finds the same 1210 radii of the earth in \emph{Almagest} V 15.}\par
\par \ArPage{91}The cube, which has length, breadth and depth, that results from multiplying one by itself and then by one is one: I mean the cube of the earth. The cube that results from multiplying 5½ by itself and then by 5½ is 166 and a quarter and an eighth. The cube that results from multiplying 18⅘ by itself and then by 18⅘ is 6644½. The cube that results from multiplying the diameter of the moon, which is the line HḤ, by itself and then by the diameter of the moon is one part of 39¼ of the cube of one. So the size of the sun is 166 and a quarter and an eighth times the size of the earth, and 6644½ times the size of the moon; and the size of the earth is 39¼ times the size of the moon.\footnote{The translation reads «\RL{\ArNote والعُمْق}», «and depth», for «\RL{\ArNote والغَمْق}» (p. 90, line 15), a misprint that the corrigenda do not list.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{Ptolemy (\emph{Almagest} V 16) makes the sun about 170 times the size of the earth, the quotient of 6644½ by 39¼; al-Battānī cubes the rounded ratio 5½.}\par
\par Further, if the line MN is one part, the line FḌ is 0;45,38, and the line FN in the same measure is 64;10. If the whole line SN is made one part, the line FS of it is 0;45,38 and the line FN 0;14,22, which remains to complete the one part. Since the line SF is [to the line FN] in the ratio of 45 minutes 38 seconds to 14 minutes 22 seconds, the line SF is 203 and a half and a third in the measure in which the line FN is 64⅙. When the line FN is added to the line SF, the line SN, from the end of the shadow to the centre of the earth, is about 268 times the radius of the earth; but [the line] from the centre of the sun to the end of the shadow, the line DS, is 1478 times the radius of the earth, the line MN. These are the ratios that Ptolemy derived, and the distances, on the basis of the size of the diameter of the shadow and the sizes of the diameters of the moon and the sun with which Ptolemy worked.\footnote{The words in brackets are supplied by Nallino. SN is to FS as MN is to FḌ. Ptolemy's values are in \emph{Almagest} V 15.}\par
\par \ArPage{92}Since we have made clear the difference that appeared in this, that the diameter of the moon at its farthest passage is only 0;29,30, the radius of the shadow at the farthest passage of the moon 0;38,30, and the diameter of the sun, as has been said, 0;31,20, on this basis we find that the diameter of the moon falls short of the diameter of the sun by a minute and a half and a third. If we take its amount out of the 5 minutes and a half and a third by which the moon varies in its distance from the earth and its nearness to it, we find it to be about a third less a fifth of a tenth of that. When we take [that part] of the 10⅓ parts that are the whole diameter of the epicycle, by which the distance of the moon from the earth varies at the times of the conjunctions and oppositions, it comes to about 3⅕ parts. When we subtract that from the 64;10 that is the farthest distance of the moon from the earth, we find that the distance of the moon from the centre of the earth at the place where its diameter is 0;31,20, where it can hide the whole sun from sight, is about 60 and a half and a third and a tenth and a third of a tenth times [the radius of the earth]. When the diameter of the moon is 0;31,20, the radius of the shadow at the place of the moon's passage is about 0;40,40, which is close to the amount with which Ptolemy worked. When we multiply the 18⅘ by the 60 and a half and a third and a tenth and a third of a tenth, the distance of the sun from the centre of the earth when it is at its farthest distance is 1146 times the radius of the earth; and when we divide that by the 4½ parts that are, approximately, between the diameter of the earth and the diameter of the sun, [the line] from the centre of the earth to the end of the shadow is 254⅔ times the radius of the earth. Since the whole diameter of the epicycle of the sun, which is twice the distance between the centres, as appeared earlier in this book, is about 4⅙ parts, the variation of the distance of the sun from the centre of the earth is about 76 times the radius of the earth, and half of that, 38, is the share of the mean distance. So the nearest distance of the sun from the centre of the earth is 1070 times the radius of the earth, its mean distance 1108, and its farthest distance 1146, as has been shown; and the moon hides the sun from sight when there are about 1085 times the radius of the earth between it and the sun. This is the ratio that appeared to us from the solar eclipses.\footnote{The distance is 60 29/30 radii of the earth; 18⅘ × 60 29/30 = 1146, and 1146 − 60 29/30 = 1085 (Nallino).}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{Al-Battānī's distances of the sun, 1070 to 1146 radii of the earth, are like Ptolemy's 1210 about twenty times too small: the mean distance of the sun is about 23,500 radii of the earth.}\par
\par As for the moon's receiving light, it is from the sun, and the increase and decrease of its light are according to its distance from it and its nearness to it. For of every round body the eye falls only on the half that faces it. So the half of the globe of the moon that faces the earth is the half that faces the sun when we see the moon full of light, and that is at the times of the middle of the lunar month. When the half that faces the earth is other than the half that faces the sun, and the half that faces the sun is the remaining one, we see no light in it at all; that is the time of the dark of the moon. In between, we see the light in it according to how much of the light of the half that faces the sun falls in the half that faces the eyes. The farther it goes from the sun from the time of the dark of the moon, the more light there is in it, and it increases until the moon is opposite the sun, about a diameter of the sphere away, and is full of light. Then it begins to decrease, as it did in increasing, until it vanishes at the end of the month.\par
\par \ArPage{93}We draw an example of this: a circle for the sphere of the moon about the centre Y, with the diameter ZN. We extend the diameter ZN to the point B, take the point B as centre and draw about it a circle for the sun, on the diameter AG. Let the point Y be the centre of the earth, and the line BY represent the distance of the sun from the earth. We take the point N of the inclined lunar sphere as centre and draw about it a circle for the moon at the time of the conjunction, when the centre of its circle is under the centre of the circle of the sun in direction, I mean on the line that passes through the centre of the sun and of the earth. Then the centre of the circle of the moon moves away from the point N on its sphere according to its course in a day, or in more than a day or less, until it reaches the opposition, where the centre of its circle is the point Z. We mark the centres of the circles of the moon between the points Z and N, one after another, with the sign D. From the two ends of the diameter of the sun, the signs A and G, we draw lines to all the circles of the moon, so that two lines touch each of the circles at the two ends of a diameter of the circle of the moon; we mark on them in all the circles the signs K and Ṭ, and join the points K and Ṭ of each circle by a line passing through the centre D. Then we colour red the bright half of the circle of the moon, which faces the sun, and blot out the other half, which the sun does not see. Then we draw from the point Y, the centre of the earth, to each of the circles of the moon two lines touching the circle of the moon, mark the places of contact on each circle with the signs M and L, and join them by a line passing through the point D of each circle. So we find the half of that circle on which the eye falls, which is the half that faces the earth; and according to how much of the bright half that faces the sun falls in this half that faces the earth and on which the eye falls, is the amount of light seen in the moon. It is clear in this figure that the points M and L of the circle of the moon fall, at the dark of the moon, at the place of the points K and Ṭ. When the moon moves away from the sun, a small segment of the half of its circle that faces the sun falls in the half that faces the earth; the more its distance from the sun increases, the more the segment grows, until the quarter of the month is reached, when the [lit] half amounts to half of the half that faces the earth. Then the segment continues to grow beyond that amount until the moon is opposite the sun, when the whole half that faces the sun is the half that faces the earth, and the points M and L come to the places of K and Ṭ.\footnote{In the printed figure the dark halves are hatched and the bright halves left white. Its letters are Nallino's: the figure of the codex and that of Plato of Tivoli have none. Nallino leaves out the lines from A and G that touch the circles of the moon at K and Ṭ, to keep the figure clear, and the lines from Y that touch them at M and L.}\par
\par We shall draw this figure and place ten circles of the moon in it; the distance of the moon from the sun in each of the nine circles after the circle of the conjunction is 20 parts of the parts of the half circle, and the shape of the light in the circle of the moon is according to the amounts of these distances from the direction of the position of the sun, which is the point B. Since this is so, the increase and decrease of the light of the moon has become clear in this figure, as you see it on the plane of the circle. In the round body, however, it is twice each segment, because its angle, in the spherical way, is unlike this figure; we shall represent it in what follows, if God Most High wills, and success is from God.\footnote{That is, the circles stand 20 degrees apart: nine steps of 20 degrees lead from the conjunction to the opposition. For «what follows», Nallino suggests chapter 43.}\par

\par \ArPage{94}It has become clear from what has appeared that, in the measure in which the circle of the moon in which the light is seen complete is 15, each 12 parts of its distance from the sun are one part, up to the full 180, which make up the whole 15; and if the circle of the moon is 12 parts, each 15 parts of the distance are one of the parts of the light. On these ratios we have drawn the two circles and explained them, if God Most High wills.\footnote{That is (Nallino): if the disc of the moon in the figure is made 15 degrees of its orbit, twelve discs fill the half orbit; if it is made 12 degrees, fifteen.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 31. On the description of the spheres of the five stars and their states}{31}{\ArPage{95}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: As for the description of the spheres of the five stars and of their states, which have become known by way of demonstration from the anomaly of their course: each of them has four spheres, in the manner of the spheres of the moon. The first is made like the ecliptic: its centre is the centre of the ecliptic, it lies level beneath it, and its motion is like its motion. The second is the inclined sphere: its centre is the centre of the parecliptic sphere and its size the size of that sphere, and its greatest inclination from the circle of the parecliptic sphere, towards the north and the south, is the whole latitude of the star. Inside this sphere is another sphere, whose centre lies outside the centre of the two spheres; it hangs from it and touches it at a point, which is the point of the farthest distance. From the amount between the centres of the two spheres the equation of the anomaly and the centre of each of the stars is known, as was shown for the moon. The fourth sphere is the epicycle of the star. Its centre runs on this eccentric sphere from the point of the farthest distance in the order of the signs, by the amount of the mean motion of the star in longitude in a day; and the star moves on the epicycle, from the point of the distance that is seen from the centre of the ecliptic, also in the order of the signs, by the amount of the star's own motion in anomaly each day. The radius of each of the epicycles of the stars is the amount of [the sine of] its mean equation. It has a displacement in the lower and the upper part of the circle: it grows in the lower part, exceeding the mean, and shrinks in the upper part, falling short of the mean. This mean equation is what is set down in the sixth column of the tables of the equation of the star; the amounts of the shortfall are set down in the fifth column, and the amounts of the excess in the seventh. What is set down in the fourth column is the minutes in proportion to which [a part] of the excess and the shortfall is taken, in the way that I gave for the moon for the excess. What is set down in the third column is the equation of the anomaly and the centre, which results from the distance between the centres. These are the figures of the spheres that we said we would draw.\footnote{The words in brackets, «the sine of», are added by Nallino's emendanda (Part II, p. XXXI). The «centre» is the distance of the centre of the epicycle from the farthest distance of the eccentric, counted at the centre of the eccentric; the «equation of the anomaly and the centre», which the eccentricity produces, is applied to both (Nallino).}\par
\par\begin{center}\ArPage{96}\begin{tikzpicture}\draw[line width=1.35pt] (143.42pt,130.06pt) circle[radius=99.43pt];

\draw[line width=1.32pt] (143.53pt,130.06pt) -- (224.68pt,124.71pt);
\draw[line width=1.32pt] (143.66pt,114.88pt) -- (242.41pt,126.89pt);
\draw[line width=1.32pt] (242.41pt,126.89pt) -- (270.62pt,153.56pt);
\draw[line width=1.32pt] (242.41pt,126.89pt) -- (277.59pt,110.50pt);
\draw[line width=1.23pt] (143.66pt,114.88pt) -- (265.04pt,81.12pt);
\draw[line width=1.23pt] (223.95pt,124.88pt) -- (217.30pt,95.74pt);
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (144.00pt,246.84pt) {\normalsize A};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (135.90pt,202.50pt) {\normalsize Ḥ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (138.48pt,136.38pt) {\normalsize M};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (139.20pt,108.78pt) {\normalsize H};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (137.70pt,57.36pt) {\normalsize K};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (137.34pt,38.46pt) {\normalsize G};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (144.30pt,0.00pt) {\normalsize Z};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (0.00pt,138.36pt) {\normalsize F};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (38.52pt,128.28pt) {\normalsize B};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (35.64pt,115.26pt) {\normalsize Ṣ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (69.12pt,121.08pt) {\normalsize Ṭ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (75.72pt,106.92pt) {\normalsize S};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (6.54pt,90.66pt) {\normalsize Q};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (49.20pt,69.18pt) {\LARGE ʿ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (15.66pt,58.20pt) {\normalsize N};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (274.02pt,161.10pt) {\normalsize LA};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (217.08pt,149.58pt) {\normalsize W};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (218.70pt,134.34pt) {\normalsize L};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (246.78pt,136.98pt) {\normalsize D};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (284.04pt,106.32pt) {\normalsize Y};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (205.50pt,89.34pt) {\normalsize Sh};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (265.56pt,73.62pt) {\normalsize Ḍ};\end{tikzpicture}\footnote{Nallino prints the figure as the codex gives it, «most ineptly» in Nallino's words. Schiaparelli, in the commentary, points out that the circle of the ecliptic and the inclined circle cannot be drawn in one plane as two circles about different centres, that the nodes B and D are put 90 degrees from the line of the apsides, which is true of no planet, and that the lines of sight need not touch the epicycles. Schiaparelli suspects that the circle drawn about M as large as the ecliptic was at first the equant of Ptolemy's theory, the circle about which the centre of the epicycle moves uniformly.}\end{center}
\par Al-Battānī said: The first thing I drew was the circle of the parecliptic sphere, marked ABGD, about the centre H; another circle, for the inclined sphere, marked ḤBZD, whose centre is also the point H, as it falls on the globe; and a third circle, for the eccentric sphere, marked ḤṬKL, with M at its centre. Clearly the point Ḥ is the farthest distance of the eccentric sphere and the point K its nearest distance. We take the point Ṭ of the eccentric sphere as the centre of the circle of the epicycle and draw about it the circle of the epicycle, marked FQS. We draw from it the line MṬQ and the line HṬF, take the position of the star on the epicycle to be the point ʿ, and draw the line HʿN, along which the motion of the star on the ecliptic is seen. Clearly the diameter AZ passes through the centres. Again we take the point L of the eccentric sphere as centre and draw about it a circle for the epicycle, marked Y LA Sh; we draw the two lines M L Y and H L LA, put the star at the position Sh of the epicycle, and draw the line H Sh Ḍ, along which it is seen on the ecliptic.\footnote{The text has S for Sh, in the letters of the second epicycle (the codex has ʿ) and for the position of the star on it; the figure and what follows have Sh, as Nallino's translation reads.}\par
\par \ArPage{97}It is clear in these circles that, when the point A is the direction of the farthest distance on the ecliptic, the star is at the point ʿ of the epicycle whose centre is Ṭ, and the centre of the epicycle is at the point Ḥ, the line that goes out from the point H passes through the centre M and the points A and Ḥ, and we find the point of the farthest distance on the epicycle at the sign Q, on the line MṬQ. For the line MṬQ is then in the place of the line HMA, and the place of Q is then the place of A; so the mean and the true farthest distance are the same, without any difference, since the line HṬF, along which the true point of the farthest distance is seen, then also comes into the place of the line HA. But when the centre of the epicycle comes to the place Ṭ, between the points Ḥ and K of the eccentric sphere, which is less than half its circle, the place of the true distance on the epicycle is the point F and the place of the mean farthest distance the point Q; from Q the course of the star on its own epicycle is counted, which is the arc Qʿ, and so its course in anomaly is greater than the arc Qʿ by the arc QF, which is the difference. Likewise the position of the centre of the epicycle that is seen from the point H on the ecliptic is less than the one seen from the centre M, again by the arc QF, since the point F is nearer to the point M than the point Q is. Likewise, when we put the centre of the epicycle in the second half of the circle, at the point L, the position of the centre of the epicycle in which it is seen on the ecliptic from the centre H becomes greater than the position in which it is seen from the centre M, by the arc LA Y, and the true farthest distance, which is seen from the centre H, becomes less than the mean farthest distance, which is seen from the centre M, again by the arc LA Y. For the star is at the mark Sh of the epicycle; its mean motion on its own epicycle is from the point Y to the point LA and then to the point Sh, and its true motion is from the point LA, so that the arc LA Sh of the epicycle is smaller than the arc Y LA Sh by the arc Y LA. Therefore the equation of the anomaly and the centre is added to the centre when the motion of the centre of the epicycle is between the point of the farthest distance of the eccentric sphere and its point of the nearest distance on the side of the sign L, and it is subtracted from the anomaly; and when the centre of the epicycle is in the second half of the eccentric sphere, on the side of the sign Ṭ, the equation of the anomaly and the centre, which is the arc Y LA, is subtracted from the centre and added to the anomaly. When the true position of the centre on the ecliptic is known, it gives the shares of the difference, of the excess and the shortfall, that belong to the epicycle at its displacement, since it is on the eccentric sphere.\footnote{The text has the centre of the epicycle move uniformly about the centre M of the eccentric, and so counts the «centre» at M; in Ptolemy's theory the uniform motion is about the equant point, which lies as far from M as H does, on the other side (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary). On the side of Ṭ the equation is the arc QF; the text names the arc Y LA of the other half (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{98}Further, when the star is at the position ʿ of the epicycle, its distance from the point Q is less than half the circle of the epicycle, so the position in which it is seen on the ecliptic is greater than the position of the centre Ṭ on the ecliptic by the arc on the perpendicular ʿṬ. When the star is at the position Sh, the arc LA Sh of the epicycle is more than half its circle, so the position in which it is seen on the ecliptic is less than the position in which the centre L is seen by the arc that falls on the perpendicular L Sh. Therefore the mean equation of the star, corrected for the displacement of the epicycle, is added to the corrected centre when the corrected anomaly of the star is less than 180, and is subtracted from it when the corrected anomaly is more than 180; what results after that addition or subtraction is the distance of the star on the ecliptic from the point of the farthest distance of the eccentric sphere, whose position on the ecliptic is determined.\footnote{«The arc on the perpendicular ʿṬ» is the arc whose sine is the perpendicular from Ṭ to the line of sight Hʿ, that is, the angle at H between the centre of the epicycle and the star; in the figure the line of sight touches the epicycle at ʿ (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par As for the cause of the retrogradation of the wandering stars, it is that the change of the equation of the star, in its increase and decrease in one day, is at some of its places on the epicycle greater than its mean course, which is the motion of the centre of the epicycle on the eccentric sphere in a day. When the true position of the star is computed for a day, and its course for another day is then added to its mean position, and from that is subtracted an equation that exceeds the first equation by more than its mean course in that day, or to it is added an equation that is less than the first equation by more than the mean course of the star in the day, the position of the star on the ecliptic is less than the position in which it was at first. This can happen only when the star is in the nearer half of its epicycle, which is necessarily smaller than the upper half, since the arc of the eccentric sphere bounds these two halves: the lower half is the arc Sh W, and the upper half the arc W LA Y Sh. So when the course of the star from the point of the farthest distance on the epicycle reaches the point W, it is there like one standing set up in the circle, for it then falls on the line that touches the epicycle and goes out from the point H. The decrease and increase of the change of its equation in a day are then equal to its mean course in a day, and no motion appears in it until it passes the point W and falls in the lower half. Then its motion on the ecliptic continues to be seen towards the preceding side of the signs until it reaches the sign Sh and falls on the tangent line a second time, when it is like one standing raised in the circle on that side. When it rises from the point Sh, its motion begins to be seen in the order of the signs, as long as it is in the upper half of the epicycle. Yet the star in itself has no retrogradation in its course; this befalls it only for us, because of the difference of the centres of its spheres and its state on the epicycle.\footnote{In modern terms, the motion in longitude in a day is the mean motion together with the change of the equation; when the change is negative and greater than the mean motion, the star goes backwards. The sentence from «When the true position» restates this obscurely (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{Delambre, cited by Nallino, points out that at the point of contact the star moves along the line of sight, so that its motion on the epicycle adds nothing to the motion of the centre: the star is not stationary there, and the stations fall between the points of contact and the nearest point of the epicycle. The points of contact are not where the epicycle cuts the eccentric either, as the figure itself shows (Nallino).}\par
\par As for the sun and the moon, this does not befall them for us, because the course of each of them in a day is many times greater than the change of its equation in a day. So the state of retrogradation is not evident in them, although they too must in themselves have a course in the nearer half of their epicycles; but it is not perceived in them.\par
\par \ArPage{99}We have tested the motion of each of these five wandering stars many times at places on their epicycles: at one, when the star was at their point of the farthest distance; at a second, when it was at the point of the mean distance; at a third, when it was at the point of the nearest distance; and at other places of the centre of the epicycle on the eccentric sphere, farther from and nearer to its point of the farthest distance. So we established what appeared of their mean motions in longitude in excess of the motions set down in Ptolemy's book, and also what appeared of the anomaly of their motions and of the positions of their farthest distances on their spheres eccentric to the ecliptic. We corrected it and entered it in the tables, after adding to their course in longitude the correction that we found in each of them. Their equations we found close to those in Ptolemy's book, and the positions of their distances likewise, and we entered them as they were, except the farthest distance of Jupiter: we measured it by the moon many times, according to the visible position of the moon at the times of the measurements, and found that it was less than the amount set down by about 8 parts.\footnote{In the 740 years between the epochs of Ptolemy's tables (AD 137) and al-Battānī's (AD 880), al-Battānī's mean motions in longitude exceed Ptolemy's by 2° 54′ for Saturn, 3° 5′ for Jupiter and 4° 12′ for Mars, and are better than Ptolemy's (Nallino). Al-Battānī's farthest distances of Saturn and Mars are 20° 18′ greater than Ptolemy's (for Mars with Nallino's correction of the minutes), that of Jupiter only 12° 19′, the 8 degrees of the text. Al-Battānī had raised Ptolemy's longitudes of the fixed stars by 11° 10′; Nallino does not know why al-Battānī used 20° 18′ for the farthest distances, and supposes that the value rests on al-Battānī's own observations.}\par
\par Since the motions of the upper stars on their epicycles are what remains of the mean course of the sun when the mean motion of the star in longitude is taken from it, and the mean course of Venus and Mercury is equal to the mean course of the sun, their anomaly is obtained from the tables. We found the anomaly of Venus greater than its anomaly as set down by about 4½ parts, and that of Mercury by about 2½ parts. We divided that over the time between us and Ptolemy, and added what fell to one day to the course in anomaly of each of them for a day. As far as we were able, we have left nothing from which we saw that a defect might arise without putting it right, although their motions cannot be grasped exactly, as the motions of the two luminaries can, since their observations were made only when they came close to some of the fixed stars. Since their farthest distances also move with the motion of the sphere of the fixed stars, we have had no need to set down tables of the motions for the anomaly of each of the upper stars and for the course of the two lower stars, or of the measurement of their distances from the Heart of the Lion or from other fixed stars. We have also left out the mention of the observations of them that we made at the places mentioned, for the sake of brevity and so as not to lengthen the discourse in the explanation that we are attempting for each of them.\footnote{Over the 740 years between the epochs of the two sets of tables, al-Battānī's anomalies exceed Ptolemy's by 4° 28′ for Venus and by 0° 57′ for Mercury; Nallino's translation therefore has 1 degree for Mercury, where the codex has 2½ and Plato of Tivoli 11½. Such tables, with the positions measured from the Heart of the Lion (Regulus), are in Theon's tables (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{100}As for the latitudes of the three upper stars, I mean Saturn, Jupiter and Mars, they come close to the amounts that we found in Ptolemy's book, and we kept them as they were for finding their latitudes. But in the latitudes of Venus and Mercury we found a great discrepancy, which arose, as we think, from the procedure used to find the latitude; so we changed the method of working that we found for them in Ptolemy's book to one that we saw comes close to and agrees with the latitudes that we find for them by observation. It may be that what happened in the procedure in Ptolemy's book came from the translator of Ptolemy's Greek words, or from a fault in the copy from which the book was translated; and God knows best.\footnote{Al-Battānī's procedure for the latitudes of the five stars is in chapter 47.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 32. On knowing the eras of the Arabs, the Romans, the Copts and the Persians, and on converting them into one another}{32}{\ArPage{100}}{\footnote{The translation reads «\RL{\ArNote المستقبلة}» for «\RL{\ArNote المستقباة}» (p. 105, line 4) and «\RL{\ArNote بتأريخ}» for «\RL{\ArNote يتأريخ}» (p. 105, line 9), misprints that the corrigenda do not list.}}
\par Al-Battānī said: The names of the months of the Arabs are al-Muḥarram, Ṣafar, Rabīʿ I, Rabīʿ II, Jumādā I, Jumādā II, Rajab, Shaʿbān, Ramaḍān, Shawwāl, al-Qaʿda and al-Ḥijja.
