They are a month of 30 days and a month of 29 days, alternately, except Dhū l-Ḥijja, which has 29 days and a fifth and a sixth.\footnote{A fifth and a sixth make 11/30 of a day: the lunar year has 354 11/30 days, which rest on the synodic month of 29;31,50 days (Nallino). Over thirty years Dhū l-Ḥijja gains 11 days, and the year in which it has 30 days is a leap year.}\par
\par The names of the months of the Romans, with the beginning [of the year] of the Greeks and the people of Egypt: Aylūl, 30 days; Tishrīn I, 31 days; Tishrīn II, 30 days; Kānūn I, 31 days; Kānūn II, also 31 days; Subāṭ, 28 days and a quarter of a day: the quarter is added to the quarter for three successive years, and the year is intercalated in the fourth, Subāṭ then having 29 days; Ādhār, 31 days; Nīsān, 30 days; Ayyār, 31 days; Ḥazīrān, 30 days; Tammūz, 31 days; Āb, 31 days. So the days of the non-Arab year are 365 and a quarter of a day in all, and in the leap year 366; this is the year in which Subāṭ has 29 full days, if God wills.\footnote{These are the Syrian names of the Julian months, from September to August. Other Arabic writers begin the year with Tishrīn I, October, as was the custom of the Syrians; al-Battānī begins it with 1 September, as the Byzantine indiction did (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{101}The names of the months of the Persians: Farwardīn-māh [its first day is the Nayrūz], Ardibihisht-māh, Khurdādh-māh, Tīr-māh, Murdādh-māh, Shahrīwar-māh, Mihr-māh, Ābān-māh — the twenty-sixth day of Ābān-māh [begins] the Farwardajān, which is ten days: five of them are the rest of Ābān-māh, up to the thirty that are due to it, and five are days that are dropped and not counted among the months; the sixteenth day of Mihr-māh is the Mihrajān — Ādhar-māh, Day-māh, Bahman-māh and Isfandārmadh-māh. Each of these months has 30 days, and five days after Ābān-māh are a cast-off addition; so the days of the Persian year are 365 in all, without a fraction, if God wills.\footnote{\emph{Māh} is Persian for «month»; \emph{Nayrūz}, «new day», is the Persian new year. The words in brackets stand after Ardibihisht-māh in the codex; Nallino moves them here. The five added days stood after Ābān-māh in al-Battānī's time; the Farwardajān is the festival of the last ten days of the year.}\par
\par The names of the months of the Copts: Tūt, Bābah, Atūr, Kiyahk, Ṭūbah, Amshīr, Barmahāt, Barmūdhah, Bashans, Baʾūnah, Abīb and Misrā. Each of these months has 30 days, and five days, which are cast after the months, are called the added days; so the days of the Coptic year are 365 and a quarter of a day in all, and in the fourth year 366 days. The era of the Romans and the Copts is, for the people of Egypt, from the death of Alexander the Macedonian, and for the Romans from the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn Alexander; between the two there are twelve Egyptian years.\footnote{The era of Dhū l-Qarnayn is the Seleucid era, which al-Battānī begins on Saturday 1 September 312 BC; the era from the death of Alexander is the era of Philip, which al-Battānī takes from Theon's tables (Nallino). The Coptic era of this chapter, as the rules below show, is the Alexandrian era of Augustus, whose first day al-Battānī puts on Friday 29 August 25 BC, 287 Julian years less 3 days after the era of Dhū l-Qarnayn (Nallino).}\par
\par If you wish to know, by the years of the Hijra, the beginning of any month of the years of the Arabs that you want, take the complete years of the Hijra and multiply them by 354 days and a fifth and a sixth of a day. Look at the result: if there is a fraction in it, and the fraction is less than half a day, drop it and do not count it; if it is more than half a day, count it as a day and add it to the sum of the days. The number of days that results is the number of days that have passed from the beginning of the Hijra to the end of that complete year; it is the root, so keep it. Then take this root, add 5 days to it, and cast out the sum seven by seven; what remains, less than seven or seven, is the sign of the coming year. Cast it from Sunday, and the reckoning brings you to the day on which al-Muḥarram begins in the year you are in, which is the incomplete year. If you want another of the months, add to the sign of the year, for the months of the complete year that have passed, two days for one month and one day for the next, which makes three days for every two full lunar months; if there is a single month, or a single month remains, take two days for it. Then cast that out seven by seven, and cast what remains, less than seven or seven, from Sunday; the reckoning stops at the day on which the month whose sign you sought begins. This is the reckoning on which the zījes and the chronologies are made; do not go past it to another, whether it gives more or less.\footnote{To cast a number from Sunday is to count it off from Sunday: 1 is Sunday, 2 Monday, and 7 Saturday. With no complete years the sign is 5, a Thursday: the astronomers' first day of the Hijra, 15 July 622; in civil use the era began on Friday (Nallino). Months of 30 and 29 days leave 2 and 1 when the weeks are cast out.}\par
\par \ArPage{102}If you wish to know the beginnings of the Roman months by the era of Dhū l-Qarnayn, with the beginning [of the year] of the Egyptians, take the complete years of Dhū l-Qarnayn and add to them a quarter of them. If there is a fraction in the result, do not count it, whether it is more than a half or less; then multiply the total by 365 days and cast out the result seven by seven. What remains, less than seven or seven, is the sign of the year; cast it in the first way, and you come to the first day of Aylūl of the coming year, the one you are in. If the fraction is exactly half, the year that is coming in on you, I mean the coming year, is a leap year; if it is more than half or less, it is not. If you want another month than Aylūl, add to the sign of the year, for the complete months of the year that have passed, two days for each month of 30 days and three days for each month of 31 days, and take nothing for Subāṭ unless the year is a leap year, when you take one day for it. Cast out the result seven by seven and proceed with it in the way of casting given before, and you come to the first day of the month that you want, if God wills.\footnote{Since 365 is 52 weeks and 1 day, the multiplication by 365 does not change what remains when the sevens are cast out. As stated, the rule leaves out the leap day in the year after a leap year: for 1 Aylūl 1192 of Dhū l-Qarnayn, 1 September 880, a Thursday, it gives Wednesday. Counting a fraction of three quarters as a whole day gives the right day in every year.}\par
\par If you wish to know the beginnings of the Persian months by their known years, take the complete years of Yazdajird son of Shahriyār son of Kisrā, king of the Persians, always add three to them, and multiply them by 365. Cast out the result seven by seven, and cast what remains, less than seven or seven, from Sunday; the day at which the count stops is the first day of Farwardīn-māh, which is the day of the Nayrūz. If you want another of the Persian months, add to the sign of the year, by which you found the day of the Nayrūz, two days for each of the complete months of the year that have passed, except Ābān-māh, for which you take nothing. Then cast that out seven by seven, proceed in the way of casting it from Sunday, and take the day at which the count stops as the beginning of the month that you sought.\footnote{The era of Yazdajird began on Tuesday 16 June 632 (Nallino). Ābān-māh with the five added days has 35 days, a whole number of weeks (Nallino).}\par
\par Know that the Copts precede the Greeks of the people of Egypt at the entry of Aylūl by three days, and that they [the Greeks] outrun them in the era by one day every four years. If you wish to know the beginnings of the months of the Copts, take the complete years of Dhū l-Qarnayn, always add six to them, and multiply it by the days of the year. Cast out the result seven by seven and carry out what remains, less than seven or seven, in the way given before; where the count brings you is the first day of Aylūl, which is Tūt, of the coming year. If you want another of the months, add to the sign of the year two days for each complete month of the year that has passed; cast out the result seven by seven and cast what remains, less than seven or seven, from Sunday; the day to which the count brings you is the beginning of the month that you want. If all the months have run out, cast five more days after that, and then the coming year begins, because those days are the added days, which are not counted among the months, if God wills.\footnote{In the year before a Roman leap year the Copts begin their year only two days before Aylūl: the Alexandrians put their extra day at the end of that year (Nallino, after a chronological fragment ascribed to Heraclius). The six makes the count begin on a Friday, the first day of the Coptic era as al-Battānī reckons it; the codex has 7, Plato of Tivoli 5 (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{103}If you wish to know the era of the Romans by the era of the Hijra — this is the conversion — and so to know the day of the Roman months on which you are and how many years [have passed] of Dhū l-Qarnayn, take the Arab root that I told you to keep and add to it 317. Add to the result the Arab months and days of the year that have passed, and divide the total by 365 days and a quarter of a day. What comes out is complete years; always add 932 years to them, and the total is the complete years of Dhū l-Qarnayn, so keep them. From the days that remain short of a year cast off, for each month, the number of its days, beginning with Aylūl: what comes out is complete months, and what does not make up a month is the days that have passed of the month at which you stopped, which is the current month, the one you are in. If a fraction is left over, do not count it; if the fraction is exactly a half, the year you are in, which is the coming year, not included in the number of years that you kept, is a leap year, so take 29 full days for Subāṭ in that year, if God wills.\footnote{The 317 days run from 1 Aylūl 933 of Dhū l-Qarnayn, 1 September 621, to the first day of the Hijra, 15 July 622; 932 years of the era were then complete (Nallino).}\par
\par If you wish to know the era of the Copts exactly from the era of the Romans, take the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn together with the year that you are in, even if only one day of it has begun, and take 287 from them. Take a quarter of what remains: if there is a fraction in it, do not count it; if there is no fraction, the year that you are in is a leap year. When there is no fraction, take one day from what the quarters give you, unless Subāṭ has run out; once Subāṭ has run out, add that day to the quarters. Always add to the result the three days by which the Copts precede the Greeks at the entry of Aylūl, which is Tūt, and add to the result [the days] from the first of Aylūl to the day that you are in. If that is more than 365, cast off 365 from it, and add a complete year to the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn that you have. If the year is a leap year and Subāṭ has run out, take 29 days [for it] and cast off 366 days from the days that you have collected. The days that remain after that are the days that have passed of the Copts in the year of the Copts that you are in; cast off 30 days for each month, beginning with Tūt. What comes out is complete months, and what remains below 30 is the days that have passed of the month of the Copts that you are in. With this era the motions of the stars are found by Theon's canon, after 12 years have been added to the years, so that they run from the death of Alexander the Macedonian; and do not count the first month set down in the tables among the months.\footnote{287 is the number of years between the era of Dhū l-Qarnayn and the Coptic era of this chapter (Nallino). Theon's tables give the motions at the beginnings, not the ends, of the months, days and hours, so that their first month adds nothing (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{104}If you wish to know the era of the Persians from the era of the Hijra by computation, take the Arab root that I told you to keep, and add to it, for the months of the year that have passed, 30 days for one month and 29 days for the next; add also the days that have passed of the Arab month that you are in. The result is the number of days that have passed from the beginning of the Hijra to the day that you want. Take from it 3624 days, the days between the Hijra and Yazdajird, and divide the remainder by 365: what comes out is complete years from the death of Yazdajird. From what remains below 365 take for each month the number of its days, beginning with Farwardīn-māh; the day at which you stop is the day that has passed of the current month of the Persians that you want. When you count Ābān-māh, reckon 35 days for it, for the five added days that go with it, I mean that are dropped after it and not counted. The day that follows the day on which the number of the days of the Persian year is complete is the day of the Nayrūz among the months of the Persians. Know this and understand it, and you will find what is right, if God wills.\footnote{The 3624 days run from the first day of the Hijra, 15 July 622, to the first day of the era of Yazdajird, 16 June 632. «The death of Yazdajird» is a slip for Yazdajird's accession, which is not rare in Arabic writers (Nallino).}\par
\par If you wish to know the era of the Hijra from the era of the Romans, with the beginning of the Egyptians, take 932 years from the complete years of Dhū l-Qarnayn and multiply the remainder by 365 days and a quarter of a day; if there is a fraction, keep it. Then take 317 days from the days that you have collected, and add to the remainder the days that have passed of the year that you are in, from the first of Aylūl to the day that you are in. The result is the number of days that have passed from the first year of the Hijra to the day that you want. Divide it by 354 days and a fifth and a sixth of a day: what comes out is complete years that have passed from the beginning of the Hijra. If what remains below that has a fraction, and it is less than a half, drop it and do not count it; if it is more than a half, make it up to a day and add it to the days. Then take the days out from al-Muḥarram, for each month the number of its days, a month of 30 days and a month of 29 days. What comes out is complete months that have passed of the coming year, I mean the current year of the years of the Hijra that you are in, which is not included in the number of years; and the days that remain below a month are what has passed of the month of the Arabs that you are in.\par
\par If you seek the era of the Hijra from the era of the Persians, take the complete years of Yazdajird and multiply them by 365 days; add to the result [the days] from the first of Farwardīn-māh to the day that you want, and add to the total 3624. The result is the days that have passed from the beginning of the Hijra; make them Arab years in the way given before.\par
\par \ArPage{105}If you wish to know what has passed of the era of the Persians from the era of the Romans by computation, take the complete years of Dhū l-Qarnayn and cast off 943 years from them; what remains is the years that you want, so keep them. Then take a quarter of them; if there is a fraction in it, do not count it. To the days of the quarters always add 77 days, and add to the result [the days] from the first of Aylūl to the day that you want. If the total is more than 365 days, cast off 365 days from it and add another year to the years that you kept; from the days that remain cast off for each month the number of its days, beginning with the first of Farwardīn-māh, in the way given before. If the fraction that comes from the quarters is three quarters, that year is a leap year, so take 29 days for Subāṭ in it. If you need to know on which day of the Roman months the Nayrūz of the coming year falls, take what you have collected from the quarters together with the 77 and always subtract it from 366; cast off what remains from Aylūl, for each month the number of its days. The day of that Roman month at which you stop is the day of the Nayrūz, which is the first day of the coming year of the years of the Persians that you are in. What follows the Nayrūz in the days and months of the Persians is as I have described.\footnote{943 years of Dhū l-Qarnayn were complete on 1 September 632, and the 77 days run from the beginning of the era of Yazdajird, 16 June 632, to that day (Nallino). The Persian year has no quarter day, so the days of the quarters are added.}\par
\par If you wish to know what has passed of the era of the Romans from the era of the Persians by computation, take the complete years of the Persians and multiply them by 365 days; add to that [the days] from the first of Farwardīn-māh to the day that you want, and divide the result by 365 days and a quarter of a day. What comes out is complete years; add to them 943 years, and the result is the complete years of Dhū l-Qarnayn. Cast off what remains of the days from Aylūl, for each month the number of its days, and do not count a fraction; if no fraction remains, the year is a leap year, so allow 29 days for Subāṭ in that year.\footnote{As stated, the rule counts the days of the Persian era from 1 Aylūl, although the era began on 16 Ḥazīrān, 16 June 632, 77 days earlier: the 77 days must first be taken from the days, as the converse rule above adds them.}\par
\par If you wish to know the era of the Romans by the era of the Copts, take the years of the Copts, which are the Egyptian years of Dhū l-Qarnayn, the complete ones, and cast off 287 from them. Find a quarter of what remains, and whatever it is, take it from the days that have passed of the coming year of the years of the Copts that you are in, from the first of Tūt to the day that you want. Cast off three days from what remains, and cast off the rest from the first of Aylūl: where you arrive is the day that has passed of the Roman month that you are in. If the days of the quarters are more than the days collected from the first of Tūt, take a year from the years of the Copts, add 365 days to the days that you have, and take from them the days that come from the quarters; take out what remains from Aylūl in the way given before. When there is a fraction in the quarters, do not count it.\par
\par If you add 12 years to the complete years of the Copts, they run from the death of Alexander the Macedonian; if you then add to that 424 Egyptian years, the total is the years of Ptolemy's book, with which Ptolemy worked in finding the motions, from the beginning of the reign of Nabonassar to the year at which you arrive of the years of the Copts and their days of the coming year. We have made tables for the eras of the Arabs and the Romans, by which they are found from one another, and tables by which the beginnings of their months are found, and we have explained the working with them at those tables, so that whatever is needed of this may be easily known at any time you wish, if God wills.\footnote{The Arabic calls Nabonassar «the first Bukhtanaṣṣar»; the era of Philip began 424 Egyptian years after the era of Nabonassar (Nallino).}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 33. On knowing the mean position of the sun and its true position, in which it is seen on the ecliptic, by the eras of the Romans and the Arabs}{33}{\ArPage{106}}{}
\par \ArPage{107}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the position of the sun on the ecliptic by the era of the Romans, take the complete years of Dhū l-Qarnayn, and do not count the broken year that you are in until the last day of Subāṭ has run out, at noon on it; then count it. Look for the same number of years as you have in the column of numbers of the tables of the collected Roman years, which go in steps of twenty years. Wherever you find the same number, or the nearest one below it, take what stands opposite it of the degrees, minutes and seconds set down in the column of the mean motion of the sun, and write them down. Then subtract the years that you found in the table from the years that you had: what remains is single years. Look for the same number in the column of numbers of the tables of the single Roman years, take again what stands opposite it in the column of the mean motion of the sun, of degrees, minutes and seconds, and write each kind under its kind, under what you wrote first. Then look in the table of the Roman months for the name of the complete Roman month before the month that you are in, take what stands opposite it in the column of the mean motion of the sun, and write those degrees, minutes and seconds under what you wrote before, each kind under its kind. Then enter with the number of the days that have passed of the Roman month that you are in into the table of the days of the column of the mean motion of the sun, take again what stands opposite it of degrees, minutes and seconds, and write them with what you wrote from the three places mentioned before. Then begin by collecting the seconds and add them up; take 60 from them, reckoning a minute for each time you take it, and carry that to the minutes, and write what remains below 60 under the seconds. Then add up the minutes, with what has been carried to them from the division of the seconds; take 60 from the sum, reckoning a degree for each time you take it, and carry that to the degrees, and write the minutes that remain below 60 under the minutes. Then add up the degrees, with what has been carried to them from the division of the minutes. If the sum is more than one circle, or several circles, the amount of a circle being 360 degrees, cast off the circles from it and write what remains below 360 under the degrees. The degrees, minutes and seconds that result are the mean motion of the sun collected from the four entries; it is the position of the sun by its mean course on the ecliptic, from the beginning of Aries. Cast off 30 from the degrees for each sign; what remains below that is the degrees, minutes and seconds that the sun has traversed of the sign that it has not completed, the one that does not make up 30.\footnote{The collected years (\emph{al-sinūn al-majmūʿa}) are tabulated in steps of twenty years, the Arab ones in steps of thirty; the single years (\emph{al-mabsūṭa}, «spread out») run from one upwards. The years of the tables begin on 1 Ādhār, March, so that the leap day is the last day of the year; the mean motions at the epochs are for noon on 1 Ādhār (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par If you wish to know the mean position of the sun by the years of the Arabs, take the years of the Hijra together with the year that you are in and enter them in the tables of the motion of the sun, in the column of the collected years of the Arabs, which go in steps of thirty; look for the same number or the nearest one below it, with the number of the single years, and follow the procedure given before, which I set out with the years of the Romans and their months. What is collected of the motion of the sun from the four entries, after the circles are cast off, is the mean position of the sun; it comes to the same whichever of the two eras you have worked with. Let this description be a model for you in finding the mean positions of the other stars and their arguments.\par
\par When you know the mean position of the sun, subtract its farthest distance from its mean position: its anomaly remains, so write it down under the mean position of the sun. Then enter with the anomaly of the sun into the tables of the equation of the sun, in the two columns of numbers, take the degrees, minutes and seconds that stand opposite it in the first column after the two columns of numbers, headed «equation of the sun», and write them under the anomaly. Then look: if the anomaly of the sun with which you took the equation is less than 180 degrees, subtract the equation from the mean position itself, and if this anomaly is more than 180, add the equation to the mean position. What results after the addition or the subtraction is the true position of the sun, in which it is seen on the ecliptic. Cast it from the beginning of Aries, giving each sign 30 degrees, and the count stops at the second of the minute of the degree of the sign in which it is.\par
\par This equation is for the time of noon at the city of al-Raqqa on the day for which you have computed. If there are minutes with the anomaly, take the equation that stands opposite the complete degree and keep it, and find the difference between it and the equation that stands opposite the degree one above it. Take of the result in the proportion of those minutes to 60, and subtract what you obtain from the equation that you kept if that one is the greater, or add it to it if it is the less. The equation opposite the complete degrees that you kept, after the addition or the subtraction, is the exact equation for that anomaly. Let this be a model for you to work with for all the minutes that you find with the anomaly of the moon and of the stars, if God wills.\footnote{Al-Battānī begins the day at noon, as the astronomers do (chapter 29), not at sunset (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{108}The farthest distance of the sun was, in the year 1191 of Dhū l-Qarnayn, on the first day of Ādhār, at about Gemini 22;15, which is 82;15 from the beginning of Aries. If you wish to compute the position of the sun for before this year or after it, find the difference between this year and the year for which you wish to compute, and take one degree for each 66 Roman years. Subtract the degrees and minutes that result from 82;15 if the time of the computation is before the year mentioned, and add them to it if it is after it; the distance of the sun that results after the addition or the subtraction is its farthest distance in the year for which you compute. For its farthest distance moves with the motion of the sphere of the fixed stars, which is, as we found by observation, one degree in each 66 Roman years; that is also about one degree in each 68 lunar years. If your computation is by the era of the Arabs, carry it out as I have described for you.\footnote{1 March 880 (Nallino). In chapter 28 al-Battānī found the farthest distance at Gemini 22;17 from observations of the year 1194. One degree in 66 years is 54 seconds 33 thirds a year (chapter 51; the modern value is about 50 seconds), and 66 Roman years of 365¼ days are 68 lunar years of 354 11/30 days.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 34. On knowing the hours of the computation in every locality, which are the equal hours, also called the mean hours, after noon at the city of al-Raqqa}{34}{\ArPage{108}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the hours of the computation in any locality you want — the hours by which the motions of the stars are found in this book, since we have set the computation of the stars in it for the time of noon, at the city of al-Raqqa, of the day for which you compute, which is the time of noon of the morrow — the seventh seasonal hour of the daytime becomes for this reason the first hour of the day that follows it; and so on to sunset, which is its sixth hour; and so on to the sixth hour of the night, which is its twelfth; and so on to sunrise of the morrow; and at noon of the morrow the day that follows also comes to an end.\footnote{Al-Battānī's day begins at noon, whereas the civil day of the Muslims begins at sunset (Nallino, after chapter 29).}\par
\par \ArPage{109}When you know the number of the hours from the time of noon to the given hour of the daytime or of the night, multiply all that are hours of the daytime by the times of the hours of the daytime, which are taken with the degree of the sun, from its degrees, in the given clime, and multiply what are hours of the night by the times of the hours of the night, which are taken with the counterpart of the degree of the sun. If the hours are equal hours, multiply them all by 15. From what results, whichever kind it is, subtract the degrees and minutes set down under the degree of the sun in the tables of the equation of the days with their nights, which is set down in the ascensions of the right sphere, in the sign in which the sun is. Divide the remainder by 15: the result is the equal, mean hours, converted from the irregular days into the mean days, after noon. If your computation is at the city of al-Raqqa, these are the hours of the computation. If it is at another city, take the amount between al-Raqqa and that city of the longitude set down in the tables of the longitudes of the cities, whatever it is, and divide it by 15: the hours and parts of an hour that result are the hours of the distance, so keep them. Then look: if the longitude of the city is greater than the longitude of al-Raqqa, which is 73;15, subtract the hours of the distance from those equal, mean hours after noon that have resulted at that city, for that city is then to the east of al-Raqqa; if the distance of the city is less, add the hours of the distance to those hours. What results after the addition or the subtraction is the equal hours after noon at al-Raqqa, which are the hours of the computation. Enter with them into the tables of the hours, take what stands opposite them of the motion of the sun, the moon and the stars, and add it to their mean positions found for the time of noon of the day for which you compute.\footnote{The times of a seasonal hour are the degrees of the equator that rise in it. The equation of the days with their nights is that of chapter 29. Plato of Tivoli has 75;15 for the longitude of al-Raqqa (Nallino). Theon's tables give similar rules for the mean equal hours of Alexandria (Nallino).}\par
\par If the given hours are before noon of the day for which you compute, take one day from the days that have passed of the month up to the day for which you compute; then take the hours from the time of noon of the day before to the given hour, and proceed with them as you proceeded at first.\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 35. On setting up the ascendant and the twelve houses from the hours, and on knowing the hours from the ascendant}{35}{\ArPage{109}}{}
\par \ArPage{110}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the ascendant and the rest of the twelve houses from the hours that have passed of the daytime or of the night — although this has been mentioned before in this book, briefly, in the chapter on knowing the hours from the altitude — look: if the time is daytime, take the hours from sunrise to the given hour, and if the time is night, take them from sunset. If they are equal hours, multiply them by 15, whichever of the two times they belong to, I mean the night or the daytime. If they are seasonal hours and are hours of the daytime, multiply them by the times of the hours of the daytime, and if they are hours of the night, multiply them by the times of the hours of the night. Add the times that result from the multiplication of the hours of the daytime to the times of the ascensions set down under the degree of the sun in the given clime, and add what results from the multiplication of the hours of the night to the times of the ascensions set down under the degree opposite the degree of the sun in the clime. If either of them comes to more than a circle, cast off a circle from it. With what results after that, or before it if it was less than a circle, find the ascendant and the middle of the sky in the way set out at the beginning of the book: carry the number that you have to the tables of the ascensions of the clime and take what stands opposite it of the degrees of the signs, by the common [interpolation] in that way; the result is the rising degree of the sign in which you found the number. Likewise enter with that same number into the ascensions of the right sphere and take what stands opposite it of the degrees of the signs: the result is the degree of the middle of the sky. When you know the ascendant, the setting point is its counterpart, and the degree of the fourth is the counterpart of the cardine of the middle of the sky.\footnote{The chapter on knowing the hours from the altitude is chapter 16; the ascensions and the hours of the clime are those of chapter 13 (Nallino). Al-Battānī counts the right ascensions from the beginning of Capricorn (chapter 5), so that the ascension of the ascendant in the clime, counted from Aries, is the right ascension of the middle of the sky (Nallino). The cardines are the four principal points of the ecliptic: the ascendant, the setting point, the middle of the sky and the fourth, or lowest, point.}\par
\par If you wish to know the ascendant from the hours taken from the time of noon, take their count from the time of noon to the given hour. If they are equal hours, multiply them by 15; if they are seasonal hours, multiply those of them that are hours of the daytime by the times of the hours of the daytime, and those that are hours of the night by the times of the hours of the night. To what results, whichever of the two times it is, add the ascension of the degree of the sun itself in the right sphere; with the result you find the ascendant and the middle of the sky in that way, if God wills.\par
\par \ArPage{111}If you wish to set up the rest of the twelve houses, take the times of the hours of the degree of the ascendant in that clime, double them, and add them to the ascensions by which you found the ascendant and the middle of the sky, which are the times of the ascensions of the degree of the ascendant in the clime. Look up the result in the ascensions of the right sphere and take what stands opposite it of the degrees of the signs: the result is the beginning of the eleventh house, in the sign in which the number falls. Then add the doubled times of the ascensions of the degree of the ascendant again to these times by which you found the beginning of the eleventh house, and take what stands opposite the result of the degrees of the signs in the ascensions of the right sphere: the result is the beginning of the twelfth house, in the sign in which the number falls. Then add those same doubled times again to the number by which you found the beginning of the twelfth house, and take what stands opposite it in the ascensions of the right sphere: it agrees with the degree of the ascendant exactly. Then subtract the doubled times from 60: the remainder is the rest of the doubled times, so keep it. Add it to the number by which you found the degree of the ascendant in the right sphere, which is the one that I told you agrees with the degree of the ascendant, and take what stands opposite the result of the degrees of the signs, again in the ascensions of the right sphere: the result is the beginning of the second house. Then add the rest of the doubled times again to the number by which you found the beginning of the second house, and take what stands opposite the sum of the degrees of the signs in the ascensions of the right sphere: the result is the beginning of the third house. Likewise, if you added the rest of the times to this number by which you found the beginning of the third house, it would agree with the degree of the fourth. When you know the beginnings of these houses, the beginning of the fifth is the counterpart of the beginning of the eleventh, the beginning of the sixth the counterpart of the beginning of the twelfth, the beginning of the eighth the counterpart of the beginning of the second house, and the beginning of the ninth the counterpart of the beginning of the third. The meaning of «counterpart» is the degree opposite it on a diameter of the sphere, which stands 180 degrees from it. When you have done this, you have computed the beginnings of the twelve houses in degrees of the signs.\footnote{The houses are divided as al-Qabīṣī (Alcabitius) and Ibn Ezra teach: the half arc of the day of the degree of the ascendant, which is six of its seasonal hours of the daytime, and its half arc of the night are each divided into three equal parts, and circles through the poles of the equator mark the beginnings of the houses (Nallino). The «doubled times» are the times of two seasonal hours of the daytime of the ascendant; what they fall short of 60 is the times of two seasonal hours of its night, since the hours of the day and of the night of a degree together make 30 times. Nallino would add, among the counterparts, «the beginning of the seventh is the counterpart of the ascendant».}\par
\par As for knowing the hours from the ascendant: look at the given ascendant. If it is between the degree of the sun and its counterpart, in the order of the signs, the time is daytime; if it is between the degree of the counterpart of the sun and the degree of the sun, the time is night. If the time is daytime, subtract the ascension of the degree of the sun from the ascension of the degree of the ascendant in the clime: the remainder is what has turned of the sphere from sunrise to the rising of that degree. If the time is night, subtract the times of the ascension of the counterpart of the degree of the sun from the times of the ascension of the degree of the ascendant: the remainder is what has turned of the sphere from the setting of the sun to the rising of that degree. If the time is daytime, divide what has turned of the sphere by the times of the hours of the daytime: what comes out is hours, and what remains is a fraction of an hour. If the time is night, divide what has turned of the sphere by the times of the hours of the night: the result is what has passed of the night, in seasonal hours. If you divide it by 15, what comes out is equal hours.\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 36. On knowing the true position of the moon on the ecliptic}{36}{\ArPage{111}}{}
\par \ArPage{112}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the true position of the moon, in which it is seen on the ecliptic, at any time you wish, find the mean position of the moon and its anomaly for the day that you want and the hour sought, in the way that I showed you, from the four entries and the hours of the computation, and find also the mean position of the sun for that time. Then subtract the mean position of the sun from the mean position of the moon and double the remainder: the result is the doubled distance; if it is more than a circle, cast off a circle from it. Enter with the remainder in the two columns of numbers of the tables of the equation of the moon, and take what stands opposite it in the third column, and also the minutes that stand opposite it in the fourth column; keep each of the two apart, under its own name. That is: what results from the third column is the equation of the anomaly, so write it down under the anomaly; what results from the fourth column is the proportional parts of the difference, so write them down apart. Then look: if the doubled distance with which you found the equation is less than 180 degrees, add what you obtained of the equation of the anomaly to the anomaly, and if it is more than 180 degrees, subtract it from the anomaly. What the anomaly of the moon comes to after the addition or the subtraction is the corrected anomaly. Look for the same number in the columns of numbers of the tables of the equation of the moon again, take what stands opposite it in the fifth column, headed «nearest distance», and write it down under the minutes of the fourth column. Then take also what stands opposite that corrected anomaly in the second column, headed «single equation»: what results is its single equation, so write it down apart. Then find what part of 60 the minutes of the fourth column are, and name it by its ratio, whether a half, a quarter or a third, or less or more than that, and take as much of what you wrote down from the fifth column; always add the result to the single equation that you wrote down, and the result is the compound equation. Add this compound equation to the mean position of the moon if its corrected anomaly exceeds 180 degrees, even by a minute, and subtract it from it if it is less than 180 degrees. What the mean position of the moon comes to after the addition to it or the subtraction from it is the true position of the moon, in which it is seen on the ecliptic. Cast it from the beginning of Aries in the way given before, and you come to the degree and the minute of the sign in which it is, if God wills.\footnote{The rules are Ptolemy's (\emph{Almagest} V 8). The translation follows Nallino's emendanda (Part II, p. XXXI) in reading «\RL{\ArNote حِدَته}», «apart», at p. 112, line 4. The four entries are those of chapter 33, the hours of the computation those of chapter 34. The columns are counted without the columns of numbers (Nallino). The third column holds the arc between the true and the mean farthest distance of the epicycle, the second the single equation, the fifth the increase of the equation at the nearest distance of the eccentric, and the fourth the sixtieths of that increase that apply at a given doubled distance (chapter 30).}\par
\par Then look: if the corrected anomaly of the moon is less than 180, the moon is increasing in its course, and if it is more, it is decreasing in it. When it is from 0 to 95, its course is less than the mean course; when it is from 95 to 180, its course is more than the mean course; likewise from 180 to 265 its course is more than its mean course, and from 265 to the end of the circle less than the mean course. So too with the sun, in the same way.\footnote{The true motion equals the mean motion where the moon is on a line from the earth that touches the epicycle, at the anomaly of 90 degrees and the greatest equation, here 5 degrees: hence 95 and 265. For the sun, whose greatest equation is about 2 degrees, the numbers are 92 and 268; and at the quadratures, where the greatest equation of the moon reaches 7;40, they are 97;40 and 262;20 (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 37. On knowing the position of the northern node, which is called the head of the jawzahar}{37}{\ArPage{113}}{\footnote{\emph{Jawzahar}, from the Persian \emph{gawzahr}, means the orbit of the moon, its nodes, and above all its ascending node (Nallino).}}
\par Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the position of the northern node, which is called the head, find its mean position for the time sought in the way given for finding the mean positions, and always subtract the result from 360 degrees: the remainder is the position of the northern node on the ecliptic. Cast it from the beginning of Aries in the usual way. As for the position of the southern node, which is called the tail, it lies on the diameter of its sphere: it is the degree opposite the degree of the head, degree for degree, and does not depart from it.\footnote{The nodes move backwards, against the order of the signs, so the motion taken from the tables is subtracted from 360 (Nallino).}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 38. On knowing the latitude of the moon from the belt of the signs, and its directions}{38}{\ArPage{113}}{}
\par \ArPage{114}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the latitude of the moon, which is its distance from the belt of the signs, subtract the computed position of the head from the computed true position of the moon: the remainder is the argument of the latitude. Or, if you wish, add to the true position of the moon the mean position of the head; if the result is more than a circle, cast off a circle from it, and what results, after that or before it, is the argument of the latitude; the meaning is the same in the two ways. When you know the argument of the latitude, whichever of the two ways it was found, enter with it in the two columns of numbers of the tables of the equation of the moon and take what stands opposite it in the sixth column, headed «latitude of the moon»: the result is its latitude at that time. If you wish to know it by computation, take the sine of the argument of the latitude, multiply it by 5 parts 13 minutes, which are the sine of the whole latitude, and divide the product by the radius; find the arc of the quotient, and the arc that results is the latitude of the moon. When you know the latitude of the moon, whichever of the two ways it was found, look: if the argument of the latitude is from 0 to 180, the latitude is on the northern side of the ecliptic, and if it is from 180 to 360, the latitude is on the southern side.\footnote{The mean position of the head is the motion taken from the tables, before it is subtracted from 360 (chapter 37). The columns are counted without the columns of numbers (Nallino). 5;13 parts are the sine of 5 degrees, the greatest latitude of the moon (5;13,46 in al-Battānī's table of sines; Nallino); Plato of Tivoli has 5;30. In modern terms the rule is sin β = sin 5° · sin u, with u the argument of the latitude.}\par
\par If you wish to know whether it is ascending or descending on its side, look: if the argument of the latitude is from 0 to 90, the moon is increasing in latitude and ascending in the north; if it is from 90 to 180, it is decreasing in latitude and descending from the north; from 180 to 270 it is increasing in latitude and descending in the south; and from 270 to 360 it is decreasing in latitude and ascending from the south. In short, when the moon leaves the head it is northern until it reaches the tail, and when it passes the tail it is southern until it reaches the head, because from the node of the head its passage is towards the north and from the node of the tail its passage is towards the south, if God wills.\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 39. On knowing the parallax that occurs in the moon in longitude and latitude, and the cause from which it occurs, and on knowing it by computation and by the table}{39}{\ArPage{114}}{}
\par \ArPage{115}Al-Battānī said: The parallax of the moon is the amount by which the position in which it is seen by measurement differs from the position in which it really is, which is given by computation. For the size of the earth relative to the sphere of the moon is greater than relative to the spheres of the other stars, because the moon is near it, and [it grows less] until the ecliptic is reached, relative to which the size of the earth is like a point. Since the centre of the earth is the centre of the ecliptic, which is the place of true sight, the sight differs [only] by the difference between the centre of the earth and its surface, which is the place from which the eyes see, and the amount of that is the radius of the earth. For this reason the parallax is greater in the moon than in the others and more evident to the senses. It occurs, and varies as it occurs, from two different causes: one is the variation of the distance of the moon from the earth, and the second the variation of its distance from the point of the zenith in the regions of the sphere, on the circle of altitude, which passes through the pole of the horizon, that is, the zenith, and through the moon and the horizon. When the place where this circle cuts [the ecliptic] is half way between the setting degree of the ecliptic and [the ascendant], at a distance of 90 from the ascendant, which is half the semicircle, half of the 180, and the amount of a right angle of the four angles of the sphere, the parallax then falls in latitude alone, not in longitude. This cannot happen on the meridian line except at two points of the ecliptic, the two solstitial points, I mean the beginning of Cancer and the beginning of Capricorn. For when either of them is on the meridian line, one of the two equinoctial points, I mean the beginning of Aries and the beginning of Libra, is on the eastern horizon and the other on the western horizon, everywhere on the earth, and so each of the four angles is a right angle; the amount of a right angle is 90 parts.\footnote{The text has «where the point of this circle» and, before it, «until it passes» for «which passes»; the translation follows the readings that Nallino proposes.}\par
\par As for the other parts of the sphere, when they are on the meridian line their distances from the horizon differ, being more or less than 90, and the angles differ, growing smaller or larger. When one of the parts of the ecliptic between the beginning of Cancer and the end of Sagittarius is on the meridian line, the place half way between the rising and the setting degree of the ecliptic appears in all the climes inclined from the meridian line towards the west; when one of them there is between the beginning of Capricorn and the end of Gemini, the place half way between the ascendant and the setting point inclines towards the east. In these two inclinations the parallax on the meridian line falls in longitude and latitude together. So too in the other regions of the sphere, when the distance of the degree in question from the ascendant is more or less than 90, since the angle is then less than a right angle.\footnote{The translation reads «appears» (\RL{\ArNote يظهر}) with Nallino for «rises» (\RL{\ArNote يطلع}); «all the climes» means our climes, north of the tropic (Nallino).}\par
\par The ratio of the parallax in latitude to the parallax in longitude is as the ratio of the sine of the angle to the sine of what remains of it to a right angle, and the squares of the two parallaxes added together are equal to the square of the parallax between the point of the zenith and the moon on the circle of altitude. The inclination of the parallax in latitude is always towards the side of the degree that this circle cuts, when it is the circle of the middle of the sky, from the point of the zenith; and the inclination of the parallax in longitude is towards the side of the horizon to which the degree of the moon inclines, I mean that the moon is inclined, being nearer, towards that horizon, the eastern or the western, rather than towards the other.\footnote{With A the angle between the ecliptic and the circle of altitude through the moon and p the parallax in altitude, the parallax in latitude is p sin A and that in longitude p cos A (Nallino, after \emph{Almagest} V 19). The parallax in latitude falls on the side to which the arc from the zenith through the moon points when it is continued beyond the ecliptic; the «middle of the sky» here is the zenith (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{116}What compels us most of all to the knowledge of this anomaly is the cause of the solar eclipses, for they cannot be grasped without grasping the amount of this anomaly at all places of the horizon. In the lunar eclipses we have no need of it, since the moon is not the cause of its own eclipse, as it is the cause of the eclipse of the sun: the cause of its eclipse is something else. This anomaly is also perceptible in Venus and Mercury, but more in Mercury, because it is nearer the moon, above all when Mercury is at its nearest distance, for its parallax is then like the parallax of the moon at its farthest distance. In the sun it is not perceptible as it is in the other three mentioned; it is, as Ptolemy set it down, in the ratio of one to the 1210 that Ptolemy made the distance of the sun from the centre of the earth, without distinction. We find the visible position of the sun agreeing with its true position, because the parallax of the sun entered into the computation of the sun at the time of the observation, since the limit of the ecliptic and its distance from the equator were known only by observation of the sun.\footnote{«Compels»: so Nallino's translation; the printed word, «\RL{\ArNote يقطم}», is obscure. Ptolemy (\emph{Almagest} IX 1) holds the parallaxes of the five stars imperceptible. Al-Battānī, like al-Farghānī, follows those who make the spheres touch, so that the nearest distance of Mercury is the farthest distance of the moon (chapter 50); hence their parallaxes there are equal (Nallino).}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{The ratio of 1 to 1210 makes the greatest parallax of the sun 2′ 50½″, about 3 minutes, the value of Ptolemy, Theon and the Arabs (Nallino).}\par
\par It appeared before that the point of the farthest distance of the moon's eccentric sphere is 60 parts from the centre of the earth. If the radius of the earth is one part, the distance of the moon from the surface of the earth is then 59 parts, and in this measure the 5¼ parts that are the radius of the epicycle are about 5⅙ parts and the whole diameter of the epicycle 10⅓ parts. So when the centre of the epicycle is at the point of the farthest distance of the eccentric sphere, which happens at the times of the mean conjunctions and oppositions, and the moon is at the point of the farthest distance of the epicycle, its farthest distance from the earth is 64;10, and this is the first limit; when it is at the bottom of the epicycle, its distance from the earth is 53;50, and this is the second limit. When the centre of the epicycle is at the point of the nearest distance, whose distance from the centre of the earth was shown to be 39;22, which is 38;43 in that measure — this happens only at the two quarters of the month on either side of the full moon — then, if the moon is at the top of its epicycle, its distance from the earth is 43;53, and this is the third limit; and if it is at its nearest point on the epicycle, its distance from the earth is 33;33, and this is the fourth limit. Between these four limits there are limits of differing distances.\footnote{These are Ptolemy's four limits (\emph{Almagest} V 13 and 17).}\par
\par \ArPage{117}If you wish to know the distance of the moon from the earth, take the corrected anomaly of the moon: if it is less than 180, work with it, and if it is more than 180, subtract it from 360 and work with the remainder. The way to work with it is this. Look: if the number with which I told you to work is less than 90, take its sine and the sine of what remains of it to complete 90; multiply each of the two sines by 5;15, which is the radius of the epicycle, divide what results from each of them by the radius, and keep the results. Add what results from the sine of the complement of the number to 60, multiply the sum by itself, add to it what results from the sine of the number multiplied by itself, and take the root of the total. If the number with which I told you to work is more than 90, cast off 90 from it; find the sine of the remainder and the sine of what remains to complete the number to 90, multiply each of the two sines by 5;15 and divide by the radius. Subtract what results for the sine of the number from 60, multiply the remainder by itself, add to the product what results for the sine of the complement of the number multiplied by itself, and take the root of the total. What results from either of the two roots is the hypotenuse of the moon, I mean its distance from the centre of the earth at the times of the mean conjunction and opposition. Subtract a minute from each degree of this distance: the remainder is the distance of the moon from the earth.\footnote{«Hypotenuse» renders \emph{quṭr}, literally «diameter»: the line from the centre of the earth to the moon. In modern terms the distance is √((60 + r cos a)² + (r sin a)²), with r = 5;15 and a the anomaly. Taking a minute from each degree reduces the distance to the measure of the limits above, in which the 60 parts of the eccentric are 59; Ptolemy has no such rules (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{118}If the moon is between the conjunction and the opposition, on either side, take what results from multiplying the minutes of the fourth column of the tables of the equation of the moon into the fifth column of them, which is what I told you to add to the single equation of the moon at the time of the computation, and always add it to the 5 parts 1 minute, which are the whole single equation. Find the sine of the sum: the result is the radius of the displaced epicycle. Use it in place of the 5¼ parts in that same way: the result is the hypotenuse of the moon corrected for the displacement of the epicycle, so keep it. Then take the doubled distance between the sun and the moon by their mean course: if it is from 0 to 180, work with it, and if it is from 180 to 360, subtract it from 360 and work with the remainder. The way to work with it is this. Look: if the number with which you work is less than 90, subtract it from 90, and if it is more than 90, cast off 90 from it; find the sine of whichever of these you have: that is the first sine, so keep it under this name. Then subtract the number whose sine you found from 90 and find the sine of the remainder: that is the second sine. Then take the radius of the eccentric sphere, which has been shown to be 49;41, and multiply it by itself: the product is 2468 degrees 26 minutes. [Then multiply] the second sine by 10;19, which is the amount between the centres, divide the product by the radius, multiply the quotient by itself, and subtract it from the 2468 degrees 26 minutes; take the root of the remainder: that is the corrected side, so keep it. Then multiply the first sine by 10 parts 19 minutes as well, divide the product by the radius and keep the result. If the number with which you worked is less than 90, add what you kept to the corrected side, and if it is more, subtract what you kept from the corrected side. Always subtract from 60 what the corrected side comes to after the addition or the subtraction: the remainder is the share of twice the distance between the centres. Subtract it from the hypotenuse of the moon corrected for the displacement of the epicycle, take a minute for each degree of the remainder, and what remains is the distance of the moon from the earth.\footnote{Words are missing after «the radius of the displaced epicycle» (Nallino). Nallino's restoration: «[divide 5;15 by it and multiply the result by 60: the result is the distance of the centre of the epicycle from the earth;] use it in place of [the farthest distance of that centre at the syzygies]», so that the hypotenuse is found as in the paragraph before. Schiaparelli reads what follows as a second way, without the tables: the hypotenuse found with the epicycle at the farthest distance of the eccentric, less the share of twice the distance between the centres, which is nothing at the farthest distance and twice that distance at the nearest.}\textsuperscript{,}\footnote{The words in brackets are supplied by Nallino; only their last letter can be read in the codex. The corrected side is √(49;41² − (10;19 · sin 2η)²), with 2η the doubled distance; together with 10;19 · cos 2η it gives the distance of the centre of the epicycle from the earth (Schiaparelli, after \emph{Almagest} V 13).}\par
\par By that first procedure in this chapter the distance of the sun from the earth is known, if you use the 2 parts 4 minutes and a half and a quarter that are between the centres for the sun in place of the 5¼ parts that are the radius of the epicycle for the moon. Multiply the distance of the sun from the earth that results by 18;46,24: the result is the distance of the sun from the earth according to its position, known by the computation given before in the pine-shaped figure.\footnote{The pine-shaped figure is that of chapter 30. The factor turns parts of which the mean distance of the sun is 60 into radii of the earth. Nallino reads 18;46,24 after Plato of Tivoli's 18;46,50; the codex has 38;46,24. With the mean distance of 1108 radii of the earth the factor would be 18;28; 18;47 belongs to 1127, half way between the mean and the farthest distance (Schiaparelli).}\par
\par If you wish to know the parallax of the moon on the circle of altitude, take the altitude of the moon at the time that you want and find what the altitude falls short of 90, which is the distance of the moon from the point of the zenith. Find the sine of each of them and multiply it by one minute, so that each degree of the sine becomes a minute and returns to the one part that is the amount of the radius of the earth. Subtract what results of minutes of the sine of the altitude from the distance of the moon from the earth: the remainder is the corrected distance, so keep it. Then multiply the minutes of the sine of the distance of the moon from the point of the zenith by 60 and divide the product by the corrected distance that you kept: the result is minutes; find their arc, and the arc that results is the parallax of the moon on the circle of altitude that passes through the zenith and the moon. Ptolemy set down the amount of this parallax in the tables for the four limits mentioned for the moon, and for the sun for one distance.\footnote{The altitude here is that seen from the centre of the earth (Schiaparelli). In modern terms the rule is sin p = sin z / (D − cos z), with z the distance from the zenith and D the distance of the moon in radii of the earth; the exact rule has tan p for sin p. Ptolemy's tables are those of \emph{Almagest} V 18.}\par
\par \ArPage{119}If you wish to know the parallax of the moon on the circle of altitude by those tables, which we have set down in this book of ours in that way, so as to know by it the position of the moon in which it is seen on the ecliptic in longitude and latitude, from the arcs and angles that arise from the intersection of the ecliptic and the circle of altitude, find the degree that falls in the middle of the sky and the degree that falls on the eastern horizon, I mean the rising degree of the ecliptic, at the time when the moon happens to be above the earth in the given clime. Then find the amount, of the degrees of the signs, between the rising degree and the degree that falls in the middle of the sky, and also between the degree in question, in which the moon is, and the rising degree. Then find the altitude of the degree that falls in the middle of the sky, as I shall describe, although this was given at the beginning of the book. Look at the declination of the degree in the middle of the sky: if it is northern, subtract it from the latitude of the clime, and if it is southern, add it to it; subtract what the latitude of the clime comes to after the addition or the subtraction from 90: the remainder is the altitude of the degree of the middle of the sky. If the declination of the degree of the middle of the sky is northern and you wish to subtract it from the latitude of the locality, but find the latitude of the locality less than that declination, take the difference between them and subtract it from 90: the remainder is the altitude of the degree of the middle of the sky above the northern horizon, and the computation is then reversed. When you know what I have described to you, multiply the sine of the distance of the degree in question from the ascendant by the radius, divide the product by the sine of the [arc] between the ascendant and the degree of the middle of the sky, multiply the quotient by the sine of the altitude of the degree of the middle of the sky, divide the product by the radius, and find the arc of the quotient: the arc that results is the altitude of the degree in question at that time, on whichever side of the horizon it is, I mean in the east or in the west. Keep it, and keep the sine by which you found it. Then subtract the altitude of the degree in question from 90: the remainder is the distance of the degree in question from the point of the zenith. Keep it: with it you know the amount of the parallax on the circle of altitude from these tables.\footnote{The altitude of the degree of the middle of the sky is found as in chapter 15. In modern terms the rule is sin h = sin d · sin h\_M / sin d\_M, with d and d\_M the distances of the degree in question and of the degree of the middle of the sky from the ascendant, and h\_M the altitude of the latter.}\par
\par Then look: if the distance of the degree in question from the ascendant is 90 degrees, the angle is a right angle, the parallax then falls in latitude only, not in longitude, and the parallax that results on the circle of altitude is the parallax in latitude. If the distance of the degree in question from the ascendant is less than 90, subtract it from 90 and work with the remainder; if it is more than 90, subtract 90 from it and work with the remainder. The way to work with it is to take the excess over 90 or the shortfall from it, find its sine and multiply it by the sine of the altitude of the degree in question at that hour, which is the sine that I told you to keep under its name; divide the product by the sine of the distance of the degree in question from the ascendant, multiply the quotient by the radius, divide the product by the sine of the distance of the degree in question from the point of the zenith, and find the arc of the quotient: the arc that results is the amount of the angle of longitude, out of the whole of one right angle. Subtract it from the 90 parts that are the amount of a right angle: the remainder is the amount of the angle of latitude. Keep all this, each on its side and under its name; unless the altitude is from the northern horizon, when the matter is reversed, and the arc that results for you from the table becomes the angle of latitude and its complement to 90 the angle of longitude. This cannot happen in the other localities, whose latitude is greater than the declination together with the latitude that the moon may have when it is northern.\footnote{The angle of latitude is the angle between the ecliptic and the circle of altitude through the moon, and the angle of longitude its complement. In modern terms the rule is cos(angle of latitude) = tan h · |cot d|. Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary, holds the words on the northern horizon to be a reader's interpolation, and false.}\par
\par \ArPage{120}Then enter with the distance of the degree in question, in which the moon is, from the point of the zenith, which I told you to keep, into the tables of the parallax of the moon on the circle of altitude, in the column of numbers that goes in steps of two parts, and take what stands opposite it in the four columns that follow the column of the sun headed «parallax of the sun», namely the third, fourth, fifth and sixth columns; write down what results from each of them on its side. Then take the corrected anomaly of the moon: if it is less than 180, take half of it, and if it is more than 180, subtract it from 360 and take half of the remainder. Enter with whichever of these two halves you have into the two columns of numbers of these tables and take the minutes that stand opposite it in the seventh and the eighth column, headed «epicycle». Multiply what results from the seventh column by the minutes that you took from the fourth column, and what results from the eighth column by the minutes that you wrote down from the sixth column, and divide what results from each of them by 60. Add the minutes that result from the seventh column to what you wrote down from the third column, and the minutes that result from the eighth column to what you wrote down from the fifth column. If you wish to work in another way, see what part of 60 the minutes of the seventh column are, take that part of what you wrote down from the fourth column, and add it to what you wrote down from the third column; then see what part of 60 the minutes that resulted from the eighth column are, take that part of what you wrote down from the sixth column, and add it to what you wrote down from the fifth column. The meaning is the same: whichever of these two you work with, you will be right. What the third column and the fifth column each come to after what you add to it is the amount of the parallax of the moon on the circle of altitude at the two limits of the farthest and the nearest distance of the epicycle; keep them, and find the excess between them. That is the parallax of the moon and the sun together.\par
\par \ArPage{121}Then take the distance of the moon from the sun by their mean motions, either from the mean degree of the sun or from the degree opposite it, whichever is nearer to it, ahead or behind, so that the greatest distance is 90 parts. Enter with the result into the column of numbers of those tables again, and take the minutes that stand opposite it in the ninth column, headed «eccentric». Find what part of 60 the minutes are, and take that part of the excess between the corrected third and fifth columns that I told you to keep; always add the minutes that you obtain from that excess to the corrected third column that you kept. The result is the parallax of the sun and the moon together on the circle of altitude, according to the position of the moon and its distance from the earth; keep it. Then take what stands opposite the arc of the distance of the degree in question from the point of the zenith in the parallax of the sun, set down in the second column; always add to the minutes and seconds that result an eighth of them, because of the change that has come about in the distance of the sun from the earth, and keep the result. Then enter with the anomaly of the sun into the table of the computation and take what stands opposite it in the third column, of the minutes of the shares; see what part of 60 it is, and take that part of the 13 seconds by which the parallax of the sun varies between its farthest and its nearest distance. Add the result to what you kept: what the parallax of the sun comes to after these two operations is its parallax on the circle of altitude according to its position in distance from the earth. Subtract it from the parallax of the sun and the moon on the circle of altitude that you kept at the end of the work [before]: the remainder is the parallax of the moon on the circle of altitude, which appears in the moon at the true position of the sun; keep it, and work with it. Then take the angle of longitude, find its sine, multiply it by this parallax of the moon on the circle of altitude that I have mentioned, and divide the product by 60: the result is the parallax of the degree of the moon in longitude; keep it. Then take the sine of the angle of latitude, multiply it by the parallax of the moon on the circle of altitude as well, and divide the product by 60: the result is the parallax of the moon in latitude.\footnote{«An eighth»: Plato of Tivoli has «an eighteenth», which is right. Al-Battānī's farthest distance of the sun, 1146 radii of the earth, is 64 less than Ptolemy's 1210, so the parallaxes of the sun taken from Ptolemy must be increased by about an eighteenth; with al-Battānī's distances of 1146 and 1070 radii the parallax of the sun at the horizon is 3′ 0″ and 3′ 13″, which differ by the 13 seconds of the text (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{122}If you wish to know it in another way, see what part of the 60 that are the radius the sine of the angle of longitude and the sine of the angle of latitude each are, and take that part of the parallax of the moon on the circle of altitude: what results for the angle of longitude is the parallax in longitude, and what results for the angle of latitude is the parallax in latitude. Whichever of the two ways you work with, the meaning is the same in the two amounts. When you know this, add the parallax in longitude to the true position of the moon on the ecliptic if the distance of the degree in which the moon is from the ascendant is less than 90, because the moon is then nearer to the eastern horizon; and if the distance of the degree in which the moon is from the ascendant is more than 90, subtract the parallax in longitude from the true position of the moon, because the moon is then nearer to the western horizon. The position of the moon after the addition or the subtraction is the position of the moon in which it is seen on the ecliptic in the course of longitude. As for the parallax of the moon in latitude, look: if the position of the moon is to the south of the point of the zenith when the degree of the moon comes to the middle of the sky, the parallax is then towards the south; and if the position of the moon on the circle of the middle of the sky is to the north of the point of the zenith, the parallax in latitude is then towards the north. It is always southern in the localities whose latitude is greater than the declination of the sun together with the northern latitude that the moon may have, approximately. If the true latitude of the moon and the parallax of the moon in latitude are on one side, add them together; if they differ, subtract the smaller from the greater and find the side of the remainder. The result after the addition or the subtraction is the latitude of the moon in which it is seen by measurement.\par
\par If the degree of the moon in question is on one of the two horizons, it is known that its distance from the point of the zenith on the circle of altitude is then 90 parts. If you wish to know its angle on the eastern horizon, find the declination of the degree that then falls in the middle of the sky: if its declination is northern, subtract it from the latitude of the clime, and if it is southern, add it to it; what the latitude of the clime comes to after the addition or the subtraction is its corrected latitude, so keep it. Subtract it from 90, find the sine of the remainder, multiply it by the radius, and divide the product by the sine of the [arc] between the degree of the middle of the sky and the degree of the ascendant, which is then the degree in question, in which the moon is, if the degree in question is on the eastern horizon. Find the arc of the result: the arc that results is the amount of the angle of longitude; subtract it from 90, and the remainder is the amount of the angle of latitude. Each of them is [the angle] of the degree in question on the eastern horizon, by which I mean the ascendant. If the latitude of the clime is less than the declination of the degree of the middle of the sky, when the declination is northern, take the difference between them, find its sine, multiply it by the radius, divide the result by the sine of the [arc] between the ascendant and the middle of the sky, and find the arc of the result: the arc that results is the amount of the angle of latitude. We have explained this before, in the knowledge of the angles, when the declination is more than the latitude of the clime. If the degree in question is on the western horizon, find the angle of the degree opposite it, which is then the rising degree, in the way given by which the angle of a degree on the eastern horizon is known: the result is the angle of that degree on the western horizon.\footnote{The rule for a declination greater than the latitude is not exact: it agrees with the rule before only when the ascendant is 90 degrees from the degree of the middle of the sky. Where it applies, at latitudes less than the greatest declination, it errs by less than a degree; Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary, calls it false. The rule before serves in this case too, with the altitude of the degree of the middle of the sky counted from the northern horizon.}\par
\par \ArPage{123}If the degree in question is on the meridian line, its distance from the point of the zenith is then the amount by which the altitude of the degree in question in the middle of the sky falls short of 90, and its angle is found by the procedure that we have set out [in the first of these chapters]; its amount is the same everywhere on the earth. If you wish to know it in another way, take the distance of the degree in question from the beginning of Aries or the beginning of Libra, whichever is nearer, before it or behind it, so that it does not exceed 90. Find the sine of this distance and the sine of what remains to complete this distance to 90; then take the declination of the degree in question and find its sine and the sine of what remains to complete the declination of the degree in question to 90. Multiply the sine of the declination of the degree by the sine of the complement of the distance, divide the product by the sine of the complement of the declination of the degree, multiply the quotient by the radius, divide the product by the sine of the distance of the degree, and find the arc of the result: the arc that results is the amount of the angle of longitude. Subtract it from 90: the remainder is the amount of the angle of latitude in the middle of the sky, which is also its angle at the horizon at the place of the line of equality. These angles mentioned are the amount of the azimuth of the degree in question on the circle of the horizon, if you take it from the azimuth of the rising or the setting degree towards the middle of the sky, according to the position of the degree in question; for the arc of the circle of the horizon between the azimuth of the rising of the ascendant and the azimuth of the degree in question is equal to the amount of the angle of latitude. Since the parallax that we have described is known truly in these ways only when the moon is on the belt of the signs, while, when it departs from the girdle of the ecliptic in latitude, the angles and arcs vary and change, what falls in the parallax on this account is mostly about 6 minutes; at the solar eclipses the most that can fall on this account is a minute and a half, at the extreme, and in places far from the equator.\footnote{The words in brackets stand so in the codex for «at the beginning of this chapter» or «earlier in this chapter» (Nallino); Plato of Tivoli has «at the beginning of this chapter». In modern terms the second rule is cos(angle of latitude) = tan δ · cot λ, with δ the declination of the degree and λ its distance from the equinoctial point. Schiaparelli finds the sentence on the azimuth corrupt or interpolated: the arc of the horizon between the ascendant and the azimuth of the degree is not the angle of latitude.}\par
\par \ArPage{124}If you wish to make this exact, so that no change falls on its account, take the distance from the point of the zenith of the degree in which the moon is, and the angle of latitude and the angle of longitude that result for that degree. Then find the true latitude of the moon, take its sine, and multiply it by the sine of the angle of latitude and by the sine of the angle of longitude; then divide each of the two by the radius. Find the arc of what results for the angle of latitude, and subtract the arc from the distance of the degree in which the moon is from the point of the zenith if the moon is on the side of the zenith from the ecliptic, and add it to it if the ecliptic is nearer to the zenith than the moon. Find the sine of what the arc of the distance of the degree in which the moon is from the point of the zenith comes to after the addition or the subtraction, multiply it by itself, add to the product what resulted for the angle of longitude by the division, multiplied by itself, take the root of the result and find its arc: the arc that results is the corrected arc of the distance of the moon from the point of the zenith; use it in place of the first arc of the distance of the degree of the moon from the point of the zenith. Then take also what resulted for the angle of longitude from that division and find its arc: the result is the difference of the angle. If the corrected arc is less than the first arc, subtract it from the angle of latitude and add it to the angle of longitude; if the corrected arc is more than the first, add it to the angle of latitude and subtract it from the angle [of longitude].
