What results for each of the two after that is the corrected angle; use it in place of the two first angles.\footnote{The words in brackets are supplied by Nallino. The corrected distance from the zenith is found as the hypotenuse of a small right triangle: Ptolemy (\emph{Almagest} V 19) adds the squares of the arcs, al-Battānī those of their sines; the exact rule is cos z = cos a · cos b, and both ways change the parallax by no more than 3 or 4 seconds (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary). For the difference of the angle, the result must also be multiplied by the radius and divided by the sine of the corrected distance from the zenith, words that Nallino suggests have fallen out.}\par
\par If you wish to know the amounts of the parallax of the moon by the tables that Theon, the astronomer of Alexandria, made, which we have set down in this book as Theon made them. Theon set down the parallax of the moon in longitude and latitude for seven climes, at intervals of half an hour in the longest day, on the assumption that the moon is at the beginnings of the signs, after taking the parallax of the sun from the parallax of the moon in the way set down in Ptolemy's book, according to the inclination used there; and made it known by the equal hours of the distance of the degree of the moon from the circle of noon. So the finding of the parallax by these tables varies near noon, which is the meridian line, by day and by night. These amounts are not like those that result from working with the angles and arcs, for various reasons that interfere in them, although they are easier to obtain than those.\footnote{Theon's tables are computed with Ptolemy's inclination of 23;51, 16 minutes more than al-Battānī's; the greatest effect of the difference, at al-Raqqa, is about 16 seconds (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{125}The way to work with these tables is as I describe. Find the distance of the degree in which the moon is from the meridian line, by night or by day, so as to know how many equal hours the degree of the moon is from noon or from midnight towards the east or the west, on whichever side the moon is. This is known as I describe. Take the times of the ascensions of the right sphere that stand opposite the degree of the middle of the sky, and also the times of the ascensions of the right sphere that stand opposite the degree in which the moon is. Subtract the times of the ascensions of the degree of the middle of the sky from the times of the ascensions of the degree of the moon if the moon is on the eastern side of the meridian line, and subtract the times of the ascensions of the degree of the moon from the times of the ascensions of the degree of the middle of the sky if the moon is on the western side of the meridian line. Divide the result, whichever of the two it is, by 15: what comes out is the hours of the distance of the moon from the meridian line, in equal hours, on the side where the moon is. Then see whether the moon is above the earth or below it: if the degree of the moon is between the setting degree and the rising degree on the side of the middle of the sky, it is above the earth, and if otherwise, below the earth. When you know that the moon is above the earth, enter with the hours of the distance of the degree of the moon from the middle of the sky into the tables of the parallax for the given clime to which the latitude of your locality is nearest, and look for the same number in the column of the hours set down in the table of the sign in which the moon is, from the horizon. That is, if the moon is on the western side of the meridian line, you look in the hours after the meridian, and if it is on the eastern side, in the hours before the meridian; the hours that you have will be less than the hours set down at the ends of the tables for the signs, and they can be more only if the degree of the moon is below the earth. Then take what stands opposite those hours in the table of the sign in which the moon is and in the table of the sign that follows the sign of the moon, of the minutes of longitude and the minutes of latitude set down there, with interpolation. That is: if there is a fraction with the hours, see what part of an hour the fraction is and take that part of the difference between the complete hour and the hour one above it; add what results for the longitude to the longitude opposite the hour if this is the lesser, and subtract it from it if it is the greater, and do the same with what results for the latitude. Then look at the degrees that the moon has traversed in its sign and find what part they are of the 30 degrees of the sign; take that part of the difference between the minutes of longitude that you wrote down for the sign of the moon and for the sign that follows it, and add the result to the minutes of longitude of the sign of the moon if these are the lesser, or subtract it from them if they are the greater; do the same with the difference of the minutes of latitude. What the minutes of the sign of the moon in longitude and latitude come to after the addition or the subtraction are the minutes of the degree of the moon; keep them.\footnote{«From the horizon» is obscure; Nallino translates «of the sign with which the moon is on the horizon».}\par
\par \ArPage{126}Then enter with the corrected anomaly of the moon at that time into the two columns of numbers of the tables of the computation, which go in steps of six parts, and take what stands opposite it in the fourth column. See what part of 60 the minutes that result are, take that part of the minutes of longitude and of the minutes of latitude, and add what results for the longitude to the longitude and what results for the latitude to the latitude: what the minutes of longitude and the minutes of latitude come to after that are the minutes corrected by the fourth column, so keep them. Then enter with the distance between the sun and the moon by their mean course, doubled, which is the doubled distance that we mentioned in the computation of the moon, into the two columns of numbers of the tables of the computation of the parallax as well, and take what stands opposite it in the fifth column. See what part of 60 the minutes that result are, and take that part of the minutes of longitude and latitude corrected by the fourth column; add to itself what each of them comes to, as you did at first, I mean add what results from what you took of the longitude to the longitude, and what results from what you took of the latitude to the latitude. What each of them comes to after that is the minutes corrected by the fourth and the fifth column, and that is the parallax of the moon in longitude and latitude according to its distance from the earth; keep it, and work with it. Then find the true latitude of the moon and its side, as we have set it out in the chapter on knowing the latitude of the moon, and find the side of the parallax in latitude from the mark in the line of the latitude. If the latitude of the moon and its parallax in latitude are on one side, add them together; if they differ, subtract the smaller from the greater and find the side of the remainder: the result after the addition or the subtraction is the latitude of the moon seen by measurement, on the side on which it results.\footnote{The doubled distance is that of chapter 36; the chapter on the latitude of the moon is chapter 38.}\par
\par As for the parallax in longitude, add it to the true position of the moon if the distance of the moon from the ascendant is less than 90, and subtract it from it if its distance from the ascendant is more than 90 degrees; what the true position of the moon in longitude and latitude comes to is the position in which the moon is seen on the ecliptic.\par
\par \ArPage{127}It may happen that the moon, near the middle of the sky, by an hour or less, up to an hour and a fraction, is to the west of the middle of the sky by measurement while it inclines to the east in its distance from the ascendant, or is to the east of the middle of the sky by the same distance while it inclines to the west. Then examine the parallax in longitude alone, not the latitude, when you take it from these tables near the middle of the sky, on the side on which the parallax in longitude in the hour that follows the meridian is less than its parallax at the meridian, or in the second hour from the meridian less than in the first hour that follows the meridian, on either side, so that you know where the parallax of the moon in longitude should vanish and be nothing at all: that is where the distance of the degree of the moon from the ascendant is exactly 90 parts. When the matter falls out in this way, and the hours of the distance are on the side on which the minutes of longitude vanish, and near the middle of the sky, the way to work is to add together the minutes of longitude for the meridian and for the hour that follows it, or for that one and the hour that follows it, according to the fraction of the hour that you have. If what results is more than the first longitude, which stands opposite the complete hour, or less than it, find its excess over it or its shortfall from it: that is the parallax for the sign in which the moon is, or for the sign that follows it, whichever the matter happens to fall in as we have described, or for both together. Then take the difference between the longitude for the sign of the moon and for the sign that follows it, multiply it by the parts of the moon in the sign in which it is, and divide the product by 30; add the result to the minutes of the sign of the moon if these are the lesser, or subtract it from them if they are the greater. Correct the result by the fourth and the fifth tables of the computation in that way, and proceed with it in the same way in the addition to or the subtraction from the true position of the moon. It may happen that what results for the sign of the moon is opposite to that of the sign that follows it in its inclination to one of the two horizons. When this is so, add together what results for each of the two signs and take of it in proportion to the degrees that the moon has traversed in its sign, out of 30; if the result is more than the parallax of the sign of the moon, take its excess over it, and if it is less, take its shortfall; correct the excess or the shortfall that results by the fourth and the fifth column in that way, and proceed with it in that way. Then you will have found the parallax of the moon on its side in longitude and latitude, and this is nearer to the truth when the moon is on the belt of the signs, if God wills.\footnote{The point of the ecliptic 90 degrees from the ascendant is on the meridian only when the beginning of Cancer or of Capricorn culminates; otherwise it lies east or west of the meridian, at a distance that may exceed an hour, and there the parallax in longitude vanishes and changes its side. The parallaxes found from the tables may be wrong by 4 or 5 minutes when the moon is off the ecliptic (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 40. On knowing the distance of the moon from the earth from its parallax on the circle of altitude, when that is known}{40}{\ArPage{127}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the distance of the moon from the earth from its parallax on the circle of altitude, whether this is found by observation or from the tables: add to the corrected parallax of the moon in longitude and latitude, when you have taken it from the tables of Theon, one part in eighteen of it. Multiply what each of the two parallaxes comes to by itself, add them together, and take the root of the sum: that is the parallax of the moon with the sun on the circle of altitude. If you have taken it from the tables of the parallax on the circle of altitude, do not subtract the parallax of the sun from it, so that it is its parallax with the sun on the circle of altitude.\footnote{Nallino reads one part in eighteen with Plato of Tivoli; the codex has one in thirty-eight. Theon's tables give the parallax of the moon less that of the sun, and Theon adds a twentieth for it; 3 minutes, the parallax of the sun, are an eighteenth of 54 minutes, about the parallax of the moon at the first limit (53′ 34″), so that the eighteenth is right (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{128}If you wish to find it by observation, you find it as I describe. Observe the altitude of the moon when it is 90 parts from the ascendant, with a large quadrant or with the two long alidades whose making is described in Ptolemy's book, so that the taking of the altitude is more correct and precise. When you know its altitude at that place, keep it. Then find the true position of the moon on the ecliptic in longitude and latitude, and so know its distance from the equator in the way that we have explained at the beginning of the book. If its distance from the equator is to the north, subtract it from the latitude of the locality, taken by observation, and if it is to the south, add it to it; subtract what the latitude of the locality comes to after the addition or the subtraction from 90: the remainder is what its altitude in the middle of the sky must be. Then from its altitude in the middle of the sky find what its altitude must be when it is 90 parts from the ascendant, in the way that we have shown for the altitude of the degree of the moon, and compare this with the altitude of the moon that you found by observation when it was 90 parts from the ascendant. Whatever the altitude taken by observation falls short of the altitude known by computation is the parallax of the moon with the sun on the circle of altitude. It is greater the farther the moon is from the zenith, that is, when it is in the southern signs, and above all at the beginning of Capricorn: for, besides what we have described, if its latitude is to the south, its distance from the equator is the whole declination together with the latitude of the moon; and likewise if its latitude is to the north, its distance from the equator is the amount of the whole declination less the latitude of the moon, since the declination and the latitude then, and only then, come out on one arc. At the beginning of Cancer, which is like the beginning of Capricorn in this respect, the parallax is small, because the moon is near the zenith.\footnote{The two long alidades are Ptolemy's parallactic instrument, the long alidade of chapter 4 (\emph{Almagest} V 12), also described in chapter 57 (Nallino). At 90 parts from the ascendant the circle of latitude through the moon is the circle of altitude, so that the whole parallax falls in latitude; Tycho Brahe sometimes observed the moon there for this reason, while Ptolemy (\emph{Almagest} V 13) observed it on the meridian near the beginning of Capricorn (Nallino). The altitude at 90 parts from the ascendant is found as in chapter 39.}\par
\par When you know the parallax of the moon with the sun on the circle of altitude, take its visible distance from the point of the zenith, which is what remains to complete the altitude of the moon to 90, and find its sine and the sine of the visible altitude of the moon as well. Then find the sine of the parallax on the circle of altitude: if it is more than a degree, make it all minutes and keep it; if it is less than a degree, it is minutes. Then multiply the sine of the distance by the radius and divide the product by the sine of the parallax of the moon that I have set out for you: the result is parts. Add to them one minute for each degree of the sine of the altitude: what the parts come to after that is the distance of the moon from the earth in the measure in which the radius of the earth is one part.\footnote{Two rules run together here in the codex and in Plato of Tivoli. Nallino restores the missing words after «the result»: «[is the distance of the moon from the earth. If you wish, take the true distance of the moon from the zenith, which is what the true altitude of the moon falls short of 90, find its sine and the sine of the true altitude of the moon, multiply the sine of the distance by the radius and divide the product by the sine of the parallax of the moon that I have set out for you; what results] is parts». With the visible distance from the zenith ζ and the parallax p, the distance of the moon is sin ζ / sin p radii of the earth; with the true distance from the zenith z, sin z / sin p is the distance from the observer, and the minute for each degree of the sine of the altitude adds the radius of the earth times cos z (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 41. On the seeing of the crescent at the beginnings and the ends of the months, the azimuth of its place in which it is seen at its altitude and its depression, and the shape of its figure according to the light in it, and the levelness of its horns and their inclination to the belt of the signs}{41}{\ArPage{129}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: The knowledge of the seeing of the crescent at the beginnings and the ends of the months is among the most useful of the things whose knowledge has been given, since the era of the Arabs and the beginnings of their months depend on the seeing of the crescents. Knowing it truly has some difficulty, from various sides: among them the nearness of the moon to the sun and its distance from it, its distance from the earth and its nearness to it, the variation of the latitude of the moon on the northern and the southern side, then the parallax that occurs in the longitude and the latitude of the moon in every locality, the shortness and length of the risings and the settings of the signs in the climes, and the abundance and scarcity of the light in it. For this reason some people of our time who sought the knowledge of it fell into error in knowing the seeing of the crescents, and fell short of reaching the truth of things. They even imagined that the distance of a star from the equator and the latitude of the star come out together on one arc; they worked on the assumption that the parallax of the moon does not come from its variation on the circle of altitude, but arises from its distance from the middle of the sky in degrees of the signs; and they multiplied arcs into sines, with principles that they had adopted before, which measurement does not require and demonstration does not confirm.\footnote{The Muslim months begin with the first sighting of the crescent after the conjunction, attested by witnesses; computation was admitted only by a few jurists of the Shāfiʿite school (Nallino). Nallino supposes that those who erred were jurists or the timekeepers of the mosques.}\par
\par As for the ancients, they were not compelled to know this, because the era with them, with which they worked, is the years of the sun, and the beginnings of the lunar months were known to them from the times of the conjunctions, whose truth computation shows. For this reason they set aside any mention of it, despite how much arises in it of what we have mentioned, except in general terms: they mentioned that the crescent cannot be seen in less than a day and a night. When the causes of the seeing are examined, this statement is found to be the principle on which one works. For the amount of the seeing found by observation, although it comes close to the amount that appears in this way mentioned, is known, when the matter is examined, not to be graspable with the most exact truth: what is grasped of it is grasped only approximately.\par
\par \ArPage{130}Since the knowledge of the seeing of the crescent found by observation is right only from the amounts of the arcs of the equator that lie between the sun and the moon at sunrise or at sunset — when these arcs are observed in one of the climes, the amount is known in one clime, and when it is known in one clime it is known in all the climes — that on which people's opinions agree for the amount of the arc of seeing is, as we found by observation, about 12 parts of the times of the equator. It is clear that the course of the moon, when it parts from the sun, in a day and a night, when the mean course of the sun in a day and a night is taken from it, is 12 parts 11 minutes; that is the amount of the distance that arises between the sun and the moon in parts of the signs, and this agrees approximately with what is taken by observation when these parts are of the equator. It is evident that the amount of these times mentioned is about four fifths of an hour, and we find that the moon gains on the sun in this amount of an equal hour about two fifths of a part. So when the sun sets with about 11 and a half and a quarter times of the equator between it and the moon, the moon does not set until the 12 parts 11 minutes are complete. On this reckoning, therefore, the mean arc of seeing is 11 parts and a half and a quarter of a part of the times of the equator, which are the risings and the settings of the signs in the localities. What is lit of the circle of the moon when the distance of the moon from the sun is the amount of these parts of the ecliptic [is] about four fifths of a part, if the whole circle of the moon is 12 parts. The moon may be farther from the sun than this amount, or less, at the times of seeing, so that the light in it is more or less according to the amounts of the distance, and it is seen at less than this arc or at more; moreover, it is near the earth or far from it at those times because of its position on the epicycle, which adds to these amounts or takes from them. For this reason the crescent cannot be seen at one and the same arc: it is seen at different arcs.\footnote{The times of the equator are degrees of the equator, 15 to the hour. 12;11 is the mean daily gain of the moon on the sun (chapter 30): the moon gains about two fifths of a degree in the four fifths of an hour that 12 times take to set, so that 11;47, which al-Battānī rounds to 11;45, is the mean arc of seeing (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary). Four fifths of a part of light follows the rule of chapter 30, one part for each 15 parts of distance. The bracketed word is supplied by Nallino, who also restores the order of the words of the codex.}\par
\par \ArPage{131}If you wish to know whether the crescent will be seen or not, in this way, compute the sun and the moon for the time of sunset of the second day from the conjunction, which is the 29th day of the Arab month, and find their true positions on the ecliptic in the locality that you want; find also the true latitude of the moon and its side. Then find the amount of the parallax of the moon at the time of sunset in longitude and latitude in those ways, so that the visible position of the moon on the ecliptic in longitude and latitude and the side of the latitude come out right for you. When you know that, find its visible distance from the equator and the degree that culminates with it, and with them find half the arc of the day of the moon, which is half its stay above the earth, in the way explained at the beginning of the book, in the chapter on knowing the distance of a star from the equator and the degree that culminates with it from the latitude of the star and the declination of the degree in which it is, and in the chapter on knowing half the arc of the day of a star from its distance from the equator. Add what results of half the arc of the day of the moon to the times of the ascensions of the degree that culminates with it in the right sphere: the result is the times of the ascensions of the counterpart of the degree with which the moon sets in that clime. Subtract from them the times of the ascensions that stand opposite the degree opposite the degree of the sun in that clime: the remainder is the distance between the sun and the moon in degrees of the settings, so keep it. Then find the true degree in which the moon was, and its true latitude; take what lies between the true degrees of the sun and of the moon, multiply it by itself, add to it the latitude of the moon multiplied by itself, and take the root of the sum: the result is the distance of the moon from the sun, approximately.\footnote{The crescent is looked for at the sunset that ends the 29th day, which begins the night of the 30th, the Muslim day beginning at sunset; Nallino translates «[after] the 29th day». The chapters cited are 18 and 19. The distance between the sun and the moon in degrees of the settings is counted with the oblique ascensions of the opposite degrees, since a degree sets with the rising of its counterpart.}\par
\par \ArPage{132}If you wish to know this from what we mentioned at the beginning of the book, in the chapter on knowing the distances between the stars in their arrangement on the sphere, that is more correct and more exact. If the distance of the moon from the sun is [more than] 12;11, take what exceeds 12;11, and if it is less, find what it falls short of it, and note the amount of the excess or the shortfall with its mark. Then see what part of 12;11, which is the amount of the light in the moon for seeing, that excess or that shortfall is, and take that much of the excess or of the shortfall: that is the part, by which the arc of seeing grows or decreases. Then enter with the corrected anomaly of the moon into the table of the computation and take the minutes that stand opposite it in the third column, in which the shares of the distances of the moon are set down. If those minutes are exactly 30, the moon is at its mean distance from the earth; then, if that part has the mark of the shortfall, add it to 11;45, which is the amount of the arc of seeing, and if it has the mark of the excess, subtract it from 11;45. If the minutes are more than 30 or less than 30, look at what exceeds 30 or falls short of it, find what part of the 30 minutes it is, and take as much of the part. Of the result take half a sixth, since the diameter of the moon varies so that its excess over its mean diameter and its shortfall from it are about half a sixth of its mean diameter. Add what you obtain of the half sixth to the part if the part has the mark of the excess and the minutes of the third column are more than 30; if the minutes of the third column are less than 30, subtract that half sixth from the part. If the part has the mark of the shortfall and the minutes are more than 30, subtract the half sixth that you obtained from that part, and if the minutes are less than 30, add it to the part. Then look at what the part comes to after the addition or the subtraction: if it has the mark of the excess over 12;11, subtract it from 11;45, and if it has the mark of the shortfall, add it to 11;45. The result is the amount of the arc of seeing corrected for the increase and the decrease of the light of the moon in its distance from the earth at that time. If what you kept of [the arc] between the sun and the moon in degrees of the settings is equal to the corrected arc of seeing or more than it, the crescent will be seen; if it is less than the corrected arc of seeing, it will not be seen in that locality.\footnote{The chapter on the distances between the stars is chapter 26. The words in brackets are supplied by Nallino; the codex has 41;45 for 11;45 here and below. As the text stands, the fraction found is taken of the excess or the shortfall itself. In Schiaparelli's account of the rule (Nallino's commentary) the arc of seeing, 11;45, is reduced in the inverse ratio of the elongation to 12;11, a greater elongation giving a broader crescent, so that to first order the part is that fraction of 11;45; in the parallel rule below Nallino supplies «of those 10;50». The arc is then corrected for the distance of the moon: the third column, counted with the two columns of numbers, has 30 at the mean distance, 0 at the farthest and 60 at the nearest, and the apparent diameter of the moon, 0;29,30 to 0;35,20 (chapter 30), varies by about a twelfth on either side of its mean.}\par
\par Clearness and purity of the air help the seeing of the crescent, and its thickness and turbidity hinder it, besides the differences that arise between eyes in looking, in strength and weakness. The twilight may be thick and then grow thin before the moon sets from the horizon and reaches the limit of setting, and then the crescent is seen after the time of seeing on which one works. For this reason one should not despair of seeing the crescent until it is known to have set, if it is at a place of seeing, and it is certain that it has gone down below the horizon; then one may despair of it. For these reasons it may be seen in one place and not in another; the like happens also because of the difference of the risings and the settings of the signs in the localities, in length and shortness.\par
\par \ArPage{133}As for what opinion inclines to, and whose truth is not doubted, according to what the ancients set down about the seeing of the crescent in what they laid down and described, that it cannot be seen in less than a day and a night: if we take the distance of the moon from the sun when the moon moves with its least course and the sun with its greatest, that is, when the moon is at its farthest distance on the epicycle and the sun at its nearest distance, we find its distance from the sun in a day and a night to be 10 parts and a half and a third of a part, and that is the amount of the arc of seeing on the equator by this reckoning. When the moon moves with its greatest course and the sun with its least, that is, where the moon is at its nearest distance and the sun at its farthest distance on the epicycle, we find the distance of the moon from the sun in a day and a night to be about 13⅔ parts. So we use this amount of the ecliptic for the light that is in the moon at the time of seeing, and we say that when there are 10 parts and a half and a third of the times of the equator between the sun and the moon, and its distance from the sun in parts of the signs is 13⅔ parts, it is at the place of its seeing, unless something of the state of the air that we have mentioned prevents it; no doubt enters into this for us. Since the moon may be farther from the sun than these parts of the ecliptic mentioned, or less, and may move on the epicycle away from the point of the farthest distance towards its nearest distance, the amount of the seeing changes for that reason, as we said just now.\footnote{Schiaparelli calls this explanation of the two numbers a rather strange one (Nallino's commentary). With them the rule that follows takes the moon at its farthest distance, with an arc of seeing of 10;50 at an elongation of 13;40, where the first rule gives 11;45 × 13/12 × 12;11/13;40 = 11;21; so the second rule sometimes finds the crescent visible where the first does not. With the hourly motions of chapter 30 (the moon 0;30,12 and 0;36,10, the sun 0;2,33 and 0;2,23) the least and the greatest gain in a day and a night come to about 11;4 and 13;31. Plato of Tivoli has 13;30.}\par
\par \ArPage{134}If you wish to know the truth of the seeing in this way, compute the sun and the moon for the time mentioned in those ways, so that you know its distance from the sun in parts of the settings of the locality. Then find the distance of the moon from the sun in parts of the signs according to the latitude of the moon, in that way. If it is more than 13;40, find the amount of the excess, and if it is less than that, find the amount of the shortfall; see what part of 13;40 either of them is, and take that much of it: that is the part. If the moon is at its farthest distance, in which it was at the time of the given amount of the arc of seeing — which happens when the corrected anomaly of the moon is about 360, with no excess or shortfall except of no account — subtract that part from 10;50 if it has the mark of the excess, and add it to 10;50 if it has the mark of the shortfall: the result after that is the corrected arc of seeing. If the moon has left its farthest distance, enter with its corrected anomaly into the tables of the computation and take the minutes that are in the third column; find what part of 60 they are, and take that part of the part. Take a fifth of the result, since the excess of the greatest diameter of the moon over its least diameter is that part of its least diameter. Subtract what you obtain of the fifth from that part that has resulted for you if the part has the mark of the shortfall, and add it to it if it has the mark of the excess. See how much the part comes to after the addition or the subtraction in times of the equator; add the result to 10;50 if the part has the mark of the shortfall from 13;40, and subtract it from it if it has the mark of the excess: the result is the amount of the corrected arc of seeing. If it is equal to the distance between the sun and the moon in times of the settings, or less than it, you know that the moon is at the place of seeing, no doubt about it, whether some of what we have mentioned hinders it or not; if the corrected arc is more than the times of the settings, you know that the crescent cannot be seen in that locality. We know how much the part that results comes to in times of the equator in this way. We enter with the times that we described as the times of the ascensions of the degree opposite the degree of the moon in the clime, and find what stands opposite them of the degrees of the signs: that is the degree opposite the degree with which the moon sets. We add to it what has resulted of the part, and keep the result; this we do if the part has the mark of the excess. If the part has the mark of the shortfall, we subtract what has resulted of the part from the degrees of the signs that we have obtained, and keep the remainder. Whichever of the two happens, we find what stands opposite it of the times of the ascensions, and see how much they exceed those first times, the times of the ascensions of the degree opposite the degree of the moon, or how much they fall short of them: the result is the amount of the part that has resulted, in times of the equator. We subtract it from the arc of seeing or add it to it, as is due, if God wills.\footnote{«Of it» is of the 10;50, as Nallino understands it (compare the note on the first rule). The codex has 64;50 for 10;50 here and below, the Maghribi \RL{\ArNote ص} being 60 (Nallino). The greatest diameter of the moon, 0;35,20, exceeds the least, 0;29,30, by about a fifth of the least (chapter 30).}\par
\par As for seeing the moon in the mornings at the ends of the months, it is on this pattern, except that you use the times of the ascensions of the degree of the sun itself and the times of the ascensions of the degree of the moon itself. You find the times of the ascensions of the degree of the moon by subtracting half the arc of the day of the moon from the times of the ascensions of the degree that culminates with it in the right sphere: the remainder is the times of the ascensions of the degree that rises with the moon in the clime. Subtract from that the times of the ascensions of the degree of the sun: the remainder is the amount between the sun and the moon in times of the ascensions, when the moon is on the eastern side. If the arc of seeing that results is equal to the distance between the sun and the moon in times of the ascensions, or less than it, the moon is seen before sunrise in the morning; if it is more, it has been hidden by the rays and is not seen. The computation of the sun and the moon should be made for the time of sunrise of the 28th day of the Arab month, which is a day before the conjunction.\par
\par \ArPage{135}If you wish to draw the shape of the crescent in the state in which it is seen, with its two horns level or inclined, and the amount of the light that is in it: divide the distance between the sun and the moon in parts of the signs, according to the latitude of the moon, by 15, so that the result is a part out of 12 parts of the circle of the moon: the result is the digits of light. Then draw a circle of any size you wish, divide it into quarters by two lines that intersect at the centre at right angles, mark on the ends of the lines their directions on the horizon, and divide each quarter of the circle into 90 parts. Then mark, according to the true latitude of the moon, from the point of the east and the point of the west towards the side of the latitude of the moon, two marks, so that the amount of each of the two arcs is as much as the latitude of the moon. Then put the edge of the ruler on the two marks and draw along it a straight line that passes through the two marks and is parallel to a diameter of the circle, and extend it beyond the circumference of the circle towards the east by the amount of the radius of the circle. On this line is the passage of the moon in longitude at that time, and at the other times according to the latitude that it may have, from the time of its first appearance to the time of its half light, for the centre of its circle then falls at the point where the circumference of the circle meets this line; and from the time of its half light to the time of its fullness the centre of its circle is on the line that goes out from the circumference of the circle and runs towards the east, until it reaches the end of the line, between its circle and the circle of the sun. So the first circle, drawn for the sun, is the circle of the moon at its fullness. Then count on the circumference of the circle, from the point of the north towards the east, the same number as is between the sun and the moon, and likewise from the side of the south towards the east, mark two marks on it, and join one of the marks to the other by a straight line: where the two lines intersect is the centre of the circle of the moon. Draw about it a circle the size of the first circle: the crescent that falls between the two arcs is in the shape of the crescent and the image of its appearance. Then join the two points at which the two circles intersect by a straight line, which is a second diameter of the circle, and draw also a straight line that passes through the centres of the two circles and through the two arcs and divides the crescent into two halves. From this it becomes clear to you how much each of the two horns of the crescent inclines from the middle of the belt of the signs, by the parts into which you divided the circumference, for the ecliptic then has a known limit on the horizon, from the azimuth on the circle of the horizon of what rises and sets with it at that time.\footnote{Twelve digits are the whole visible half of the moon at the full, each 15 degrees of the semicircle of its disc (Nallino); Plato of Tivoli has 15 for these 12 parts. The construction gives the inclination of the line of the horns to the ecliptic quite exactly, as long as the elongation is not much greater than on the first evening of visibility. The figure between the two arcs is the visible lune; the crescent actually seen is much thinner, since that part of the moon's surface is seen very obliquely (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{136}Let the circle of the sun be the one on which ABGD, about the centre H, with the diameters AG and BD. We take A as the direction of the south, G the direction of the north, B the direction of the east and the point D the direction of the west. We take the latitude of the moon to be 5 parts to the north and its true distance from the sun 12 parts. We cut off from the circle, from the points B and D, the amount of the latitude of the moon towards the side of the north, which is the point G, mark on it Ṭ and K, join them by a straight line, the line ṬK, and extend it to the sign L; let the line KL be equal to the line HB. From the points A and G we cut off two arcs towards the side of B, the amount of each of them equal to what lies between the sun and the moon; we mark at the ends of the two arcs the signs M and S, join them by the straight line MS, and mark the place where it cuts the line ṬK with the sign Z. We take Z as centre and draw about it a circle for the moon the size of the first circle, and mark at the intersections of the two circles the signs F and Q. We draw also the line HZ and extend it to the sign ʿ of the first circle; the sign ʿ falls on the middle of the arc FQ. We mark the sign Ḥ on the circumference of the circle of the moon where the line ZHʿ cuts it. The line ʿḤ is the middle of the curve of the crescent and the place of the middle of the light, which is the amount of the digits of light in the moon. The signs F and Q are the two ends of the crescent, and their inclination to the line of levelness standing upright on the ecliptic is known by the arc AF and the arc GQ. For the point D is then in the direction of the setting degree and the point B in the direction of the rising degree on the circle of the horizon, so that the line BD is the line of half the ecliptic. By this figure you know the shape of the light of the crescent at all the times of the month, according to its distance from the sun and the amount of latitude that it has. Whenever the moon is at its nearest distance, the two horns are sharper, because its circle is then greater than the sun's. This is what we wished to explain, if God wills.\footnote{The translation reads D (\RL{\ArNote د}) for the text's A (\RL{\ArNote ا}), with Nallino's translation, which corrects the printed Arabic text here (p. 136, line 5); the codex and Plato of Tivoli have A. The line ʿḤ is the breadth of the visible lune, not of the thinner crescent actually seen (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary). Plato of Tivoli has «the tips will be less sharp».}\par

\par \ArPage{137}If you wish to know the place of the sky in which the crescent is seen, according to its altitude above the western horizon at the beginnings of the months, and the azimuth of its place in which it is seen on the circle of altitude that passes through the zenith, through the moon and through the horizon, by a pointer, so that the line of sight goes out along its direction to the place of the crescent: add about four minutes to the degree that culminates with the moon, so that this is the degree that culminates with it at the time of seeing; for the rays of the sun prevent its being seen at sunset until the sun has gone down below the horizon about an eighth of an hour. Then find the visible altitude of the moon about an eighth of an equal hour after sunset, and find the azimuth of the moon on the circle of the horizon at that time in the ways that we have set out. Then go to a place where the horizon is open and set up there a column, or something like a column, whose height above the horizon is a person's height, so that the observer can [look] from it at the moon. Let its surface be flat, levelled with the plumb line and parallel to the plane of the horizon. Take a centre on it and draw about it a circle of any size you wish; mark the directions of the east, the west, the south and the north in the way given for finding the meridian line, and divide the quarter of the circle on the side of the crescent into 90 parts. Then take a flat ruler or a hollow tube, and put the edge of the ruler, or the middle of the thickness of the tube, on the centre of the circle and on the amount of the distance of the azimuth of the crescent from the point of the east or of the west, whichever side the crescent is on, in the direction in which the azimuth is. Then hang the astrolabe from your hand, after setting the end of the alidade at the visible altitude of the moon that you have found, and raise the end of the ruler or of the tube that is towards the crescent from the surface of the circle, with something to support it, without its turning aside from the azimuth of the moon or from the centre of the circle, so that the end towards the crescent rises and the end towards the eye goes down, and the sight passes through the two holes of the alidade along the edge of the ruler or the middle of the tube, so that there is a straight line from the place of the eye to the place of the crescent in that direction. When the observer looks at the time of seeing, the crescent will be seen along the direction of the edge of the ruler, or through the tube. This is the figure of what we have described, if God wills.\footnote{The translation follows Nallino's emendanda (Part II, p. XXXI) in reading «\RL{\ArNote سمت القمر}», «the azimuth of the moon», for «\RL{\ArNote القمر}» at p. 137, line 1. In an eighth of an hour the moon moves about 4 minutes, its mean motion in an equal hour being 0;32,56 (Nallino). «Look» is added after Nallino's suggestion. The meridian line is found as in chapter 12. The translation follows the corrigenda in reading «\RL{\ArNote الدائرة}», «the circle», for «\RL{\ArNote الدئراة}» (p. 137, line 10). This is the earliest mention of sighting tubes among the Arabs; a tube keeps off much of the diffuse light of the sky, which hinders seeing (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par\begin{center}\begin{tikzpicture}\draw[line width=1.41pt] (153.60pt,115.06pt) circle[radius=76.43pt];
