\par \ArPage{138}Al-Battānī said: We draw the circle of the horizon mentioned, marked ABGD, about the centre H, and let the point H be the place of the centre of the circle of the horizon on the plane, which is the zenith; the point A the point of the south, the point B the point of the east, the point G the point of the north, and the point D the point of the west. We draw the two lines AG and BD. We take the moon to be on the western side, which is the quarter AD, and make the point B of the ecliptic the beginning of Aries, so that the point D is the beginning of Libra: these are the rising and the setting points of the ecliptic. We take the southern half of the ecliptic to be the arc DLB; then the line GL is clearly the place of the beginning of Capricorn, which is on the meridian line. Let the degree that culminates with the moon be the point Ṭ of the ecliptic, which is the beginning of Scorpio. We put the sign Z at the place of the moon in its southern latitude, and make the line KHṬZḤ the place of the edge of the ruler, or of the middle of the thickness of the tube, which passes through the centre of the circle, through the place of the moon and through the degree that culminates with it. We find the arc DḤ of the horizon. Clearly the arc ṬḤ is the altitude above the horizon of the degree that culminates with the moon, and the arc ZḤ the altitude of the moon above it; likewise the arc AL is the altitude of the beginning of Capricorn in the middle of the sky, and the arc DṬ of the ecliptic is that from the point of the beginning of Libra to the degree that culminates with the moon; and the point Ḥ is the azimuth of the moon. So the arc DḤ of the horizon is the distance of the azimuth of the moon from the point of the equinoctial setting. When the line KḤ is raised from the point H and the point Ḥ, by the amount of the altitude of the moon set on the astrolabe, towards the air, the place K of it goes down towards the earth, the sight passes through the two holes of the alidade of the astrolabe, which are the points M and K, and the whole line is joined, so that the whole line MḤ becomes one straight line. When the observer looks from the place K or the place M, the crescent will be seen with that pointer in the direction of the line KḤ, if the air is clear and thin, no doubt about it. If the air is changeable and turbid, it prevents its being seen in that town; but it is seen in other towns whose distance from the equator is like the distance of that town, since a change of the air need not extend over every locality. For this reason also it may not be seen in the villages and dwellings near it.\footnote{The words «which is the beginning of Scorpio», «and through the degree that culminates with it», «the arc ṬḤ is the altitude above the horizon of the degree that culminates with the moon, and» and «the arc DṬ of the ecliptic is that from the point of the beginning of Libra to the degree that culminates with the moon», though they are in the codex and in Plato of Tivoli, should be struck out (Nallino, after Schiaparelli): the degree that culminates with the moon lies on the great circle through the moon and the pole of the equator, not on the line of sight, and serves no purpose here. Nallino's translation accordingly names the line KHZḤ. In the last sentence Nallino translates «it may be seen in the villages near it».}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 42. On knowing the computation of the conjunctions and the oppositions of the sun and the moon by the era of the Romans and the era of the Copts, and on knowing their times in every locality}{42}{\ArPage{138}}{}
\par \ArPage{139}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the computation of the conjunctions or the oppositions in any month you wish of the months of the Romans, take the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn, and do not count the year that you are in until Subāṭ has run out. Look for the number of years that you have in the column of the collected years of the tables of the conjunction or of the opposition, whichever you want. Wherever you find the same — I mean the same number — or the nearest one below it, take what stands opposite it in the four columns that are for the days, the mean position of the sun and the moon, the anomaly of the moon and the motion in latitude. Then see what remains with you of the years in excess of those that you found in the table, enter with it in the column of the single years, and take what stands opposite it in those four columns; write down what you find in each of them with its counterpart, I mean each kind under its kind. Then take what stands opposite the complete month before the month in which you wish to compute, of the days of the lunar months set down in the first of the four columns, and add it to the sum of the days that came from the collected and the single years that you wrote down. If the sum of all that is more than the number of the days of the Roman months set down under that complete month, and less than the days under the month that you are in, which is the month in which you wish to compute, write down those days that you found opposite the complete month and what is under them in the three remaining columns. If the sum of the days is more than the Roman days set down under the month in which you wish to compute, take the days that stand opposite the month before the complete month, and what is under them in the three remaining columns, and write it down with what you wrote from the tables of the collected and the single years. What each of the four columns comes to after you have added it up, write down in the manner given before. Then subtract the Roman days that stand opposite the complete month before the month in which you wish to compute from the days that came from the sum of what is in the three tables of the collected years, the single years and the months: the days and minutes that remain are days that have passed of the month in which you wished to compute, and equal hours after noon of the last day that has passed of that month up to the time of the conjunction or the opposition of the sun and the moon at that time by their mean course. [From the three remaining columns come the positions of the sun and the moon at that time by their mean course,] the anomaly of the moon and the motion in latitude. [The mean position of the moon] is the mean position of the sun at the time of [the conjunction; at the time of] the opposition the mean position of the moon is necessarily opposite the mean position of the sun.\footnote{The years of the tables begin on 1 Ādhār, March, six months before the year of Dhū l-Qarnayn with the same number, which begins on 1 Aylūl, September; so the year that you are in is counted only when Subāṭ, February, is over (Nallino; see chapter 33). The first bracketed words are supplied by Nallino after Plato of Tivoli. The printed text then reads «and it is the mean position of the sun at the time of the opposition, and the mean position of the moon is necessarily opposite the mean position of the sun»; the other bracketed words follow the correction that Nallino proposes and translates.}\par
\par \ArPage{140}If you wish to compute the conjunction and the opposition by the era of the Copts, take the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn together with the year that you are in, even if only one day of Aylūl has entered. Then cast 287 from the years, and take a quarter of what remains: the result is the days of the quarters. If a fraction falls in it, do not count it; if no fraction falls, that year is a leap year. When the year is a leap year, drop one day from the days of the quarters, unless Subāṭ has run out and come out at 29 days; when Subāṭ has run out, add the day that you had taken away back to the days. To the days of the quarters that result always add the three days by which the Copts precede the Greeks in Tūt, and to what the days come to after that add the days from the beginning of Aylūl to the end of the Roman month before the month that you want, the month in which you compute. If the sum of the days is more than 365 days, cast 365 from it and add a year to the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn from which you subtracted nothing; if the year is a leap year and Subāṭ has run out, you count the year as 366 days. What results of the days after casting off the year, if they were more than a year, or the days themselves if they were less than a year, are the days of the Copts; write them down apart. Then enter with what you have of the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn, with the year added on account of the days if there is one, into the table of the collected Egyptian years, which go in steps of 25, in the column of the collected years in the tables of the conjunction or of the opposition, whichever you want. Wherever you find the same years, or the nearest number below them, take what stands opposite them in the four columns in that way. Look for the years that remain in the column of the single years, and take again what stands opposite them in the four columns. Then take the days of the Copts and cast them out by thirties; enter with the number of complete months that results into the column of numbers of the tables of the Coptic months, take what stands opposite it in the column of the days, and add it to the days that came to you from the two tables of the collected and the single years. If the sum is equal to the number of the days of the Copts, or more than it by less than a lunar month, write down those days and what is under them in the three remaining columns. If the sum of the days is more than the days of the Copts by more than a lunar month, take one month from the number of the complete Coptic months with which you entered the table; take what stands opposite the number of complete Coptic months that remains in the four columns and write it down. Then add it up in the usual manner, and subtract the days of the Copts from the days that result: the days and minutes that remain are the days and the hours of the conjunction or the opposition that have passed of the month in which you wished to compute, and what results from the three columns is the mean position of the sun and the moon, the anomaly of the moon and the motion in latitude.\footnote{The days of the Copts, the days that have passed of the Coptic year, are found as in chapter 32.}\par
\par \ArPage{141}When you know that by whichever of the two eras you wish, look at the minutes that are added up with the days, and count each 2½ minutes of them as an equal hour; what does not make up 2½ minutes is parts of an hour. The days and hours that result are the days of the mean conjunction or opposition and its hours after noon at the city of al-Raqqa; keep them.\footnote{A day is 60 minutes, so that 2½ minutes are an hour. The translation reads «\RL{\ArNote الأيّام}», «the days», for «\RL{\ArNote الأبّام}» (p. 141, line 2), a misprint that the corrigenda do not list.}\par
\par Then write down the mean position of the sun and the moon in another place, making one of the two places for the sun and the other for the moon, and compute the true positions of the sun and the moon as usual, except that for the moon you then need only the single equation, for the distance between the sun and the moon is not such that a sensible error enters from the second equation. If the sun and the moon are equal to the minute, that is the time of the true conjunction or opposition. Then do with the motion in latitude what you did with the mean position of the moon: add the single equation to the motion in latitude if you added it to the mean position of the moon, and subtract it from it if you subtracted it from the mean position of the moon.\par
\par If the positions of the sun and the moon differ, take the excess between them in degrees and minutes, and find its sixth and its eighth. If the excess is the sun's, add that sixth and eighth to the anomaly of the moon, and if the excess is the moon's, subtract them from it; what the anomaly comes to after the addition or the subtraction is the corrected anomaly. Enter with it into the tables of the equation of the moon, in the two columns of numbers, and take what stands opposite it of the single equation, set down in the second column. If this anomaly is less than 180, subtract this equation from the mean position of the moon itself and from the motion in latitude itself; if the number of the anomaly is more than 180, add the equation to the mean position of the moon and to the motion in latitude. What the mean position of the moon comes to after the addition to it or the subtraction from it is the true position of the moon.\footnote{The excess is the sun's when the true sun is ahead of the true moon, so that the true conjunction comes after the mean one. The reason for adding a sixth and an eighth, 7/24, of the excess to the anomaly is given below. The second column is counted without the two columns of numbers (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{142}Then take again the excess between the sun and the moon and know it. Then take the motion of the sun and of the moon in an hour: enter with the corrected anomaly of the moon, with which you found the equation of the moon, and the anomaly of the sun, with which you found the equation of the sun, into the tables of the unequal course of the sun and the moon, in the two columns of numbers, which go in steps of 6 parts, and take what is under them in the column of the course of each of them, by interpolation, after adding to the course of the moon, or subtracting from it, the seconds that you find set down under the excess between the sun and the moon, in the way that we have explained in that chapter, at those tables. Then subtract the motion of the sun from the motion of the moon: the remainder is the unequal gain of the moon on the sun in an hour. Divide the excess between the sun and the moon by the gain of the moon: the hour and part of an hour that result are the hours of the excess; keep them. If the excess is the sun's, add the hours of the excess to the hours of the mean conjunction that came from the tables, and if the excess is the moon's, subtract them from them. What the hours of the mean conjunction come to after the addition or the subtraction are the plain hours of the true conjunction. If they are more than 24 hours, subtract 24 hours from them and add one day to the days that have passed of the month. If you need to subtract the hours of the excess from the hours of the mean conjunction, and the hours of the excess are more than the hours of the mean conjunction, take one day from the days that have passed of the month, count it as 24 hours, and add them to the hours of the mean conjunction; then take the hours of the excess from the sum. What results of the hours of the mean conjunction after the addition or the subtraction is the equal hours that have passed after noon at the city of al-Raqqa of the day that you obtained from the days that have passed of the month.\footnote{That chapter is a short unnumbered chapter on the hourly motion of the moon, which stands with the tables in the Escorial codex alone and which Nallino prints after chapter 57 as chapter H. The seconds correct the hourly motion of the moon for its elongation from the sun: about one second an hour for each degree of elongation, taken from it in the upper part of the epicycle and added in the lower (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary). The plain hours are mean hours; they are converted into hours of the irregular days below.}\par
\par Multiply the hours of the excess by the course of the sun and the course of the moon in an hour, and keep the products. If the excess is the sun's, add what results for the sun to the position of the sun, and what results for the moon to the position of the moon and to the motion in latitude; to the motion in latitude add besides the motion of the northern node in the amount of those hours. If the excess is the moon's, subtract in all of this instead of adding, so that you obtain the correct positions of the sun and the moon at that time.\footnote{The motion in latitude is counted from the northern node, which moves backwards; so the node's motion in those hours is added to it as well.}\par
\par If you wish to work approximately in another way, find half a sixth of the excess between the sun and the moon. If the excess is the sun's, add half a sixth of the excess to the sun, and the whole excess with half its sixth to the moon and to the motion in latitude; if the excess is the moon's, subtract half a sixth of the excess from the sun, and the whole excess with half its sixth from the moon [and from the motion in latitude]: then the two are equal to the minute. Then divide [the whole excess] and half its sixth by the unequal motion of the moon in an hour: the result is the hours of the excess. Subtract them from the hours of the mean conjunction if the excess is the moon's, and add them to them if the excess is the sun's, in that manner. But the first procedure is more correct.\footnote{The bracketed words are supplied by Nallino after Plato of Tivoli. Half a sixth, a twelfth, of the excess is the motion of the sun while the moon gains the excess on it: the moon moves about 13 times as fast as the sun, so that in that time the sun moves about a twelfth of the excess (Nallino). This is Ptolemy's method in \emph{Almagest} VI 4 (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{143}You should also know the anomaly of the moon at the time [of the conjunction and the opposition]: enter with the hours of the excess into the table of the hours, take the course of the anomaly of the moon in them, and add it to the corrected anomaly of the moon if the excess is the sun's, and subtract it from it if the excess is the moon's. What it comes to after that is the corrected anomaly of the moon at the time of the conjunction, if your computation is for the conjunction; if your computation is for the opposition, it is the corrected anomaly of the moon at the time of the opposition. Then add 180 degrees to the position of the moon that you had made the same as the mean position of the sun, so that the true position of the moon is opposite the true position of the sun, where it is seen.\footnote{The bracketed words are supplied by Nallino after Plato of Tivoli.}\par
\par When you know the plain equal hours of the conjunction, which are the mean hours, convert them into hours of the irregular days. Enter with the degree of the sun into the tables of the right sphere and take the degrees and minutes that stand opposite it in the column of the equation of the days, set down in the sign of the sun; divide what there is by 15: what results is hours, and what remains is part of an hour. Always add it to the mean hours of the true conjunction: what they come to after that is the equal hours of the true conjunction converted into the irregular days, which are found by measurement, after noon at the city of al-Raqqa. Then convert them into hours of the locality that you want. You do this by taking the excess between the longitude of the city that you want and the longitude of the city of al-Raqqa, which is 73;15, and dividing it by 15. Add the hour or part of an hour that results to the hours of the true conjunction that you have obtained if the longitude of the city is greater than the longitude of al-Raqqa, and subtract it from them if the longitude of the city is less than the longitude of al-Raqqa. What the hours come to after the addition or the subtraction is the true equal hours after noon in that city.\footnote{The equation of the days with their nights is that of chapter 29. Here it is added, since the hours are converted from mean days into irregular days; chapter 34 subtracts it for the opposite conversion.}\par
\par If you wish to know the ascendant of the conjunction, multiply all these hours by 15, add the product to the ascensions of the degree of the sun in the right sphere, and with what results you know the ascendant and the middle of the sky as usual.\footnote{At noon the sun is in the middle of the sky, and 15 times of the right sphere pass the meridian in each equal hour; the ascendant and the middle of the sky are then found as in chapters 16 and 35.}\par
\par If you wish to convert these equal hours into seasonal hours, multiply them by 15 degrees and keep the product. Then find the hours of the night and of the daytime from the degree of the sun in that clime. From what you kept of the product take out the times of the hours of the daytime, up to a total of six hours; if it is less than six, add to the hours that come from it six seasonal hours, those from sunrise to noon: the sum is what has passed of the daytime from sunrise to the time of the conjunction, in seasonal hours. If what you took out is six complete hours and a remainder is left with you, take it out by the times of the hours of the night, up to a total of 12 hours; if yet another remainder is left, take it out by the times of the hours of the daytime again: what comes from it is what has passed of the hours of the daytime from sunrise of the morrow. If you wish to know the ascendant from these hours, you know it in the usual way.\par
\par \ArPage{144}It is clear that if the hours of the conjunction are more than half the equal hours of the daytime of that day, and you subtract them from 12 hours if they are less than 12, the remainder is the amount in equal hours by which the conjunction precedes the time of midnight. If the hours are more than 12, up to the completion of half the equal hours of that night, and you cast 12 hours from them, the remainder is the amount in equal hours by which the conjunction comes after the time of midnight. If they are more than 12 together with the half of the hours of the night that is added to them, subtract them from 24 hours: the remainder is the amount in equal hours by which the conjunction precedes the time of noon of the morrow. By what we have described you know the time of the conjunction or the opposition, and the positions of the sun and the moon, the anomaly of the moon and the motion in latitude at those times.\par
\par What compels us to correct the anomaly of the moon by a sixth and an eighth of the excess is that the time of the mean conjunction cannot be, and need not be, at every time the time of the true conjunction. If we neglect what falls in the anomaly on account of the doubled distance between the sun and the moon, a variation can fall in the time of the conjunction or of the opposition whose greatest amount can reach about a quarter of an hour. For if the equation of the sun amounts to 2 parts and the equation of the moon to 3 parts, then, when one of the two equations is added to the mean course and the other subtracted from it, they make together 5 parts, and twice that is 10 parts, which is the doubled distance; and you find the equation of the anomaly at such a distance, by addition or by subtraction, about a degree and a half, which is about a sixth and an eighth of the excess. When the moon is at the place on the epicycle where its corrected equation must be 3 parts, the share of the degree and a half in it is about an eighth of a part, and that comes, by the gain of the moon, to about a quarter of an hour.\footnote{The doubled distance is twice the elongation of the moon from the sun; away from the mean conjunctions the anomaly needs a correction that depends on it. Schiaparelli (Nallino's commentary) derives the correction from Ptolemy's lunar model, with the distance 10;19 between the earth and the centre of the eccentric in parts of which the centre of the epicycle is 60 from the earth at the conjunctions: it is 20;38/70;19 = 0.2934 of the elongation, against al-Battānī's 1/6 + 1/8 = 0.2917, and for an elongation of 5 parts it is 1;28. At the mean hourly gain of the moon, 0;30,28, an eighth of a part takes about a quarter of an hour.}\par
\par Ptolemy, on the other hand, based the reckoning in this on the greater of the two equations, where the equation of the moon is 5 parts and that of the sun 2 parts and 23 minutes, according to the computation on which Ptolemy worked; the excess between the sun and the moon then comes to 7 parts and 23 minutes, and twice that is about 14 parts 46 minutes. On this reckoning no more than an eighth of an hour need arise from it, as was mentioned. But when the equation of the moon is 5 parts, the share of the one or two parts that is then added to the anomaly of the moon or subtracted from it is only a small thing of no account; it varies more at 3 parts than at 5 parts. For this reason the matter is as we have said.\footnote{Ptolemy's values are those of \emph{Almagest} VI 5, on the limits of the eclipses, which take 5;1 for the moon, so that the excess is 7;24 (Nallino). Schiaparelli remarks that that problem has nothing to do with this correction, and would read «an eighth of a degree» for «an eighth of an hour», as in the preceding paragraph (Nallino's commentary). The neglected correction matters most where the equation of the moon is about 3 parts: at the greatest equation the equation hardly changes, and near the farthest and the nearest distance on the epicycle the elongation is only the equation of the sun (Schiaparelli). With a greatest equation of 5;1 and an equation of the sun of 2 parts, it changes the equation of the moon by at most about 0;7, where the equation is about 3 parts.}\par
\par \ArPage{145}It is clear also that if we divide the excess between the sun and the moon by the gain of the moon taken with the anomaly that holds between the mean and the true conjunction, that is more correct and more exact. You know it by taking half the excess between the sun and the moon and adding to it half its sixth; then subtract that from the corrected anomaly if the excess is the moon's, and add it to it if the excess is the sun's: so we obtain correctly the anomaly of the moon for the middle between the true and the mean conjunction. With it take the course of the moon in an hour, subtract from it the course of the sun, and use the remaining gain of the moon only in dividing the excess.\footnote{Half the excess with a twelfth of that half is the motion of the moon, and so nearly of its anomaly, in half the time between the mean and the true conjunction; the twelfth comes from the motion of the sun (Nallino).}\par
\par If you wish to compute the times in another way, by the method of minutes, which is the method by which the day and night are 60 minutes, take the true equal hours of the conjunction after noon in the city and multiply them by 2½ minutes. If the product is 30 minutes, the conjunction is at midnight; if it is less than that, it is before midnight; and if more, after midnight. Put the minutes that you obtain in the place of degrees, so that each minute of them becomes a degree and each second a minute. Then find the times of the hours of the daytime and of the night. If those degrees are less than the times of the hours of the daytime, the conjunction is in the daytime; divide them by a sixth of the times of the hours of the daytime: the result is seasonal hours after noon. If those degrees are more than the times of the hours of the daytime, up to 30, subtract the times of the hours of the daytime from them and divide the remainder by a sixth of the times of the hours of the night: the result is the seasonal hours that pass from the beginning of the night up to midnight. If they are more than 30, cast 30 from them; if the remainder is less than the times of the hours of the night, divide it by a sixth of the times of the hours of the night: the result is seasonal hours after midnight. If the degrees are more than the times of the hours of the night, subtract the times of the hours of the night from them and divide the remainder by a sixth of the times of the hours of the daytime: the result is seasonal hours that have passed from sunrise of the morrow, if God wills.\footnote{Taken as degrees, the minutes from noon to sunset equal the times of a seasonal hour of the daytime, each being 1¼ times the length of the daytime in equal hours; so the degrees can be compared with those times directly, and a sixth of them is one seasonal hour. The same holds for the night.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 43. On knowing the eclipse of the moon by computation and by the table, and knowing the amounts of the eclipse, its times, and the direction of the darkening and the clearing on the circles of the horizons, which differ for the localities}{43}{\ArPage{146}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the eclipse of the moon, examine the mean motion in latitude at the oppositions. If it is between the eclipse limits set down on the page of the months of the conjunctions and the oppositions, the moon can be eclipsed; if it is more than those amounts or less, it cannot be eclipsed. If it can be eclipsed, look at the corrected motion in latitude at the time of the opposition. If it is exactly 360 degrees, the moon is at the very node of the head, and if it is exactly 180 degrees, the moon is at the very node of the tail. If it is more than either of these two numbers, it has passed the node by the amount of the excess, and if less, it is short of the node by the amount of the shortfall. When the moon is at the very node, the eclipse is the most complete that can be. If its distance from either of the two nodes is more than 12 degrees, before it or behind it, it cannot be eclipsed; if it is less than that, it is eclipsed, and its eclipse is according to its distance from the node and its nearness to it. If the time of the opposition is at night, or near sunrise or sunset, the eclipse is seen whole or in part, according to the time.\footnote{In the codex the eclipse limits stand on the page of the single Egyptian years and on that of the hourly motions (Nallino). At 12 parts from a node the latitude of the moon is 1;2, about the greatest sum of the radii of the moon and the shadow, 0;17,40 + 0;46 = 1;3,40 (chapter 30).}\par
\par \ArPage{147}When you know that it is eclipsed and that the eclipse, or part of it, is seen, enter with the corrected motion in latitude at the time of the opposition into the tables of the equation, take the latitude of the moon and find its side; or, if you wish, find it from the distance of the moon from the node, the meaning being the same in both ways. Keep what results of the latitude of the moon [true for the middle of the eclipse]. Then enter with the corrected anomaly of the moon at the time of the opposition into the tables of the computation and take what is under it in the third column, in which are the shares of the distance. Find what part of 60 the minutes come to, and take the same part of the 5 minutes and a half and a quarter by which the diameter of the moon varies; always add the result to 29 minutes and 30 seconds, the diameter of the moon at its farthest distance: the sum is the corrected diameter of the moon, so keep it. Likewise take, of the 7½ minutes by which the radius of the shadow varies, the part that the minutes of the third column are of 60, and add the result to 38½ minutes, the amount of the [radius] of the shadow at the farthest distance of the moon: the sum is the corrected radius of the shadow.\footnote{The words in brackets are supplied by Nallino, the first after Plato of Tivoli. The diameter of the moon varies from 0;29,30 to 0;35,20 (chapter 30), by 5⅚ minutes; the codex and Plato have 5¾, and the codex has 0;29,40 for 0;29,30 (Nallino). The radius of the shadow grows from 0;38,30 to 0;46 (chapter 30). The text has «the corrected diameter of the shadow»; Nallino translates «radius», as the numbers require.}\par
\par If you wish to know that by computation in another way, take its unequal motion in an hour, multiply it by six less an eighth, and take a sixth of the minutes that result: that is the amount of the corrected diameter of the moon. If you wish to know the corrected radius of the shadow, multiply half the [corrected] diameter of the moon by two and three fifths: the product is the amount of the corrected radius of the shadow.\footnote{Six less an eighth, divided by six, is 0.979, about the ratio of the diameter of the moon to its hourly motion, 0;29,30 to 0;30,12 and 0;35,20 to 0;36,10 (chapter 30). Two and three fifths is the ratio of the diameter of the shadow to that of the moon, 92 to 35⅓ minutes at the nearest distance and 77 to 29½ at the farthest (Nallino, from chapter 30). «Corrected» is supplied by Nallino.}\par
\par When you know the diameter of the moon and the radius of the shadow, in whichever of the two ways you wish, take half the corrected diameter of the moon and add it to the corrected radius of the shadow: the sum is half the two diameters, so keep it. Then look: if the true latitude of the moon is equal to half the two diameters, the moon touches the outermost line of the shadow, and nothing of it is eclipsed. If it is less than half the two diameters, subtract it from half the two diameters and look at the remainder: if it is equal to the diameter of the moon, the moon is eclipsed whole and has no stay; if it is more than the diameter of the moon, it is eclipsed whole and has a stay; if it is less, it is not eclipsed whole. Multiply the minutes that remained to you when you subtracted the latitude of the moon from half the two diameters by 12 and divide the product by the corrected diameter of the moon: the quotient is the amount of the diameter of the moon that is eclipsed, in the measure in which its diameter is 12 parts; these are called the digits of the eclipse, so keep them. If the moon has a stay, subtract the corrected diameter of the moon from those remaining minutes: the remainder is the minutes of the stay. Multiply them also by 12, divide the product by the diameter of the moon, and add the digits that result to the 12 digits that are the whole diameter of the moon: the sum is the digits of the eclipse from the beginning of the eclipse to its middle; keep them. Or, if you wish, multiply those minutes that remain of half the two diameters, whether they are less than the diameter of the moon or more, by 12, and divide the product by the diameter of the moon: the result is the digits of the eclipse.\footnote{Half the two diameters is the sum of the radii of the moon and the shadow. Here the minutes of the stay are the amount by which the eclipse exceeds the diameter of the moon; below they are half the path of the moon in the shadow during the total eclipse, and «the whole stay» is their greatest value, for a central eclipse, half the difference of the diameters of the shadow and the moon (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{148}Then multiply half the two diameters by itself, subtract from the product the latitude of the moon multiplied by itself, and take the root of the remainder: the result is the minutes of the fall and the stay together, if the moon has a stay, and if it has no stay, the minutes of the fall. Whichever of these two it is, divide it by the gain of the moon: the result is the hours of the fall, or the hours of the fall and the stay, as the case may be. Subtract them from the hours of the opposition, which are the hours of the middle of the eclipse: the remainder is the hours of the beginning of the eclipse. Add them also to the hours of the middle of the eclipse: the sum is the equal hours of the end of the clearing. If the moon has a stay, subtract the corrected diameter of the moon from half the two diameters: the remainder is the minutes of the amount of the whole stay. Multiply them by themselves, subtract from the product the latitude of the moon multiplied by itself, take the root of the remainder, and divide it by the gain of the moon: the quotient is the hours of the stay. Subtract them from the middle of the eclipse: the remainder is the hours of the beginning of the stay; and add them also to the hours of the middle of the eclipse: the sum is the hours of the beginning of the clearing.\footnote{The root is the path of the moon relative to the shadow from the contact to the middle of the eclipse, the latitude being taken as constant; the contact is where the distance of the centres is half the two diameters, or, for the stay, the whole stay.}\par
\par If the moon is not eclipsed whole, the eclipse has three times, and likewise if it is eclipsed whole and has no stay; if it has a stay, the eclipse has five times. These times mentioned are given in general terms and are not the most exact truth in the computation, for the latitude of the moon changes between the beginning of the eclipse and its middle, and from its middle to the end of the clearing, so that the amounts of the times on the two sides of the middle of the eclipse change with the change of the latitude of the moon; the middle of the eclipse clearly does not change.\par
\par If you wish to make that exact, so that no flaw enters the computation from it, take the minutes of the fall and the stay, or the minutes of the fall, whichever it is — those that I told you to divide by the gain of the moon — and add to them half their sixth. Subtract the sum from the corrected motion in latitude at the time of the opposition: the remainder is the motion in latitude for the plain beginning of the eclipse, so keep it. Then add those minutes with half their sixth also to the corrected motion in latitude at the time of the opposition: the sum is the motion in latitude for the plain end of the clearing. Find the latitude of the moon at each of the two times from the motion in latitude at it. Then multiply the latitude of the moon at the beginning of the eclipse by itself and subtract it from half the two diameters multiplied by itself; to the remainder add the difference between the latitude of the moon at the beginning of the eclipse and its latitude at the middle of the eclipse, multiplied by itself, and take the root of the sum: the result is the minutes of the fall from the beginning of the eclipse to its middle. Divide them by the gain of the moon and subtract the hours that result from the hours of the opposition: the remainder is the hours of the exact beginning of the eclipse. Then multiply the latitude of the moon at the end of the clearing by itself and subtract it from half the two diameters multiplied by itself; to the remainder add the difference between the latitude of the moon at the middle of the eclipse and its latitude at the end of the clearing, multiplied by itself, and take the root of the result: the result is the minutes of the fall and the stay. Divide them by the gain of the moon and add the result to the hours of the opposition: the sum is the hours of the exact end of the clearing.\footnote{The plain times are those of the general rule above. Half a sixth is added because the moon moves in latitude about 13/12 as fast as it gains on the shadow, the node also moving backwards. At the contact the moon is the root of D² − b² from the middle in longitude, D being half the two diameters and b the latitude at the contact; with the change of the latitude as the other side, the root gives its path to the middle.}\par
\par \ArPage{149}Likewise, if you wish to make exact the time of the beginning of the stay and the time of the beginning of the clearing, you subtract the minutes of the stay that I told you to divide by the gain of the moon, with half their sixth, from the motion in latitude at the time of the opposition, and add them also to the motion in latitude at the time of the opposition, so that you know the motion in latitude for the two times. Then with it you find the latitude of the moon in that way at each of the two times, and subtract it from half the two diameters; you take the excess of the remainder over the diameter of the moon and multiply it by itself, and subtract the product from the total of the minutes of the stay multiplied by itself, and keep what remains to you of each of the two. Then you add to it what lies between the latitude of the moon at the middle of the eclipse and its latitude at that time, take the root of the sum and divide it by the gain of the moon. What results for the time of the beginning you subtract from the hours of the opposition, and what results for the time of the clearing you add to the hours of the opposition: what each of them comes to is the hours of the beginning of the stay and of the beginning of the clearing.\footnote{The passage is corrupt in the codex and in Plato of Tivoli. As Schiaparelli restores it (Nallino's commentary), the latitude of the moon at each of the two times is squared and subtracted from the square of the minutes of the whole stay, half the difference of the diameters of the shadow and the moon; the square of the difference between that latitude and the latitude at the middle of the eclipse is added, and the root of the sum is divided by the gain of the moon.}\par
\par \ArPage{150}If the moon is not eclipsed whole and you wish to count the digits of the eclipse by computation, so as to know the area of the part of the circle of the moon that falls in the circle of the shadow, in the measure in which the area of the circle of the moon is 12 parts — these are what are called the digits — take half the corrected diameter of the moon and subtract from it 0;14,50, the radius of the moon at its farthest distance. Multiply the remainder by six and divide it by the radius of the moon at its farthest distance mentioned; add the digit, or part of a digit, that results to the six digits that are the radius of the moon at its farthest distance: the digits that result are the digits of the corrected radius of the moon, so keep them. Then double that: the result is the digits of the whole diameter of the moon. Multiply them by 3 parts 8½ minutes: that is the measure of the circumference of the moon. Take half of it and multiply it by the digits of the radius of the moon: the result is the area of the circle of the moon, so keep it. Then take the excess of the corrected radius of the shadow over 0;38,30, double it, multiply the result by 12 and divide it by 77, the whole diameter of the shadow at the farthest distance; add the digits that result to 31 digits and a fifth of a digit, the least diameter of the shadow, at the farthest distance of the moon: the sum is the digits of the diameter of the shadow. Multiply them by 3 parts 8½ minutes: the result is the circumference of the circle of the shadow. Take half of it and multiply it by the digits of the radius of the shadow: the result is the area of the circle of the shadow. Then add together the digits of the diameter of the shadow and the digits of the diameter of the moon: the sum is the digits of the two diameters, so keep it. Then multiply the digits of the eclipse by the digits of the diameter of the moon and divide the product by 12: the quotient is the corrected digits of the eclipse. Double them and subtract the result from the digits of the two diameters: the remainder is twice the distance between the two centres. Then subtract the corrected digits of the eclipse from the digits of the diameter of the moon, multiply the remainder by the corrected digits of the eclipse, and divide the product by twice the distance between the centres: the result is the sagitta of the circle of the shadow. Subtract it from the corrected digits of the eclipse: the remainder is the sagitta of the circle of the moon. Subtract that from the digits of the diameter of the moon, multiply the remainder by the sagitta of the circle of the moon, and take the root of the product: that is half the common chord, so keep it.\footnote{0;14,50 is half of 0;29,40, the codex's reading above for the farthest diameter of the moon. With that diameter as 12 digits the 77 minutes of the diameter of the shadow are 31.15 digits, with 0;29,30 they are 31.32; the text has 31⅕. 3;8,30 is Ptolemy's ratio of the circumference to the diameter (\emph{Almagest} VI 7; chapter 3). The corrected digits of the eclipse are the digits on the moon's actual diameter turned into these digits, in which its farthest diameter is 12. With a and c the radii of the shadow and the moon, b the distance of their centres and e the corrected digits, e = a + c − b, the sagitta of the shadow is e(2c − e)/2b, the sagitta of the moon is e less that, and half the common chord is the root of the sagitta of the moon times the diameter of the moon less that sagitta. Schiaparelli, who proves these rules in Nallino's commentary, did not remember finding them in other writers and calls the solution most elegant. The translation reads «\RL{\ArNote التي}» for «\RL{\ArNote الني}» (p. 149, line 12), a misprint that the corrigenda do not list, and follows the corrigenda in reading «\RL{\ArNote وخُمْس}», «and a fifth», for «\RL{\ArNote وحُمْس}» (p. 149, line 17).}\par
\par Then take the corrected digits of the eclipse: if they are less than the digits of the radius of the moon, subtract them from the digits of the radius of the moon, and if they are more, subtract from them the digits of the radius of the moon. What results from the shortfall, add to the sagitta of the shadow; what results from the excess, take the difference between it and the sagitta of the shadow. Multiply what results in either way by half the common chord: the product is the area of the triangle of the moon, so keep it. Then take the digits of the radius of the shadow, subtract the sagitta of the shadow from them, and multiply the remainder by half the common chord: the product is the area of the triangle of the shadow, so keep it. Then multiply half the common chord by six, divide the product by the digits of the radius of the moon, and multiply the result by 10 parts, so that it becomes its share of the radius; find its arc from the table of the halved chords. Multiply the arc that results by the digits of a [quarter] of the circumference of the circle of the moon: the result is the share of the arc. Divide it by 90: the result is the arc of the moon. Multiply it by the digits of the radius of the moon: the result is the area of the arc of the moon; keep it in mind. Then take half the common chord again and multiply it by 15 parts and three fifths of a part, which are the digits of the least radius of the shadow; divide the product by the digits of the radius of the shadow, and multiply the result by 3 parts 50 minutes and a half and a quarter of a minute and a [fifth] of a tenth of a minute, so that it becomes its share of the radius; find its arc in the tables of the halved chords. Multiply the arc that results by a quarter of the circumference of the circle of the shadow and divide by 90: the result is the arc of the shadow. Multiply it by the digits of the radius of the shadow: the result is the area of the arc of the shadow. Add it to the area of the arc of the moon, and subtract from the sum the area of the triangle of the moon together with the area of the triangle of the shadow: the remainder is the area of the eclipsed segment of the circle of the moon. Multiply it by 12 and divide by the area of the circle of the moon that you kept at the beginning: the digits that result are the amount of the circle of the moon that is eclipsed, in the measure in which its whole area is 12.\footnote{Where the text has «the sagitta of the shadow» in this paragraph, the codex and Plato of Tivoli have «the circle of the moon»; Nallino corrects them, and Schiaparelli's proof confirms the correction. «Quarter» is supplied by Nallino after Plato, and «fifth» is Nallino's conjecture for a word eaten by worms: 60/15⅗ = 3;50,46 (Nallino). Half the common chord over the radius, in a radius of 60, is the sine of half the angle of the eclipsed arc at the centre; the arc so found, as a length, times the radius is the area of the whole sector, and the sector less the triangle is the segment, for the moon and for the shadow alike (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary). As given, the rule is exact while the common chord lies between the centres of the moon and the shadow. In a deeper eclipse, where the excess of the corrected digits over the radius of the moon is greater than the sagitta of the shadow, the eclipsed segment of the moon is more than half its disc: the table of the halved chords then gives the smaller arc where the larger is needed, and the triangle of the moon has to be added, not subtracted, so that the rule gives too little.}\par
\par \ArPage{151}If you wish to know the azimuth of the side from which the darkening begins on the circle of the moon, and the side from which it clears, on the circle of the horizon, and the figure of the eclipse, set up the ascendant of each of the times of the eclipse and find the azimuth of the ascendant of each of them on the circle of the horizon in the manner given at the beginning of the book. Then take the latitude of the moon at the time of the beginning of the eclipse and at the time of the end of the clearing, if the moon is not eclipsed whole; if the moon is eclipsed whole and has a stay, take also its latitude at the beginning of the clearing. Multiply each of the latitudes that you have by the radius and keep the products. Divide what results for the beginning of the stay and the beginning of the clearing by all the minutes of the whole stay, and what results for the beginning of the eclipse and the end of the clearing by half the two diameters: what results is degrees. Find their arc in the table of the halved chords: the arc for each of those times is the amount of the deviation of the darkening of the eclipse at that time; keep each of them with its side. If the true centre of the moon is on the belt of the signs, I mean that it has no latitude, at one of the times: if that is at the beginning of the eclipse or the beginning of the clearing, the beginning of the darkening and the beginning of the clearing are on the side of the azimuth of the ascendant at each of the two times; if it is at the beginning of the stay or the end of the clearing, both are on the side of the azimuth of the setting degree at each of the two times.\footnote{The azimuth of the ascendant is found as in chapter 7; such azimuths are amplitudes, counted from the east and the west points (Nallino). The latitude times the radius, divided by the distance of the centres at the contact, is the sine of the angle that the line of the centres makes with the ecliptic; the darkening begins, or the clearing ends, where that line meets the edge of the moon.}\par
\par If the moon is not on the belt of the signs but has a latitude on one of the two sides, lay off the deviation of the eclipse at the time of the beginning of the eclipse and of the end of the clearing: at the time of the beginning of the eclipse, from the limit of the azimuth of the ascendant at the beginning of the eclipse on the circle of the horizon, towards the side opposite to the latitude of the moon; at the time of the end of the clearing, from the limit of the azimuth of its setting degree, again towards the side opposite to the latitude of the moon. At the time of the beginning of the clearing and the time of the beginning of the stay, you lay off the deviation of the darkening of the eclipse at the time of the beginning of the clearing from the limit of the azimuth of the degree that rises at it, towards the side of the latitude of the moon, and likewise you lay off the deviation at the time of the beginning of the stay from the limit of the azimuth of the degree that sets at it, towards the side of the latitude of the moon. Where the count ends for you on the circle of the horizon at each of the times, towards the azimuth of that part of the circle of the horizon is the deviation of the darkening and of the clearing on the circle of the moon.\par
\par \ArPage{152}If the moon is not eclipsed whole, the deviation of the darkening at the middle of the eclipse always falls at a right angle to the ecliptic, where it is bounded by the arc that passes through the two poles of the ecliptic, through the place of the moon and through the circle of the horizon. You know it by taking the angle of longitude known for the time of the middle of the eclipse, in the way that we described in finding it for knowing the parallax of the moon, and laying it off from the limit of the azimuth of the ascendant at the middle of the eclipse towards the side opposite to the latitude of the moon, when the moon is towards the east; when it is towards the west, you lay it off from the limit of the azimuth of the setting degree at the middle of the eclipse, again towards the side opposite to the latitude of the moon. Where the count ends for you on the circle of the horizon, to that part of it runs the inclination of the azimuth of the darkening at the middle of the eclipse. This is when the latitude of the moon is to the north; when it is to the south and the moon is on the eastern side, you lay off the angle from the azimuth of the setting degree, and when it is on the western side, from the azimuth of the ascendant, towards the side opposite to the latitude of the moon.\footnote{The angle of longitude is the one found for the parallax in chapter 39. At the middle of the eclipse the centres of the moon and the shadow differ only in latitude, so that the line of the centres is at right angles to the ecliptic.}\par
\par If you wish to know the eclipse of the moon approximately by the table, enter with the true latitude of the moon at the time of the opposition into the two tables of the eclipse of the moon, for the farthest distance and for the nearest distance. If you find it in the table of the nearest distance alone and not in that of the farthest, take what stands opposite it of the digits and the minutes of the fall, and take of each of them the part that the minutes of the shares of the distance, set down in the third column of the tables of the computation opposite the corrected anomaly of the moon at the time of the opposition, are of 60 minutes: what results of each of them is the amount of the digits of the eclipse and the amount of the fall. If the latitude of the moon falls in both tables, take what stands opposite it in each of them of the digits, the fall and the stay, if the moon has a stay, and write down what comes from each of the two tables on its own side. Take the difference between the two tables in the digits, the fall and the stay, and take of each of these differences the part that the minutes of the third column of the tables of the computation, opposite the anomaly of the moon, are of 60 minutes; always add what results from each of them to its counterpart from the first table, that of the farthest distance. What the digits of the first table and the minutes of the fall and the stay in it come to after the addition is the digits of the eclipse of the diameter of the moon, the amount of the fall, and the amount of the stay, if you find a stay for the moon. If these digits are less than 12, the moon is not eclipsed whole, and you will find no stay for it then; if they are more than 12, the moon is eclipsed whole and has a stay according to how far it enters the darkness; and if the digits are exactly 12, the moon is just eclipsed whole.\par
\par \ArPage{153}Then divide the minutes of the fall by the gain of the moon, and likewise the minutes of the stay, if the moon has a stay: the results are the hours of the fall and the hours of the stay, if the moon has a stay. If you find no stay for it, subtract the hours of the fall from the hours of the opposition: the remainder is the hours of the beginning of the eclipse; and add them also to the hours of the opposition: the sum is the hours of the end of the clearing. The hours of the middle of the eclipse are the hours of the opposition. If the moon has a stay, add the hours of the fall and of the stay together and subtract the sum from the hours of the opposition: the remainder is the hours of the beginning of the eclipse; add it also to the hours of the opposition: the sum is the hours of the end of the clearing. Then subtract the hours of the stay alone from the hours of the opposition: the remainder is the hours of the beginning of the stay; and add them also to the hours of the opposition: the sum is the hours of the beginning of the clearing, approximately. If the digits of the eclipse are less than 12, enter with them into the table of the amounts of the eclipses, in the column of numbers, and take what is under them in the second column, headed «amounts of the eclipse of the moon»: what there is, is the amount of the circle of the moon that is eclipsed, in the measure in which its area is 12.\par
\par If you wish to know the side from which the darkening of the eclipse begins and the side from which the clearing ends, enter with the digits of the eclipse, those of the diameter of the moon, in the column of the number of the digits of the tables of the deviations of the darkenings, and take what stands opposite them in the third column, and also in the fourth column if the moon has a stay. What results from the third column is the deviation at the time of the beginning of the eclipse and of the end of the clearing, and what results from the fourth column is the deviation at the time of the stay and of the beginning of the clearing; keep it. Then look in the circle of the azimuths, in which the seven circles for the seven climes are drawn, and take the azimuth set down under the rising and the setting sign in the clime in question and under the sign that follows it. Then take the difference between the azimuths of the two signs, multiply it by the degrees of the ascendant, and divide the product by 30; add the result to the azimuth of the rising sign if that is the smaller, and subtract it if that is the greater. What the azimuth of the ascendant or of the setting point comes to after the addition or the subtraction is the azimuth of the degree of the ascendant at each time and of its setting degree. Know that the azimuth of the setting point is like the azimuth of the ascendant, on the opposite side: if the azimuth of the ascendant is northern, the azimuth of the setting point is southern, so whichever of the two azimuths you know, you know the other by it. The side of the azimuth is known from the drawing of the circle, by the marked directions on which the summer and the winter risings and settings fall, the summer ones being northern and the winter ones southern.\footnote{The circle of the azimuths, with seven circles for the seven climes, is a diagram in the codex (Nallino); its azimuths are amplitudes, counted from the east and the west points (chapter 7). The azimuth of a degree between the tabulated signs is found by proportion.}\par
\par \ArPage{154}When you know that, lay off the parts of the third column from the limit of the azimuth of the ascendant at the beginning of the eclipse towards the side opposite to the latitude, I mean the latitude of the moon, and lay them off also from the limit of the azimuth of the setting point at the end of the clearing towards the side opposite to the latitude of the moon. If the moon has a stay, you lay off the parts of the fourth column from the limit of the azimuth of the setting point at the beginning of the stay and from the limit of the azimuth of the degree that rises at the beginning of the clearing, towards the side of the latitude of the moon. Where the count ends for you on the circle of the horizon is the azimuth of the darkening and of the clearing that occur on the circle of the moon. This is the figure of the eclipse in its position, with the directions of the darkening and the clearing in it, if God wills.\par
\par\begin{center}\begin{tikzpicture}\draw[line width=1.27pt] (130.37pt,128.70pt) circle[radius=110.62pt];
