\node[inner sep=0pt] at (130.74pt,252.30pt) {\scriptsize South};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (133.38pt,232.50pt) {\normalsize A};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (138.78pt,202.44pt) {\normalsize Ḍ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (48.66pt,160.50pt) {\normalsize M};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (135.60pt,162.78pt) {\normalsize L};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (134.70pt,146.64pt) {\normalsize H};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (78.18pt,143.52pt) {\normalsize Ḥ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (135.84pt,137.04pt) {\normalsize Ṭ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (220.32pt,141.12pt) {\normalsize K};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (185.46pt,131.88pt) {\normalsize S};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (0.00pt,125.70pt) {\scriptsize East};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (28.20pt,135.66pt) {\normalsize B};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (237.00pt,123.60pt) {\normalsize D};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (260.82pt,124.26pt) {\scriptsize West};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (134.64pt,119.52pt) {\normalsize Z};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (29.76pt,108.96pt) {\normalsize Q};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (226.08pt,92.82pt) {\normalsize LA};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (123.18pt,26.46pt) {\normalsize G};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (130.26pt,0.00pt) {\scriptsize North};\end{tikzpicture}\end{center}
\par \ArPage{155}Al-Battānī said: We begin, with the help of God, by drawing a straight line and dividing it into as many equal parts as you wish, provided they are as many as the number of half the two diameters or more. Then take from this line the amount of half the two diameters and draw a circle with it: this is the circle of half the two diameters, on which the centre of the moon falls at the time of the beginning and at the end of the clearing. Then take also the amount of the radius of the shadow and draw with it, about the centre of the first circle, a circle that falls inside that circle: this is the circle of the shadow. Then divide the two circles into equal quarters, and write on the ends of the lines the directions of the east, the west, the north and the south. Then take from the divided line, with the compasses, the amount of the latitude of the moon at the middle of the eclipse; put one of the two ends on the centre of the two circles and turn the other end towards the side of the latitude of the moon. Where it falls on the line of the north or of the south, mark a sign on the line, which is the centre of the moon at the middle of the eclipse. Then take from that line also the amount of the latitude of the moon at the beginning of the eclipse, do the same with it, and mark a second sign at its place on the line, on the side of the latitude of the moon; do the same with the latitude of the moon at the end of the clearing and mark another sign at its place on the line, on the side of the latitude. Then from the two signs of the latitude of the moon at the beginning of the eclipse and at the end of the clearing draw lines parallel to the line between the east and the west: the one for the beginning of the eclipse you draw towards the west from the centre of the circle, and let the one for the end of the clearing be drawn from the centre of the circle towards the east. Mark two signs where the two lines meet the circumference of the circle of half the two diameters, and join them by a straight line, which passes through the centre of the moon at the middle of the eclipse: on that line is the passage of the moon from the beginning of the eclipse to the end of the clearing. The line from the western circumference of the circle to the sign of the latitude of the moon at the middle of the eclipse is the amount of the minutes of the fall and the stay from the beginning of the eclipse to its middle, and the line that remains, from that sign to the eastern point of the circle, is the amount of the minutes of the fall and the stay from the middle of the eclipse to the end of the clearing; necessarily each of the two lines differs from the other in amount.\footnote{The translation follows the corrigenda in reading «\RL{\ArNote القطرَيْن}», «the two diameters», for «\RL{\ArNote القطربْن}» (p. 154, line 10).}\par
\par Then take from the divided line the radius of the moon as well, and draw with it three circles. The centre of one of them is the western point of the circle of half the two diameters, where the parallel line meets it, and the centre of the second circle the eastern point of the circle. These two circles each necessarily touch the circle of the shadow: the one about the western centre is the circle of the moon at the beginning of the eclipse, and the one about the eastern centre is its circle at the end of the clearing. As for the circle whose centre is the sign of the latitude of the moon at the middle of the eclipse: if it falls wholly within the circle of the shadow, the moon is eclipsed whole and stays in the darkness according to what lies between its circle and the circle of the shadow; if the circle of the moon is inside the circle of the shadow and touches it, the moon is eclipsed whole and has no stay; and if the circle of the moon does not fall wholly within the circle of the shadow, as much of the circle of the moon is eclipsed as the circle of the shadow cuts off from it, which is the segment that falls in the circle of the shadow, whose diameter and area are known.\par
\par \ArPage{156}Let the example of this be the circle of the two diameters, on which are K and M, about the centre Z, and the circle of the shadow inside it, on which are S and Ḥ, whose centre is also the point Z. Since we wish to show the azimuth of the deviation of the darkenings and of the clearing on the circle of the horizon, we draw also about the centre Z a third, great circle, with the circle of half the two diameters inside it; let this circle be for the horizon, and mark on it A, B, G, D. We divide the three circles into quarters by two lines that intersect at the centre Z at right angles, the diameters AG and BD; let the sign A be the point of the south, the sign G the point of the north, the sign B the point of the east and the sign D the point of the west. We take the latitude of the moon to the south, and mark on its latitude at the beginning of the eclipse the point Ṭ, on its latitude at the middle of the eclipse the point H, and on its latitude at the end of the clearing the point L. We draw the two lines ṬK and LM parallel to the diameter BD, and join the point K to the point M by a line that passes through the point H. So the point K is the centre of the circle of the moon at the beginning of the eclipse and the point M the centre of its circle at the end of the clearing, and the line KHM passes through the three centres of the circles of the moon; on it is the passage of the moon from the beginning of the eclipse to the end of the clearing. It is clear that the circle whose centre is K touches the circle of the shadow at the sign S, and the one whose centre is M touches the circle of the shadow at the point Ḥ. Therefore, when the line M Ḥ Z LA and the line KSZQ are drawn, the line KSZQ is the azimuth of the beginning of the eclipse on the circle ABGD, bounded by the arc BQ, and the line M Ḥ Z LA the azimuth of the end of the clearing on the circle ABGD, bounded by the arc D LA. It is known that the point D is the azimuth of the setting degree and the point B the azimuth of the ascendant at every time. Since each of the triangles ZṬK and ZLM is right-angled, and each of the lines ZK and ZM is the known amount of half the two diameters, and each of the lines ZṬ and ZL is also known, each of the lines ṬK and LM, the remaining sides of the two triangles, is known as well. Again, since the angles of the triangles MLH and KṬH at L and Ṭ are right, and each of the lines LH and ṬH is of known amount, each of the lines KH and HM is of known amount: they are the amount of the fall and the stay, KH from the beginning of the eclipse to its middle and HM from the middle of the eclipse to the end of the clearing. It is clear in this figure, mentioned above, that the line KH is greater than the line HM. That is what we wished to show.\footnote{The translation reads «at the beginning of the eclipse» for K with Nallino's translation, as the construction requires; the text has «at the middle of the eclipse».}\par
\par It is clear also that if the circle of the moon whose centre is H, for the middle of the eclipse, enters the circle of the shadow and does not touch it on any side, the moon is eclipsed whole and enters the darkness by the amount of the stay, according to what lies between the two circles; and that the azimuth of the darkening at the middle of the eclipse, if the moon is not eclipsed whole, is always on the line ZḌ, which is at right angles to the ecliptic. For the line BD is always the line of half the ecliptic, since the point B is the point of the east from which the ascendant rises and the point D the point of the west from which the setting degree is known on the circle of the horizon; wherever [the line ZḌ] falls on it, by its distance from the point of the east or of the west, to that point the azimuth of the darkening at the middle of the eclipse inclines.\footnote{The bracketed words follow Nallino's translation.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 44. On knowing the eclipse of the sun, its amounts and its times in every locality, and the directions of its darkening and of its clearing, by computation and by the table}{44}{\ArPage{157}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: If you wish to compute the eclipse of the sun, examine the conjunctions of the sun and the moon. If the mean motion in latitude falls in them between the eclipse limits set down for the sun at the top of the page of the months of the conjunction and the full moon, the sun can be eclipsed; if it is more than that or less, it cannot be eclipsed in any of the climes. If you know that it can be eclipsed, examine the time of the conjunction, whether it falls in the daytime or near sunrise or sunset, so as to know, if an eclipse occurs, whether it can be seen whole or in part.\footnote{In the codex the limits stand on the same pages as those of the moon (Nallino; chapter 43).}\par
\par \ArPage{158}When you know that something of it is possible, find the true equal hours of the conjunction after noon in the locality that you want, and set up the ascendant and the middle of the sky on the ecliptic at that time. Then find the parallax of the moon in longitude only, by the computation of the angles and of the arcs of distance from the zenith on the circle of altitude, as I have explained at the beginning of the book, from the parallax on the circle of altitude less the parallax of the sun, found in that manner. Divide the result by the unequal motion of the moon in an hour: the result is the hours of the first parallax. If the distance of the degree of the conjunction from the ascendant is less than 90, the moon is in the eastern quarter of the sphere: subtract the hours of the first parallax from the hours of the conjunction, and the minutes of the parallax from the position of the moon and its anomaly at the time of the conjunction. If the distance of the degree of the conjunction from the ascendant is more than 90, the moon is in the western quarter: add the hours of the first parallax to the hours of the conjunction, and the minutes of the parallax to the position of the moon and its anomaly. With what the hours of the conjunction come to after the addition or the subtraction, find the ascendant again, and find the parallax of the moon in longitude again in the same way, with the second position of the moon and its anomaly. Divide what you obtain of this second parallax by the gain of the moon on the sun in that hour: the hour or part of an hour that results is the hours of the second parallax. Subtract them from the hours of the true conjunction if the distance of the moon from this second ascendant is less than 90, and subtract the minutes of the second parallax from the position of the moon and its anomaly; if the distance of the moon from this second ascendant is more than 90, add the hours of the second parallax to the hours of the true conjunction, and the minutes of the second parallax to the position of the moon and its anomaly. By «the position of the moon and its anomaly» I mean its position at the time of the true conjunction: so cancel what you had written down on account of the first parallax.\footnote{The parallax in longitude is found as in chapter 39. Between the ascendant and the point 90 parts from it the parallax increases the longitude of the moon, so that the visible conjunction comes before the true one; beyond that point it comes after.}\par
\par With the hours of the second conjunction that result, I mean these last ones, find the ascendant and the middle of the sky as usual, and with it and with the position of the moon and its anomaly find the parallax in longitude a third time in the very same way. If this third parallax is exactly equal to the second, the hours that you obtained for the conjunction with the hours of the second parallax are the hours of the middle of the eclipse, for the amount of the parallax in longitude is then equal to the minutes between the sun and the moon at that time, without excess or shortfall. If the third parallax is more than the second, the parallax at that time is more than the minutes between the sun and the moon at it by the excess of the third parallax over the second; if the third parallax is less than the second, you know that the parallax at that time is less than the minutes between the sun and the moon at it by the amount by which the third parallax falls short of the second. Therefore the time must be found at which the amount between the sun and the moon is equal to the parallax of the moon in longitude at it, so that it is the middle of the visible eclipse. Finding it, and the way of knowing it, are as I describe.\footnote{Nallino prints the two sentences on the third parallax being more or less than the second in italics, and Schiaparelli calls them false: that the difference of the two parallaxes and the difference of the visible longitudes agree when both are nothing does not make them agree when they are something (Nallino's commentary). The sentences are correct. The hours of the second parallax are the second parallax divided by the gain of the moon, so at the new time the true moon is exactly the second parallax away from the sun; the parallax at that time is the third, and it exceeds the minutes between the sun and the moon by the excess of the third over the second, the gain being taken as uniform over those hours. Schiaparelli's objection therefore fails, and with it Schiaparelli's claim that al-Battānī divides the wrong quantity by the corrected motion below. In a numerical test with a smoothly changing parallax, the rules bring the time within about a second of the visible conjunction, where the second parallax alone leaves an error of minutes.}\par
\par \ArPage{159}Look: if the parallax is more than the second parallax, subtract from the hours that you have obtained an amount that does not take you out of the whole hour. That is: if the hours that you have obtained, with which you found the third parallax, have a fraction with them, and it is more than a sixth of an hour, subtract a sixth of an hour from them; if it is less than a sixth of an hour, subtract an eighth or a tenth of an hour from them, as is possible, and as does not require you to break an hour from the hours. Use this subtraction if the distance of the moon from the ascendant is then less than 90; if the distance of the moon from the ascendant is more than 90, use addition instead of subtraction on the same condition, namely that what you add to those hours does not make up a whole hour: if the fraction is less than a half and a third, add a sixth of an hour to it, and if it is more than a half and a third, add an eighth of an hour, an amount that does not complete an hour. I have told you this only for finding it from the tables of Theon, which are set up for the climes, so that the difference of the parallax does not go outside that hour. If you work it with the arcs and the angles, you use only the addition of a sixth of an hour and the subtraction of a sixth of an hour. Then find the parallax in longitude with the hours that result after the addition or the subtraction of the sixth. Of the parallax that you obtain at whichever of the two times you use, see what exceeds the third parallax, and multiply it by six if you worked it with the angles, and likewise with the tables of Theon if you used a sixth of an hour in the addition or the subtraction; if you used an eighth of an hour, multiply it by eight, and likewise if you used a tenth of an hour, multiply it by ten, so that what results is the amount for an equal hour. When you have done that, subtract it from the gain of the moon on the sun in that hour: the remainder is the corrected motion. Divide by it the excess of the third parallax over the second: the result is a part of an hour. Add it to the hours of the parallax found with the gain of the moon: the sum is the corrected hours of the second parallax; keep them.\footnote{Theon's tables give the parallax for whole hours from noon in each clime; with them a fraction below a sixth is taken off, an eighth or a tenth, so that the second time falls on a whole hour of the table (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary). Over so short a time the change of the parallax can be taken as uniform; the corrected motion, the gain less the change of the parallax in an hour, is the gain of the visible moon on the sun (Schiaparelli).}\par
\par If the third parallax is less than the second, you work the other way round from what you did at first: add a sixth of an hour to the hours that you have obtained if the distance of the moon from the ascendant is less than 90, and subtract a sixth of an hour if the distance of the moon from the ascendant is more than 90. With the hours that result, find the parallax in longitude at the time that you obtain of the two times, in that way; then see the shortfall relative to the third parallax and multiply it by six; if you used less than the sixth, such as the eighth or the tenth, make the multiplier accordingly, so that it comes to a whole hour. Add the product to the gain of the moon on the sun at that time: the sum is the corrected motion. Divide by it what the third parallax falls short of the second: the quotient is a part of an hour; subtract it from the hours of the second parallax — and say that this does not happen except when the moon is near the horizon, the difference between the two parallaxes being small then. The remainder is the corrected hours of the second parallax.\par
\par \ArPage{160}When you know the corrected hours of the second parallax, multiply them by the unequal course of the moon in an hour and by the unequal course of the sun in an hour, and find the amount of each product. If the distance of the moon from the ascendant is less than 90, subtract the corrected hours of the second parallax from the hours of the true conjunction; subtract what results of the course of the moon from the position of the moon at the time of the true conjunction, from the anomaly of the moon and from the motion in latitude as well, and subtract also from the motion in latitude the motion of the northern node in the corrected hours of the second parallax; and subtract what results for the sun from the position of the sun — the position of the sun and of the moon is, as is known, the position of the conjunction. If the distance of the moon from the ascendant is more than 90, use addition instead of subtraction in everything that I have described to you. What the hours of the true conjunction come to after the addition or the subtraction are the hours of the middle of the visible eclipse, with the positions of the sun and the moon, the anomaly of the moon and the motion in latitude at it. Likewise, if the third parallax is exactly equal to the second, you multiply the hours of the second parallax, which have then become the corrected ones, by the course of the moon, of the sun and of the northern node in an hour, and add the products to the positions of the sun and the moon, the anomaly of the moon and the motion in latitude if you added the hours to the hours of the conjunction, and subtract them from these positions if you subtracted those hours from the hours of the conjunction; you use the motion of the node in the motion in latitude only and in nothing else, so that the position of each of them comes out right in truth. Necessarily, the amount of the parallax in longitude that you obtain at that time is then equal to what lies between the sun and the moon. It is known also that if the distance of the moon from the ascendant at the time of the true conjunction is 90, the hours of the true conjunction are the hours of the middle of the eclipse without difference, and likewise the position of the conjunction is the visible position of the moon and the position of the sun together.\footnote{The motion of the node goes with the moon's in the motion in latitude, because that is counted from the northern node, which moves backwards.}\par
\par \ArPage{161}Then set up the ascendant for the middle of the eclipse, and with it and with the position of the moon at it find the parallax of the moon in latitude in that way, which is the procedure given before; then the true latitude of the moon, from the corrected motion in latitude for the middle of the eclipse, in that way, either by the table or by computation. Find the side of this true latitude and the side of the parallax in latitude. If the true latitude of the moon and its parallax in latitude are on one side, add them together; if they differ, subtract the smaller from the greater and find the side of the remainder. What results after the addition or the subtraction is the visible latitude of the moon on the side on which it falls at the middle of the eclipse. If it is more than 0;34,30, the sun is not eclipsed; if it is less, it is eclipsed, though it may not be eclipsed until the visible latitude of the moon comes down to 0;30,25; if it is less than that, the eclipse is certain. The doubt arises only between 0;34,30 and 0;30,25, because of what the radii of the sun and the moon can come to together at their distances from the centre of the earth.\footnote{The translation reads «in latitude» with Nallino's translation, as the sense requires; the text has «in longitude» (p. 160, line 19). 0;34,30 and 0;30,25 are the greatest and the least sum of the radii of the sun and the moon, half of 0;33,40 + 0;35,20 and of 0;31,20 + 0;29,30 (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary); the codex has 0;36,39 and 0;34,36.}\par
\par When you know that the sun is eclipsed, or that it can be eclipsed, take the anomaly of the moon for the middle of the eclipse, enter with it in the two columns of numbers of the tables of the computation, and find the minutes of the third column that are under it. Find what part of 60 the result is, and take that part of the 2 minutes and a quarter of a minute by which the diameter of the sun, relative to the moon, varies between the farthest and the nearest distance of the sun; add the result to 0;31,20, the diameter of the sun at its farthest distance: the sum is the corrected diameter of the sun. [If you wish to know that by computation, you should correct the arcs for the degree of the moon from the zenith, and the angles too, with the latitude of the moon in that way, for that is more correct for the computation.] When you know the diameter of the sun in whichever of the two ways you wish, find the diameter of the moon in the way given for the eclipse of the moon. Then add together the corrected diameter of the sun and the corrected diameter of the moon and take half the sum: the result is half the two diameters; keep it. If the visible latitude of the moon is equal to half the two diameters or more than it, the sun is not eclipsed; if it is less than half the two diameters, it is eclipsed. When you know that it is certainly eclipsed, subtract the visible latitude of the moon from half the two diameters: the remainder is the amount of the diameter of the sun that is eclipsed. Multiply it by 12 and divide the product by the corrected diameter of the sun: the quotient is the digits of the eclipse by which the sun is eclipsed, in the measure in which its whole diameter is 12 parts; they are called the digits.\footnote{The translation follows Nallino's emendanda (Part II, p. XXXI), with Plato of Tivoli, in reading «\RL{\ArNote القمر}», «the moon», for «\RL{\ArNote الشمس}», «the sun» (p. 161, line 3). The diameter of the sun varies from 0;31,20 to 0;33,40 (chapter 30), by 2⅓ minutes; «a quarter» is perhaps an error of the archetype for «a third» (Nallino). The bracketed passage, which Nallino prints in brackets, has nothing to do with the diameter of the sun; Schiaparelli takes it for an unskilled substitute for a lost rule, perhaps one finding the diameter of the sun from its hourly motion, since al-Battānī makes the two proportional in chapter 30 (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{162}Then multiply the visible latitude of the moon by itself and subtract the product from half the two diameters, also multiplied by itself; take the root of the remainder: the result is the plain minutes of the fall. Divide them by the gain of the moon in an hour: the quotient is the hours of the fall. Subtract them from the hours of the middle of the eclipse: the remainder is the hours of the plain beginning of the eclipse; and add them also to the plain hours of the middle of the eclipse: the sum is the hours of the plain end of the clearing. Then find the motion of the sun and the moon in the hours of the fall by multiplying the hours of the fall by the course of the sun and of the moon in an hour, as usual, and keep what results for each of them. Then subtract what results for the sun from the true position of the sun for the middle of the eclipse, and what results for the moon from the true position of the moon for the middle of the eclipse, from the anomaly of the moon and from the motion in latitude; and add it also to these positions mentioned for the middle of the eclipse, using in the motion in latitude alone, besides, the addition and the subtraction of the course of the node in the hours of the fall. The diminished positions are their positions for the plain beginning, and the increased positions their positions at the plain clearing. Then find the true latitude of the moon at each of the two times from the motion in latitude at it, and find the side of the latitude. Then set up the ascendant of each of the two times, and find the parallax of the moon at it in longitude and in latitude in the very same ways, so that the visible position of the moon in longitude and in latitude comes out right for you as usual. Then multiply the visible latitude of the moon at each of the two times by itself, subtract it from half the two diameters multiplied by itself, and take the root of the remainder: that is the minutes of the fall for each of the two times. Write down the minutes of the fall of each time apart, with its side, and find the excess between the sun and the moon, with the visible position of the moon and the true position of the sun, at each of the two times.\par
\par If the minutes of the fall that result for the time of the plain beginning are equal to the minutes between the sun and the moon at it, the time of the plain beginning is the time of the actual visible beginning; and if the minutes of the fall for the plain clearing are also equal to the minutes between the sun and the moon at it, it goes the same way, without difference. If that differs at one of the two times or at both, the time of the actual visible beginning is not the same as the time of the plain beginning, nor is the time of the clearing either. That happens only because of the parallax of the moon in longitude and its changes at those times. The correction of the computation is as I describe. Look at the plain beginning and the plain clearing that you have found before, the positions of the moon at them corrected by its parallax in longitude, which are its visible positions, and the parallax of the moon in longitude that you obtained at them, and set these down as they are.\par
\par \ArPage{163}Then look at the time of the plain beginning. If the minutes between the sun and the moon at it are less than the minutes of the fall of the plain beginning, you know that the moon covered the sun before the time of the plain beginning. So subtract a sixth of an hour from the hours of the plain beginning, then set up the ascendant and find the parallax of the moon in longitude in that way. If this parallax is more than the parallax of the plain beginning, take the excess and multiply it by six, so that it comes to a whole hour, and subtract that from the gain of the moon; if it is less, multiply the shortfall by six and add it to the gain of the moon. What the gain of the moon comes to is the corrected motion. Divide by this corrected motion the difference between the minutes between the sun and the moon at the time of the plain beginning and its minutes of the fall: the result is a part of an hour; subtract it from the hours of the plain beginning. If the minutes between the sun and the moon are more than the minutes of the fall, the moon has not reached the place at which it can cover anything of the sun: add a sixth of an hour to the hours of the plain beginning and find the parallax of the moon in longitude in that manner. If this parallax is more than the parallax of the plain beginning, take the excess, multiply it by six and add it to the gain of the moon; if it is less, multiply the shortfall by six and subtract the product from the gain of the moon. What the gain of the moon comes to after that is the corrected motion. Divide by this corrected motion the difference between the minutes between the sun and the moon and the minutes of the fall: the result is a part of an hour; add it to the hours of the plain beginning. What they come to after the addition or the subtraction is the hours of the visible beginning.\par
\par \ArPage{164}At the time of the plain clearing, if the minutes between the sun and the moon at it are more than its minutes of the fall, you know that the moon has passed the place at which it covers the sun: so subtract a sixth of an hour from the hours of the plain clearing, and find the parallax of the moon in longitude in that manner. If this parallax is more than the parallax of the time of the plain clearing, take the excess, multiply it by six and add it to the gain of the moon; if it is less, take the shortfall, multiply it by six and subtract it from the gain of the moon. What results after that is the corrected motion. Divide by this corrected motion the difference between the minutes between the sun and the moon and the minutes of the fall of the plain clearing: the quotient is a part of an hour; subtract it from the hours of the plain clearing. If the minutes between the sun and the moon at it are less than the minutes of the fall, you know that the moon has not reached the place at which it leaves the sun, and it cannot cover the sun there: so add a sixth of an hour to the hours of the plain clearing, and find with it the parallax of the moon in longitude in the manner given before. If this parallax is more than the parallax of the plain clearing, take the excess, multiply it by six and subtract it from the gain of the moon; if it is less, take the shortfall, multiply it by six and add it to the gain of the moon. What results after the addition or the subtraction is the corrected motion. Divide by this corrected motion the difference between the minutes between the sun and the moon and the minutes of the fall: the result is a part of an hour; add it to the hours of the plain clearing. What they come to after the addition or the subtraction is the hours of the actual visible clearing.\par
\par When you know these two visible times, I mean the time of the beginning and the time of the clearing, multiply the part of an hour that resulted for the time of the corrected beginning by the unequal course of the moon in an hour, and add the product to the position of the moon for the time of the plain beginning if the time of the visible beginning is after the plain time, and subtract it if it is before; do the same in the motion in latitude. Likewise multiply the part of an hour that resulted for the clearing by the course of the moon and add the product to the place of the moon and to the motion in latitude at the time of the plain clearing if the time of the visible clearing is after the time of the plain clearing, and subtract it from them if it is before. What the position of the moon and the motion in latitude come to after the addition or the subtraction at each of the two times is its position at it. Set up the ascendant and find the parallax of the moon in latitude then in that way, and find also the latitude of the moon at each of the two times and its side, so as to know the visible latitude of the moon at each of the two times in that manner; keep it.\par
\par If you wish to correct the digits of the eclipse, so as to know the amount of the circle of the sun that is eclipsed to the sight when its whole area is 12, the way of doing it is to make the diameter of the sun always 12 digits, whether its diameter is small or large, and to multiply it by 3 parts 8½ minutes: the product is the circumference of the circle of the sun, which is 37 digits and 42 minutes of a digit. Take half these digits, which is 18;51, and multiply it by the digits of the radius of the sun, which are six: the area of the circle of the sun comes to 113;6. Then take half the corrected diameter of the moon, multiply it by six and divide it by half the corrected diameter of the sun: the quotient is the digits of the radius of the moon; keep them. Then double them: the result is the digits of the whole diameter of the moon. Multiply them by 3 parts 8½ minutes: the product is the circumference of the circle of the moon; keep it. Then add to the digits of the radius of the moon the six digits that are the radius of the sun, and subtract from the sum the digits of the eclipse: the remainder is the amount between the two centres. Double it: the result is twice the distance between the two centres. Then subtract the digits of the eclipse from 12, multiply the remainder by the digits of the eclipse, and divide the product by twice the distance between the two centres: the quotient is the sagitta of the circle of the moon. Subtract it from the digits of the eclipse: the remainder is the sagitta of the circle of the sun. Subtract that from 12, multiply the remainder by the sagitta of the circle of the sun, and take the root of the product: the result is half the common chord; keep it.\footnote{12 × 3;8,30 = 37;42, its half 18;51, and 18;51 × 6 = 113;6. The rules are those of chapter 43, with the sun as the eclipsed circle.}\par
\par \ArPage{165}Then look: if the digits of the eclipse are less than six, subtract them from six, and if they are more than six, take their excess over six. What results from the shortfall, add to the sagitta of the circle of the moon; what results from the excess, subtract from the sagitta of the circle of the moon. Multiply what the sagitta of the circle of the moon comes to after that by half the common chord: the product is the area of the triangle of the sun; keep it. Then multiply half the common chord by ten, so that it becomes its share of the radius, and find the arc of the product in the tables of the halved chords. Multiply the result by a quarter of the circumference of the circle of the sun, which is 9;25,30, and divide the product by 90: the quotient is the arc of the sun. Multiply it by six digits: the product is the area of the arc of the sun; keep it. Then take the sagitta of the circle of the moon that you obtained after adding to it the shortfall of the digits of the eclipse from six, or subtracting from it their excess over six, and subtract it from the distance between the centres that you kept; multiply the remainder by half the common chord: the product is the area of the triangle of the moon; keep it. Then multiply half the common chord by six, divide by the digits of the radius of the moon, multiply the result by 10 parts, and find the arc of the product. Multiply the arc that results by a quarter of the circumference of the circle of the moon and divide by 90: the result is the arc of the moon. Multiply it by the digits of the [radius] of the moon: the product is the area of the arc of the moon. Add it to the area of the arc of the sun, and subtract from the sum the area of the triangle of the sun together with the area of the triangle of the moon: the remainder is the area of what is eclipsed of the circle of the sun. Multiply it by twelve and divide by 113;6, which has been shown to be the area of the surface of the circle of the sun: the result is the amount of the circle of the sun that is eclipsed, in the measure in which its whole area is 12.\footnote{The radius of the sun, 6 digits, times ten is the radius 60 of the table of the halved chords (Nallino); the codex has six. The text has «the diameter of the moon» for the area of its arc; Nallino translates «radius», as the rule requires. As in chapter 43, the rule is exact while the common chord lies between the two centres. In a deeper eclipse the chord passes the centre of the sun, which happens when the excess of the digits over six is greater than the sagitta of the circle of the moon, or, when the moon is the smaller, its own centre; one of the two segments is then more than half its circle, the table of the halved chords gives only the smaller arc, and the rule gives too little.}\par
\par \ArPage{166}If you wish to know the sides of the darkening of the eclipse and of the clearing on the circle of the horizon, look: if the visible centre of the moon at any one of the times of the eclipse is on the belt of the signs, the beginning of the eclipse is on the side of the azimuth of the setting degree of the ecliptic at that time, and the end of the clearing, when it is possible, is on the side of the azimuth of the ascendant at that time; the middle of the eclipse has no side when the darkness surrounds the whole circle of the sun. If the visible centre of the moon is off the belt of the signs, take the visible latitude of the moon at each of the two times, I mean the time of the visible beginning and the time of the visible clearing, which I told you to compute, to know and to keep, multiply it by sixty and divide by half the two diameters together: what results is degrees. Find their arc from the table of the chords: the arc is the amount of the deviation of the darkening or of the clearing at each of the two times. At the beginning of the eclipse you lay off the deviation of the darkening from the limit of the azimuth of the setting degree at that time on the circle of the horizon, towards the side of the visible latitude of the moon at it; at the end of the clearing you lay off the arc that you obtain from the limit of the azimuth of the ascendant then, towards the side of the visible latitude of the moon at the time of the clearing. At the middle of the eclipse the direction of the darkening is at right angles to the ecliptic, and its azimuth on the circle of the horizon is bounded by the arc that passes through the two poles of the ecliptic, through the centre of the moon and the horizon, according to the distance of the sun and the moon from the equator.\footnote{The moon passes the sun from west to east, so that the eclipse begins on the western side of the sun and ends on its eastern side.}\par
\par If you wish to know the side of the darkening at the middle of the eclipse, find the angle of longitude at it in the way in which you find it for knowing the parallax of the moon, and lay it off from the limit of the azimuth of the ascendant at the middle of the eclipse, or of its setting degree, on the circle of the horizon, according to the place of the eclipse with respect to one of the two horizons, towards the side of the latitude of the moon. Where it ends on the circle of the horizon, towards the azimuth of that part of the horizon the darkening inclines at the middle of the eclipse: I mean, if the sun is towards the west, you count from the side of the azimuth of the setting degree, and if it is towards the east, from the side of the azimuth of the ascendant.\par
\par \ArPage{167}If you wish to know the eclipse of the sun approximately by the tables, when you know that the sun is eclipsed or that it can be eclipsed, take the distance of the true equal hours of the conjunction from the time of noon in the locality that you want. You find it by converting the hours of the conjunction into hours of that locality; then look: if the conjunction is before noon, subtract the hours from 24 hours, and if it is after noon, before sunset, take the hours themselves. What results in either way is the distance of the hours of the conjunction from the meridian line. With it find the parallax of the moon in longitude from the tables set down for the parallax of the moon in the climes, so as to know its parallax corrected by the fourth column of the tables of the computation for the degree in which the moon is, in that manner. When you know that, divide it by the course of the moon in an hour: the result is the hours of the first parallax. If the distance of the moon from the ascendant is less than 90, subtract the hours of the first parallax from the hours of the true conjunction; if the distance is more than 90, add them to the hours of the conjunction. Then take their distance from noon a second time, and find with it the parallax of the degree of the moon in longitude, corrected by the fourth column of the tables of the computation, in that way: the result is the second parallax. Divide it by the gain of the moon: the quotient is the hours of the second parallax. If the distance of the conjunction, I mean of the degree of the moon, from the ascendant is less than 90, subtract them from the hours of the true conjunction, and if it is more than 90, add them to the hours of the true conjunction. Take their distance from noon a third time as well, and find with it the corrected parallax of the degree of the moon in longitude a third time. If it is equal to the second parallax, the hours that you obtained from the hours of the true conjunction after adding the hours of the second parallax to them, or subtracting them, are the hours of the middle of the eclipse.\footnote{With the parallax corrected by the fourth column «and the fifth» must be added, here and below, as the rules of chapter 39 show; the words seem to have dropped out already in the archetype, since Plato of Tivoli also lacks them (Nallino). The tables of the parallax in the climes are Theon's.}\par
\par If the third parallax is more than the second, find its excess over it; then add a sixth of an hour to the hours of the distance of the conjunction from noon that you obtained with the second parallax, and find with that the corrected parallax of the degree of the moon in longitude in that way. Take its excess over the second parallax, multiply it by six and subtract the product from the gain of the moon in an hour: the remainder is the corrected motion. Divide by it the excess of the third parallax over the second, which I told you to keep: the result is a part of an hour; add it to the hours of the second parallax: the sum is the exact hours. If the third parallax is less than the second, take what it falls short of it, multiply it by six and add the product to the gain of the moon: the sum is the corrected motion. Divide by it the shortfall of the third parallax from the second: the result is a part of an hour; subtract it from the hours of the second parallax. What the hours of the second parallax come to after that is the exact hours.\footnote{Here the new parallax is compared with the second, where the rule at the beginning of the chapter compares it with the third, the parallax of the time from which the sixth of an hour is counted. On the excess of the third parallax over the second, see the note on the third parallax above.}\par
\par When you know the exact hours of the parallax, in whichever of the two ways they came out, multiply them by the unequal courses of the moon and of the sun in that hour, and keep the product of each of them. If the distance of the moon from the ascendant at that time is less than 90, subtract the exact hours of the second parallax from the hours of the true conjunction, and the motion of the sun in that amount from the position of the conjunction; likewise subtract the motion of the moon from the position of the conjunction, from the anomaly of the moon and from the motion in latitude, and subtract from the motion in latitude besides the course of the node in the amount of the hours of the second parallax. If the distance of the moon from the ascendant is more than 90, use addition instead of subtraction in all of this. What the hours of the true conjunction come to after the addition or the subtraction is the hours of the middle of the eclipse, and likewise the positions of the sun and the moon, the motion in latitude and the anomaly of the moon, corrected for the middle of the eclipse. Then find the latitude of the moon and its side from the motion in latitude at the middle of the eclipse, and keep it.\par
\par \ArPage{168}Then take the distance of the hours of the middle of the eclipse from noon and find with it the parallax of the moon in latitude in that way, after correcting it with the fourth column of the tables of the computation, and find the side of the parallax. If the latitude of the moon and its parallax in latitude are on one side, add them together; if they differ, subtract the smaller from the greater and find the remainder and its side. What results after the addition or the subtraction is the visible latitude of the moon for the middle of the eclipse. Look for the same in the column of the latitude of the moon set down in the two tables of the eclipse of the sun. If you find it in the second table, that for the lesser distance, and do not find it in the greater table, take what is under it in the lesser table of the digits and the minutes of the fall. Then enter with the anomaly of the moon into the tables of the computation and take the minutes of the shares set down under it in the third column; find what part of 60 they are, and take that part of those digits and of the minutes of the fall: what results is the amount of the diameter of the sun that is eclipsed, in digits, and the minutes of the fall are the amount of the eclipse in its duration from its beginning to its middle. If the latitude of the moon falls in both tables, take what is under it in the first, greater table and in the second, lesser one of the digits and the minutes of the fall. Then find the difference between the two tables in the digits and in the minutes of the fall, and take of each the part that the minutes of the third column, opposite the anomaly of the moon in the tables of the computation, are of 60; add what results of the digits to the digits that came from the table of the greater distance, the first, and likewise add what results for the fall to the minutes of the fall of the greater table. What each of them comes to is the amount of the digits of the eclipse and the minutes of the fall. It is known that if you find the latitude of the moon in neither of the two tables, the sun is not eclipsed.\par
\par Then take the minutes of the fall and divide them by the gain of the moon: the quotient is the hours of the fall. Subtract them from the hours of the middle of the eclipse: the remainder is the hours of the plain beginning; and add them to the hours of the middle of the eclipse: the sum is the hours of the plain clearing.\par
\par \ArPage{169}If you wish to follow in this what I set out at the beginning of the chapter, so as to make right the time of the visible beginning or the time of the visible clearing as explained in that way, you may. If you wish to know it approximately, enter with the hours of the distance of each of these three times from noon into the tables of the parallax set down for the climes, and take what is under each of them in the table of the parallax in longitude only, in the sign of the moon and in the sign that follows it, in that way, so as to know the parallax of the degree of the moon in longitude without correcting it with the fourth column of the tables of the computation, so that the computation is easier. Then find the difference between the parallax of the middle time and that of each of the two times, and divide each of them by the gain of the moon: what results is a part of an hour. Put the hours of the fall that you obtained in two places; add to one of the two places one of the two quotients that you obtained from the parallax, and the other quotient to the other place. Then look at the greater of the two that you have obtained, the hours of the fall with what you added to them, and subtract it from the hours of the middle of the eclipse if the eclipse inclines towards the west from the middle of the sky, between the ascendant and the setting point, which is when the distance of the middle of the eclipse from the ascendant is more than 90; and add the smaller to the hours of the middle of the eclipse. If the eclipse is towards the east, which is when the distance of the middle of the eclipse from the ascendant is less than 90, subtract the smaller of the two from the hours of the middle of the eclipse and add the greater to the hours of the middle of the eclipse. For the longer of the two times always falls near the middle of the sphere, which is midway between the ascendant and the setting point: whichever of the two times inclines towards the middle of the sphere is the longer. When you know what the hours of the middle of the eclipse come to after the addition or the subtraction, the diminished hours are the hours of the beginning of the eclipse and the increased ones the hours of the end of the clearing.\footnote{Near the point 90 parts from the ascendant the parallax in longitude changes fastest and slows the visible moon most, so that the half of the eclipse nearer that point is the longer.}\par
\par If you wish to correct the digits of the eclipse by the table, enter with these [digits] that you obtain into the tables of the amounts of the eclipse, in the columns of numbers, and take what is under them in the second column, headed «amounts of the eclipse of the sun»: what results is the amount of the eclipse to the sight. Likewise, if you wish to know the sides of the darkening on the circle of the eclipse, enter with the digits of the eclipse that are eclipsed of the diameter of the sun, which are the digits that come from the table before you correct them, into the table of the deviations of the darkenings, and take what stands opposite them in the second column, headed «beginning of the eclipse of the sun and end of its clearing»: what results is the parts of the deviation; keep them. Then find the azimuth of the ascendant and of the setting degree at the time of the beginning and of the clearing, in the manner mentioned, from the circles drawn for the climes, as I explained in the chapter on the eclipse of the moon; and lay off the parts of the deviation themselves from the limit of the azimuth of the ascendant for the end of the clearing, towards the side of the latitude of the moon, and from the limit of the azimuth of the setting degree for the beginning of the eclipse, towards the side of the latitude of the moon. Where you end on the circle of the horizon, there is the azimuth of the darkening and of the clearing on the circle of the horizon.\footnote{Part of the word «digits» is supplied by Nallino.}\par
\par \ArPage{170}If you wish to draw the eclipse of the sun in the way in which you drew the eclipse of the moon, take from the divided line the amount of half the two diameters, draw a circle with it, and divide it into quarters by two lines that intersect at the centre at right angles. Then take also from the line the amount of the radius of the sun and draw with it a second circle about that first centre: it falls inside the circle of half the two diameters. Write the directions of the horizons on the ends of the lines of the greater circle. Then take from the line also, with the compasses, the amount of the visible latitude of the moon for the beginning of the eclipse: put one of its two ends on the centre of the two circles, turn the other end towards the side of the visible latitude of the moon, and mark where it falls on the line a sign for the beginning of the eclipse. Then take the amount of its latitude for the middle of the eclipse and its latitude for the end of the clearing, and do the same with it, until you have the three signs. Then draw from the sign of the latitude for the beginning of the eclipse, towards the west, a line parallel to the diameter, and from the sign of the end of the clearing, towards the east, a line also parallel to the diameter, and mark two signs at the ends of the two lines on the circumference of the greater circle. Then take from the divided line also the amount of the radius of the moon and draw a circle with it about the sign of the latitude of the moon for the middle of the eclipse: what falls of the circle of the sun within this circle is what is eclipsed of the sun. Draw also another circle about the sign that falls on the western circumference of the circle: it touches the circle of the sun, and it is the circle of the beginning of the eclipse; likewise draw about the eastern sign on the circumference of the circle a third circle, which is the circle of the end of the clearing.\footnote{The translation reads «\RL{\ArNote يتقاطعان}», «intersect», for «\RL{\ArNote بتقاطعان}» (p. 170, line 2), a misprint that the corrigenda do not list.}\par
\par For example, we draw the circle of half the two diameters ABGD about the centre H; let the sign A be the point of the west, the sign B the point of the south, the sign G the point of the east and the sign D the point of the north. We draw the diameters AG and BD, and about the centre H the circle of the sun, on which are Z, K, Ḍ, L. We take the visible latitude of the moon to the north, and mark on the sign of its latitude for the beginning of the eclipse the point ʿ, on its latitude for the middle of the eclipse the sign Ḥ, and on its latitude for the end of the clearing the sign M. We draw the line MS and the line ʿQ parallel to the diameter AG, and draw about the sign Q a circle for the moon at the beginning of the eclipse, which touches the circle of the sun at the point Ṭ; we draw also about the sign S another circle, for the end of the clearing, which touches the circle of the sun at the sign N; and likewise we draw another circle about the sign Ḥ, for the middle of the eclipse, within which falls the segment KḌL of the circle of the sun. We draw the two lines HQ and HS for the azimuth of the darkening and of the clearing. So it is clear that the beginning of the eclipse is from the point Ṭ, which is in the azimuth of the arc AQ of the circle of the horizon.\footnote{Nallino corrects the codex, which has M for ʿ and ʿ for M, and BKḌ for KḌL.}\par
\par\begin{center}\ArPage{171}\begin{tikzpicture}\draw[line width=1.22pt] (124.26pt,120.70pt) circle[radius=106.33pt];

\par Likewise the end of the clearing is from the point N, which is in the azimuth of the arc SG of the circle of the horizon. It is known that the point A is the azimuth of the setting degree and the sign G the sign of the azimuth of the ascendant. Likewise the azimuth of the middle of the eclipse is at a right angle where the line HD cuts the circle of the horizon, according to its distance from the middle of the sphere and its nearness to the horizon, as we have shown for the moon and the sun. This is enough and covers it; that is what we wished to show, if God wills.\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 45. On knowing the positions of the wandering stars on the ecliptic by the era of the Arabs and of the Romans}{45}{\ArPage{171}}{}
\par \ArPage{172}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the position of whichever of the wandering stars you wish, find the mean position of the star on the day that you want and at the given hour, by the hours of al-Raqqa, by whichever of the two eras you wish, as I described for knowing the mean position of the sun, and find also the mean position of the sun at the same time at which you find the mean position of the star. If your computation is for Saturn, Jupiter or Mars, subtract the mean position of whichever of them you compute from the mean position of the sun: the remainder is the anomaly of the star. If your computation is for Venus or Mercury, what comes out for you from the table is the anomaly of the star, and the mean position of the sun is used in place of their two mean positions, since it is what carries their positions along.\footnote{The mean positions are found as in chapter 33.}\par
\par When you know the mean position of the star and its anomaly, subtract the farthest distance of the star from its mean position: the remainder is the centre. Look for the same in the two columns of numbers of the tables of the equation of the star, take what stands opposite it in the third column, headed «the equation of the anomaly and the centre», and write it down under the anomaly and under the centre both. If the centre is less than 180 degrees, subtract the equation from the centre and add it to the anomaly; if the centre is more than 180, add the equation to the centre and subtract it from the anomaly. What each of them comes to after the addition or the subtraction is the corrected centre and the corrected anomaly. Enter with the corrected centre in the two columns of numbers and take what stands opposite it of the minutes of the difference of the two distances, set down in the fourth column, and find whether they are increasing or decreasing. You know this from the mark at the head of the column, or from their increasing or decreasing as the number increases — except for Mercury, whose increase and decrease is known only from the mark, because, from the speed of its motion on its epicycle, it comes into the region of its nearest distance twice in a circuit of the signs. If these minutes are decreasing, enter with the corrected anomaly of the star into the tables of the equation of the star, in the two columns of numbers, and take what stands opposite it in the fifth column, headed «the farthest distance», and also in the sixth column, headed «the mean distance»; if the minutes are increasing, take what stands opposite the corrected anomaly in the sixth column and in the seventh column, headed «the nearest distance». Multiply what results from the fifth column by the minutes that you obtained from the fourth column, divide by sixty, and subtract the quotient from what you wrote down from the sixth column; and multiply what results from the seventh column by the minutes of the fourth column, divide by sixty, and add the quotient to what you wrote down from the sixth column. Subtract what the sixth column comes to after the addition or the subtraction from the centre corrected by the third column if the number of the corrected anomaly is more than 180, and add it to it if the corrected anomaly is less than 180. What the centre comes to after the addition or the subtraction is the position of the star from the point of the farthest distance. Add to it the farthest distance of the star, which you subtracted from its mean position at the start: the result is the computed position of the star. Cast it off from the beginning of Aries, thirty for each sign, in the usual way: where it ends, there is the position of the star on the ecliptic.\footnote{The rules are Ptolemy's. The columns are counted without the two columns of numbers. In Ptolemy's model it is the moving centre of Mercury's eccentric that brings the centre of its epicycle twice in a revolution to a nearest distance (\emph{Almagest} IX 8, cited by Nallino).}\par
\par If there are minutes with the centre or the anomaly, take their share of the difference of the equation and its increments, in the manner that I showed you at the beginning of the book in the equation of the sun and the moon.\par
\par \ArPage{173}The farthest distances of the stars from the earth on their epicycles in the year 1191 of the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn, son of Philip, Alexander, were: Saturn 244;28, Jupiter 164;28, Mars 126;18, Venus 82;14, and the sun the same, Mercury 201;28. We have computed them again, verified them and set them down for each of them at the beginning of the tables of its equation. These distances that we have set down also move with the motion of the sphere of the fixed stars, one degree in every 66 solar years, and one degree in every 68 lunar years too. So take the amount of the motion in the years between this year mentioned and the year that you want: if it is after the year 1191, add it to these distances, and if it is before the year 1191, subtract it from these distances, and work with them in the usual way.\footnote{The farthest distances meant are those of the eccentrics: on the epicycle the farthest distance comes wherever the anomaly is nothing (Nallino). The year 1191 began on 1 September 879 (Nallino). Nallino would read 126;58 for Mars, «\RL{\ArNote نح}» for «\RL{\ArNote يح}», so that the farthest distances of Saturn and Mars lie equally beyond Ptolemy's (chapter 31). One degree in 66 solar years is one in 68 lunar years (chapter 33).}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 46. On knowing the retrogradation of the wandering stars}{46}{\ArPage{173}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the retrogradation of one of the wandering stars and its direct motion, enter with the centre of the star corrected by the third column into the two columns of numbers, which go in steps of six parts, in the tables of the retrogradation of the stars, and take what stands opposite it in the column of the star that you want: write down what is in the first column as the first station, and what is in the second column as the second station. When you know the first and the second station, look: if the corrected anomaly of the star is less than the second station and more than the first station, the star is retrograde; if it is more than the second station or less than the first, it is direct in its course. If the star is retrograde and you wish to know how long ago it turned back, subtract the first station from the corrected anomaly and divide the remainder by the course of the anomaly of the star in a day, which is: Saturn 0;57, Jupiter 0;54, Mars 0;28, Venus 0;37, Mercury 3;6. What results is the days since it turned back. If you wish to know in how many days it will be direct, subtract the corrected anomaly from the second station and follow this procedure with the remainder. Likewise, if you wish to know when it will turn back while it is direct, subtract its corrected anomaly from the first station and proceed with the remainder in the same way.\footnote{The daily motions in anomaly are Ptolemy's, rounded to the minute (\emph{Almagest} IX 3; Nallino); the codex has 0;36 for Venus.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 47. On knowing the latitudes of the five wandering stars}{47}{\ArPage{174}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the latitudes of the five wandering stars and their sides: if your computation is for Saturn, Jupiter or Mars, enter with the centre of one of them corrected by the third column — for Saturn with 50 added, for Jupiter with 20 subtracted, and for Mars as it is, without addition or subtraction — in the two columns of numbers of the tables of the latitudes of the five wandering stars, which go in steps of six parts, and take what stands opposite it of the minutes of the shares of the latitude, common to all of them, set down at the end of those tables, which is the ninth column of them; write down what results. If the number with which you entered falls in the upper half, which is half of the numbers, from 0 to 90 and from 270 to 360, enter with the corrected anomaly of the star in the two columns of numbers of those tables, take what stands opposite it in the column of the northern latitude of the star, called \emph{afījiyūn} of the north, and take of it the part that the minutes of the shares of the latitude are of sixty. If it falls in the lower half, which is from 90 to 180 and from 180 to 270, take what stands opposite that corrected anomaly in the column of the southern latitude of the star, called \emph{farījiyūn} of the south, and take of it the part that the minutes of the shares are of sixty. What results, whichever of the two sides it is, is the latitude of the star on the side on which you find it.\footnote{The rules of this chapter are Ptolemy's (\emph{Almagest} XIII 6; Nallino). Adding 50 for Saturn and subtracting 20 for Jupiter counts their centres from the northern limits of their latitudes instead of from their farthest distances. In the codex the column of the shares is the seventh; it would be the ninth in the table of the three upper planets (Nallino). \emph{Afījiyūn} and \emph{farījiyūn} are the Greek \emph{apogeion} and \emph{perigeion}, which al-Battānī took from the Arabic copies of the \emph{Almagest}: in Ptolemy's account the latitude of the three upper planets is northern while the centre of the epicycle is in the half of the eccentric that holds its farthest distance, and southern in the other half (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{175}If your computation is for Venus or Mercury, enter with their corrected anomaly into those tables, take what stands opposite it in the column of the inclination and the column of the slant, and write down each of them as it is. If your computation is for Venus, leave it as it is. If it is for Mercury alone, and the centre corrected by the third column falls in the upper half mentioned, subtract from the slant alone a tenth of it, I mean one part in ten of it; if it falls in the lower half, add to the slant alone one part in ten of it. What it comes to after the addition or the subtraction is the corrected slant of Mercury; write it down in place of what you obtained from the table, and cancel the first. Then add to the centre corrected by the third column 90 for Venus and 270 for Mercury, and enter with what you have, after casting off the circle if it is more than the circle, into those tables; take what stands opposite it in the ninth column, headed «the shares of the latitude for all». Find what part of sixty the minutes that you obtain are, and take that part of the inclination that you wrote down: the result is the first latitude; keep it. If the number of the centre corrected with the addition for the star, with which you found the minutes of the ninth column, and the corrected anomaly of the star both fall in one half, whichever half it is, upper or lower, the latitude that you wrote down is southern; if they differ, one in one half and the other in the other half, that latitude is northern. Know it with its side.\footnote{The inclination and the slant are Ptolemy's \emph{enklisis} and \emph{loxōsis}, the two tilts of the epicycles of Venus and Mercury (Nallino).}\par
\par Then take the centre corrected by the third column, for Venus as it is and for Mercury with 180 added, and enter with what you have of the centre, after casting off the circle if it exceeds it, into those tables as well; take what stands opposite it of the minutes of the shares set down in the ninth column, and write them down in two places. Find what part of sixty one of the two places is, and take that part of the slant: the result is the second latitude; write it down. If the number with which you found these minutes fell in the upper half and the corrected anomaly is less than 180, what you obtained of the second latitude is northern, and if the anomaly is more than 180, the latitude is southern; if it fell in the lower half and the anomaly is less than 180, the latitude is southern, and if it is more than 180, it is northern. Then take of the minutes that you wrote down in the other place their sixth, if your computation is for Venus, which is always northern, and their half and quarter, if your computation is for Mercury, which is always southern. If these three latitudes fall on one side, add them together; if they differ, cast the smaller from the greater and find the side of the remainder: that is the latitude that results for the star, on the side on which it falls.\footnote{The four sides of the second latitude are Nallino's correction; the codex has them the other way round (\emph{Almagest} XIII 6). A sixth and three quarters of the sixty minutes are 0;10 and 0;45, Ptolemy's greatest deviations of Venus to the north and of Mercury to the south. Ibn Yūnus counts an error in the side of the latitude of Mercury among al-Battānī's mistakes; Nallino finds none in the rules or the tables, which agree with Ptolemy's, and suggests that Ibn Yūnus had a faulty copy, like the codex here (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par Know that the whole latitude of Saturn to the north, as Ptolemy found it, is 3;2, and to the south 3;5; the whole latitude of Jupiter to the north is 2;4 and to the south 2;8; the whole latitude of Mars to the north is 4;21 and to the south 7;7; the whole latitude of Venus, to the north and to the south equally, is 8;56, and the whole latitude of Mercury, to the north and to the south equally, is 4;18 as well.\footnote{Nallino restores these values from Ptolemy's tables (\emph{Almagest} XIII 5) and from Theon's canon; the codex has several of them wrong, among them 8;2 and 33;5 for Saturn and 6 for both latitudes of Mars, and writes the last two without dots.}\par
\par \ArPage{176}If you wish to know whether the star is ascending or descending on the side on which it is, compute its latitude after ten days. If it is in the north and you find that its latitude has increased, it is ascending, and if you find that it has decreased, it is descending; if its latitude is in the south and you find it increased, it is descending, and if you find it decreased, it is ascending. If it was in the north and you find that it has passed to the south, you know that it was descending in the north; if it was in the south and you find that it has passed to the north, you know that it was ascending in the south.\footnote{Theon's canon has similar rules (Nallino).}\par
\par Saturn, Jupiter and Mars have another way by which this is known for them: if the latitude of one of them is in the north and its anomaly is less than 180, it is ascending, and if it is more than 180, it is descending; if its latitude is in the south and its anomaly is less than 180, it is descending, and if it is more than 180, it is ascending. As for Venus and Mercury, because of the speed of their motion around the sun, although their greatest latitude comes at their conjunction with it, it is difficult to know their state except in the way given before.\footnote{«Although» follows Nallino's translation; the text is faulty here (Nallino). «Around the sun» describes the apparent motion of Venus and Mercury to either side of the sun, as in the like phrases of the Greek and Latin writers (Nallino's commentary, after Schiaparelli). Their greatest latitudes come at their conjunctions below the sun (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's note).}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 48. On knowing the rising of the five wandering stars and their setting, which is their appearing and their disappearing}{48}{\ArPage{176}}{}
\par \ArPage{177}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know when the stars come out in the east and when they go down in the west, which is their rising and their disappearing: Saturn, Jupiter and Mars, if the anomaly of each of them is from 0 to 180, are in their morning rising, and from 180 to 360 in their evening setting. Venus and Mercury, because of their circling around the sun and their quickening and slowing, have four configurations with respect to the sun. As for Venus, if its corrected anomaly is from 0 to 137, it is in its evening rising, when it is seen on the western horizon, and it is then faster in motion than the sun; from 137 to 180 it is in its evening setting, when it slows down and turns back and the sun overtakes it; from 180 to 223 it is in its morning rising, and it is then slower in motion than the sun; and from 223 to 360 it sets in the mornings until it overtakes the sun and disappears in its rays, and it is then faster in motion than the sun. As for Mercury, if its corrected anomaly is from 0 to 112, it rises in the evenings; from 112 to 180 it sets in the evenings; from 180 to 248 it rises in the mornings; and from 248 to 360 it sets in the mornings. Its state of speed and slowness is as we have set out for Venus.\footnote{The translation follows the corrigenda in reading «\RL{\ArNote غُروبها}», «their setting», for «\RL{\ArNote عُروبها}» in the title (p. 176). Rising and setting mean here the growing and the shrinking distance of the star from the sun. For Venus and Mercury it grows from the conjunction beyond the sun, at the anomaly 0, to the greatest elongation, where the line of sight touches the epicycle, and shrinks to the conjunction below the sun, at 180. At the mean distance, with Ptolemy's radii of the epicycles, 43;10 and 22;30 in parts of which the eccentric is 60, the greatest elongation comes at the anomalies 136 and 112. For Venus the codex has 136; Nallino prints 137.}\par
\par If you wish to know the first appearance of Saturn, Jupiter and Mars in the mornings in the east, which is their first coming out from under the rays to the limit of visibility, the sun having passed them, compute for one of them: if its corrected anomaly is about 20 degrees, say that it is seen. For its setting in the evenings, when the sun overtakes it and hides it and covers it with its rays, you reckon it when the corrected anomaly is about 340. As for Venus and Mercury: if the anomaly of one of them is about 20, that is its first appearance in the evenings in the west; if it is about 340 degrees, that is its first disappearance in the mornings in the east; if it is about 160, that is its first disappearance in the evenings in the west; and if it is about 200, that is its first appearance in the mornings in the east.\footnote{Nallino takes these numbers from Plato of Tivoli and Theon's canon; the codex has 26 for 20, 348 for 340 and 170 for 200.}\par
\par If you wish to know the first rising of one of them and its disappearance by computation, find the arc of seeing on the equator for each of them. The amount of the arc of seeing is for Saturn 14 degrees, for Jupiter 12;45, for Mars 14;30, for Venus 5;40 and for Mercury 11;30. Then find the distance of the star from the equator and the degree that culminates with it, according to the latitude that it has, and find with these half the arc of its day, which is half its stay above the earth, and the times of the ascension of the degree with which it rises or sets, in the ways mentioned. If there is between the star and the sun, in degrees of the ascensions or of the settings, as much as the arc of seeing mentioned for it, the star is seen on that day, or disappears. If your computation is for the seeing and the appearance from under the rays, and what lies between it and the sun is less than the arc of seeing, it has not appeared, and if it is more, it has appeared; if your computation is for the disappearance, and the distance between it and the sun in times of the settings or the ascensions, according to the horizon at which it is, is less than the arc of seeing, it has disappeared, and if it is more, it has not disappeared.\footnote{The arcs are Ptolemy's (\emph{Almagest} XIII 7), which Ptolemy gives as arcs of the ecliptic near the beginning of Cancer; al-Battānī takes them as arcs of the equator, to be compared with the ascensions (Nallino's commentary). The codex has 16, 16;30 and 6;40 for the arcs of Saturn, Mars and Venus (Nallino). The translation reads «\RL{\ArNote والمطالع}», «and the ascensions», for «\RL{\ArNote والمطالم}» (p. 177, line 16), a misprint that the corrigenda do not list.}\par
\par You should compute the star for the rising or the setting of the sun at the time when its corrected anomaly is about what we have described.\par
\par \ArPage{178}If you wish to know how long ago it rose, or in how long it will rise, or how long ago it disappeared, or in how long it will disappear, find the amount between the arc of seeing and the distance of the star from the sun and keep it; then divide it by the true corrected course of the star. You find that as I describe: compute the star either a day after that day or a day before it, as needed; what lies between the two positions is the true motion of the star. Subtract it from the true motion of the sun in a day: the remainder is the true corrected course. This is for the three upper stars. For Venus and Mercury, you add the course of the star to the course of the sun if it is retrograde, and take the difference between them if it is direct: the result is its corrected course. What comes out of the division is the number of days and hours in which it will rise or set, or the number of days and hours since it rose or set.\par
\par I have set down for them the amounts of seeing in degrees of the signs in each clime, according to what has been observed of their appearance in each sign, and that is set down at the heads of the signs, although it is not exact, because of the variation of their latitudes. For this reason we have confined ourselves to setting down these amounts for one clime, so that knowing it from this table is easy, approximately; we have made it for the fourth clime. If you wish to know the seeing of the star and its disappearance, take, of the amounts of seeing and of disappearance set down in the table of the morning risings and the evening disappearances of the three upper stars, what is under the sign in which the star is, and what is under the sign after it. Then take the difference between the two signs, multiply it by the degrees of the star in the sign in which it is, and divide the product by thirty. Add the result to the amount of seeing or of disappearance, whichever you are computing, that you obtain under the sign in which the star is, if that is the smaller, and subtract it from it if that is the greater. What remains is the arc of seeing at that place in parts of the ecliptic, or the arc of disappearance. If that is equal to what lies between the sun and the star in degrees of the signs, it rose or disappeared on that day; if it differs, follow what I have set out and explained in this chapter. It is known that the working of the seeing by computation, in the way that I set out first, is more correct and more exact than what comes out of these tables. For Venus and Mercury, you take the four configurations mentioned in the way that we have stated for the upper stars.\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 49. On knowing the nine configurations that the fixed stars, and some of the wandering ones, have with the sun}{49}{\ArPage{179}}{}
