\par Al-Battānī said: Since the stars, fixed and wandering, revolve in their course about the two poles of the ecliptic [ever since their motion in longitude and latitude has been known], while the right sphere revolves about the two poles of the equator, their rising and their setting on the two sides of the meridian line are always of equal amount at the place of the equator, and no change in their motion has been found. In the spheres inclined to the equator, however, their rising and their setting on the two sides of the meridian line are not equal but differ: the southern ones are later in rising than the northern ones, and likewise they precede them in setting. For this reason none of them that is not on the belt of the signs rises, sets and culminates with one and the same part of the signs, but with different parts; and so their configurations differ, as do those of some of the running stars, which are the moving wandering ones, in the regions of the ecliptic and of the horizon, and with the sun and the moon too. The most evident in force are those that they have with the sun, and they come in nine kinds.\footnote{Nallino brackets these words as inept; Plato of Tivoli lacks them. The nine configurations are Ptolemy's (\emph{Almagest} VIII 4; Nallino's commentary). \emph{Tashrīq} and \emph{taghrīb}, rendered «easting» and «westing», commonly mean the heliacal rising and setting, the appearance of a star in the east before sunrise and its disappearance in the west after sunset; al-Battānī uses them more widely in this chapter (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par (1) The first kind is the morning easting, when the star is with the sun on the eastern horizon. It is of two sorts: one is called the morning one, when the star is not visible in the east and then rises afterwards, after sunrise; the other is called the morning-eastern, when it rises with the sun in the east. One also speaks of easting when the star rises before the sun.\par
\par (2) The second kind is called the culmination of the easting, when the star, at sunrise, is on the middle of the sky above the earth or below it. This too is of two sorts: one is called the morning one, when it culminates after sunrise; the other is called the morning culminating one, when the star is in the middle of the sky while the sun is in the east.\par
\par \ArPage{180}(3) The third kind is called the morning westing, when the sun is at the place of rising and the star near the western horizon. This too is of various sorts: the star may be in the west at sunrise, which is called the morning westing, when it sets after the sun rises; the setting of the star may come with the rising of the sun; or it may precede the sun in setting.\par
\par (4) The fourth kind is called the southern easting, when the sun is on the meridian line and the star in the east. It is of various sorts: it may be in the daytime, with the sun in the middle of the sky [so the star is not seen]; or at night, with the sun at the cardine of the earth, and the star is seen on the eastern horizon in those ways, either preceding the sun, or level with it, or behind it, so that it rises after the sun's culmination.\footnote{The bracketed words are supplied by Nallino after Plato of Tivoli. The cardine of the earth is the lowest point of the ecliptic, below the earth (chapter 35).}\par
\par (5) The fifth kind is called the southern culmination, when the sun and the star are in the middle of the sky. It is of two sorts. In one it is daytime and the sun is above the earth, so the star is not seen, or it is night and the sun is with it at the cardine of the earth, so it is not seen either. In the other the sun is at the cardine of the earth and the star in the middle of the sky above the earth, so that it is seen in those ways of preceding, being level and being behind.\par
\par (6) The sixth kind is called the southern west, when the sun is in the middle of the sky and the star in the west. This too is of several sorts: it may be daytime, with the sun in the middle of the sky, so the star is not seen; or night, with the sun at the cardine of the earth, so that it is seen in the ways mentioned of preceding, being level and being behind.\par
\par (7) The seventh kind is called the evening rising, when the sun is in the west and the star on the eastern horizon. It is of several sorts: the sun may set while the star is seen in the east, having risen before it; the star may rise with the setting of the sun, which is called the evening one; or it may be at the point of rising when the sun sets, so that it is not seen until it rises after the sun has set.\footnote{Nallino's translation adds «not» to the first sort, which Ptolemy calls the earlier evening rising, not visible.}\par
\par (8) The eighth kind is called the evening culmination, when the sun is on the western horizon and the star in the middle of the sky above the earth or below it. It is of several sorts: at the setting of the sun it may be above the earth, and so seen, or below the earth, and so not seen; and it may precede in its culmination, or be level, or be behind.\par
\par (9) The ninth kind is called the evening westing, when the star is with the sun on the western horizon. It is of several sorts: the star may be seen in the west before it enters the rays, and set after the sun sets; the star and the sun may be together on the horizon and set together; or the star may precede the sun in its setting, and so move away from it in setting until it appears in the east before the sun.\par
\par \ArPage{181}We have explained earlier in this book how to know the distances between the stars in their arrangement on the sphere, and shown how to know the parts of the ecliptic with which they rise, set and culminate.\footnote{Chapters 18, 20 and 26 (Nallino).}\par
\par As for the seeing of the stars that are called fixed, and their disappearing on account of the sun: when the amount at which each of the six kinds of their magnitudes mentioned appears and disappears is known, your computation for it is as we described for the seeing. It is said that the amount of the arc at which the stars of the first magnitude appear and disappear — such as the Yemeni Shiʿrā and the Syrian one, the Heart of the Lion, the two Simāks, the end of the River, and those like them in magnitude — is 15 parts, I mean the arc at which each appears and disappears; these parts are of the times of the equator. For the remaining kinds, below this magnitude, the arc exceeds 15 parts in the order of the magnitude, until it comes to the smallest of them in magnitude, which is seen and disappears at the amount of a sign, approximately.\footnote{The stars are Sirius, Procyon, Regulus, Spica and Arcturus, and Achernar (Nallino). Plato of Tivoli has 12. Ptolemy gives no such arcs. The 15 parts may come from Autolycus, who gives 15 parts of the ecliptic for all the fixed stars, a book known to the Arabs in Qusṭā ibn Lūqā's translation, corrected by Thābit ibn Qurra (Nallino's commentary). Schiaparelli supposes that earlier Arab astronomers had found by rough observation that stars of the first magnitude appear an hour from the sun, 15 times of the equator, and those of the sixth two hours, 30 times, and set the other magnitudes between (Nallino's commentary). A sign is 30 parts.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 50. On knowing the distances of the stars, their diameters, the sizes of their bodies and the extent of their spheres, set down as handed down, in the way in which the ancients and the first ones stated them}{50}{\ArPage{181}}{}
\par \ArPage{182}Al-Battānī said: The distances of the sun and the moon, their diameters and the sizes of their bodies we have explained according to what is said in the book of the \emph{Almagest} and what we found by measuring the eclipses. Let us now begin the account of the other spheres, up to the sphere of Saturn and the sphere of the fixed stars, as the later excellent sages after Ptolemy took pains to set it out. Their account of it was this. It has been shown that the farthest distance of the moon from the earth, in the measure in which the radius of the earth is one part, is 64;10, and that is the nearest distance of Mercury; the spheres of Mercury and Venus lie between the farthest distance of the moon and the nearest distance of the sun. The limit of the air and the fire is the nearest distance of the moon from the earth, which is when it is on either side of the full moon, I mean at the mean quadrature with the sun; it has been shown that its distance from the earth is then 33;33 in that measure. What is above that is the ether, in which the stars run. The two remaining elements, water and earth, end at the amount of the radius of the earth. These four elements, earth, water, air and fire, are the roots of the earthly natures and the cause of coming to be and passing away; as they differ, things differ, according to what of the sun, the moon and the stars is above them, and their change goes on within the nearest distance of the moon from the earth; by that every thing, of the animals and the plants, changes. What lies within this limit, between the centre of the earth and the ether, reaches, as we have said, 33;33 from the earth, in the measure in which the radius of the earth is one part: that is the farthest extent of the earth, the air, the water and the fire. What is above that is a fifth nature, of which nothing true can be said: the senses do not reach it, and the mind does not grasp its quality.\footnote{The translation reads «after Ptolemy» with Plato of Tivoli and Nallino's translation; the text has «before», which does not fit «the later sages». The distances of this chapter rest on Ptolemy's eccentricities and epicycles, with the spheres taken to touch one another without a gap, so that the least distance of each planet is the greatest distance of the one below it. Al-Farghānī gives the same scheme with somewhat different numbers, and Proclus reports it in the \emph{Hypotyposes} (Nallino's commentary). 64;10 and 33;33 are the greatest and the least distance of the moon (chapter 39); the codex has 38;38 for 33;33.}\par
\par In it is the sphere of Mercury, above the sphere of the moon. What appeared of its distance and size, as they grasped it, is this: it is reported that they measured its size at its farthest and at its nearest distance from the earth and found the variation of its size to be as two and a third and a quarter to one. Since the nearest distance of Mercury is equal to the farthest distance of the moon, which has been shown to be 64;10, when that is multiplied by the two and a third and a quarter, which is the variation of the size of its diameter, its farthest distance comes to 166 times the radius of the earth. When half of what lies between its farthest and its nearest distance is taken and added to its nearest distance, its mean distance is 115. Then they measured its size, when it was at its mean distance, against the sun at its mean distance, and found its diameter to be one part in fifteen of the diameter of the sun. When the 115 is divided by fifteen, the result is 7 parts and two thirds of a part. Since the diameter of the sun is five and a half times the diameter of the earth, if the mean diameter of the sun is made 1108, as we have shown by measurement, the diameter of the earth is 201½ in that measure; and when the 7⅔ parts are compared with the 201½, they are found to be about one part in 26¼ of it. Since the diameter of the earth subtends 1 degree and 57 minutes of the circle of the sphere, the diameter of Mercury subtends about 4 minutes and a half and a sixth of a minute. When that is multiplied in length, breadth and depth, the size of the body of Mercury comes to about one part in [eighteen thousand] parts of the body of the earth.\footnote{64;10 × 2 7/12 = 165;46, and (64;10 + 166)/2 = 115. Making the diameter of the sun 1108, the number of its mean distance in radii of the earth (chapter 30), puts the diameter of each planet, its mean distance divided by the ratio of its apparent diameter to the sun's, in the same measure, in which the diameter of the earth is 1108/5½ (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary); 1108/5½ is 201 5/11 (Nallino). 1;57 divided by 26¼ is 0;4,27. The text has «seventeen»; Nallino takes it for an error of the codex for «eighteen thousand», since (26¼)³ = 18,088.}\par
\par \ArPage{183}Then they looked at the size and the distance of Venus and found the variation of its size between its farthest and its nearest distance to be as two to thirteen. When the 166, which is the farthest distance of Mercury and the nearest distance of Venus, is multiplied by the six and a half that is the amount of the variation of the size of Venus relative to one, the farthest distance of Venus is 1070, which is the nearest distance of the sun; its mean distance is therefore 618. They measured the diameter of Venus against the diameter of the sun when it was at its mean distance and found it one part in ten of the diameter of the sun. When a tenth of the 618 parts is taken, it is 61 parts and four fifths; when that is divided by the 201½, it is a quarter and half a tenth of the diameter of the earth, and a little that is not to be counted. Therefore its diameter subtends 32 minutes and 27 seconds of the circle of the sphere; when that is multiplied in length, breadth and depth, the size of Venus is about one part in thirty-six of the size of the earth.\footnote{166 × 6½ is 1079; Nallino keeps the text's 1070, the nearest distance of the sun in chapter 30, since the mean distance 618 is half of 166 + 1070. 61⅘ divided by 201½ is 0.307 (Nallino).}\par
\par As for the centres of the epicycles of Mercury and Venus, their course on the circle of the eccentric is like the course of the centre of the epicycle of the sun. The size of the diameter of the epicycle of each of them is known from what lies between the farthest distance and its first station, which is its greatest distance from the sun on the side preceding in the order of the signs. The greatest distance of Mercury from the sun is 26 parts, when the sun is opposite the centre of the epicycle and the centre of the epicycle is at the farthest distance of the eccentric; Mercury is then once ahead of the sun and once behind it. The greatest distance of Venus from the sun is 46 degrees, when the sun is opposite the centre of the epicycle and the centre of the epicycle is at the point of the farthest distance of the eccentric; Venus is then once in front of the sun, once ahead of it and once behind it. The least distance of Venus from the sun, from the first station to the second, is 41 parts, and the least distance of Mercury from the sun, the amount between the two stations, is 21 parts. From this it is clear that the diameter of the epicycle of Mercury subtends 47 degrees, and the diameter of the epicycle of Venus 87 degrees.\footnote{The apparent diameter of the epicycle is the sum of the greatest and the least elongation: 26 + 21 = 47 and 46 + 41 = 87 (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary). The numbers are Ptolemy's (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{184}As for Mars, its distance and the size of its body are according to what was said of it when they measured the variation of its size. They also reported that they found its size at its nearest distance to be seven times its size at its farthest distance. Its nearest distance is the farthest distance of the sun, which by our measurement is 1146; multiplied by the seven, that comes to 8022, and its mean distance is 4584. They measured it at its mean distance and found its diameter to be one part in twenty of the diameter of the sun. When its mean distance is divided by the twenty, it comes to 229 times and a fifth; when that is divided by 201½, the diameter of the earth, its diameter is about once the diameter of the earth and less than a seventh. Therefore the diameter of Mars subtends about 2 degrees, 1 minute and 37 seconds of the circle of the sphere; when that is multiplied in length, breadth and depth, the size of Mars is once and a third the size of the earth, less a little that is not to be counted. The size and the extent of its epicycle are known from the motion of the star Mars from its first station to its second. The epicycle moves 0;31 a day, and Mars moves on the epicycle 0;28 a day, so that its motion is left at 3 minutes; from this it is known that it stays in a sign five months and six, because of the variation of its motion as it is seen. In truth it neither increases nor decreases: it is one motion, for all time, for it and for the other stars. The diameter of the epicycle of Mars subtends 82 degrees and 28 minutes.\footnote{1146 × 7 = 8022, and (1146 + 8022)/2 = 4584; 4584/20 = 229⅕, and 229⅕/201½ = 1.14. Nallino would read 82;21, which Ptolemy's ratio of the radius of the epicycle of Mars to that of its eccentric, 39;30 to 60, gives (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par As for the distance of Jupiter and its size, they found its size at its nearest distance to its size at its farthest distance to be as thirty-seven to twenty-three, which is one and a half and a ninth. When that is multiplied by the farthest distance of Mars, which is 8022, the farthest distance of Jupiter comes to about 12,924, and its mean distance is likewise 10,473. They found the size of Jupiter at its mean distance to be one part in twelve of the diameter of the sun. When its mean distance is divided, its diameter comes to about 872 and a half and a quarter; when that is compared with the 201½, it is four times and a third the diameter of the earth, less a little. When that is multiplied in length, breadth and depth, its size is about eighty-one times the size of the earth, and its diameter subtends about 8;18 of the circle of the sphere. From [its motion from] its first station to its second, and the motion of its epicycle, which is five minutes a day in the order of the signs, while its motion in the lower part of its epicycle is 54 minutes each day, seemingly towards the west, it is clear that the diameter of its epicycle subtends 22 degrees.\footnote{37/23 is 1.609, one and a half and a ninth 1.611; 8022 × 1 11/18 = 12,924 and (8022 + 12,924)/2 = 10,473. The translation reads 872 with Nallino's translation, as the division gives (10,473/12 = 872¾); the text has 892. The bracketed words are Nallino's conjecture. From Ptolemy's ratio of the epicycle, 11;30 to 60, the diameter of the epicycle comes to 22;6 (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{185}As for the distance of Saturn, the variation of its size to the sight between its nearest and its farthest distance is, as they found it, as one and two fifths to one, which is as seven to five. When that is multiplied by the farthest distance of Jupiter, the farthest distance of Saturn comes to 18,094, and its mean distance is therefore 15,509. They found the diameter of Saturn at its mean distance to be one part in eighteen of the diameter of the sun; when its mean distance is divided by that, the diameter of Saturn comes to about 861 and a half and an eighth. When that is compared with 201½, the diameter of the earth, its diameter is about four times and a sixth and an eighth the diameter of the earth; when that is multiplied in length, breadth and depth, the size of Saturn is about seventy-nine times the size of the earth. The diameter of Saturn subtends 8;12 of the circle of the sphere. The extent of its epicycle is known [from its motion] from its first station to its second: the motion of its epicycle is two minutes a day, and its motion in the lower part of the epicycle 0;57 a day, seen as if towards the west; so the diameter of its epicycle subtends 12;26. The diameter of the sun must subtend 39;48 of the circle [of the sphere].\footnote{12,924 × 7/5 = 18,094 (the codex has 18,074) and (12,924 + 18,094)/2 = 15,509; 15,509/18 is 861 11/18 (Nallino). The bracketed words are Nallino's. From Ptolemy's ratio of the epicycle, 6;30 to 60, its diameter comes to 12;26 (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary). The last sentence makes no sense here: al-Battānī's mean apparent diameter of the sun is 0;32,30 (chapter 30), and Schiaparelli takes the sentence for a senseless interpolation (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par As for the distance of the fixed stars and their size, they reported fifteen stars of the first magnitude and said that their distance is, in the manner we have described, about nineteen thousand times the radius of the earth. They measured their size against the sun and found it one part in twenty of the sun. When their distance is divided by that, the diameter of each of these stars is 950; compared with the diameter of the earth, that is about four times and two thirds and half a tenth of it. When that is multiplied in length, breadth and depth, the size of each of these stars is about one hundred and five times the size of the earth. We have divided the fixed stars that are in the constellations into six magnitudes; each class below these fifteen stars mentioned decreases in size until it comes to the sixth magnitude, where the size of a star is sixteen times the size of the earth. So the greatest of the created bodies of the world is the sun; the second, the fifteen stars mentioned of the first magnitude; the third, Jupiter; the fourth, Saturn; the fifth, the rest of the fixed stars, of the second magnitude [to the sixth]; the sixth, Mars; the seventh, the earth; the eighth, the moon; the ninth, Venus; and the tenth, Mercury, the smallest of the bodies of the world.\footnote{950/201½ is 4.71, and (4.71)³ = 105. The bracketed words are supplied by Nallino.}\par
\par \ArPage{186}Whoever wishes to repeat the test of any of these diameters should take an alidade and fit in it two pierced sights, with their holes facing each other; let the hole next to the eye be small, and the one next to the star of a size to take in the whole body of the star, neither more nor less. Let them measure the sun with another alidade in the same way, know the diameter of the hole with which they measured the sun, and compare with it the hole with which they measured the star; and let the measurement be made at one and the same place of the horizon.\par
\par What remains of what we have described of the amounts of the stars is what their diameters subtend of the circle of the sphere at their mean distance; we shall also show the amounts that they must subtend in the measurement when they are at their farthest distance and at their nearest distance. The Sun: the diameter of the sun, we have shown, is 39;48, and we keep it so at all the degrees of its distances, since its change amounts to nothing sensible. The Warrior: the diameter of Saturn subtends at its farthest distance 16;22, at the mean 36;39 and at the nearest 33;36. Jupiter: the diameter of Jupiter subtends at its farthest distance 35;36, at the mean 14;45 and at the nearest 29;36. Mars: the diameter of Mars subtends of the circle of the sphere at its farthest distance 36;10, at the mean 16;14 and at the nearest 33;36. Venus: the diameter of Venus subtends of the sphere at its farthest distance 0;10, at the mean 0;39 and at the nearest 0;36. The Scribe: the diameter of Mercury subtends of the circle of the sphere at its farthest distance 0;33, at the mean 0;6 and at the nearest 0;14. The Moon: the diameter of the moon has been shown to subtend of the circle of the sphere at its farthest distance 0;29,40, at the mean 0;39,36 and at the nearest 0;36,22.\footnote{Schiaparelli and Nallino take this passage for an interpolation. Its numbers are wrong past repair in the codex and in Plato of Tivoli alike; the mean diameters are mostly smaller than the extreme ones; the sun is again given 39;48; and only here are Saturn and Mercury called al-Muqātil, «the Warrior», and al-Kātib, «the Scribe», names used by the Arabs of north-west Africa and Spain. From al-Battānī's distances and the mean diameter of the sun, 0;32,30, Schiaparelli computes what the passage should have given at the farthest, the mean and the nearest distance: Saturn 0;1,33, 0;1,48, 0;2,10; Jupiter 0;2,12, 0;2,42, 0;3,32; Mars 0;0,56, 0;1,37, 0;6,30; Venus 0;1,53, 0;3,15, 0;12,6; Mercury 0;1,30, 0;2,10, 0;3,54; the moon 0;29,30, 0;32,25, 0;35,20 (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par These amounts vary between the distances mentioned according to the degrees of the stars in their distances, and that is known from their equations. The distance of the star from the point of the farthest distance of the epicycle, and from the point of the nearest distance, is known from the mean equation corrected by the fifth or the seventh column; the distance of the centre of the epicycle from the point of the farthest distance of the eccentric is known from the equation of the anomaly. From these two equations the degree of the star in its distance and nearness to the earth is known, when that is related to the sixty that are the radius, as we have shown in knowing the distances of the moon from the variation of its motions.\footnote{The chapter on the distances of the moon is chapter 39 (Nallino).}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 51. On knowing by observation the motion of the fixed stars, which move in their sphere or whose sphere moves with them, and on knowing the positions of those of them that are needed from the tables}{51}{\ArPage{187}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: The states of the fixed stars in their rising, their setting, their culmination and their stay above the earth and below it, and the state of those of them that are near the pole or far from it in every locality, we have mentioned before in this book. The fixed stars revolve about the two poles of the ecliptic and have not departed from it since a motion of theirs became known; likewise their latitude is one latitude, from which they do not change, and they are in this state as long as no change in their motion has been known. Likewise the distances between them have stayed in one state since they were observed, and for that reason they are called the fixed stars: what is meant is that their distances are fixed. For their motion is all together one motion, as if they were in one sphere: either they move together in it, or it moves them by its motion and carries them from the azimuth of one place of the sphere to the azimuth of another place of the sphere, from the west to the east, like the other motions of the running stars, which are the seven planets.\par
\par We found their positions in longitude and latitude in the book of Ptolemy for the first year of the reign of Antoninus, which is the year 886 of the reign of Nabonassar. One of the observations of them on which Ptolemy worked was the observation that Menelaus reported from the year 845 of the reign of that Nabonassar. Menelaus reported that the northern one of the stars between the two eyes of the Scorpion was in that year, when examined with the moon with the armillary sphere, at 5 parts and 55 minutes of Scorpio; and by what Ptolemy set down in the book, the Heart of the Lion must have been in that year at 2 parts and a sixth of a part of Leo, and the Yemeni Shiʿrā at 17 degrees of Gemini.\footnote{Ptolemy (\emph{Almagest} VII 4) gives the year 885 of Nabonassar for the first year of Antoninus, 137-138; aṣ-Ṣūfī also has 886 (Nallino). Menelaus observed the star, β Scorpii, at Rome in 98 (\emph{Almagest} VII 3; Nallino's commentary). The Heart of the Lion is Regulus, the Yemeni Shiʿrā Sirius.}\par
\par \ArPage{188}We observed these stars that we have mentioned, and other fixed stars, many times in successive years. One of the observations on which we rely was in the year 1191 of the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn: we observed with the moon and with the passage of the stars through the middle of the sky, and so found their distances from the equator and the parts of the signs that culminate with them; then from that we found at which parts of the signs their positions fall in longitude, and their latitudes from the circle of the signs on the side on which we found them. We found the northern one of the stars between the eyes of the Scorpion at 17;45 of Scorpio, the Yemeni Shiʿrā at 28;50 of Gemini, and the Heart of the Lion at 14 of Leo. The year in which we made this observation was the year 1627 of the reign of Nabonassar mentioned. When we divide these 11 parts and 50 minutes between their first positions and the positions they had reached at the time of our observation by the 782 years between the two observations, we find their motion to be one degree in every 66 solar years; so we have set it down in the table of the motions of the fixed stars, which are found from it by the collected and the single years and the months. Likewise we have added these 11 parts and half a third to the positions that we found set down in the book of Ptolemy, and set down their positions in the year 1191 of the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn. In what we observed of many stars we found no evident variation in latitude, except what is of no account and can be passed over. So we have made tables for them, in which we have set down their positions in longitude and latitude, the side and the magnitude, so that the positions that they reach after this year are known from their motions taken from the tables, when these are added to their positions in the year 1191; and likewise their positions before this year are known by what we have said.\footnote{The year 1191 began on 1 September 879 (Nallino). 17;45 − 5;55, 28;50 − 17 and 14 − 2;10 are each 11;50. 11;50 in 782 years is 54.48″ a year, and one degree in 66 years 54″ 33‴ (Nallino's commentary). «Half a third», 11;10, is Nallino's reading; the codex and Plato of Tivoli have «a half and a third», 11;50. Al-Battānī took 37′ from the 11;50 for the 41 years between Menelaus and Ptolemy's catalogue, at al-Battānī's own rate; but Ptolemy, at a rate of 36″ a year, had added only 25′ to the longitudes of Menelaus, so that 11;25, not 11;10, should have been added to Ptolemy's longitudes; aṣ-Ṣūfī avoided the error (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par The stars that Ptolemy mentions in the book of the \emph{Almagest} are one thousand and twenty-two in number, besides the stars of the Lock, the Solitary One and al-Mirzam. Ptolemy arranged their size in six degrees, the greatest of which is the first magnitude and the smallest the sixth, and stated that these stars make up forty-five figures: of them fifteen constellations are on the southern side, and six constellations of the southern signs, which are Libra, Scorpio, Sagittarius, Capricorn, Aquarius and Pisces; on the northern side eighteen constellations, and the six remaining constellations of the signs. Their sides may change over a long time, so that the northern ones become southern and the southern northern.\footnote{Ptolemy has forty-eight constellations, twenty-one of them north of the zodiac; Nallino takes the forty-five and the eighteen of the text for errors. The Lock is Coma Berenices, which Ptolemy leaves out of the count of 1022; the Solitary One is α Hydrae; al-Mirzam is a name the Arabs give to several stars that precede a bright one (Nallino, after aṣ-Ṣūfī).}\par
\par \ArPage{189}Of these northern stars in the constellations of the signs and others are: the stars of Aries, which are 13, among them the two Sharaṭ, on its horns, and al-Buṭayn, on its rump; then the stars of Taurus, 33, the Pleiades on its horn and al-Dabarān on its back; then Gemini, whose stars are 18, among them the front of the two arms, al-Haqʿa and al-Hanʿa; then Cancer, whose stars are 9, among them al-Nathra; then Leo, whose stars are 27, among them the Lock, al-Ṭarf, and al-Jabha, which is the Heart of the Lion, al-Zubra and al-Ṣarfa; then Virgo, whose stars are 26, among them al-ʿAwwāʾ and the unarmed Simāk. This is what is in the northern half of the constellations of the signs.\footnote{The named stars, apart from the Lock, are lunar mansions of the Arabs, the twenty-eight stations of the moon that divide the zodiac (Nallino's commentary). Aṣ-Ṣūfī finds several of the placements wrong: al-Buṭayn is three stars in a triangle on the belly of the Ram, and al-Dabarān is the bright red star in the southern eye of the Bull (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par In the southern side of the constellations of the signs are Libra, whose stars are 8, among them al-Ghafr; then Scorpio, which has 21 stars, among them the two Zubānā, al-Iklīl, the Heart and al-Shawla; then Sagittarius, which has 31 stars, among them al-Naʿāʾim and al-Balda; then Capricorn, which has 28 stars, among them Saʿd al-Dhābiḥ and Saʿd Bulaʿ; then Aquarius, which has 42 stars, among them Saʿd al-Suʿūd and Saʿd al-Akhbiya; then Pisces, which has 34 stars, among them the front Spout, the rear Spout [and al-Rishāʾ]. All the stars in the constellations of the signs are three hundred and forty-six; all the stars in the eighteen northern constellations named in the table are three hundred and sixty; all the stars in these fifteen southern constellations are three hundred and sixteen. All these stars counted are one thousand and twenty-two: of them fifteen are of the first magnitude, 45 of the second, 208 of the third, 474 of the fourth, 217 of the fifth and 49 of the sixth; the cloudy ones, like clouds, are five, and the dark ones 9; and the stars of the Lock, the Solitary One and al-Mirzam. Of these stars we have set down those for which we found a known temperament in the book of Ptolemy, of the constellations outside the circle of the signs, above all the great ones; for those in the constellations of the signs we have mentioned their temperament and their force, resembling the force of the two luminaries and the wandering stars.\footnote{The bracketed words follow Nallino's emendanda (Part II, p. XXXI), which add «\RL{\ArNote والرِّشَاء}», «and al-Rishāʾ», after «the rear Spout» (p. 189, line 7). With them the stars named in the twelve signs, apart from the Lock, are the twenty-eight lunar mansions of the Arabs; al-Rishāʾ, the Rope, is the last. The stars counted in the twelve signs come to 290, not 346, since the stars outside the figures are not counted among them (Nallino). 346 + 360 + 316 = 1022, and 15 + 45 + 208 + 474 + 217 + 49 + 5 + 9 = 1022. Nallino takes «temperament» here for the data that fix a star's place, so that al-Battānī listed the stars whose places were not in doubt in the Arabic copies of the \emph{Almagest} (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{190}After that we have set down, for those of the first and the second magnitude and some of the third, separate tables, in which we give their distances from the equator, half their stay above the earth, their altitude in the middle of the sky, and the parts of the signs with which they rise, culminate and set, where the altitude of the northern pole above the horizon is 36 parts, which is the latitude of the city of al-Raqqa; we have given these states of theirs described for the year 1211 of the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn. In the tables before these we have set down their positions in longitude from the beginning of Aries. If you wish to know the position of any of the fixed stars set down in the tables that you wish, take their motion in the years that come after the year 1191 of the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn, add it to their positions set down in the tables, and cast that off from the beginning of Aries: where the count ends for you, there is the position of the star in the sign to which it comes. Its latitude is that set down in the table, on the side set down, and so too its magnitude among the six. Likewise you know the parts with which they rise, set and culminate from the later tables, by taking what is in each of the three columns and casting it off from the beginning of Aries; after this year for which we have set down these states in these tables, you add to what is in the tables as much as is due, since there is much change, whose computation must be carried out anew at every time. We have set it down for our time only so that it may be known in it approximately; the method by which these things are known is explained earlier in this book. From these tables the nine configurations mentioned that the stars have with the sun are known in our time, above all for the great ones, which are those set down in the tables.\footnote{The year 1211 began on 1 September 899; the codex has 1251 (Nallino).}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 52. On knowing what the astrologers have reported in saying that the sphere has a motion of displacement, advancing and retreating, and on the evident falsity of what they say}{52}{\ArPage{190}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: It is mentioned in Ptolemy's book that the astrologers claimed that the sphere has a motion of displacement, slow in time, one degree in every 80 years, and they said that this motion goes as far as 8 parts, advancing and then retreating. What they mean is that the sphere of the signs moves 8 parts from west to east, in the same direction as the motion of the sphere of the fixed stars, and then moves 8 parts from east to west, contrary to the first motion. Yet at the same time it must move with the motion of the fixed stars, the first motion, which is from west to east; and this cannot be, nor can it come about, unless something other than it moves it, or else the fixed stars are what move upon it; for one body cannot move with two motions in two different directions at once.\footnote{Not Ptolemy but Theon of Alexandria, in the fourth century, reports this doctrine of «some of the ancient astrologers» in a work on Ptolemy's Handy Tables, adding that Ptolemy does not hold it, since without it the equinoxes computed from the tables agree with those found with instruments. Theon's remark probably means that Ptolemy passed over the doctrine in silence; al-Battānī took it to mean that Ptolemy had mentioned and refuted it (Nallino's commentary). «The astrologers» renders «aṣḥāb al-ṭilsamāt», literally «the people of the talismans», which here stands for the Greek apotelesmatikoi, the astrologers (Nallino). The doctrine is known as the trepidation of the fixed stars.}\par
\par \ArPage{191}They stated that the end of the advance was 128 Egyptian years before the reign of Augustus, which is the year 166 of Alexander the Macedonian, and that one must take the years after that and reckon one degree for every 80 years of them; what results from that [is subtracted] until it reaches 8 degrees, and what remains is added to the motions of the stars computed before. When the 8 are complete, they are dropped, and what exceeds 8 is taken as it stands and added to the positions of the stars until 8 are complete; then the first procedure is repeated.\footnote{The era of Alexander the Macedonian is the era from Alexander's death, the era of Philip (chapter 32). In Ptolemy's canon of kings the reign of Augustus begins when 294 years of this era are complete, and 294 − 128 = 166. The bracketed words are supplied by Nallino. In Theon's account the degrees found are subtracted from 8, and the remainder is added to the positions of the sun, the moon and the five planets computed from the tables (Nallino's commentary). «Computed before» renders «al-mutaqaddima», Nallino's reading for the codex's «al-muqaddama»; Nallino's translation renders the words as «the direct motion of the stars».}\par
\par The length of the year on which those who held this view worked was greater than 365 days and a quarter of a day by a fifth of an hour or about that; for that reason the mean motion of the sun in the Egyptian year comes to 359;44,43. Hipparchus, who came after them, worked with a year of 365 days and a quarter of a day only, so that the motion of the sun in the time of the Egyptian year comes to 359;45,13; and Hipparchus claimed to have established that it is less than the quarter of a day. Then Ptolemy observed 285 years after Hipparchus and found the length of the year, on which Ptolemy worked, to be 365 days and less than a quarter of a day by one part in 300, so that the motion of the sun in the Egyptian year came to 359;45,25. We observed 743 years after Ptolemy and found the length of the year to be 365 days and less than a quarter of a day by 3 parts and two fifths of a part of 360, so that the motion of the sun in the Egyptian year became 359;45,46.\footnote{The translation follows Nallino's emendanda (Part II, p. XXXI) in reading «\RL{\ArNote او نحوه}», «or about that», for «\RL{\ArNote ونحوه}» (p. 191, line 3). The year of the astrologers, 365 days 6 hours 12 minutes, is the year that chapter 27 ascribes to some of the ancient people of Egypt and Babylon; Letronne and Martin show that these astrologers lived after Hipparchus, perhaps before Ptolemy (Nallino's commentary). Ptolemy's year is 365 days 5 hours 55 minutes 12 seconds, al-Battānī's 365 days 5 hours 46 minutes 24 seconds (Nallino). The 285 and the 743 years are the intervals between the autumnal equinoxes observed by Hipparchus, Ptolemy and al-Battānī (chapter 27; the codex has 943 for 743). The four motions are 360 parts multiplied by 365 days and divided by the four years; for the first three the codex has 359;44,48, 359;41,53 and 359;44,25, which Nallino corrects.}\par
\par All these motions have been increasing since the time of Nabonassar or thereabouts. So it is refuted that what has been described agrees with anything in their statement: not in the number of the parts, not in the amount of the motion, and not in the increase and the decrease. Rather, we see this increase come without order in its slowness and speed: Ptolemy corrected Hipparchus by nearly a day in about 300 years, and we have corrected Ptolemy by 4 days and a quarter in about 750 years, besides the day by which Ptolemy had corrected Hipparchus.\footnote{«What has been described» renders «what they described»; Nallino's translation takes the sentence to say that what the astrologers held about the number of degrees, the amount of the motion and the increase and decrease is void. Nallino's translation has «about 4½ days»; neither Nallino's notes nor the corrigenda mention the difference from the Arabic. By chapter 27, al-Battānī's year is shorter than Ptolemy's by 2;12 parts of a day of 360 parts, which in 743 years make 4.54 days; Ptolemy's year is shorter than 365 days and a quarter by 1/300 of a day, which in 285 years make 0.95 of a day.}\par
\par If this excess has come about only from an error in the instruments with which the observations were made, from their division or from their changing over a long time, then it must of necessity occur in our observations after a period of time, since in our observations we measure only against those observations. And if it comes from a motion in the sphere whose true nature has not become clear to us, and which neither we nor any of those before us have grasped, then seeking the truth and following it means observing at every time, so that whatever is found and caught at that time is set right, as it was set right in the time before.\footnote{Nallino suggests that «\RL{\ArNote خطأٌ}», «an error», may be missing after «occur»; the Latin translation has «errors will of necessity befall our observations».}\par
\par \ArPage{192}As for what may be conjectured, and what the measurements require: since this excess is common to all the motions of the stars, it has come about only from the motion of the sphere of the fixed stars. For Ptolemy stated that this motion, by what was found from Ptolemy's own observations and also by what was worked with before, is one degree in every 100 years; but between Ptolemy's observations and the observations they were compared with there was not a period long enough for an evident change in such a motion to appear, for between the observation that Ptolemy made and the observation it was compared with there are only about 200 years. As the time between us and Ptolemy has grown long, the increase in this motion has become evident, so that it has been found to be one degree in every 66 solar years; and it is from the difference between these two motions that the excess in all of them has come.\footnote{«Require» follows Nallino's reading «\RL{\ArNote يوجبه}»; the codex has «\RL{\ArNote يوحيه}», «suggests». Nallino suggests reading 280, the interval between Hipparchus and Ptolemy, for the 200 years of the codex and of Plato of Tivoli. The rates of one degree in 100 years and one degree in 66 years differ by 18.5″ a year, against the 21.7″ by which al-Battānī's motion of the sun in an Egyptian year exceeds Ptolemy's (chapter 27).}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 53. On knowing the times of the revolutions of the years and their ascendants, and the positions of the stars when [the sun] returns to the part in which it was at the root}{53}{\ArPage{192}}{}
\par \ArPage{193}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the revolution of any year you like of the years of nativities and of other things that are reckoned from a fixed beginning — that is, the time at which the sun returns to the part in which it was at the time of the beginning — find the year of the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn in which the beginning was, and also the year in which you wish to know the revolution. Subtract the smaller from the larger: what remains is the complete years that have passed for the nativity or the beginning you want, up to the same day of the Roman month on which the nativity or the beginning was. Multiply these complete years by 86 degrees and 36 minutes, which is the excess of the length of the year over the complete days; cast off the circles from the product, and divide whatever remains below a circle by 15: the result is equal hours. Add them to the hours of computation at the root. If they come to less than 24, take them as they are, with the day of the month that has passed; if they exceed 24, cast off 24 from them and add one day to the days of the month that have passed. Keep the days and the hours that you then have. If the year is a leap year and Subāṭ has run out, subtract one day from the days of the month that have passed, and the result is the day of computation; if the year is not a leap year, leave the days as they are, and the days of the month that have passed and the hours that result are the days and the hours of computation. With them find the mean of the sun in the year you want, in the way described before: the mean of the sun comes out exactly the same as the mean at the root.\footnote{The revolution of a year is the moment at which the sun returns to the longitude it had at a birth, or at another beginning such as that of a city or a people; astrologers draw their predictions from it (Nallino's commentary). The root is the moment of that beginning. The heading says only «when it returns»; the text shows that the sun is meant, while Nallino's translation has the planets return to the degree they held at the beginning. 86;36 is the 5 hours 46 minutes 24 seconds by which al-Battānī's year exceeds 365 days, at 15 parts to the hour (Nallino). The rule takes the whole days cast off with the circles to be the leap days that have passed since the root, but they can be fewer, and the day then comes out one too late: after 4 years nothing is cast off, since 4 × 86;36 is 346;24, while one leap day always passes, and the rule subtracts it only when the root falls in a leap year after Subāṭ. A day's error makes the mean sun differ from that of the root by 0;59,8, the sun's motion in a day (chapter 27), and the comparison of the true sun below turns this difference into the missing 24 hours.}\par
\par Or, if you like, multiply the years you have by 3 parts and 24 minutes, which is the amount by which the length of the year falls short of the full quarter of a day above 365, and keep the product. Then cast off the years you have four by four: if one remains, take 90 parts for it; if two remain, take 180 for them; if three remain, take 270 for them; and if four remain, take 360 for them. From whichever of these you have, subtract what you kept, and for every 15 degrees of the remainder reckon an hour; add it to the hours of computation in the same way. The meaning of these two ways is one.\footnote{3;24 is the quarter of a day, 90 parts, less 86;36 (Nallino). The two ways agree: the years times 86;36 are the years times 90 less the years times 3;24, and the years times 90 leave 90, 180, 270 or 360 when the circles are cast off. From 29 years on, the product can exceed the parts taken, and 360 must then be added to them first.}\par
\par Whatever comes out for you by either of the two ways is the date. Compute the mean of the sun for it in the first manner, as usual, then compute its true position. If the true position of the sun comes out the same as the first position, that is the time of the revolution. If it exceeds the true position of the sun at the root, see how much the excess amounts to in the sun's motion in an hour and subtract that from the hours of computation; if it falls short, see how much it falls short in the sun's motion in an hour and add that to the hours of computation, so that you correct the hours of computation by which you bring the sun to the true position in which it was at the root. With them compute also the position of the moon and of the other wandering stars. Then convert them into the hours of the unequal days as they are found, by subtracting the equation of the days that stands opposite the part of the sun in the ascensions of the right sphere, after you know its amount in equal hours: what results is equal hours after noon, with which you find the ascendant and the cardines in the usual way.\footnote{Converting the hours turns mean time into true time (Nallino). The equation of the days is that of chapter 29; chapter 34 subtracts it to turn true hours into mean, so that it must be added here, as the short chapter E after chapter 57 does; the text has «subtract». The cardines are those of chapter 35.}\par
\par This variation in the true position of the sun arises only from the motion of its apogee in the years between the year of the root and the year of the revolution. When the sun is near the apogee, the change is only slight and imperceptible, and likewise near its perigee; the farther it moves from these two points, the greater the variation in it. It is known that, whenever 106 years have passed, the times of the revolutions come one day before the day of the month on which they were at the root.\footnote{The rule about the apogee is the wrong way round. At the revolution the mean sun is the same as at the root, so the motion of the apogee changes the sun's anomaly by the same arc, and the true sun moves by that arc times the rate at which the equation changes with the anomaly. That rate is greatest at the apogee and the perigee, where the equation is nothing, and nothing where the equation is greatest. With the radius 2;4,45 of the sun's epicycle (chapter 28), one degree of the apogee's motion, 66 years at al-Battānī's rate (chapter 45), moves the true sun by 2.0′ at the apogee, by 2.2′ at the perigee and by about 5″ at 90° from the apogee. The Roman year of 365 days and a quarter exceeds al-Battānī's year by 13 minutes 36 seconds, which in 106 years make 24 hours 1 minute 36 seconds (Nallino).}\par
\par We have made separate tables for the times of the mean revolutions of the years and the mean motions of the stars in them, and have explained how to work with them in the last of the chapters, at the close of the book, so that working with them is easy when it is needed.\footnote{The tables are on ff. 239v–240r of the codex; their use is explained in the fifth of the short chapters after chapter 57 (Nallino).}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 54. On establishing the amounts of the applications as they depend on the latitudes of the stars}{54}{\ArPage{194}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: Since the circle of the signs is divided into twelve signs, and nothing measures twelve, among the numbers of which it has an exact part, I mean a fixed ratio, except six twice, four three times, three four times and two six times, these amounts are used and no others. They are four configurations besides the conjunction. Among them is the opposition, which is of six signs and contains two right angles and 180 degrees; the reason for its force is evident in itself. After it comes the configuration of the quadrature, which is half the opposition and contains three signs, one right angle and 90 parts; then the trine, which contains four signs, a right angle and a third of an angle, and 120 degrees; then the sextile, which is half the trine and contains two signs, two thirds of a right angle and 60 degrees. The conjunction, however, is not a configuration. These parts of the sphere of the signs are the ones that share configurations with one another, and no others; signs in this relation to one another are called the connected signs, and the rest, which are not connected with one another and share no configuration, are those that are one sign, five signs or seven signs apart.\footnote{These are Ptolemy's aspects (\emph{Tetrabiblos} I 14; Nallino). Plato of Tivoli adds signs eleven apart, which are as unconnected as those one sign apart.}\par
\par Since the rays of the stars, when they share in the four configurations, meet only at the centre of the earth and come together there, attention to their latitudes in that case is superfluous; but their latitudes need attention mostly at the conjunction alone. For when the meeting of two stars is a bodily meeting and their latitudes are not together on one side and of one amount, their conjunction is not a true conjunction, and the lower of them does not hide the higher; for their conjunction, when it is in the state we have mentioned, takes place in longitude and in latitude, and when they are not on one side and of one amount, their conjunction is in longitude without latitude, and that conjunction is not called a true bodily union, above all when they are on different sides, for then no meeting is counted for them at all. When they are united bodily in longitude, latitude and side, they remain in conjunction until one of them moves away from the other by half of their two bodies.\footnote{That is, until their centres are apart by half the sum of their diameters. Ptolemy (\emph{Tetrabiblos} I 24) leaves the interval unstated; the two Latin versions of the \emph{Tetrabiblos} made from the Arabic give half the two bodies, as here (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{195}It is also said of the applications that when stars are going towards the sextile, the quadrature or the opposition of other stars, they are applying to them; when they equal them in the number [of degrees], the application is complete; and when the light ones have passed the heavy ones, they are separating from the heavy ones until they apply to other stars; if they do not apply to others, they are called separating.\footnote{The bracketed words are supplied by Nallino. The light stars are the faster ones, the heavy the slower.}\par
\par Likewise of the lights of the stars and their force in the applications it is said that the force of the sun in the applications reaches 15 degrees before it and as many behind it; the force of the moon reaches 12 degrees before and behind it; likewise the force of Jupiter reaches 12 degrees before and behind it as well; the force of Venus 8 parts before and behind it; the force of Mars 7 parts before and behind it; likewise the force of Mercury 7 parts before and behind it; and likewise the force of Saturn 7 parts before and behind it too. As for the force of the strong amounts, they are the amounts that we mentioned in the chapter on the size of the bodies, with the arcs of the circle of the sphere that their diameters subtend, above all of the upper ones; we have explained this fully before.\footnote{Ptolemy gives Jupiter 12 degrees and Venus 8 (\emph{Tetrabiblos} III 10); Porphyry gives the sun 15 degrees on each side, the moon 12, Saturn and Jupiter 9, Mars 8, Venus and Mercury 7 (Nallino's commentary). The chapter on the size of the bodies is chapter 50 (Nallino).}\par
\par It has also been said that the parts of the signs whose distances from the two solstitial points, the head of Cancer and the head of Capricorn, are equal, on the preceding side and on the following side, look at one another and are equal in force, because the day of each of the two parts is equal to the day of the other. For example, 10 parts of Cancer equal 20 parts of Gemini in force, because the distances of these two parts from the beginning of Cancer are equal and the day of one equals the day of the other; those of these parts that look at one another are called equal in force for this reason. Likewise the parts equally distant from the head of Capricorn are also equal in force: for example, a star at 5 parts of Sagittarius and another star at 25 parts of Capricorn are, at these two distances, equal in force.\footnote{The parts that look at one another and are equal in force are Ptolemy's (\emph{Tetrabiblos} I 16; Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{196}Likewise also the parts whose distances from one of the two equinoctial points are equal, on the preceding side of the signs and on the following side, are called the commanding and the obeying; they are also said to be the high and the low. Those that hear and obey [the others] are the low ones, and those that are obeyed are the high ones. The parts in the southern half of the sphere, from the beginning of Libra to the end of Pisces, are the low ones, and the parts in the northern half of the sphere, from the beginning of Aries to the end of Virgo, are high above those parts; for the amount by which the day increases at these northern parts is the amount by which it decreases at those southern ones, when they are equally distant from one of these two points. For example, 20 degrees of Pisces hear and obey 10 parts of Aries, because the increase of the day at 10 of Aries is equal to the decrease at 20 of Pisces.\footnote{The commanding and obeying parts are Ptolemy's (\emph{Tetrabiblos} I 15; Nallino). The bracketed words are added for the sense.}\par
\par These amounts that we have mentioned in these two kinds may coincide and fall in one of the configurations. So it happens and comes about that the beginning of Sagittarius is connected with the beginning of Aquarius and shares with it the configuration of the sextile, while their distances from the beginning of Capricorn are equal, so that it combines both; likewise the beginning of Pisces shares a configuration with the beginning of Taurus, and the beginning of Pisces hears the beginning of Taurus, so that it combines both as well. This may also happen with the trine, the quadrature and the opposition: the middle of Taurus may be in quadrature with the middle of Leo, the middle of Aquarius in quadrature with the middle of Taurus, the beginning of Taurus in trine with the beginning of Virgo, the beginning of Capricorn in trine with the beginning of Taurus, and the head of Cancer in opposition to the head of Capricorn, while the distances of these parts from the solstitial point and the equinoctial point are equal; and likewise the beginning of Aries is opposite the beginning of Libra.\footnote{The beginnings of Capricorn and of Taurus are in trine, but they are not equally distant from a solstice or an equinox; the pairs in trine that are equally distant are the beginnings of Taurus and Virgo, of Leo and Sagittarius, of Scorpio and Pisces, and of Aquarius and Gemini. Nallino's translation has the same example. The other examples hold.}\par
\par The wandering stars may also apply to the fixed stars, when there is between them the distance of a sextile, a trine, a quadrature or an opposition; likewise the wandering and the fixed stars cast their rays on the circle of the sphere of the signs at different amounts, which increase and decrease with the difference of the latitudes, so that when the amount between the two stars is known, it is known whether they are in one of the configurations of the applications. As for the fixed stars, because of the slowness of their motion no account is taken in these configurations of the application of the wandering stars to them, nor of the casting of their rays on the circle of the signs, their distance from the circle of the signs being always the same; but one looks to the configurations that they have with them at the cardines, and to the bodily union, above all with the sun. As for the wandering stars, one needs to know their distances from one another and the amounts at which they cast their rays on the circle of the signs according to their latitudes, at nativities and in the directions from one to another.\footnote{«Their distance from the circle of the signs being always the same» renders «when their distance from the circle of the signs is one distance»; Nallino's translation has «when they are distant from it by some aspect». The directions are the astrologers' way of timing events by the arc through which a point of the horoscope is carried to the place or the ray of another star.}\par
\par \ArPage{197}As for the opposition, it is evident that it is complete only if the two stars are both on the circle of the signs, or the latitude of each of the two stars is equal to that of the other and they are on different sides. If one of the two stars is on the circle of the signs and the other is inclined from it in latitude, the distance between them is then less than the distance of the opposition by the latitude of the star; and if the latitudes of the two stars are one latitude on one side, the distance between them is less than the opposition by the two latitudes together. As for the quadrature that falls from any of the stars on the circle of the signs, it is always in one state, neither more nor less than 90, whether the latitude is great or small; this is evident on the sphere on whose poles the circles fall. As for the sextile, if the star has a latitude, its ray is cast on the circle of the signs at less than 60 parts from the part in which it is, and its ray of the trine is cast at more than 120 by as much as the sextile falls short.\footnote{The quadrature stays 90° because the two points of the ecliptic 90° from the star are the poles of the great circle drawn through the star at right angles to the ecliptic, and so are 90° from its foot on the ecliptic as well; the rays of 60° and 120° lie on one great circle and meet the ecliptic at two opposite points, so that the sextile and the trine on the ecliptic together make 180° (Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary). Ibn Yūnus objects that by al-Battānī's principles a star with a latitude of 60° has no sextile at all; the astrologers, however, use the rays of the planets only, whose latitudes are far smaller (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par If you wish to know at how many parts a star that has a latitude casts its ray of the sextile and of the trine on the circle of the signs, subtract the latitude of the star from 90 and find the chord of the remainder in the tables of halved chords: it is always the full chord of the second side, which we have mentioned in the chapter on the diagonals of quadrilaterals earlier in this book, and in this chapter in which we are, at this place only. Keep it: it is the chord of the second side. Then find the full chord of the latitude of the star, by taking half its latitude, finding its halved chord and doubling it: the result is the full chord of the latitude of the star. Multiply it by itself and keep the product under its name. Then take the full chord of the second side that you kept and multiply it by 60; to the product add this product of the chord by itself that you kept under its name, and take the root of the sum. Of what results take what exceeds 60, multiply it by itself, and divide the product by the full chord of the second side that you kept; subtract what results from the division from 60: the remainder is the corrected chord. Keep it. Then take the excess of the root over 60 once more, a second time, multiply it by the full chord of the second side that was kept, and divide the product by the corrected chord: the result is the share of the correction. Keep it. Then take the full chord of the latitude of the star multiplied by itself and subtract it from 3600, which is the full chord of the sextile multiplied by itself; take the root of the remainder, and from the root that results subtract the share of the correction that you kept: the remainder is the corrected second side. Note it. Then subtract the full chord of the latitude multiplied by itself from 3600 once more, and divide the remainder by the corrected second side: the result is the chord that you want. Turn it into an arc as you turn full chords into arcs, by taking half of it and finding its arc in the table; double the arc that comes out: the arc that results is the amount of the star's sextile, on whichever side its latitude is. Subtract it from 180: the remainder is the amount of the star's trine. Subtract each of these two amounts from the part of the star, and add each of them to the part of the star as well, and note where the part of the star comes after the addition or the subtraction: the diminished position is the position of its first sextile and trine, and the increased position is the position of the second trine and sextile, from which they fall on the circle of the signs, if God wills.\footnote{The translation reads «\RL{\ArNote التي}» for «\RL{\ArNote الني}» (p. 197, line 13), a misprint that the corrigenda do not list. The chapter on the diagonals of quadrilaterals is chapter 26 (Nallino). The exact amount x of the sextile for a latitude β follows from the right spherical triangle whose hypotenuse is the ray of 60° and whose legs are the latitude and x: cos x = cos 60° / cos β. In the terms of chapter 26, with chords in a circle of radius 1, crd² x = (crd² 60° − crd² β) / cos β. Nallino takes the long rule of the text for the work of an interpolator and restores this one as al-Battānī's; the Alfonsine \emph{Libro de las taulas} gives it as al-Battānī's method (Nallino's commentary). The rule of the text begins with the chord of the second side that an arc of 60° would give, 60 cos β, and ends with the theorem of chapter 26, dividing 3600 − crd² β by a corrected second side. Read as the Arabic has it, the rule is not exact but close: it falls short of the exact amount by less than 1″ at a latitude of 5°, by 13″ at 10° and by 3′ at 20°. Nallino's translation squares the excess of the root over 60 a second time in finding the share of the correction, where the Arabic takes the excess itself; with the square the rule falls short by 7′ at 5° and by 17′ at 10°.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 55. On knowing the ascensions of the signs between the cardines, in the quarters of the sphere}{55}{\ArPage{198}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: What must follow what we have described of the amounts of the rays on the circle of the signs is the knowledge of the ascensions of the signs between the cardines. Their ascensions have been known only in the right sphere, which are their ascensions at the cardine of the middle of the sky and the cardine of the earth, and in the climes, which are their ascensions and settings at the two horizons, the cardine of the ascendant and the cardine of the setting point of the circle of the horizon of each town; and what we have described differs in amount. So what remains of the kind of the ascensions is that you know the ascensions of the signs between these cardines, in the regions of the sphere, so that you know in how many times of the equator one of the signs rises at any place of the sphere. By this you know the amount of the times of the equator that lie between the preceding degree of the sphere of the signs and the following degree, in times of the passage of the preceding degree at that place.\footnote{The translation follows Nallino's emendanda (Part II, p. XXXI) in reading «\RL{\ArNote وفي الاقاليم}», «and in the climes», for «\RL{\ArNote في الأقاليم}» (p. 198, line 5). The cardines are the four points of the ecliptic on the horizon and the meridian (chapter 35). The ascensions between the cardines are what the astrologers need for their directions: to direct one point to another is to find the arc of the equator after which the first reaches the circle of position drawn through the second (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{199}For example, the whole sign of Aries rises at the middle of the sky with 27;53 of the times of the equator and passes the cardine of the earth with as many; in the fourth clime it rises with 19;12 of the times of the equator, and in this clime it sets with the amount with which Libra rises in it, which is 36;34. Between each of these cardines and the cardine next to it there are 6 seasonal hours, which are the hours of one quarter of the quarters of the day and the night: in the two quarters above the earth its hours are hours of the day, and in the two remaining quarters, below the earth, its hours are hours of the night. When the sign of Aries moves away from one of these cardines, the amounts of its ascensions vary, becoming more or less than these amounts that we have mentioned, according to the number of seasonal hours by which the beginning of Aries is distant from the cardine with which the comparison is made. Let us first suppose the beginning of Aries to be 2 seasonal hours from the middle of the sky towards the east: the ascensions of Aries there become less than its ascensions at the middle of the sky by 2;54, which is a third of the difference between its ascensions at the middle of the sky and its ascensions in the clime at the eastern horizon, as the 2 hours by which it is distant from the middle of the sky are a third of the 6 hours between the middle of the sky and the ascendant. When the beginning of Aries is 3 seasonal hours from the middle of the sky on this side, its rising there is less than its rising in the right sphere by 4;20, which is half the difference between its rising in the right sphere and its rising at the horizon of the clime; until it comes to the ascendant, where its rising is less than its rising in the right sphere by 8;41, the whole difference between them in the ascensions. Let us suppose too the beginning of Aries to be 2 seasonal hours from the middle of the sky towards the west: since its settings in this western half are like the ascensions of Libra, its settings at this distance are greater than its ascensions in the right sphere by a third of this difference, which is 2;54; when its distance is 3 hours on this side, its passage there is greater than its passage in the right sphere by half the difference, which is 4;20; until it comes to the western horizon, where its passage and crossing in its setting is greater than its rising in the right sphere by the whole difference, 8;41. Likewise, when its distance is from the cardine of the earth towards the ascendant, it goes the way it goes between the middle of the sky and the ascendant; and when its distance is from the cardine of the earth towards the setting point, the matter is as it was between the middle of the sky and the west.\footnote{The translation follows the corrigenda in reading «\RL{\ArNote الباقِيَيْن}», «remaining», for the misprint «\RL{\ArNote البافِيَيْن}» (p. 198; the corrigenda give line 15 for line 17). 27;53 is the right ascension of Aries with al-Battānī's obliquity of 23;35; 19;12 is its ascension at a latitude of 36° with Ptolemy's obliquity of 23;51, which with al-Battānī's own gives 19;21. The rule makes the ascension change in proportion to the seasonal hours from the meridian: 27;53 − 19;12 = 8;41, of which a third is 2;53,40 and a half 4;20,30, and 27;53 + 8;41 = 36;34.}\par
\par \ArPage{200}If you wish to know the ascensions of any degree you like, in whatever region of the sphere you want, begin by finding the distance of the degree you want, or of the star you want, from one of the cardines. This is found by looking at the degree or the star you want. If it has no latitude, it goes the way of the degree of the signs in which it is: find the times of the hours of the day and of the night of the degree, whichever degree of the sphere you like, which is the degree in which the star is, or another of the degrees of the signs. If the star has a latitude, find the degree that culminates with it and the times of its hours above the earth and below it: find half its stay above the earth and take a sixth of it, which is the times of an hour above the earth; subtract that from 30, and the remainder is the times of its hours below the earth, as we explained at the beginning of the book. You use the degree that culminates with it in place of the degree in which it is [if it has a latitude, and you use the degree in which it is] if it has no latitude; and likewise the times of the hours of the star in place of the times of the hours of its degree that [culminates with it]. If whichever of the two degrees you use is above the earth, take its distance from the part of the middle of the sky by the ascensions of the right sphere; if it is below the earth, take its distance from the part of the cardine of the earth, again by the ascensions of the right sphere. Subtract the ascensions of the degree of the middle of the sky from the ascensions of the degree that you use if it is on the eastern side of the middle of the sky, and subtract the ascensions of that degree from the ascensions of the degree of the middle of the sky if it is on the western side; and do the same with its ascensions and the ascensions of the part of the cardine of the earth in the right sphere, so that you know the distance between the degree you want and the degree of the middle of the sky or of the cardine of the earth in the right sphere. Divide the times of the distance that you have by the times of the hours of the day if the degree used is above the earth, or by the times of the hours of the night if it is below the earth: the hours that result are the distance of the star or of the degree from one of the two cardines, either the cardine of the middle of the sky or the cardine of the earth. Whether the star or the degree is below the earth or above it is known as I shall describe: look at the part that you want to use of the two parts; if it lies between the degree of the ascendant and the degree of the setting point in the order of the signs, that degree is below the earth, and if it lies between the degree of the setting point and the degree of the ascendant in the order of the signs, it is above the earth. You know this another way too: look at half the stay of the star above the earth; if it is more than the times between the degree of the middle of the sky and the degree that culminates with the star, in the right sphere, you know that the star is above the earth, and if half its stay above the earth is the less, you know that it is below the earth. When you know how many seasonal hours the star, or the degree in which it is, is distant from the middle of the sky or from the cardine of the earth, and you wish to know its distance from the ascendant or from the setting point, subtract those hours from 6: the remainder is its distance from one of these two cardines, I mean the cardine of the east or the cardine of the west.\footnote{The words in the first brackets are supplied by Nallino after Plato of Tivoli; for the words in the second the codex has «in which it is», and Nallino follows Plato. For a star with latitude al-Battānī takes the degree that culminates with it for the right ascensions and the degree that rises with it for the oblique ones, where Ptolemy, like Alcabitius and others, treats the points as if they had no latitude (Nallino's commentary). The hours above and below the earth together make 30 times, a twelfth of the circle, since the arcs of the day and of the night make 360.}\par
\par \ArPage{201}Whenever you wish to know the ascensions of any degree you like at the place of the regions of the sphere where that degree happens to be, it is clear from what we have described that by this the amount of the times of the equator between the preceding degree of the sphere of the signs and the degree on the side following it is known, as the amount between two degrees is known by the ascensions of the clime and the ascensions of the right sphere: that is, to know in how many times of the equator the following degree of the sphere of the signs travels to the place where the preceding degree was. Look: if the given preceding degree is between the middle of the sky and the cardine of the earth on the eastern side, it is in the eastern half of the sphere, and if it is between the middle of the sky and the cardine of the earth on the side of the west, it is in the western half. If it is in the eastern half of the sphere, find how many seasonal hours it is from the middle of the sky, the cardine of the earth or the ascendant, whichever you like, and keep them. Then look at the following degree: if it is with it in the eastern half of the sphere, take the distance between the two degrees by the ascensions of the right sphere and the distance between them by the ascensions of the given clime. If the two numbers are equal, that is the distance of the preceding degree from the following degree in times of the equator. If they differ, subtract the smaller from the larger and take a sixth of the remainder, which is the share of one hour of the difference; multiply it by the hours of the distance of the preceding degree from whichever of the cardines you like to make the comparison with, I mean the middle of the sky, the cardine of the ascendant or the cardine of the earth. If you multiplied it by the hours of the distance of the degree from the middle of the sky or the cardine of the earth, add the product to the times of the ascensions between the two degrees in the right sphere if they are less than those between them by the ascensions of the clime, and subtract it from them if they are the greater; and if you multiplied it by the hours of the distance of the degree from the ascendant, add the product to the times between the two degrees by the ascensions of the clime if they are the less, and subtract it from them if they are the greater. What the times of the ascensions of the cardine with which you compared come to, after the addition to them or the subtraction from them, is the distance between those two degrees by the ascensions of the place of the preceding degree, where it is in the eastern half of the sphere.\footnote{Nallino's commentary works out the rule as: arc = α + (β − α) × the seasonal hours of the preceding degree from the meridian ÷ 6, where α is the difference of the right ascensions of the two degrees and β the difference of their ascensions in the clime.}\par
\par \ArPage{202}If the given preceding degree is in the western half of the sphere and the following degree is with it in this half too, take the times of the ascensions between them in the right sphere and the times of the ascensions in that clime between the two degrees opposite those two degrees, which is the amount between the two degrees by the times of the settings of the clime. Then take a sixth of the excess between these two numbers and multiply it by the hours of the distance of the degree from whichever cardine you like, the cardine of the earth, the cardine of the west or the cardine of the middle of the sky, whichever you want.
