Add the product to the times of the ascensions or the settings that you have from the cardine with which you compared, if they are the less, and subtract it from them if they are the greater, in the manner given before: that is, if your comparison is with the cardine of the west, add it to the settings between the two degrees in the clime if they are less than what is between them in the right sphere, and subtract it from them if they are the greater; and if you compared with the cardine of the earth or the middle of the sky, add it to what is between the two degrees by the ascensions of the right sphere if they are the less, and subtract it from them if they are the greater. The result is the distance between the two degrees by the settings of the place of the sphere where the preceding degree was.\footnote{In every clime a sign sets with as much as its counterpart, the opposite sign, rises with (chapter 13).}\par
\par If the place of the given preceding degree is in one of the two halves of the sphere and the following degree in the other half, find the amount between the preceding degree and the middle of the sky if it is in the western half, or, if it is in the eastern half, between it and the cardine of the earth, by this method that I have described to you; to the result add what lies between the part of the middle of the sky or of the cardine of the earth and the following degree by the ascensions of the right sphere: the sum is the amount between those two degrees.\footnote{Beyond the meridian the following degree must go on, along its own parallel, as many of its own seasonal hours as the preceding degree is from the meridian; Nallino notes that the codex and Plato of Tivoli do not say that the hours of the preceding degree are to be multiplied by the times of the hours of the following one, and that without this the rule is not right (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{203}If you wish to know this another way, find the hours of the distance of the given preceding degree from the cardine, as I have described to you. Then look: if the preceding and the following degrees are between the middle of the sky and the ascendant, or the preceding part is there and the following part between the ascendant and the cardine of the earth, that is, both in the eastern half, multiply the times of the hours of the day of the following part, which are the times of the hours of that part itself, by the hours of the distance of the preceding part from the middle of the sky, and subtract the product from the times between the part of the middle of the sky and the following part by the ascensions of the right sphere. If the preceding part and the following part are in the western half of the sphere, which is from the cardine of the earth to the middle of the sky on the side of the west, multiply the times [of the hours of the night of the following part by] the hours of the distance of the preceding part from the cardine of the earth, and subtract the product from the times of the ascensions between the part of the cardine of the earth and the following part in the right sphere. If the preceding part is in one half and the following part in the other, that is, the preceding part between the ascendant and the cardine of the earth and the following part between the cardine of the earth and the setting point, multiply the times of the hours of the night of the following part, taken from its counterpart, by the hours of the distance of the preceding degree from the cardine of the ascendant, and subtract the product from the times of the ascensions between the rising part and the following part by the ascensions of the clime. If the preceding part is between the west and the middle of the sky and the following part between the middle of the sky and the ascendant, that is, in different halves, multiply the times of the hours of the day of the following part by the hours of the distance of the preceding part from the cardine of the west, and subtract the product from the times of the ascensions, in the given clime, between the degree opposite the degree of the setting point and the degree opposite the following degree. What remains, whichever of the numbers it is, is the distance between the two degrees in times of the ascensions of the first degree or of its settings. In the same way, conversely, one also knows how many times of the equator ago the preceding part left the place of the following part. These amounts are needed above all in nativities, in directing the significators in their places; this is what Ptolemy mentioned in the book of the Four Treatises that Ptolemy composed on foreknowledge of events from the science of the stars; and in the same way the haylājs are directed for the lengths of life.\footnote{The bracketed words are supplied by Nallino after Plato of Tivoli. This rule is Ptolemy's, who sets it out with a worked example in the \emph{Tetrabiblos} III 10 (Nallino's commentary). Ptolemy counts as the same place of a point the place that has the same position with respect to the horizon and to the meridian, that is, the same number of seasonal hours from the meridian; on that definition both rules of this chapter are exact and give the same arc. Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary, takes the circle of position to be the great circle through the north and south points of the horizon, on which Ptolemy says such places lie only nearly, and holds the error of the rules small for the declinations of the planets in the latitudes of al-Raqqa, Antioch and Baghdad. At a latitude of 36°, three hours from the meridian, that circle departs from the seasonal hours by 3.8° of the equator for a point on the equator and one 24° north, and by almost 10° for points 24° south and 24° north; in a direction for a nativity a degree of the equator counts as a year (Nallino's commentary). The significator is the star or point that governs a life, which the Arabs also call by the Persian name haylāj; Nallino takes the last words, on the haylājs, to be probably an interpolation.}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 56. On making a horizontal and a vertical instrument, with each of which the seasonal hours that have passed of the day are known in every town; it is also called the sundial}{56}{\ArPage{203}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the seasonal hours that have passed of the day, from sunrise to sunset, with the horizontal instrument, from the sun's shadow on its surface, take a slab of marble or a plate of copper with a level surface and a smooth face, of any size you like; it is best that its width be two thirds of its length. Mark a point at two thirds of the width, on the middle of the length; take it as a centre and draw about it a circle of any size you like. Then divide the circle into quarters by two lines that cut each other at its centre at right angles and divide the circle into equal quarters, and divide each quarter into 90 parts, exactly, at intervals of a degree or more, as the size of the circle allows. Then find the shadow of the beginning of Cancer and of the head of Capricorn for 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6 seasonal hours, and the azimuth of the shadow at each of these hours on the circle of the horizon, by the methods given to you at the beginning of the book, in the chapter on knowing the azimuth of the shadow and of the altitude for the parts of the signs in every town: that is, find the altitude of each of these hours, and from it find its shadow and its azimuth in the manner given before, in any town you like.\footnote{«Sundial» renders «al-rukhāma», literally «the marble slab». The chapter on the azimuth of the shadow and of the altitude is chapter 11 (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{204}Then take a ruler with straight edges, one of whose faces is divided into as many equal parts as you like, provided they are as many as the number of the shadow of the head of Capricorn or more. Put the first point, from which you began, on the edge of the ruler, on the point of the centre of the circle, and lay the edge of the ruler along the azimuth of the shadow of one hour of the hours of Capricorn, towards the wide side of the slab, beginning the count of the azimuth from the point of the east on the circumference of the circle. Then count off on the ruler, from the point of the centre, as many parts as the shadow of the first hour, and mark there, at the edge of the ruler, a point, which is the mark of the shadow of one hour. Do the same for the shadow of two hours and the azimuth of two hours, and of three, four and five, until you come to six hours, when you mark the place of the shadow on the line that cuts between the north and the south, on the wide side, which is the meridian line. Then turn the ruler to the other quarter, next to the meridian line, and do in it as you did in the quarter before, so that the shadows of one hour, two, three, four and five fall on both sides of the meridian line, on the wide side of the slab, towards the east and the west, for the beginning of Capricorn; mark a point at the shadow of each hour. Then do the same with the azimuths of the hours of the head of Cancer, putting their shadows on the other, narrow, side of the slab, as you did with the hours of Capricorn, on both sides of the meridian line, until the shadow of the end of the sixth hour falls on the meridian line.\par
\par It is known that when the azimuth is northern, it lies towards the narrow side of the slab from the line between the east and the west, and when it is southern, towards the wide side of this line. Then join the points marked for the hours of the head of Cancer and of the head of Capricorn with straight lines, running from the point of the first hour of the hours of Cancer to the point of the first hour of the hours of Capricorn, and likewise from the point of two hours to the point of two hours, up to the end of the five hours on both sides of the meridian line. Likewise join all the points of the hours of Capricorn to one another, and the points of the hours of Cancer, by lines across the slab, which end on both sides, from the point of the first hour to the sixth, marked on the meridian line; so that the place of the shadow is a known passage on the slab, which it does not go beyond.\footnote{«Narrow» is Nallino's reading here and below; the codex has «\RL{\ArNote الصيفية}», «summer», for «\RL{\ArNote الضيّقة}» in several places.}\par
\par \ArPage{205}Then set up at the centre of the circle on the slab a style of copper or iron, round, turned on the lathe, with a pointed head, and let what shows of it above the surface of the slab be 12 parts of the parts of your ruler with which you took the amounts of the shadow. Check this style with the compasses, from the sides of the circle to its pointed end, so that you know that it stands truly upright on the centre. Make the hole that you bore for the style, at the place of the centre, go through to the other side of the slab, so that the end of the style that enters the hole is fastened from the other side firmly, so that it neither shakes nor moves. Then make the wide side of the slab its northern side and the narrow side its southern side, so that the point of the north falls on the meridian line on the wide side and the point of the south on the meridian line on the narrow side, and the point of the east and the point of the west fall in their places on the line at right angles to the meridian line, which is the line between the east and the west. Begin the hours from the side of the west, in the wide part of the slab, and write the first hour, the second and the third under each of the points of the hours of Capricorn, up to the end of the eleventh. With the dial one cannot know more than the time from the end of the first hour of the day to the end of the eleventh, because of the length and spread of the shadow at the two ends of the day, for which a very large instrument would be needed to receive the surface of the shadow. If you wish to divide between the hours into halves and thirds, more or less, find the azimuth and the shadow of each fraction that falls between those hours and mark it as you wish; that is not difficult.\par
\par When you have finished making the dial, go to a place where the horizon lies open from the first hour of the day to the end of the eleventh; draw a circle there and find in it the meridian line in the way described at the beginning of this book. Then lay the meridian line drawn on the dial on the meridian line that you found with the circle, fitting it exactly, neither inclined nor turned aside, so that the southern direction of the dial, which is the narrow side, faces the south of the line along its direction, and the wide, northern, side accordingly lies along the meridian line towards the north. Let the upper surface of the dial be parallel to the plane of the horizon, levelled with the plumb line and inclined to no side. Then from the place where the end of the style's shadow falls on the lines of the hours, the seasonal hours that have passed of the day are known in every town whose latitude is the same as the latitude for which you made the dial.\footnote{The meridian line is found in chapter 12 (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{206}The setting of the dial can also be adjusted another way. Find the altitude whose azimuth has no inclination, in the way that I explained to you at the beginning of the book; then watch the altitude, and when it has come to the amount of the altitude you worked out [for it], turn the dial until the shadow of the style falls on the line between the east and the west. When that is right, the setting of the dial is right, provided its face is levelled and not inclined. If you like, find the altitude of one hour, or of two or three: when you know it by computation, watch the shadow, and when the altitude has come to the one you want, turn the dial until the shadow of the style falls on the line of the hour for which you found the altitude. You can also find the azimuth of the altitude you want; then watch the altitude, and when it is equal to the altitude whose azimuth you found, turn the dial until the shadow of the style falls at the amount of the azimuth of that altitude on the circle drawn. If the shadow does not reach the circumference of the circle, tie a thin thread to the foot of the style and stretch it out to the amount of the azimuth from the limit of the east or of the west, on the side on which it is at the time of the observation; then turn the dial until the middle of the shadow of the style falls on that thread. Then the dial is set right, and the line of the sixth hour falls in line with the meridian line, along its direction, if God wills.\footnote{The altitude whose azimuth has no inclination is the altitude of the sun when it stands due east or due west, where its azimuth, counted from the east and west points, is nothing (chapter 12; Nallino). The bracketed words are Nallino's conjecture.}\par
\par If you wish to find from this chapter the azimuth of Mecca, which is the azimuth of the qibla for prayer, so as to draw a line along it from the centre of the circle, the line being the azimuth of the qibla in that town, find the latitude of the town in which you are and the latitude of Mecca, and find the direction of Mecca, God guard it, from that town, whether it is north of it or south. Find the longitude of Mecca and the longitude of the town and subtract the smaller from the larger, so that you know the amount between them in longitude, and where Mecca lies from that town, towards the east or towards the west: if the longitude of Mecca is greater than the longitude of the town set down in the tables of the latitudes and longitudes of the towns, Mecca is east of the town, and if it is less, Mecca is west of the town. Then put the end of the ruler at the number of the latitude between them, counting it from the line of the east towards the side on which Mecca lies in latitude, and likewise from the line of the west towards that side, on the circumference of the circle, so that the edge of the ruler passes at the amount of the latitude between them; and draw along the edge of the ruler a line joining the eastern mark and the western. Take also the difference between them in longitude and count as much on the circumference of the circle from the meridian line towards the side on which Mecca lies in longitude, on the southern part of the circumference, and count as much again on its northern part; put the edge of the ruler on the two marks and draw along its edge a straight line. Where these two lines cut each other is the place of Mecca in its azimuth from that town. Put the edge of the ruler on the centre of the circle and on the place of the intersection, and draw along it a straight line, carrying it out on the dial to the southern circumference of the circle: that line is the azimuth of the qibla in that town.\footnote{The qibla is the direction of the Kaʿba at Mecca, towards which Muslims turn in prayer (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{207}If you wish to know the amount of the azimuth of the qibla by computation, take the chord of what lies between the two towns in longitude and the chord of what lies between them in latitude; multiply each of them by itself, add the two, and take the root of the sum: the result is the hypotenuse of the triangle, which subtends the right angle, and it is the distance between the centre of the circle and the place of the intersection made by the lines of the longitude and the latitude in the circle. Keep it. Then go back to the chord of what lies between the two towns in latitude, multiply it by the half diameter and divide by the hypotenuse of the triangle; turn the result into an arc: the arc that results is the azimuth of Mecca. Count as much on the circumference of the circle from the point of the azimuth of the east or of the west, according to the place of Mecca from that town in longitude, towards the side on which Mecca lies in latitude; where it ends, make a mark on the circumference of the circle, and draw a straight line from the centre of the circle to that mark: that line is the azimuth of Mecca from that town.\footnote{The chords here must be half chords, sines, as in the construction, where the two lines stand at the sines of the two differences from the line of east and west and from the meridian line; Nallino's translation has sines. With a the azimuth from the east or the west point, the rule is sin a = sin Δφ / √(sin² Δφ + sin² Δλ), Δφ and Δλ being the differences of latitude and longitude; the construction gives the same line. This is not the azimuth of the great circle to Mecca. Nallino explains the simplification as meant for the builders of mosques, for whom an error of a few degrees did not matter (Nallino's commentary). With al-Battānī's coordinates, al-Raqqa at 73;15 of longitude and 36;0 of latitude and Mecca at 77;53 and 21;40, the rule gives 71;56 from the east towards the south and the great circle 73;1. Nallino puts the error at 3° or 4° in Syria and Mesopotamia; with the positions of the towns as now known it is about 1° there, and less than 1½° at every place between 30° and 37° north within 12° of longitude of Mecca.}\par

\par Divide the two remaining quarters in the manner of the division of these two quarters, if God wills.\footnote{In the figure the south is at the top and the east at the left; the tens of its scale, in letters in the Arabic, are given in figures. The codex adds that the picture of the dial is drawn on the other side of the leaf; its drawing is very poor, and Nallino's translation has one drawn again by Schiaparelli from the rules of the chapter for a latitude of 36° (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{208}Al-Battānī said: Take a wide, oblong, four-sided marble slab and mark its corners A B G D; take a centre at two thirds of its width and the middle of its length, marked H, draw a circle about it and divide it into quarters by two lines that cut each other at right angles, carried out to the edges of the slab. Make the one, longer, line, which runs along the length of the slab, the line between the east and the west, and the shorter line, which runs across the width of the slab, the line between the north and the south, and write on it «meridian line»; write the directions of the horizon at the ends of the lines. Make the beginning of the azimuth on the circumference of the circle at the two points of the east and the west on the longer line: what of it is southern we count towards the north, and what is northern we count towards the south, after dividing each quarter of the circle into 90 parts in black or in red, so that it leaves no lasting trace on the face of the slab; and the circle likewise. As for the two diameters of the circle, which are the two lines mentioned, we draw them by cutting, so that their trace remains on the surface of the slab. On each of the azimuths of the hours of Cancer we write the mark M, and on the azimuth of each of the hours of Capricorn the mark L, and at the place of the shadow of each hour something by which its number is known, beginning from the side of the west; and we join the points along the length and the width of the slab with the lines between the figure of the hours and their shadows on it. We put Mecca in the region of the east and the south, and mark on the arc between them in latitude M K; we take as much on the side of the west and draw through the two marks K a line parallel to the line of the east and the west. We mark on the arc between them N Ḍ, and at the place where the two lines cut each other LA, and draw the line H LA ʿ, which is the azimuth of Mecca. We make the length of the style from the mark H — it is the line H S that shows — and set it upright on the centre H. That is what we wished to show. We have made tables of the azimuths of the hours of Capricorn and of Cancer, of their shadows and of their altitudes, for a latitude of 36 degrees.\footnote{The text is defective after «N Ḍ»: the codex and Plato of Tivoli lack the marking of the difference in longitude and the line through it parallel to the meridian line, which Nallino's translation supplies from the rule above (Nallino). The tables are on f. 240r of the codex; the tables for latitudes of 36° and 38° that Plato of Tivoli has here seem to be the work of a reader of that version, and do not agree with al-Battānī's (Nallino).}\par
\par The making of the vertical dial, whose upright face faces the south, is by this same method in the azimuth; only the shadows change, in the way that we have described in the knowledge of the vertical shadow. When you have finished the dial with the amounts of the vertical shadow and set its face upright on the line of the east and the west, the face of the dial looks to the south, lying across between the east and the west, with the wide side towards the earth and the narrow side upwards. It is known that the longest shadow on this dial is at the head of Cancer and the shortest at the head of Capricorn. Let the style here too be 12 parts of the parts of the ruler by which the shadow is measured; then from the place where the end of the shadow falls on the lines of the hours you know all the seasonal hours that have passed of the day. The hours are known in many ways and with various instruments, but these two instruments are the most correct of those that I have worked with, and the safest for this knowledge, if God wills.\footnote{The vertical shadow is that of chapter 10. Taken as written, the rule for the vertical dial is not right. On a wall facing south, a style 12 parts long at right angles to the wall casts the end of its shadow 12 tan A parts sideways and 12 tan h / cos A parts down from its foot, for the sun at altitude h and at azimuth A from the south; the rule puts it 12 tan h parts from the foot, in the direction of the azimuth. The two agree only when the sun is on the meridian: with the sun at 30° of altitude and 45° from the south, the rule gives a shadow of 6.9 parts at 45° below the level, the wall one of 15.5 parts at 39° below it. «The safest» renders Nallino's reading «\RL{\ArNote أسلمه}»; Nallino also considers «\RL{\ArNote أسهله}», «the easiest», for the codex's «\RL{\ArNote اسلهه}». Plato of Tivoli lacks the passage (Nallino).}\par
\ChapterHead{Chapter 57. On the close of the book, and the making of the egg, the brick and the alidade for observation}{57}{\ArPage{209}}{}
\par \ArPage{210}Al-Battānī said: What we have mentioned and set down in our book of the causes of things and of the derivations of the principles of the computation, which proceeds by the way of geometrical proof, is in a state that does not change, and no doubt touches it in any circumstance, through all the ages. But what is established by measurements, observations, trials and tests may be found to need correction, by excess or by defect. What of it comes from establishing the very truth of the thing, and falling short of it, becomes small when it is divided over a long time, even if it is perceptible, and what is divided over a short time becomes large, even if it is small. As for an error that arises from the instrument, in its division, its setting up and its adjustment: if one tests with that same instrument while it is in its first state, the error is the same at both times; and if the error comes from the division alone, its setting up and adjustment may be corrected if they are changed, while the error from the division remains as it was, and when one observes with another instrument the difference appears. It may also happen that over a long time the instrument changes from its state, in widening, contracting, bending and the like; the error that comes from this may increase or decrease, according as these are small or great. When one measures after that with a correct measurement in which there is no doubt, there must accumulate in it later, over a period like the one between the two times, an error like the first, if it proceeds in one way without changing. Things of this kind are sound only when the measurement is made with two instruments perfect in all respects, or with one correct instrument that has not changed from its first state in anything; and what there is in them of the shortcoming of human beings, by their nature, in reaching the truths of things in act as they reach them in potency, is slight and imperceptible with diligence and care, above all over long periods. Talent helps, and so do zeal, true observation, the use of thought and patience with things, even when they are hard to grasp; and much of this is hindered by impatience and by the love of glory and of favour with earthly kings, in grasping quickly what cannot truly be grasped, or in grasping what by its nature no one can grasp.\footnote{Ptolemy makes the same points on the errors of instruments and on long intervals (\emph{Almagest} III 1; Nallino).}\par
\par Since we have come in this book to this place [and have dealt, as far as we could, with everything that had to be mentioned first, we think it right to close at this place with our description of] the instrument whose form is the form of the sphere, which is called the egg, and of the two instruments described for observation, if God wills.\footnote{The bracketed words are restored by Nallino in the commentary, in place of «\RL{\ArNote فوصفنا}», «we have described», where the printing left them out (Part III, p. 210, line 2).}\par
\par The making of the instrument in the form of the sphere, on which the stars of the ether are drawn, which is called the egg. Al-Battānī said: We take a ball of copper, perfectly round, true on every side, with a smooth surface, turned on the lathe, of any size you like. We mark on it two poles opposite each other on its diameter, halve the distance between the poles on the outside of the ball, and draw at the halfway mark a circle that cuts the ball in halves; we divide it into equal quarters and mark a point at each quarter. We take one of the points as a centre and draw about it a circle as large as the first, which passes through the first two poles of the ball and cuts the first circle into two opposite halves. We divide one of the quarters of the first circle into 90 and take from it the amount of the whole declination, which is 23 parts and 35 minutes; we take with the compasses as many of the parts of the quarter, put one of its points on one of the two poles and turn the other point to the second circle, whose pole is one of the points, and mark a point on it. We do the same with the other pole, turning the point of the compasses to the side opposite the first, so that one of these two points is opposite the other on the diameter of the circle as well. We take one of them as a pole and draw about it a circle midway between these two points; so we have drawn two circles that cut each other at two opposite points. We make one of the two circles the circle of the equator and the other the circle of the sphere of the signs. It is known that the pole of the circle of the sphere of the signs lies below the pole of the equator, towards the north, and that the circle that passes through the poles is the circle of Cancer and Capricorn; the point of the circle of the sphere of the signs that is above the equator is the point of the head of Cancer, and the point below the equator the point of the head of Capricorn; of the two points at which the circle of the sphere of the signs and the circle of the equator cut each other, one is the point of the head of Aries and the other the point of the head of Libra.\footnote{The instrument joins the uses of Ptolemy's spherical astrolabe (\emph{Almagest} V 1) and of the solid sphere (\emph{Almagest} VIII 3); al-Battānī may have been the first to devise it (Nallino's commentary). 23;35 is al-Battānī's obliquity of the ecliptic.}\par
\par \ArPage{211}We draw the signs in their order, making each quarter three signs, divided equally, each sign into six cells with 5 parts in each cell, and we write on the cells the totals of the count in the letters of the abjad, up to 30 parts. We complete the division of the circle of the equator into 360 parts, in which there are 72 cells, and write in each cell its number in the letters of the abjad, up to 360 parts, beginning the writing from the point that cuts the head of Aries, so that the end of the 360 also falls at the beginning of this point, which is the end of its twelfth sign.\footnote{The letters of the abjad have the values of numbers.}\par
\par We draw the positions of the fixed stars in all the constellations, or those of them that we like, in the way that I shall describe. We take with the compasses from the circle of the equator the amount of the latitude of the star, put one point of the compasses on the part in which the star is and turn the other point towards the side of the latitude, drawing a faint line that leaves no lasting trace on the ball. Then we take other compasses, opening their points to a quarter of the circle that goes round the ball, and put one point on the quadrature of the star's part on the circle of the signs, 90 parts from the star's degree; the other point then necessarily falls on the part in which the star is. We turn it towards the line that we drew for the latitude with the other compasses: where the two lines cut each other is the centre of the star, and we draw it there; and so until we have finished all that we want of them in this way, each according to its place in longitude and latitude, having drawn first, if we like, through each sign a circle passing through it and through the two poles of the sphere of the signs, to show the divisions of the signs more clearly. So there are twelve circles on the outside of the ball, passing through the poles of the sphere of the signs and dividing the signs.\footnote{The second compasses, opened to a quarter of a great circle with one point 90 parts from the star's longitude, draw the circle through the poles of the ecliptic and the star's degree; on it the star lies at its latitude from the ecliptic.}\par
\par Then you take a ring of copper with square faces, true in its roundness and its edges, its thickness the breadth of a thumb and its breadth as much as its strength requires, so that it does not shake; and you take another ring like it, made in the same way, striking its inside with the compasses of the inside of the first and its outside with the compasses of its outside, and you file it until it is even on every side and true in its roundness. Make the width of each of these two rings the amount of the diameter of the ball, so that the ball turns inside these two rings, fitting them closely. You take two other rings, making the thickness of one of them a third of the thickness of one of the two [first] rings and the other two thirds of that thickness, so that when the smaller of these two is set in the larger they are like one of the two rings: that is, you strike the inside of the smaller with the compasses of the inside of the two rings and its outside as is fitting, and the inside of the larger with the compasses of the outside of the smaller and its outside with the compasses of the outside of the two rings. You take also a fifth ring, striking its inside with the compasses of the outside of the large rings mentioned above and its outside as is fitting, so that these rings turn inside this ring, fitting it closely, without play or shaking in any of these rings; and let its faces be even.\footnote{The text passes from «we» to «you» here and back to «we» below, as the codex does (Nallino).}\par
\par \ArPage{212}Then you take one of the two first rings as the ring of the horizon, and divide it and the rest of the remaining rings into equal quarters, dividing each quarter into 18 cells of 5 parts each, so that each quarter has 90 parts; you write in the cells, in the letters of the abjad, what is due to them, beginning the count from one of the quarters up to the end of the 90 on both sides; you divide the opposite quarter in the same way and write it in the letters of the abjad as well, so that the 90, which would fall in four places of the ring, falls in two places of it, fixed at the end of each quarter. On one of the two places where the 90s meet you write «the point of the north», and on the place opposite it «the point of the south». You set a mark on the small ring at one of its quarters and make it the north pole, and the one opposite it, half the ring away, the south pole; you bore this ring at these two opposite places with a hole in the middle of its breadth and thickness, and likewise you bore the two poles of the equator in the ball, and fix the ball in this small ring at these two places. We nail it with two nails filed flush with the outside of the ring, so that the ball turns on the two poles of the equator, which are these two poles. Then we fit on it the ring inside which this one lies, after dividing it into 360 parts and 72 cells and writing on it in the letters of the abjad as we wrote before, except that the letters written in the cells reach to the edge of the ring and those that fall in the circle of the horizon two thirds of that; we make the writing in the manner given before, so that the 90s meet in two opposite places, twice in each place.\par
\par \ArPage{213}Then, from the place where the count began, towards the lower part of the ring, we cut a notch sunk into this ring to half its thickness, making the width of the notch the thickness of the ring of the horizon; this notch is on the outside of this ring; and we cut a like notch at the opposite place as well. Then we cut in the ring of the horizon, on its inside, a notch as deep as the notch in the other ring together with the thickness of the small ring, making the notch on both sides of the line of the north and the south, evenly, as wide as the thickness of the ring in which we cut the first notch. Then we fit one of the two rings into the other on the ball, so that the plane of the circle of the horizon bounds the upper half of the ball, the thickness of the ring lying towards the lower half, and from every side of the plane of the ring of the horizon to the top of the dome there are 90 parts. Then we cut on the outside of the ring of the horizon, on both sides of the line of the east and the west, two even, opposite slots, half its thickness deep, and in the inside of the other remaining ring, on both sides of its two opposite quarters, a notch as deep as the notch of the other ring, and we fit it on the ring of the horizon, having also cut on the outside of the ring of the middle of the sky, on both sides of the line of the dome and of the cardine of the earth opposite the dome, a notch half the thickness of the upper ring deep, and cut in this ring, on its inside, on both sides of the two remaining quarters, a notch as deep as that notch together with the thickness of the small ring in which the poles are. When we have done this, the ring that stands on the ring of the horizon and cuts between the north and the south is the ring of the middle of the sky, with the line of the middle of the sky in the middle of its thickness; and the other ring, which cuts between the east and the west, bounds the part of the ball between the north and the south, with the line of the east and the west in the middle of its thickness.\footnote{The top of the dome is the zenith, 90 parts from the horizon all round, and the line of the dome the upper end of the meridian, opposite the cardine of the earth below; Nallino compares the dome of the earth of chapter 6.}\par
\par Then you divide the quarters of the great ring, in which these rings turn, into 90 parts and 18 cells, and we set in each cell its number in the letters of the abjad up to 90, as we did before. We bore in the middle of the thickness of this ring a hole going through, on both sides of the line of the quarter from which we began the division, and above it we cut a slot in the top of the ring, on both sides of the line, as long as a quarter of the ring. We make a square piece of copper, of the thickness of the ring and the width of the slot, and cut in its middle a straight line dividing it into two equal halves; we file it on both sides of this line with the file and thin it towards the lower part of the piece, filing it round, and make its lower end sharp like a nail; its length is such that it goes through the edge of the ring and its sharpened lower end touches the face of the ball. We notch the rest of its square part, in the slot, to the depth of the slot; and what shows of it above the ring is the size of a thumb, or as is fitting, so that this part that shows is an indicator of the ray and of the altitude; we fix it in its place whenever we wish. Then we mount this ring in two clamps like the pin of the astrolabe, with two pointed ends, for which we bore holes in the middle of the thickness of the ring of the middle of the sky and of the ring between the east and the west; the ring runs in the housings of these two pins, as with the horse in the pin of the astrolabe, towards the north and the south. We make the upper of them a loop and a ring, by which the ball is hung as the astrolabe is hung. We contrive to fasten the ends of the two pins so as to fix the ring in its place, and it turns with the turning of the pins towards the east and the west; we contrive this by cutting in the great ring a slot as long as the end of the pin that goes into the hole, so that when the ring is set right in its place we fasten it with a piece of copper that fills it, and it does not move from its place, if God wills.\footnote{The horse is the wedge through the pin of the astrolabe that holds its parts together (Nallino's commentary). Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary, explains the instrument with a drawing: the globe turns on the poles of the equator in the smallest ring, a movable meridian, which turns in its own plane inside a fixed meridian joined to the horizon and the prime vertical; this frame turns about the vertical line inside the great ring, a vertical circle that carries the indicator of the sun's rays.}\par
\par \ArPage{214}When we wish to take the altitude in any town we like, we raise the north pole of the equator, marked on the small ring, above the northern horizon by the latitude of the town and fix it in that state; then we fit the indicator of the ray and the altitude in its place, hang the ball by our hands as the astrolabe is hung by its handle, and turn the indicator towards the sun in the quarter of the horizon in which it is; we turn the ring towards the north and the south until the indicator shades itself, which happens only when it points straight at the sun. Then we find the part of the signs in which the sun is, turn that part to the quarter in which the sun is, and keep the ring as it is: the parts of the quarter that rise above the circle of the horizon are the amount of the altitude. When we move the ring towards the part of the sun, we keep moving it and moving the part of the sun until the sharp end of the indicator that touches the ball falls on the part of the sun drawn on the line of the sphere of the signs; and this can happen as we have described only at the place of the sphere where the sun is at that time, according to its altitude above the horizon. When it falls so for us, the sphere stands for us in its configuration at that time: the part of the sphere of the signs that the ring of the horizon cuts on the side of the east is the part rising at that time, and the part that it cuts on the side of the west is the part setting; the part of the sphere of the signs cut by the middle of the thickness of the ring of the middle of the sky is the part in the middle of the sky, and likewise the cardine of the earth opposite it.\par
\par When we wish to know the hours that have passed of the day, we look at the arc of the equator that the ring of the horizon cuts from the time when the sun's part rises on the ball until the part rising at that time rises: that is what has turned of the sphere from sunrise to the hour of the measurement, and every 15 degrees of it are one equal hour; divided by the times of the hours of the sun's part, it shows the seasonal hours. Whenever we raise the pole, the turning of the signs and the increases of the day become clear to us, until we raise it 90 parts; and the ascensions of the signs in every town become clear in the usual way, and other things. We should write on the ring of the horizon, in the third of it that remains, towards the north, «the summer risings», and towards the south of the line of the east «the winter risings»; likewise from the line of the west, towards the north, «the summer settings», and towards the south «the winter settings»; so that we have shown all that is needed of the azimuths of the risings and the settings. When we have set the part of the sun, with the indicator in its state on it, facing the sun, the ring of the middle of the sky faces the meridian line.\footnote{Nallino takes «the third» for an error for «the quarter».}\par
\par This is the picture of the brick for observation, so that the account may refer to it.\footnote{The brick is Ptolemy's plinth (\emph{Almagest} I 12; Nallino). In the figure the quarter of the circle is divided from the side A B, 5 to 90; the letters of these numbers are given in figures.}\par

\par Al-Battānī said: You take a square brick of copper, of stone or of wood, its side two cubits; the larger it is, the more accurate. It is the brick A B G D. We take the point A as a centre and draw about it, with the amount of A B, A G, the arc B G, and divide it into 90 divisions, the parts of the quarter, by lines that pass through the centre and the divisions marked on the arc, and between the parts into as many minutes as possible. Let the face of the brick be smooth, perfectly level, neither inclined nor shaking, so that the divisions on it are true.\par
\par \ArPage{216}Then we take two pegs of copper of equal size, turned on the lathe, pointed at the ends, and fix one at the centre of the point A and the other at the centre of the point G. We have first found the meridian line, which is the line H Z, letting the plumb line down from the end of the rod at the centre A onto the end of the peg at the centre G, so that neither the face of the brick nor its setting is inclined; the face with the drawings and the divisions faces the east, and its side on which A B are lies along the direction of the south. We observe the shadow at the times of noon and note the place of the shadow of the peg at the centre A among the divisions of the quarter each day. We take a piece of copper fitted to the curve of the arc B G, the piece Ṭ, and draw in its middle a line, the line at the place Ṭ, so that this piece is put under the place of the shadow and its place among the parts shows clearly, so that its marking is not confused for us; the line Ṭ is on the middle of the width of the peg's shadow, and so we know on which line of the parts of the divisions and of their minutes it falls. From that we know the extreme distances of the sun from our zenith in summer and in winter: let the point Ḥ be the summer limit and the point K the winter limit; so the arc K Ḥ is the arc between the two solstices, and its half is the mark L. Whenever the sun passes the vernal or the autumnal equinoctial point, the shadow of the peg at the place A falls on the point L of the arc B G. By this the distance of the sun from the point of the zenith is always known, every day, and its altitude above the horizon, if God most high wills; success is from God. The brick must also be truly square, its angle a right angle in agreement with the count, if God most high wills.\footnote{For G the codex has B, here and below; «the rod» is, Nallino thinks, the peg; for «the south» the codex has «the qibla», and for «autumnal» «winter» (Nallino). Nallino takes «which is the line H Z» for a wrong addition of the codex, which Plato of Tivoli has too: in the codex's figure H and Z stand near B and D, and the meridian line, along which the brick stands, can only be A B. The noon zenith distances of the two solstices are the latitude less and more the obliquity, so that the arc K Ḥ is twice the obliquity and L stands at the latitude of the place; Ptolemy found the arc between the solstices with this instrument (\emph{Almagest} I 12).}\par
\par This is the picture of the long alidade.\footnote{The long alidade is Ptolemy's parallactic instrument (\emph{Almagest} V 12); other Arabs call it «the one with two branches» (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par\begin{center}\begin{tikzpicture}\draw[line width=2.19pt] (21.77pt,234.33pt) circle[radius=3.87pt];

\node[inner sep=0pt] at (16.50pt,223.85pt) {\normalsize Z};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (15.70pt,75.15pt) {\normalsize Ṭ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (108.60pt,78.15pt) {\normalsize L};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (187.00pt,99.85pt) {\normalsize K};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (209.90pt,104.90pt) {\normalsize M};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (0.00pt,46.95pt) {\normalsize A};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (45.05pt,45.45pt) {\normalsize D};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (30.80pt,31.40pt) {\normalsize Ḥ};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (8.20pt,0.10pt) {\normalsize B};
\node[inner sep=0pt] at (43.10pt,0.00pt) {\normalsize G};\end{tikzpicture}\end{center}
\par \ArPage{217}Al-Battānī said: We take three rulers of wood, straight, with square faces, and draw along the middle of each of them a line running along the length of its face; we make this figure a picture of the lines that run along the middles of the rulers: they are the ruler Z Ḥ, the ruler Z L and the ruler Ṭ M. We put on the ruler Z Ḥ, on its line, the mark Ṭ, making the line Z Ṭ five cubits, and fix the line Ṭ Ḥ, the rest of the ruler, in a stone or a pillar, firmly, so that it neither moves nor changes nor shakes. Then we take the second ruler, which is smaller than the third, and make its line Z L equal to the line Z Ṭ; we put on it two sights of copper of equal size, like the sight of the astrolabe, across its breadth, which passes over the face of the ruler Z Ṭ, fixing them firmly on the face of the ruler, and make in their middles two holes opposite each other; we fix one of the two sights near the point Z and the other near the point L. We bore these two rulers at the mark Z and join them with a pin, fastening it as we fasten the pin of the astrolabe, so that we can move the ruler Z L towards the north and the south as we like, without shaking, play or twisting. Then we take the ruler Ṭ M and make its line Ṭ K equal to each of the lines Z Ṭ and Z L; we divide the line Ṭ K into 30 parts, and between the parts into as many minutes as possible, a true division of equal sizes, and divide the line K M, the rest of the ruler, by the size of those divisions, as finely or as coarsely as we like, up to the halved chord of 45 parts, so that the most that the divisions of the line Ṭ M reach is about 42½ parts; what remains of the ruler we cut off. Then we bore the rulers Z Ṭ and Ṭ M at the point Ṭ with two round holes, as we bored the first ones, and join them with a pin, fastening it as we join the pin of the astrolabe, so that we can move the ruler Ṭ M to the north and the south as we like, without play or shaking.\footnote{Nallino expects «the smallest of the three», as Plato of Tivoli has; for «these two rulers» the codex has «these two sights». The cubit of the astronomers is the common cubit of 24 digits, about half a metre (Nallino).}\par
\par In the ruler Ṭ M, along the line Ṭ M, we cut a slot in half its breadth, its upper, outer half, as deep as half the thickness of the ruler, over its whole length; likewise we cut in the end of the ruler Z L a notch of half the thickness and the breadth of the ruler Ṭ M, and take off the corners of the square of the ruler Z L on both sides, little by little, so that its turning and its motion along the line Ṭ M are easy and smooth; because of the slots that we have cut, the faces of the two rulers lie in one plane, neither rising above the other nor standing out from it. Then we turn the pillar A B G D, in which we have fixed the ruler Z Ṭ Ḥ, until the line B G of its square stands on the meridian line, and the plumb line, let down from the point Z, falls on the point Ṭ, so that the ruler stands at right angles; the face of the ruler stands upright on the meridian line, levelled on it, facing the east; so do the two sights fixed on the second ruler, and the divisions on the ruler Ṭ M face the east too, drawn along the half of the ruler in whose other half the slot was cut.\footnote{For Z the codex has D (Nallino).}\par
\par When the sun passes the meridian line, we move the ruler with the two sights towards the north and the south until the upper sight shades [the lower sight] entirely and the ray of the sun passes from the hole of the upper sight into the hole of the lower sight; at the same time we draw out the ruler Ṭ M and move it towards the north and the south until we bring the line Ṭ M, along the breadth of the slotted ruler, against the point L of the ruler Z L, by means of the two slots that we cut; and we note at which number of the divisions on the ruler Ṭ M the point L falls, enter it in the table of the halved chords and find its arc: doubling the arc that comes out, the result is the distance of the sun from the point of the zenith, if the count of the ruler begins from the point Ṭ. Likewise, if we divided the line Ṭ K into 60 parts, by the amount of the half diameter, and divided the line K M up to 85 parts, then took the number on which the point L falls, took its half, found its arc and doubled the arc that came out, the meaning would be the same.\footnote{The bracketed words are supplied by Nallino. Ṭ L is the chord of the sun's zenith distance in a circle whose radius Z Ṭ is 30 parts, so that its length is the halved chord, in the table of radius 60, of half the zenith distance; at 90° from the zenith it is 42.4 parts, and with Z Ṭ of 60 parts 84.9, whose half is entered in the table. Ptolemy marks the scale on the upright ruler and refers the third ruler to it; al-Battānī marks it on the third ruler itself, as Regiomontanus was later to do (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{218}Observation with this ruler is more accurate, because it is made on a circle whose diameter is ten cubits. Likewise, if we made the length of the ruler Z L twice as much, or less, so that we pass on to the mark ʿ, and fixed the sight that is near Z at the place ʿ, the distance between the two sights would be greater, and so more accurate. The altitude can be taken with these rulers at any time, if the ruler Z Ḥ is mounted firmly in the pillar A B G D so that we can turn it to the regions of the horizon wherever the sun is. Likewise, if the altitude of the moon or of another star needs to be taken with them, it is known how great the altitude is when the arc of the distance from the zenith is subtracted from 90: it is what remains from the zenith to 90 when the arc of the distance is subtracted from it, if God wills.\par
\SectionHead{A. On finding the beginnings of the years of the Arabs and of their months, which are worked with in dating by the tables}{57A}{}{\footnote{Eight short chapters without numbers follow chapter 57 in the Escorial codex; Nallino marks them A to H. Plato of Tivoli has none of them. Nallino holds them genuine except D and probably F: A, B and C complete chapter 32, at whose end al-Battānī promised them, E is announced at the end of chapter 53 and H in chapter 42, and they explain al-Battānī's own tables, beside which some of them probably once stood (Nallino's commentary).}}
\par \ArPage{219}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the sign of the year you are in, of the years of the Arabs, which are the years of the Hijra, look at how far the years of the Hijra exceed 210 years: cast them off by 210s and work with what remains below that, for in every 210 years they return to their first order, always. When you know the years that result, with the year you are in, even if only one day of it has begun, look for the same number in the rows of the numbers of the tables of the collected years and take the sign of the years opposite it; if you do not find the same number in the collected years, look for the nearest to it below it, and where you find it take the sign of the years under it. Then see what years remain up to the year you are in, enter them in the row of the single years and take the sign of the years under them, and add it to the first sign, which you kept from the collected years: if the sum is more than 7, cast off 7 from it, and what remains is the sign of the year you want. If you entered the number in the collected years and nothing remains to enter in the single years, always add one to the number you find under the collected years; or, if you like, take in the collected years what is 30 less than the number you have, take what stands opposite 30 in the single years and add the one to the other: you will hit the right result whichever way you work, and it is the sign of the year you are in. Cast off the number from Sunday: the day at which your count ends is the day on which the year begins, the first day of al-Muḥarram of that year. If you want another of the months, take the sign of the month you want from the table of the months and add it to the sign of the year; if the sum is more than 7, cast off 7 from it, and cast off what remains, or what you have below 7, from Sunday: the count stops at its end at the name of the day on which the month you want begins. By this reckoning you work in finding the motions of the stars in the tables set out for the era of the Arabs; pay no attention to any other, if it gives more or less.\footnote{The sign of a year is the day of the week of its first day, counted from Sunday as 1. A common Arab year of 354 days moves it on by 4, a leap year of 355 days by 5, thirty years by 5, and so 210 years by 35, a whole number of weeks; for the astronomers the epoch of the Hijra was a Thursday, so that the sign of the first year is 5. The signs of the collected years are one less, since they count from a year before the Hijra, and so one is added when they are used alone (Nallino's commentary). The words «at which your count ends» are partly eaten by worms in the codex (Nallino).}\par
\par If you wish to know the leap years of the Arabs, cast off the years of the Hijra by 30s and compare what remains with these letters: any year that agrees with one of these letters is a leap year, and one that does not agree with them is not a leap year. The letters are 2, 5, 7, 10, 13, 16, 18, 21, 24, 26 and 29. I shall show this in the single years of the Arabs in the table for finding the signs, if God most high wills; success is from God and from no other.\footnote{The letters are numerals. Nallino restores 7, 18 and 26, the leap years of almost all the Arab astronomers and of civil use, for the codex's 8, 19 and 27, which contradict the signs of the years in al-Battānī's own tables and may come from Ḥabash (Nallino's commentary). Eleven leap years in thirty make 10631 days.}\par
\SectionHead{B. On knowing the beginnings of the Roman months by the era of Dhū l-Qarnayn, cast off by 28, with the addition of a year}{57B}{}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the beginning of any month you want of the Roman months, and on which day of the week it falls, take the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn with the year you are in, counted from the entry of Aylūl, even if only one day of it has begun, and add to that one year, as a base already past, always. Cast off the sum by 28s, and look for what remains below 28, or 28 itself, in the tables of the single Roman years, and take what stands opposite it in the row of the month you want of the Roman months: the result is the sign of that month. Cast it off from Sunday: it brings you, against the number, to the name of the day on which that month begins. The first of Aylūl is the first day of the year. The leap year is known from the mark that you find against the month of Subāṭ: you find «leap year» on it in every four years, if God wills.\footnote{«Already past» is Nallino's conjecture for the codex's meaningless «\RL{\ArNote ماصلا}», here and in C. A common Roman year of 365 days moves the day of the week on by 1 and a leap year by 2, so that after 28 years, 10227 days or 1461 weeks, the signs of the months return in the same order. Nallino holds the rule false: adding a year to the given years would be right if every Roman year were common, but after a leap year the sign grows by two, so that the rule fails whenever the given year is a leap year; one should enter the table with the given year and add one to its sign (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\SectionHead{C. On knowing the eras of the Arabs and of the Rūm, each from the other, from these tables}{57C}{\ArPage{220}}{}
\par \ArPage{221}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to know the era of the Rūm from the era of the Hijra, look in the years of the Hijra written in the first rows for the same number as the years you have, and where you find it, take what is under it of the number of the Roman years in the table in which the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn are written, and the number of the days passed of the month written under the years: the years, the complete months and the days passed of the Roman month that result are what has passed of the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn, with the year you are in, and the day passed of the Roman month, up to the first day of al-Muḥarram of the year of the Hijra that you are in. Then find on which day of the week it falls, by taking what stands under that year of the Hijra that you are in, of the number of the days of the week, in the column headed «the first of al-Muḥarram»: it is the day passed of the Roman month that you found in the table, and it agrees with the era set down at the beginning of the book. If you want another of the Roman months, from the month of the Arabs that you are in, find on which day of the week the beginning of the Arab month you want falls: take the number of the days by which you knew the first of al-Muḥarram and add to it the sign of the month you want of the Arab months, set down in the table of the signs of the Arab months; if it is more than 7, cast off 7 from it, and what does not make up 7 cast off from Sunday: the day at which you stop is the first day of the Arab month that you wanted. Find how many days of that month have passed, from its beginning; then count from the first of al-Muḥarram to the day you want of that month, reckoning for each month of the year that has passed its number of days, one month 30 days and one month 29 days; from all these days, together with the days passed of the month you want, subtract one day, and to what remains add the number of the days passed of the Roman month that you found in the table. Cast off the sum from the beginning of that Roman month, for each month its number of days: the day to which you come, of the month in which that number ends, is the day you are in of the Roman month, of the year of Dhū l-Qarnayn that you found in the table, until you come to Aylūl; if one day of Aylūl has begun, add another year to the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn; and if the year is a leap year and the count reaches Subāṭ, take 29 days for it in that year. The leap year is known from the mark set down in the table made for the signs of the Roman months.\footnote{The era set down at the beginning of the book is that of chapter 32.}\par
\par In the same way you know the day you are in of the Arab month, of the year of the Hijra you are in, from the era of the Rūm: look in this table, in the row of the years of Dhū l-Qarnayn, and take what stands against the year of Dhū l-Qarnayn that you are in, of the days passed of the Roman month: on that day passed of the month falls the first of al-Muḥarram of the year of the Hijra that you are in. Keep it. Then take the days that remain of that Roman month and add to them what you have of the months and days after that month up to the day you want, taking for each month its number of days; add one day to the sum, as a base already past, always; then cast off the sum from al-Muḥarram, for each month its number of days: the day to which you come is the day of the Arab month that you are in, of the year that you found of the years of the Hijra, until you come to al-Muḥarram; when you come to it and one day of it has begun, add another year to the years of the Hijra that you have. On this era your work is done, if God wills.\par
\SectionHead{D. On knowing the direct motion of the stars, their stations and their retrogradations, and their crossing of their epicycles}{57D}{}{}
\par \ArPage{222}Al-Battānī said: Of the stars, Saturn moves directly from the top of its epicycle for 118 days and a half; then it stays for 2 days, a half, a quarter and an eighth; then it goes back in its course for 135 days, a third and a quarter; then it stays a second time, like its first stay, and then moves directly like its first direct motion until it returns to the top of its epicycle. All its days, in which it crosses the epicycle, are 378 days and 2 hours, approximately. When its corrected anomaly is from 0 to 112;45, it is direct; when it passes that up to the end of 115;29, it is stationary; when it passes that up to the end of 244;31, it is retrograde; when it passes that up to the end of 247;15, it is stationary; and up to the end of the circle, direct, if God wills. Jupiter moves directly from the top of its epicycle for 137 days and a half; then it stays for 3 days, a quarter and a fifth; then it goes back in its course for 117 days; then it stays a second time like its first stay and then moves directly like its first direct motion. All its days, in which it crosses its epicycle, are 399 days less 2 hours, approximately. When its corrected anomaly is from 0 to 124;5, it is direct; up to the end of 127;11, stationary; up to the end of 232;49, retrograde; up to the end of 235;55, stationary; and up to the end of the circle, direct.\par
\par Mars moves directly from the top of its epicycle for 341 days and a fifth; then it stays for 25 days and a third; then it goes back in its course for 46 days and two thirds of a day; then it stays a second time like its first stay, and then moves directly like its first direct motion again. All its days, in which it crosses its epicycle, are 780 less a tenth of a day, approximately. When its corrected anomaly is from 0 to 157;33, it is direct; up to the end of 169;14, stationary; up to the end of 190;46, retrograde; up to the end of 202;27, stationary; and up to the end of the circle, direct. Venus moves from the top of its epicycle directly for 269 days and half a sixth of a day; then it stays for 4 days; then it goes back in its course for 37 days and four fifths of a day; then it stays a second time like its first stay, and then moves directly like its first direct motion. All its days, in which it crosses its epicycle, are 583 days, two thirds of a day and a quarter of a day, approximately. When its corrected anomaly is from 0 to 165;53, it is direct in its course; up to the end of 168;21, stationary; up to the end of 191;39, retrograde in its course; up to the end of 194;7, stationary; and up to the end of the circle, direct.\par
\par The Scribe moves directly from the top of its epicycle for 46 days, a third and a quarter of a day; then it stays about 20 hours; then it goes back in its course for 21 days and a tenth of a day; then it stays a second time like its first stay, and then moves directly like its first direct motion. All its days, in which it crosses its epicycle, are 115 days, a half, a quarter and an eighth of a day, approximately. When its corrected anomaly is from 0 to 144;40, it is direct; up to the end of 147;13, stationary; up to the end of 212;47, retrograde; up to the end of 215;20, stationary; and up to the end of the circle, direct in its course; and God knows best.\footnote{The Scribe is Mercury, a name used by the western Arabs, as in the passage interpolated into chapter 50. Schiaparelli, in Nallino's commentary, takes the chapter for an interpolation that misreads the tables of the stations of the planets, Ptolemy's in \emph{Almagest} XII and al-Battānī's own: the anomaly of the first station changes with the place of the epicycle on the eccentric, from 112;45 for Saturn when the epicycle is at the apogee to 115;29 at the perigee, and the chapter makes that range a stay, which for Mars becomes 25⅓ days. Ptolemy's and al-Battānī's own rule for the stations is that of chapter 46. Nallino corrects the numbers only to the interpolator's meaning, taking the anomalies from al-Battānī's tables of the stations and dividing them by the mean daily motion in anomaly; the codex has 112;44 and 247;14 for Saturn, 116 days, 369 days and 235;0 for Jupiter, 24⅓ days, 700 in words (with 780 in numerals above), 167;32 and 202;26 for Mars, 3 days and 483 days for Venus, and 215;0 for Mercury. Each count of days is the arc of anomaly divided by 360 parts in the days of the whole period, to within about a tenth of a day, and the periods are the synodic periods of the planets.}\par
\SectionHead{E. On knowing the times of the revolutions of the years, their ascendants, and the excesses of the mean motions of the stars in them over their mean motions at the root, by the tables set out for this at the end of the book}{57E}{\ArPage{223}}{}
\par Al-Battānī said: Whenever you wish to know the times of the revolutions of the years, their ascendants and their excesses in the mean motions over the mean motions of the stars at the root, take the complete years that have passed for the nativity, from the year of the root to the year of the revolution, having found the mean and the true position of the sun at the root. Then look for the same number as the complete years you have in the table of the collected Roman years, and where you find it, or the nearest to it below it, take what you find under it of the times of the ascensions and keep it; then enter with the remainder of the years that you have in the table of the single years, take what stands opposite it of those times too, and add it to the first. What results, after casting off the circle if it is more than a circle, keep, and divide it by 15: the result is equal hours. Add them to the hours of computation at the root: if the hours come to more than 24, cast off 24 from them and add a day to the days passed of the month of the root, and if they are less than 24, work with them. Look at the days passed of the month and the hours that you then have: if the year is a leap year and Subāṭ has run out, subtract one day from the days passed of the month, and if it is not a leap year, subtract nothing. Then compute the sun at that date of that year: the mean of the sun comes out for you like the first mean, of the root; compute its true position by the equation, according to the place of its apogee in the year of the revolution. If the true position of the sun comes out like the first, [that is the time]; if not, find its excess over the first, or its shortfall from it, and divide that excess or that shortfall by the varying motion of the sun in an hour: the hour that results, add to the hours of computation if the second position of the sun is less than the first, and subtract from them if it is the greater. The hours of computation that you have after that, convert into the hours of the unequal days, by looking at what stands opposite the sun's part of the equation of the days set down in the right sphere: find its amount in equal hours and add it to the hours of computation: the result is the hours of the time of the revolution. Multiply them by fifteen, add to them the ascensions of the sun's part in the right sphere, and find from this the ascendant and the middle of the sky as usual. Then enter with the number of the complete years in the tables of the motion of the moon and of the stars in the years of revolution, and take what stands opposite it.\footnote{The bracketed words are added for the sense. The equation of the days is added here to turn the mean hours into true ones, as the rule of chapter 34 requires, which subtracts it to turn true hours into mean; chapter 53 has «subtract». To be added to the ascensions the hours must be multiplied by fifteen; the text, and Nallino's translation, have «divide». These tables are on ff. 239v–240r of the codex (chapter 53).}\par
\par \ArPage{224}What results is the excess of the mean motions of the stars and of the node. For the moon, its anomaly and the node, you add what results for each of them to its mean at the root; for the three upper ones, add it to their means at the root and subtract it from the anomaly of each of them at the root; for Venus and Mercury, you add what results for each of them to its anomaly at the root, while their means are like the mean of the sun. When you have done that, compute them as usual. Know that the times of the revolutions fall short by one day in every hundred and six years: whenever 106 years have passed, always subtract one day from the days passed of the month of the root, if God wills. This does not come about in nativities; it comes about only in the lives of cities, dynasties and religions, when their revolutions are needed, if God most high wills.\footnote{For 106 the codex has «a hundred and sixty», twice (Nallino's notes to the Arabic; the Latin notes give 166). The year of the revolution, 365 days 5 hours 46 minutes 24 seconds, is 13 minutes 36 seconds shorter than the Roman year, which in 106 years make a day (chapter 53).}\par
\SectionHead{F. On directing the degrees from wherever you like to wherever you like, approximately}{57F}{}{}
\par Know that this directing is what Ptolemy explained in the directing of the haylāj, where the ascensions in it are called al-jūy-rāst, saying the jūy-rāst of Capricorn, which are the ascensions of the right sphere, and the jūy-rāst of the town, which are the ascensions of the town; it is the correction of their distance from the cardines, if God most high wills.\footnote{Nallino takes this chapter for spurious: it repeats chapter 55 more briefly, leaves out the planets with latitude and uses words and turns foreign to al-Battānī, found otherwise among the arithmeticians of western Africa and Spain. \emph{Jūy-rāst} is Persian, «right ascension»: rāst is «right»; the codex always has «\RL{\ArNote جواراسب}». The one printed Arabic text in which Nallino found the word gives it for the ascensions of the right sphere, and Kūshyār's tables «by the jūy-rāst» are right ascensions counted from the beginning of Capricorn, so that its use here for the ascensions of a town seems false. «In the directing» follows Nallino's later reading «\RL{\ArNote تسيير}» for «\RL{\ArNote تسيُّر}» (Nallino's commentary).}\par
\par \ArPage{225}Al-Battānī said: If you wish to direct a degree to some place, look at the degree from which you wish to direct, which is the first degree, and at the second degree, to which you wish to direct, which is the other. Then subtract the ascensions in the right sphere of the first degree from the ascensions in the right sphere of the second degree: the remainder is the first residue; keep it. Then subtract the ascensions of the first degree in the clime from the ascensions of the second degree in that clime, as you did first with them in the ascensions of the right sphere: the remainder is the second residue; keep it as well. Then take the difference between the first residue and the second residue, and see to which of them the excess belongs, the first residue or the second; keep it and mark it with its name: it is the difference of the two residues. Then take the distance of the first degree from the degree of the cardine before it. If the cardine before it is the tenth, express this distance as a fraction of half the arc of the day and take that fraction of the difference of the two residues: the result is the share. Then look: if the excess belongs to the first residue, subtract the share from the first residue, and if the excess belongs to the second residue, add the share to it; what the first residue comes to after the addition to it or the subtraction from it is the number of the degrees of the direction, if God wills. If the cardine before it is the fourth, take the distance between the degree from which you direct and the degree of the fourth, express it as a fraction of half the arc of the night, and take that fraction of the difference of the two residues: the result is the share. Then look: if the excess belongs to the first residue, subtract the share from the first residue, and if the excess belongs to the second residue, add the share to it, I mean to the first residue: what the first residue comes to after the addition to it or the subtraction from it is the number of the degrees of the direction.\footnote{«Express as a fraction of» and «that fraction» render «name from» and «that name», technical words of the arithmeticians of western Africa and Spain for dividing a smaller number by a greater: the rule takes the difference of the residues times the distance divided by half the arc of the day, that is, times the seasonal hours of the distance divided by 6, as in chapter 55 (Nallino's commentary). The tenth and the fourth are the cardines of the middle of the sky and of the earth, the beginnings of the tenth and the fourth house.}\par
\par If the cardine before it is the ascendant, take the space between the rising part and the degree from which you direct, take its ratio to half the arc of the night, and take in that ratio of the difference of the two residues: the result is the share; keep it. Then look: if the second residue is more than the first residue, subtract the share from the second residue, and if it is the less, add the share to the second residue: what the second residue comes to after the addition or the subtraction is the number of the degrees of the direction. If the cardine before it is the seventh, take the distance between the degree from which you direct and the degree of the seventh cardine, take its ratio to half the arc of the day, and take in that ratio of the difference of the two residues: the result is the share; keep it. Then look: if the second residue is more than the first residue, subtract the share from the second residue, and if the second residue is less than the first residue, add the share to it: what the second residue comes to after the addition to it or the subtraction from it is the number of the degrees of the direction, which are the corrected parts, mixed from the ascensions of the town and of the right sphere.\par
\SectionHead{G. On knowing the ascensions of the signs in the right sphere at intervals of ten parts, and the halved chords of these ascensions, to make the ascensions of the signs easy to know for any town you like}{57G}{}{}
\par \ArPage{226}We have set down the ascensions of every ten parts, summed with what comes before them, up to 90, in the right sphere, and drawn the halved chords of the ascensions under them. When we wish to know the ascensions, we work in the manner given at the beginning of the book: we take the chord of half the excess of the longest day in that town and multiply it by the chords of these tens set down under them, and find the arc of the result: the arc that results is the share of the collected tens in the difference of the day. We proceed so until we tabulate it for all the signs at intervals of ten parts; this suffices for what is needed of the knowledge of the single ascensions for the town and for places whose latitude agrees with it. This work is done with the little table set down under the square of the ranks of the declination.\footnote{The chords here are halved chords, sines. The rule is exact: since the tangent of the declination of a degree is the tangent of the obliquity times the sine of its right ascension, the sine of the difference of the day at that degree is the sine of half the excess of the longest day times the sine of the right ascension. The difference of the day, subtracted from the right ascension, gives the ascension in the town (chapters 13 and 19).}\par
\SectionHead{H. Chapter}{57H}{}{}
\par If you wish to know the varying motion of the moon in an hour, for the computation of the conjunction and the opposition, take the exact difference between the sun and the moon and find what stands opposite it in the tables of the increase and the decrease of the motion of the moon in an hour — this is for each degree in particular. The minutes that there are, subtract from the varying motion of the moon in an hour when the corrected anomaly of the moon, for which you found its motion in an hour, is from 0 to 95 and from 265 to 360, and add them to that motion when that anomaly is from 95 to 265: the result after the addition or the subtraction is the exact motion. Subtract from it the motion of the sun in an hour: the remainder is the gain of the moon.\footnote{This is the short chapter announced in chapter 42, where Schiaparelli's explanation is given: the correction is about one second an hour for each degree of the elongation from the sun, taken off in the upper part of the epicycle and added in the lower (Nallino's commentary).}\par
